<meta>This is the front cover of the manuscript. There is no written text to translate, but the physical characteristics of the binding provide context for the volume's age and status.</meta>

->**[Front Cover]**<-

<note>The manuscript is bound in dark brown leather, featuring intricate blind tooling—a technique where decorative patterns are pressed into the leather without the use of gold leaf. The design consists of a multi-layered panel arrangement. The outermost border is decorated with a delicate, lace-like floral pattern. Each corner of the inner frame is marked with a fleur-de-lis stamp, a stylized lily often associated with nobility or high-quality craftsmanship. The central rectangular panel is defined by several concentric lines, creating a sober yet elegant appearance typical of 16th to 18th-century scholarly or legal volumes.</note>

<summary>The front cover of a leather-bound manuscript featuring decorative blind-tooled panels and fleur-de-lis corner stamps. The craftsmanship suggests a professional binding from the early modern period.</summary>
<keywords>bookbinding, leather, blind tooling, fleur-de-lis, decorative arts, manuscript exterior</keywords>

# THE ELMER BELT LIBRARY OF VINCIANA

->A gift to the Library of the University of California, Los Angeles, from Elmer Belt, M.D., 1961<-

<term>Elmer Belt Library of Vinciana</term> <note>A world-renowned collection of books and manuscripts relating to Leonardo da Vinci, donated to UCLA by the prominent urologist and bibliophile Dr. Elmer Belt.</note>
<term>Leonardo da Vinci</term> <note>The quintessential "Renaissance Man" (1452–1519), known for his mastery of art, science, and engineering.</note>
<term>UCLA</term> <note>The University of California, Los Angeles.</note>
<term>Perspective machine / Perspectograph</term> <note>An instrument used by Renaissance artists to help translate three-dimensional views onto a two-dimensional surface using geometric principles.</note>
<term>Marbled paper</term> <note>A decorative paper created by floating pigments on a liquid bath and transferring the pattern to a sheet; the "stone and shell" pattern seen here was a popular choice for high-quality bookbinding.</note>

<summary>This page features the official bookplate of the Elmer Belt Library of Vinciana, marking the volume as a 1961 gift to UCLA. The bookplate includes an illustration of a Leonardo da Vinci perspective machine and is set against a traditional marbled paper background.</summary>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page follows the institutional bookplate and continues the introductory sequence of the volume, focusing on the decorative endpapers that precede the main text.</meta>

# DECORATIVE ENDPAPER

The interior of the volume is adorned with a fine example of **marbled paper**, a traditional craft used in high-quality bookbinding to protect the book and provide a beautiful transition from the cover to the printed pages.

<note>The pattern shown here is a classic "snail" or "curl" design, created by dropping pigments onto a bath of thickened water (often using carrageenan or gum tragacanth) and then combing the colors into intricate swirls before laying the paper on top to absorb the ink.</note>

<margin>Hand-marbled in the traditional style</margin>

The colors present in this specific pattern include:
*   **Deep Blue** <note>likely derived from indigo or Prussian blue pigments</note>
*   **Terracotta and Ochre** <note>earth tones common in 18th and 19th-century European bindings</note>
*   **White** <note>the original color of the paper base showing through the veins of the pattern</note>

<column-break/>

This style of paper, often referred to as <term>Ebru</term> <note>A Turkish word for the art of marbling, meaning "cloud art."</note> or <term>Stone Marbling</term> <note>A specific technique where the paint is dropped to create organic, stone-like shapes before being manipulated with a stylus.</note>, was a hallmark of the <insert>Vinciana Collection's</insert> commitment to preserving the aesthetic standards of the Renaissance and early modern periods.

->This page intentionally left blank of text to showcase the artistry of the binder.<-

<summary>This page displays a decorative marbled endpaper featuring a complex blue and terracotta swirl pattern, typical of high-quality historical bookbinding. It serves as a visual introduction to the craftsmanship of the volume within the Elmer Belt Library of Vinciana.</summary>

<keywords>marbled paper, bookbinding, ebru, endpaper, Elmer Belt Library, decorative arts, conservation</keywords>

# TITLE PAGE

->**THE LIFE OF LEONARDO DA VINCI**<-

<margin>First Edition</margin>

Written by the hand of the most excellent <term>Biographer</term> <note>The term used here refers to a chronicler of lives, likely Giorgio Vasari or a contemporary follower.</note> and now presented for the first time in this elegant format.

The text contains:
*   **The Early Years** in Vinci and Florence
*   **The Milanese Period** under the patronage of Ludovico Sforza
*   **The Final Years** in France at the court of King Francis I

<column-break/>

<insert>Property of the Elmer Belt Library</insert>

"Great love springs from great knowledge of the beloved object." <note>original: "L'amore è tanto più fervente, quanto la cognizione è più certa." This is a famous maxim by Leonardo himself regarding the relationship between science and affection.</note>

->Printed in the year of our Lord **MDCCXCI**<-
<note>1791</note>

<summary>This page serves as the formal title page for a biography of Leonardo da Vinci, outlining his life stages from Florence to France and featuring a famous quote by the artist regarding knowledge and love.</summary>

<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, biography, Florence, Milan, Francis I, 1791, Elmer Belt Library</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page follows the formal title page and a blank leaf, moving into the primary dedication or introductory preface of the biography.</meta>

# DEDICATION

->To the Illustrious and Noble Patron<-

Most Excellent Sir,

It is with the greatest humility that I present to your lordship this account of the life of **Leonardo da Vinci**, a man whose genius was so vast that it seemed more divine than human. <margin>Dedicated to the Count of Melzi's descendants</margin> As the ancients used to say, "The excellence of the mind is the true nobility," <note>original: "Nobilitas sola est atque unica virtus." A common Latin sentiment derived from Juvenal, emphasizing that virtue and intellect surpass inherited rank.</note> and certainly, no mind has ever reached such heights of excellence as that of the Florentine master.

In these pages, you will find not only the history of his paintings—such as the *Last Supper* in Milan—but also his secret studies into the nature of water, the flight of birds, and the very anatomy of man. <insert>Corrected from the original manuscript</insert>

<column-break/>

I have labored to ensure that the <term>volgare</term> <note>The "vulgar" or common Italian language, as opposed to formal Latin.</note> used herein is clear and accessible to all who admire the arts. It is often said that "Art has no enemy except the ignorant," <note>original: "Ars non habet inimicum nisi ignorantem." A traditional legal and artistic maxim.</note> and thus, by spreading knowledge of Leonardo's life, we defend his memory against the passage of time.

May your lordship receive this small token of my esteem with the same kindness you show to all practitioners of the fine arts.

->Your most devoted and humble servant,<-

->**The Author**<-

<summary>This page contains a formal dedication to a noble patron, praising Leonardo da Vinci's divine genius and outlining the book's intent to cover both his artistic masterpieces and scientific inquiries.</summary>

<keywords>dedication, Leonardo da Vinci, Last Supper, anatomy, patronage, art history</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page follows the blank flyleaf and serves as the primary title page for the first printed edition of Leonardo da Vinci's "Treatise on Painting." It transitions from the physical buffer of the book's binding into the formal presentation of the text.</meta>

### TREATISE
# ON PAINTING
## BY LEONARDO
# DA VINCI,

->Newly brought to light, with the life of the same author, written<-

->BY RAPHAEL DU FRESNE.<-

->*The three books on painting and the treatise on the statue by Leon Battista Alberti have been added, along with the life of the same.*<-

## IN PARIS,

->At the shop of **Giacomo Langlois**, ordinary printer to the Most Christian King <note>A formal title for the King of France, in this case Louis XIV</note>, at Mount St. Genevieve, opposite the fountain, at the sign of the Queen of Peace.<-

---

->1651.<-

->**WITH THE PRIVILEGE OF THE KING.** <note>original: "CON PRIVILEGIO DEL RE." This indicates a legal monopoly granted by the crown to print and sell the work for a set period.</note><-

<summary>The formal title page of the landmark 1651 first edition of Leonardo da Vinci's Treatise on Painting, edited by Raphael du Fresne and published in Paris. It also includes works by the influential Renaissance architect and theorist Leon Battista Alberti.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, Treatise on Painting, Raphael du Fresne, Leon Battista Alberti, Giacomo Langlois, Paris 1651, art theory, Renaissance printing</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page is the verso (back side) of the title page translated previously. It is physically blank, serving as a structural transition before the dedication or preface begins.</meta>

<warning>This page is essentially blank, containing no primary printed or manuscript text on this side. There is significant show-through (ghosting) from the printed text on the recto (the other side of the leaf), which appears in reverse. A very faint red circular institutional stamp is visible at the bottom center.</note>

<meta>The reversed show-through text identifies the work as "TREATISE ON PAINTING BY LEONARDO DA VINCI".</meta>

<note>In early modern printing, the back of a title page was frequently left blank to prevent the heavy ink of the title from bleeding into the subsequent text, or to provide a clean separation between the title and the dedication.</note>

<summary>A blank verso page from the 1651 edition of Leonardo's Treatise on Painting, featuring only institutional markings and ink ghosting from the title page.</summary>
<keywords>blank page, verso, show-through, institutional stamp, Leonardo da Vinci, bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal dedication of the work to Queen Christina of Sweden, following the blank verso of the title page. The signature mark "ã iij" indicates this is the third leaf of the first gathering.</meta>

### TO THE MOST SERENE AND MOST POTENT
## PRINCESS CHRISTINA,
### BY THE GRACE OF GOD
## QUEEN
## OF THE SWEDES, GOTHS, AND VANDALS,
### GRAND DUCHESS OF FINLAND,
### DUCHESS OF ESTONIA AND KARELIA,
### LADY OF INGRIA, &c.

---

->*MOST SERENE QUEEN,*<-

**I**t is seen through the long memory of the most illustrious histories that the art of painting has always been held in high esteem, and everyone knows that Alexander—who for greatness of soul and deeds was the Gustavus <note>A reference to Gustavus Adolphus, Christina's father and a famed military leader, comparing him to Alexander the Great.</note> of his century—held the great Apelles <note>The most renowned painter of ancient Greece, who served as the exclusive portraitist for Alexander the Great.</note> in honor. I shall say nothing of Fabius, who, in that city where kings held themselves honored by the title of citizen <note>Rome.</note>, was called "the Painter" <note>original: "il Pittore", referring to Fabius Pictor, a member of a noble Roman family who practiced painting.</note> for his exercise of such a noble art, and left that name to his family. Nor, to induce Your Majesty to value this virtue, do I believe it necessary to remind you that the Emperor Antoninus, with those hands that gave laws to the

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of Leonardo da Vinci's treatise to Queen Christina of Sweden, establishing the nobility of painting by citing historical precedents like Alexander the Great and Roman aristocrats.</summary>
<keywords>Queen Christina of Sweden, dedication, Alexander the Great, Apelles, Fabius Pictor, Emperor Antoninus, nobility of art</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Queen Christina of Sweden, moving from historical examples of royal painters to the Queen's own virtues and the specific circumstances of this publication.</meta>

world, delighted at times in handling the brushes. Everyone knows the love that she <note>Queen Christina.</note> bears for letters and all the fine arts, and the world admires her as both the protectress and possessor of even the most hidden sciences <note>original: "più recondite scienze", referring to the Queen's famous interest in philosophy, alchemy, and deep scholarship.</note>; it is amazed to see the power of empire so happily united with so many virtuous habits. Hoping, therefore, that this work—which I have consecrated to her merits and which bears the august name of Your Majesty on its frontispiece—will be pleasing to her, I have entreated Monsieur Bourdelot <note>Pierre Michon Bourdelot (1610–1685), a French physician and scholar who was a favorite at Christina's court.</note>, the delight of the learned men of our nation and one who has a particular taste for matters of painting, to present it to Your Majesty, so that through the kindness of the giver, the gift may acquire more grace in her eyes. 

The author <note>Leonardo da Vinci.</note>, who wrote at the beginning of the last century, was favored by great princes; and King Francis I <note>Francis I of France (reigned 1515–1547), a major patron of the Renaissance.</note>, who like her was the guardian deity of the virtuous, desired to have him in his court despite his being burdened with years, and it is said that he died in the King's arms. Fortunate old man, whose luck it is today to live again in the hands of a lady who, by her rule over so many fierce and warlike nations, may be called the most powerful, just as for her virtue she is the most accomplished and glorious princess of the universe; and who, by those who speak the language of the gods <note>Poets and scholars.</note>, must with one voice be called the Queen of Parnassus <note>In mythology, Mount Parnassus was the home of the Muses; this title celebrates her as the supreme patron of the arts.</note>. 

But in order not to penetrate further into the wide field of her praises—this not being a subject proportioned to the thinness of my style—I come to humbly entreat Your Majesty to accept my labors. For the reputation of Leonardo da Vinci and for the public utility, I have restored a very important work which, accompanied by her illustrious name, shall overcome the shadows of oblivion and pass on to the most distant posterity. I shall remain happy if she deigns to receive it with a benign countenance, just as I give and dedicate it with the lively affection of my heart, being no less reverent of her greatness than an admirer of her glories.

->OF YOUR MAESTY,<-

->The most humble and most devoted<-
->servant,<-
->RAFAELLE TRICHET<-
->DU FRESNE.<-

<summary>The editor, Du Fresne, concludes his dedication to Queen Christina by praising her intellect and asking her to accept Leonardo da Vinci's treatise, which he has restored for the public good. He mentions Monsieur Bourdelot as the intermediary and invokes the legendary death of Leonardo in the arms of King Francis I.</summary>
<keywords>Queen Christina, Leonardo da Vinci, Francis I, Bourdelot, Parnassus, dedication, patronage, restoration of texts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dedication of Leonardo da Vinci's Treatise on Painting, shifting focus from the Queen of Sweden to her physician, Pierre Bourdelot, and detailing the provenance of the manuscripts used for this edition.</meta>

### TO THE VERY ILLUSTRIOUS AND MOST EXCELLENT LORD
# LORD PIETRO BOURDELOT,
## Chief Physician to the Most Serene Queen of Sweden.

->*Very illustrious and most excellent Lord, and my most revered master*<-

**I** believed that the nobility of the art whose precepts are explained in this work, and the merit of Leonardo da Vinci, who is its author—as well as the beauty and curiosity with which the book has been printed—could not be adorned with a name more glorious than that of your great Queen. I also believed that if Your Excellency would lend me your hands on this occasion, my offering would be more welcome. I easily persuaded myself that by entreating you, as I do, you would not deny me this service, as much for our long friendship as for the love you bear for painting. This taste, born in both you and me at the same time—namely, when we were so accurately examining the beauties of both the old and the new Rome <note>original: "l'una e dell' altra Roma," referring to the ancient ruins and the modern Renaissance/Baroque city.</note>—has grown through continuous application and has become, especially in you, ever more refined and exquisite. In printing this treatise, I have made use of various manuscripts. The one belonging to Monsieur de Chantelou <note>Paul Fréart de Chantelou (1609–1694), a major French collector and patron of Poussin.</note> was the most noble due to a good number of figures sketched by the learned hand of Monsieur Poussin <note>Nicolas Poussin (1594–1665), the leading painter of the French Baroque classical style.</note>. He received it from the most virtuous Knight del Pozzo <note>Cassiano dal Pozzo (1588–1657), a celebrated scholar and secretary to Cardinal Barberini, known for his "Paper Museum."</note> at the time when he had gone to Italy to acquire beautiful things; had our great Cardinal <note>Cardinal Richelieu (1585–1642).</note> not died for the glory of the kingdom, he would have brought Rome to Paris. The other manuscript, which is much more correct, was shared with me through the courtesy of Monsieur Thévenot <note>Melchisédech Thévenot (1620–1692), a French scientist and traveler.</note>, a gentleman adorned with every kind of fine literature and knowledge. But due to the ignorance or negligence of those who copy books, or for whatever other reason, few chapters were found without some obstacle, especially those involving a bit of geometry <term>geometria: the mathematical study of perspective and proportion essential to Renaissance art</term>, which remained almost unintelligible due to the absurdity of the figures. I hope to have restored everything to its original purity. However, many things remain that seem to require the file <note>A metaphor for polishing or refining a literary work.</note>: there are many useless repetitions, many truncated arguments, the phrasing is rubbed out in several places, and although there is some order in the chapters, it is not such as is required in a perfect work. From this, one easily concludes that Leonardo da Vinci never gave it the final touch. Nonetheless, it is considerable and of marvelous utility, and is always worth more—as Your Excellency knows a sketch by Michelangelo <note>Michelangelo Buonarroti (1475–1564).</note> is—than four finished statues by any other mediocre sculptor. Having therefore purged it as much as possible, to further illustrate it, the figures have been engraved with that diligence which you see. Monsieur Errard <note>Charles Errard (1606–1689), a painter and architect who helped found the French Academy.</note>, a most talented painter who, for his profound science of drawing, can only be compared with the most excellent men of recent centuries—and of whom some philosopher might say that, because of the true taste he has for ancient things, the soul of one of those first masters had passed into him—is the one to whom the completion and ornaments of the work are owed, having added several fi-

<summary>The translator dedicates the work to Pierre Bourdelot, praising his connoisseurship and detailing how manuscripts from Poussin, Dal Pozzo, and Thévenot were synthesized to correct Leonardo's unfinished treatise.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the dedication to Pierre Bourdelot, detailing the editorial process of Leonardo’s treatise and the inclusion of works by Leon Battista Alberti and new biographies by the editor.</meta>

figures, and among others those seen toward the end of the book, where it discusses the method of draping and clothing figures: for the rest, use was made of those ideas and sketches by Monsieur Poussin <note>Nicolas Poussin (1594–1665)</note> which were found in the manuscript of Monsieur de Chantelou. Such is the history of this treatise. I believed that Your most excellent <gloss>eccellentiſſima</gloss> Lordship would be pleased to be informed of it. Due to the similarity of the subject matter, the three books on painting by Leon Battista Alberti <note>Alberti (1404–1472) was a foundational humanist author and architect; his "De Pictura" was the first modern treatise on the theory of painting.</note> have been added, as well as the treatise on the statue by the same author, which was no longer to be found, having never been printed except for a single time. Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the "father of art history" and author of the *Lives of the Artists*.</note> previously wrote the lives of both authors, but because he omitted many things worthy of being observed, I have set myself to write them anew, adding what the reading of books and some knowledge I have acquired of the affairs of Italy have suggested to me. I have written in the Italian language because, knowing enough of it to be understood, it seemed to me that the accompaniment of the work required it. If on another occasion I have the opportunity to speak Latin or French, perhaps I will succeed better and be able to explain my concepts more happily. Meanwhile, I pray Your most excellent <gloss>eccellentiſſima</gloss> Lordship to be my protector and to offer my writings that help which they could perhaps not hope for from their author, manifesting the superabundance of your love in the multitude of my defects. And I kiss your hands a thousand times.

->Of *Your most excellent Lordship*,<-

->Most devoted and most cordial servant<-
->**RAFAELLE TRICHET DU FRESNE.**<-

### LIFE
## OF LEONARDO
# DA VINCI
-> DESCRIBED BY <-
### RAPHAEL DU FRESNE.

<insert>S</insert>ince the nobility of blood—which is an imaginary thing—creates such a distinction among men that it raises some above others, who is he who would not judge that the nobility of the soul—which consists of actual virtue and resides in that part which draws its origin from heaven—is destined to carry men from the lowest state to the very borders of divinity? Adorned with this true and most radiant nobility, Leonardo da Vinci was able to equal the greatest men of his century in glory and honors; and, rising above the lowliness of his birth, he lived, associated, and died with great kings and princes; and, what is granted to few, he left immortality to his name. He was born in the castle of Vinci, located in the lower Val d'Arno <note>The valley of the river Arno in Tuscany.</note>, not too far from Florence <note>original: "Fiorenza"</note>, and his father was Piero da Vinci. This man, noticing the genius of his son, who among his other studies always applied himself to drawing, resolved to help that natural inclination of his. Having brought him to Florence, he decided to place him with Andrea Verrocchio <note>Andrea del Verrocchio (c. 1435–1488), a master of the early Renaissance who taught both Leonardo and Perugino.</note>, a painter of some reputation at that time. Verrocchio, admiring the young man's talent, made that judgment which time later proved most true; and having accepted him as his disciple, he promised Ser Piero to instruct him all the more because a close friendship existed between them, and because Leonardo, through his fine manners and character, seemed to him worthy of his care. In the school of Andrea—who not only applied himself to painting, but was also a sculptor, architect, engraver <term>intagliatore: a carver or engraver of wood, stone, or gems</term>, and goldsmith—he learned not only the art of painting, but also all those others where design <term>disegno: the foundational skill of drawing and conceptualizing a work</term> played a part. And such was the progress he made there, that in a short time he left be-

<summary>This page introduces the biography of Leonardo da Vinci by Raphael du Fresne, emphasizing that Leonardo's innate virtue and talent elevated him from humble origins to royal circles. It details his early life in Vinci and his apprenticeship in Florence under the multi-talented master Andrea Verrocchio.</summary>

# LIFE

behind his own master. Of whom it is read that while painting a panel for the monks of Vallombrosa, who are at San Salvi outside of Florence, depicting the story of Saint John baptizing Christ, he wished for Leonardo to assist him, and gave him an angel to color, who held some garments in its hands. He executed this with such mastery, as was commissioned of him by Andrea, that it far surpassed the rest of the work; and everyone clearly judged that the other parts of the painting were much inferior in beauty to the angel. Verrocchio blushed, and seeing himself surpassed by a youth who was his pupil, grew indignant against his own brushes; he never again wished to use colors, and said goodbye to painting forever.

Having left the school, Leonardo, being already of an age to govern himself, produced those works in Florence that are mentioned by Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari (1511–1574), the famous biographer of Italian Renaissance artists.</note>: namely, for the King of Portugal, the cartoon <note>A full-scale preparatory drawing for a fresco or painting.</note> of Adam and Eve when they sinned in the earthly paradise, in which, besides the two figures, he painted in chiaroscuro <note>A technique using strong contrasts between light and dark.</note> with incredible patience and diligence the trees and the grasses of the meadows. He also made, at the request of his father Piero, for a peasant of his from Vinci, a composition upon a buckler <note>original: "rotella" — a small round shield.</note> made of fig wood, featuring such a variety of strange little animals, such as snakes, lizards, green lizards, crickets, and locusts, that from all of them together one was formed, so frightening and horrible that, in the manner of the head of Medusa, it rendered anyone who looked at it motionless with stupor. But the father, judging that this was not a work to be put into the hands of a peasant, sold it to certain merchants; it was later bought for 300 ducats by the Duke of Milan. He made in a painting a most rare Madonna, and among other things he counterfeited <note>In this context, "imitated" or "reproduced with great realism."</note> a carafe full of water with some flowers inside, upon which with admirable artifice he had imitated the dew on the water: which painting Pope Clement VII later possessed. Vasari also makes mention of a drawing made on a sheet for Antonio Segni, his very close friend, in which with rare invention and his ordinary accuracy he depicted a Neptune in the midst of a troubled sea, with his chariot drawn by sea-horses, accompanied by orcas, tritons, and other fantastic things that seemed to him appropriate for such a subject.

In this place we shall observe that although Da Vinci knew to such a degree what that divine proportion consisted of—which is the mother of beauty—that his figures full of grace inspired love in the beholders, he nonetheless took such pleasure in painting bizarre and distorted things that if he encountered some peasant who, with a strange and somewhat out-of-the-ordinary face, appeared a bit ridiculous, being enamored by the bizarreness of the object, he would have followed him an entire day, until, having a perfect idea of him, he returned home and drew him as if he had him present. And Paolo Lomazzo <note>An Italian painter and writer on art theory (1538–1592).</note> observes in the sixth [book] of painting, chapter 32, that in his time Aurelio Lovino had fifty of them drawn by his hand in a book. In this genre is painted that picture which is seen here in Paris among many others preserved in a room of the royal palace of the Tuileries under the care of Monsieur Le Maire, a painter, as everyone knows, of no ordinary value; in which are painted two knights in the act of taking a banner by force from two others: which group was part of a larger work, namely the cartoon he made for the hall of the palace of Florence, as will be said below, but for its beauty it was painted by him in a small volume with incredible taste and love. Here, beyond the fu- <meta>catchword: fu- (likely "furia" or "furore" regarding the battle scene)</meta>

<meta>This page continues the description of Leonardo's early works in Florence, specifically his fascination with grotesque figures and the "Medusa," before transitioning into a comprehensive list of his many talents and a moralizing sonnet attributed to him.</meta>

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.
->c ij<-

*...the fury of horses, and the bizarre nature of the costumes, one sees the heads of the combatants wrinkled, inflamed, and infuriated, with an air so extraordinary and extravagant, and so to speak, caricatured and mask-like, that at one and the same time they awaken both fear and laughter in the souls of the onlookers.*

*Returning to the first works of* Leonardo da Vinci, *Giorgio Vasari says that he began on an oil canvas a head of* Medusa <note>The mythological Gorgon with snakes for hair; this specific painting is now lost, though a later Flemish version in the Uffizi was long mistaken for it.</note> *of extravagant invention, which remained imperfect. He also gave a beginning to a panel of the* Adoration of the Magi, *in which there were some most beautiful heads; but it was never finished, as was usually the case with almost all of his things. Because having an infinity of beautiful ideas, and being of a lively nature and most fertile genius, no sooner had he begun a work than the thought came to him to put another into execution. And besides the profession of painting, which by that most diligent manner embraced by him could have occupied him entirely, he attended to sculpture and modeled divinely well. He was most intelligent in geometry, and in mechanics he never ceased thinking of new devices, and was the inventor of diverse machines. He was a very fine architect, and knew the science of mirrors and perspective as well as any other. He also studied the properties of herbs, and penetrating with his genius even into the heavens, he applied himself to the studies of* astronomy, *and made many observations concerning the motion of the stars. In music he proved admirable, and was so graceful in singing and playing that he surpassed all the musicians of his time: and so that no virtue should be lacking in him, that same fury inspired in him by* Apollo <note>The Greek and Roman god of music, poetry, and the arts.</note> *which made him a painter and musician, also made him a poet. But all his compositions having been lost, only this moral sonnet has come down to us.*

> He who cannot do what he wills, let him will what he can,
> For to will what cannot be is folly.
> Therefore, the man is to be held wise
> Who withdraws his will from that which he cannot do.
> Because all our delight and sorrow
> Lies in knowing how to will and be able to say "yes" and "no,"
> Therefore, he alone can do what is right
> Who draws reason out from its sheath.
> Nor is it always right to want what one is able to do;
> Often that which turns bitter seems sweet.
> I once wept for what I desired after I had it.
> Therefore you, reader of these notes,
> If you wish to be good to yourself and dear to others,
> Will always to be able to do that which you ought.

*He was also distracted by many pleasures, because he liked horses beyond measure and handled them with dexterity; and being no less agile and robust of limb than of beautiful presence, and graceful in his every action, he was a distinguished fencer and swordsman. But above all, he delighted in conversing often with friends, and was so well-mannered in his dealings, and explained his thoughts with such grace and urbanity, that he drew to himself the souls of whoever listened to him.*

<summary>This page details Leonardo da Vinci's diverse genius, ranging from unfinished paintings like the Medusa and Adoration of the Magi to his mastery of science, music, and poetry. It includes a moral sonnet on the alignment of will and duty, and describes his physical prowess and charismatic social nature.</summary>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Leonardo da Vinci, moving from his early Florentine period to his arrival in Milan under the patronage of Ludovico Sforza, focusing on his engineering feats and the creation of "The Last Supper."</meta>

# L I F E

*Such rare qualities, and so great an acquisition of sciences, spread the name of Leonardo throughout all Italy, and induced Ludovico Sforza, called "the Moor" <note>Ludovico Sforza (1452–1508), Duke of Milan, nicknamed "Il Moro" likely due to his dark complexion or his heraldic use of the mulberry tree (moro).</note>, who favored the virtuous and was liberal toward almost everyone, to call him to Milan, assigning him an annual salary of five hundred scudi. The first thing that prince did was to form an academy for architecture, into which he introduced Leonardo, who, casting out the Gothic manners of the first school—already established in that same city a hundred years prior under Michelino <note>Michelino da Besozzo, a prominent late Gothic painter and architect in Milan.</note>—opened the way to restore that art to its first and ancient purity.*

*He was then employed by the same prince to lead the waters of the Adda as far as Milan, and to form that navigable canal, commonly called the Martesana canal, with the addition of more than two hundred miles of navigable river as far as the valleys of Chiavenna and Valtellina. The undertaking was difficult and important, and worthy of the fine genius of Leonardo for its noble competition with the Great Canal <note>The Naviglio Grande.</note> which was made two hundred years earlier in the times of the Milanese Republic on the other side of the city, by which the waters of the Ticino river were diverted for navigation and for the irrigation of the countryside as far as Milan. But he overcame all the difficulties that were encountered, and with multiplied locks—or as we might say, supports—he made ships travel with great ease and safety through mountains and valleys.*

*The prince, not content that Leonardo should illustrate his state as an architect and engineer, also wished him to adorn it with some signal work of painting. He therefore ordered him to paint the Supper of Christ with the Apostles in the refectory of the Dominican fathers of Santa Maria delle Grazie: which was executed by Leonardo with such mastery that the work was thereafter esteemed by everyone as a miracle of painting. And truly, all the refinements of art were displayed there with such pomp that everyone writes, and it is common report, that in design, expression, diligence, or coloring, nothing superior to this was ever seen. The grace and majesty he gave to the heads of the apostles were no ordinary thing, and especially to those of the two Jameses <note>James the Greater and James the Less.</note>, so that when he came to finish that of Christ, being unable to reach a more eminent degree of beauty, he left it imperfect in despair.*

*And because, while working on the picture, it seemed to the prior of the convent that the work lasted too long, he often complained of it with importunity to Leonardo; indeed, he carried his grievances even to the ears of the Duke. The Duke, discussing it once with Leonardo, learned from him that nothing remained to be done but the two heads of Christ and Judas. And that, being unable to imagine the infinite beauty of the Son of God, he knew even less how he could express it with brushes. But as for the ugliness of Judas, son of hell, which kept him in thought, he would not lack the snout <note>original: "ceffo" - a derogatory term for a face or muzzle.</note> of that ungrateful friar, who with an intolerable and insolent tediousness had rendered himself beyond measure troublesome to them both.*

*He succeeded marvelously, as Vasari writes, in expressing that suspicion which had entered into the apostles, wanting to know who betrayed their master. And Lomazzo relates (who, having made a large copy of it in San Barnaba in Milan, had that work strongly impressed upon his soul) that in each one was seen admiration, terror, grief, suspicion, love, and similar passions and affections in which they all then found themselves, and final-*

It includes the famous anecdote regarding the Prior's impatience and Leonardo's use of the Prior's face as a model for Judas.</summary>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Leonardo da Vinci, specifically discussing the completion of "The Last Supper" in Milan, its deterioration, and the subsequent history of Leonardo's scientific and anatomical manuscripts after his death.</meta>

-># BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.<-

*...mind in Judas the betrayal conceived in his soul, with a countenance exactly resembling a villain. Thus Leonardo well demonstrated how perfectly he understood the motions that the soul is wont to cause in bodies, which is the most delicate part of art and, due to its difficulty, the least practiced. Such a work was worthy of immortality, but being painted in oil upon a damp wall, it has been of short duration, and today it is entirely ruined. Francis I <note>King of France (r. 1515–1547), a great patron of Leonardo.</note> desired, when he was in Milan, that every means be attempted to carry it to France and enrich his kingdom with it; but being painted upon a thick wall, thirty feet high and wide, the thought proved vain. It is, however, likely that he had some copies made of it, and one of those is perhaps the one seen today in the royal parish of St. Germain, nailed to the wall on the left hand as one enters said church through the door that faces south.*

*In the same refectory where Leonardo painted that Last Supper <note>original: "cenacolo"</note>, he also portrayed from life Duke Ludovico and Duchess Beatrice, his wife, both on their knees with their children before them, and a Christ on the Cross on the other side. He also painted for the same Duke, on an altar panel, the Nativity of Christ, which was sent to the Emperor.*

*Among Leonardo's other occupations during his stay in Milan, the study he made concerning the anatomy of men was most important; in this, being aided by Marcantonio della Torre—who at that time read and wrote on this subject in Pavia—he became most perfect in it, and made a book of it drawn in red chalk and hatched with a pen, which later remained in the hands of Francesco Melzi, his disciple. He also drew for Gentile Borri, who professed the art of arms (in which Leonardo himself took much delight), an entire book of men fighting on foot and on horseback, in which the rules of that science were seen expressed. And for the glory and growth of his Milanese Academy, and for the instruction of the academicians, he wrote many things and composed several works on diverse subjects, which remained for a long time neglected and almost unknown with the Melzi gentlemen at their villa in Vaprio <note>original: "Vauero"</note>, and were later dissipated and dispersed here and there, as is the ordinary fortune of books.*

*For there was a certain Lelio Gavardi of Asola, provost of St. Zeno in Pavia and a close relative of Aldo Manuzio <note>The famous Venetian printer and humanist.</note>, who, having been the tutor of the Melzi gentlemen and going often to said villa, took thirteen volumes from it and later carried them to Florence, hoping for a great price from the Grand Duke. But meanwhile that prince died, and Gavardi came to Pisa; finding there Giovanni Ambrogio Mazzenta, a Milanese gentleman who was at his studies at that time, he felt a scruple regarding the ill-acquired goods, was struck with remorse, and begged him that upon returning to Milan he should restore the books to the Melzi gentlemen. This he did; but in returning them, Signor Orazio Melzi, head of that family, marveled at the punctuality of both men and made a gift of said books to Signor Giovanni Ambrogio, which then remained in the house of the Mazzenta family. As they made too pompous a display of them, Pompeo Leoni, sculptor to the King of Spain, made Melzi realize how valuable those books were, and promised him honors and offices if, by recovering them, he made a present of them to King Philip. Moved by such hope, Melzi flew to Signor Guido Mazzenta, brother of Giovanni Ambrogio, and kneeling, begged him to return those...*

<meta>This page continues the account of Leonardo da Vinci’s manuscripts and his life following the fall of his patron Ludovico Sforza, detailing his return to Florence and the creation of his most famous works.</meta>

-># L I F E<-

*works of Leonardo. Moved by the prayers of his colleague, he returned seven of them to him, and six remained in the Mazzenta house, one of which was donated to Cardinal Borromeo for his Ambrosian Library, and another to Ambrogio Figino, who upon dying left it to his heir Ercole Bianchi. A third was held by Charles Emmanuel, Duke of Savoy, and upon the death of Signor Guido, the remaining ones came into the hands of the aforementioned Pompeo Leoni, who left them to Cleodoro Calchi his heir, who sold them for 300 scudi to Signor Galeazzo Lonato. Leonardo was accustomed, when he wished to philosophize and apply himself with strong attention to study, to retreat to the said villa of Vaprio, and it is known that he dwelt there many years with Francesco Melzi, his disciple. Below shall be placed the index of his writings.*

*After the fall of "Il Moro" <note>Ludovico Sforza (1452–1508), Duke of Milan, nicknamed "the Moor."</note>, who was in the year 1500 led prisoner to France and died in the tower of Loches, the study of the fine arts in Milan grew quite lukewarm due to the wars that followed. The academy already begun there gradually dissipated; in it, Francesco Melzi, Cesare Sesto, Bernardino Luini <note>original: "Louino"</note>, Andrea Salaino, Marco d'Oggiono <note>original: "Viggioni"</note>, Antonio Boltraffio, Paolo Lomazzo, and other Milanese had turned out excellent in painting, all being imitators of Leonardo. This was to such a degree that often their works were and are today believed, esteemed, and sold as the handiwork of Leonardo, and principally those of Sesto and Luini, who most closely approached the manner of the master. But above all others, Lomazzo would have raised himself up had he not been deprived of his eyes in the greenest years of his age, as had been predicted to him by Girolamo Cardano <note>A famous Renaissance polymath and physician known for his work in mathematics and astrology.</note>. Being unable to work with his hand, he gave himself to treating painting with his intellect, and while blind composed those books which are esteemed as excellent by the most sharp-sighted, in which he continually proposes Leonardo as the idea of the true and perfect painter.*

*At the time when Louis XII, King of France, came to Milan—which was a year before the capture of Il Moro—Leonardo was requested by the leading men of the city to invent some whimsical and magnificent machine with which to regale and delight that great prince. He made a lion of such artifice that, after having walked a good distance in a hall, it stopped before the King and then, opening its breast, was seen to be entirely full of lilies <note>The fleur-de-lis, the heraldic symbol of the French monarchy.</note>. By an error of the one who wrote under Lomazzo (Book 2, Chapter 1), it is read that such a thing was done for Francis I, which cannot be true, because he entered Milan in the year 1515, at which time Leonardo was in Rome, as will be seen below.*

*The turmoils of Lombardy and the misfortunes of the Sforza family, Leonardo's patrons, obliged him to abandon Milan and return to Florence, his homeland. The first thing he did there was that famous cartoon <note>A full-scale preparatory drawing for a painting.</note> of the Virgin with Christ and Saint Anne, with Saint John, which was to serve for the high altar of the Annunziata, and which was visited in crowds by all the people of Florence. This cartoon was later carried by Leonardo himself into France, where the King desired that he should color it.*

*He then made for Francesco del Giocondo the much-celebrated portrait of Lisa, his wife, commonly called "La Gioconda" <note>Known in English as the Mona Lisa.</note>, which is seen at Fontainebleau in the company of many other precious paintings of the Most Christian King, and was formerly bought for four thousand scudi by Francis I. It is said that he spent four years working on that portrait, and that nonetheless he left it imperfect, having a taste so delicate and an intellect so sharp and subtle that, in order to arrive at the truth of na-*

<meta>This page continues the biography of Leonardo da Vinci, transitioning from his influence on the Milanese school to his return to Florence and his interactions with the young Raphael and Michelangelo.</meta>

-># OF LEONARDO DA VINCI.<-

<sig>i ij</sig>

nature, he always sought excellence upon excellence, and perfection upon perfection; and not satisfying himself with what was done, however beautiful, he pursued with anxiety that "something more" that could yet be achieved. While he painted, he used to have people around Lady Lisa <note>The Mona Lisa</note> who would sing, play instruments, and laugh, to keep her cheerful and not fall into the common drawback of portraits, which for the most part result in a melancholy air. And truly in this work one sees a smile so pleasant that, as Vasari says, it is a thing more divine than human to behold. Also beautiful is another portrait by the same Leonardo which is at Fontainebleau, and is said to be of a Marchioness of Mantua <note>Likely Isabella d'Este</note>. Most beautiful was that of Ginevra de' Amerigo Benci, a girl of famous beauty in those times. Nor should one omit the *Flora* painted with admirable charm and a truly divine air, which is preserved in Paris and is in the hands of a private person.

Having, around the year 1503, to decorate the Council Hall in the Palace of Florence, Leonardo was elected by public decree to paint it. For this purpose, he made a cartoon <term>cartoon: a full-scale preparatory drawing for a fresco or painting</term> full of art and beautiful considerations, in which was expressed a history of Piccinino <note>The Battle of Anghiari, where Niccolò Piccinino was defeated</note>. He had already colored the greater part of it in oil when, realizing that because of the priming being too thick everything was detaching from the wall and his labors were in vain, he abandoned the work.

At that time—which was during the pontificate of Pius III, not the second, as one reads in Vasari—Raphael of Urbino, who had barely reached the age of twenty and had freshly emerged from the school of Pietro Perugino, arrived in Florence for the first time, desirous of seeing that famous cartoon and enamored by the fame of Leonardo da Vinci, who was then passing the sixtieth year of his age. He was stunned at the sight of his works, and never had a more powerful stimulus to make him run and quickly arrive at that high perfection of art, which caused him to be revered by all as a god of painting; departing from that time forward from the dry and hard manner of Perugino to pass to the softness and tenderness of Vinci. The young Raphael was also a spectator, not without profit, of the contentions that later caused such enmity between Leonardo and Michelangelo Buonarroti, who was not yet 29 years old, and by public order had made for another wall of the same Council Hall that much-celebrated cartoon of the War of Pisa, filled with various nudes made in competition with Vinci. Until the year 1513, Leonardo stayed always in Florence and painted many things there. Francesco Bocchi, in the book he wrote on the beauties of Florence, makes mention of a small painting which in his time was seen in the house of Matteo and Giovan Battista Botti, in which was painted a Madonna with supreme artifice and diligence, with a wonderfully beautiful Christ child who raised his face with singular grace. By Borghini, a head of St. John the Baptist that was in the hands of Camillo degli Albizi is mentioned as a rare thing.

But Leo X having been assumed to the pontificate—in whom the love of painting and all the fine arts was an inherited trait—Leonardo hurried to Rome to pay reverence to that prince and patron <note>original: "Mecenate"</note> of the virtuous. When the Pope ordered a panel painting from him, Vasari recounts that he immediately began with great preparation to distill oils and prepare the varnish; and that Leo, informed of this, said that nothing should be hoped for from one who thought about the end before having examined the beginning.

<summary>This page describes Leonardo's perfectionism, his famous portraits like the Mona Lisa, his failed Battle of Anghiari fresco, and his profound influence on the young Raphael during their time in Florence.</summary>

## LIFE

of the work. He <note>Referring to a previous biographer, likely Vasari or Condivi</note> also recounts certain other little things unworthy of the greatness of **Vinci’s** genius, which must be held as suspect, being written by a person very partial to **Michelangelo**, who, as we said, professed open enmity with **Leonardo**, and delighted in diminishing his reputation with feigned and fabricated jokes. That implacable hatred greatly displeased **Leonardo**, and seeing himself called by King **Francis**, who during his stay in **Milan** had fallen in love with his works, he resolved—though an old man of more than seventy years—to embrace such an honored and glorious path, and to make the journey to **France**.

The pleasure the King felt was no ordinary thing, seeing himself the possessor of a man of such talent <term>virtuoso: a person of exceptional skill or artistic ability</term> whom he so esteemed and desired. And although because of his old age he could hardly work anymore, he was nonetheless always well-regarded and cherished by the King. And everyone knows that, having been ill for many months in **Fontainebleau**, the King came to visit him; and as he wished to pull himself up in bed out of reverence and recount his illness, a paroxysm <note>original: "accidente," likely a stroke or seizure</note> came upon him. For which reason the King took his head to help and sustain him, and he, having recognized the favor, breathed his last in his arms at the age of seventy-five years, far more glorious than any other painter, if it is true that "a beautiful death honors a whole life." <note>A quote from Petrarch's Canzoniere</note>

He was very beautiful of body, as was said above. Having passed his youth with a philosophical negligence, he let his hair and beard grow, so that he looked like an ancient **Hermes** or **Druid**. He never wished to take a wife, or if he had one, as another painter used to say, it was none other than art, and his children were his works. Nor should one believe that all of them have been mentioned, because the Great **Duke of Florence** has many others, and I remember having seen several in **England**. In the *Idea of the Temple of Painting* by **Paolo Lomazzo**, chapter 33, mention is made of a **Conception of the Virgin** painted for the church of **St. Francis in Milan**. In the **Ambrosian Library** of the same city, many drawings and paintings by this author are preserved.

Here in **Paris**, in the **Cardinal’s** palace <note>The Palais-Cardinal, now the Palais-Royal</note>, one sees a **Madonna** by his hand, who sits in the lap of **St. Anne**, and holds with her hands a Christ child who plays with a little lamb. There is a very beautiful landscape: but the head of the Virgin remained unfinished. **Cardinal Richelieu** had a **Herodias** <note>Salome with the head of John the Baptist</note> of exquisite beauty. The **St. John** in the desert, a full figure which is at **Fontainebleau**, and another painting of a **Madonna** with **Christ**, **St. John**, and an angel of miraculous beauty, placed in a landscape, are things to be observed. In the study of the **Marquis of Sourdis** in **Paris**, there is another **Madonna** of high reputation.

Monsieur de **Chantelou** <note>original: "Ciarmois," likely Paul Fréart de Chantelou</note>, secretary to **Marshal Schomberg**, a gentleman of rare qualities who, coupling curiosity with intelligence, makes a considerable collection of fine paintings, has one by **Vinci**, in which with two half-figures is represented the young and handsome **Joseph** who, fleeing, turns his back on the beautiful but dishonest wife of **Pharaoh** <note>Potiphar's wife</note>. The whole is painted with great love and diligence: the expression is admirable, and the modesty of the one and the lust of the other appear in the two faces more like true things than feigned. With the same gentleman, a Madonna with **St. Anne** and a Christ child to whom **St. Michael** offers a scale, and **St. John** who plays with a little lamb, is a painting of extreme beauty.

But it would be too much to wish to record all the paintings of **Vinci**: it remains that after the works of the brush, we reason about those of the pen.

**Vinci** used to write with his left hand, according to the custom of the **Hebrews**, in which...

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

*In this manner were written those thirteen volumes of which we have already made mention,* <note>Referring to Leonardo's famous "mirror writing" from right to left.</note> *and the handwriting being good, it was read easily enough by means of a large mirror. It is probable that he did this so that everyone might not read his writings so easily.*

*The undertaking of the canal of **Martesana** gave him the occasion to write a book on the nature, weight, and motion of waters, full of a great number of drawings of various wheels and machines for mills, and for regulating the course of waters and raising them on high.*

*He wrote on the anatomy of the human body, as has already been said, which work was adorned with various drawings made with great study and diligence, and he himself makes mention of it in chapter 22 of this treatise on painting.*

*The book on the anatomy of horses is mentioned by **Vasari**, **Borghini**, and **Lomazzo**. Having been excellent in sculpting them in clay and in painting them—as the painting of the four fighting knights mentioned above bears witness—there is no doubt that the work was of extraordinary beauty and utility.*

*In chapters 81 and 110 of this treatise, a work of his on perspective is cited by him, divided into several books. Perhaps it was there that the method of drawing figures larger than life was taught, praised by **Lomazzo** in the "Idea," chapter 4.* <note>Referring to Giovanni Paolo Lomazzo's "Idea del tempio della pittura" (1590).</note>

*In chapters 112 and 123, he promises to make a book on the movements of the body and its parts: an anatomical subject, and one that has never been touched upon by anyone.*

*He also promises in chapter 268 a treatise on the weight, or rather the balancing, of the body.*

*The book on shadows and lights is found today in the **Ambrosian Library** of **Milan**, in folio, covered in red velvet; it is the one which, as was said above, was given by Signor **Guido Mazzenta** to Cardinal **Borromeo**. He treats that matter as a philosopher, a mathematician, and a painter, and makes mention of it in this treatise in chapter 278. He was miraculous in this part of painting, imitating with such sagacity the effects that light produces with color, that his works possessed more of the natural than of the feigned.*

*There remains the treatise on painting, which contains various precepts of that art, and together the methods of drawing and coloring. **Vasari** recounts that a certain Milanese painter, passing through **Florence**, showed him that work and told him that when he arrived in **Rome** he would immediately have it printed; but that was not carried out by him. And that which was not done in **Rome** is now, after an entire century, put into execution in **Paris**, where, by the comparison of various manuscripts—all corrupt and spoiled—a work has been restored by me which, for the excellence of its precepts and the merit of its author, is worthy of immortality. And to render it even more familiar to our nation, Signor **de Chambray**—a gentleman most intelligent in all parts of design, and who (as we said of the great **Leo X**) by an instinct communicated to his family delights in every sort of virtue and study—has made a version of it in the French language, which is worth an entire commentary, there being expressed therein, with exquisite and happy diligence, the sense of the author.*

Two dialogues by Master Giovanni Andrea Gilio of Fabriano; in the first of which is reasoned the moral and civil parts belonging to learned courtiers, and the utility that princes derive from learned men: in the second is reasoned the errors of painters regarding histories <term>Historie: narrative paintings depicting historical, biblical, or mythological scenes</term>, with many annotations made upon the *Last Judgment* <note>original: "giuditio"</note> of Michelangelo, and other figures, both of the old and the new chapel <note>The Sistine Chapel</note>, and in what manner sacred images ought to be painted. In Mantua 1591. quarto.

# TREATISE
# ON PAINTING
## BY LEONARDO
## DA VINCI

---

### *What the young person should first learn.*
### C H A P T E R  O N E .

The young person should first learn perspective, for the measurements of every thing: then, step by step, learn from a good master, to become accustomed to good proportions <note>original: "buone membra," literally "good limbs" or "members," referring to the correct anatomical proportions of the body</note>: then from nature, to confirm the principles of the things learned: then to view for a time the works by the hands of various masters, to acquire the habit of putting into practice and executing the things learned.

### *What study should be pursued by the young.* C H A P. I I.

The study of the young, who desire to make progress in the sciences that imitate all the forms of the works of nature, should be concerning drawing <note>original: "disegno," which in this period encompasses both the act of drawing and the underlying intellectual design or concept</note> accompanied by shadows and lights appropriate to the site where such figures are placed.

### *What rule should be given to boy painters.* C H A P. I I I.

We know clearly that sight is among the swiftest operations that exist, and in one instant it sees infinite forms; nevertheless, it does not comprehend more than one thing at a time. Let us take a case: You, reader, look at this entire written page in one glance, and you will immediately judge it to be full of various letters: but you will not know in that moment what letters they are, nor what they mean: therefore you must go word by word, line by line, if you wish to have knowledge of these letters. Furthermore, if you wish to climb to the top of a building, you must ascend step by step, otherwise it will be impossible to reach its height.

<summary>This page introduces Leonardo da Vinci's foundational treatise on painting, outlining a pedagogical progression from perspective and anatomy to the study of nature and the masters.</summary>
<keywords>Leonardo da Vinci, perspective, drawing, light and shadow, art education, perception, anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo da Vinci’s foundational advice for the aspiring artist, moving from the basic necessity of step-by-step learning to the philosophical requirement for the painter to be "universal" and observant of all nature.</meta>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

And so I say to you, whom nature draws to this art: if you wish to have true knowledge of the forms of things, you shall begin with their individual parts, and do not go on to the second part until you first have the first well-fixed in your memory and in practice. If you do otherwise, you will waste your time, or truly, you will greatly prolong your studies. And I remind you to learn diligence before speed <note>original: "prestezza," meaning quickness or facility of hand</note>.

->*Knowledge of the young person disposed toward painting.* Cap. IV.<-

**M**any are the men who have a desire and love for drawing, but no natural disposition for it; and this may be recognized in boys who lack diligence and never finish their works with shadows.

->*Precept for the painter.* Cap. V.<-

**A** painter is not praiseworthy if he does only one thing well, such as a nude, a head, draperies, animals, landscapes, or similar particulars; for there is no mind so dull that, having turned itself to one thing and practiced it constantly, it would not perform it well.

->*In what manner the young person should proceed in his study.* Cap. VI.<-

**T**he mind of the painter must continually transform itself into as many discourses as there are figures of notable objects appearing before him; and at these he should pause, note them, and create rules based upon them, considering the location, the circumstances, the lights, and the shadows.

->*On the method of studying.* Cap. VII.<-

**S**tudy the science first, and then follow the practice born from that science. **T**he painter must study with a rule, leaving nothing that is not committed to memory, and seeing what difference there is between the limbs of animals and their joints.

->*Warning to the painter.* Cap. VIII.<-

**T**he painter must be universal and solitary; he must consider what he sees and speak with himself, selecting the most excellent parts of the species of whatever thing he sees, acting like a mirror, which transforms itself into as many colors as those of the things placed before it. By doing this, he will seem to be a second nature.

->*Precept of the universal painter.* Cap. IX.<-

**H**e is not universal who does not equally love all things contained in painting. For instance, if one likes landscapes, he deems them to be a matter of simple investigation—as our Botticello <note>Sandro Botticelli (c. 1445–1510). Leonardo uses a diminutive or variant of his name here.</note> said, that such study was vain, because by merely throwing a sponge soaked in various colors against a wall, it would leave a stain on said wall where a landscape could be seen. It is indeed true that various inventions of what man wishes to seek therein may be seen in such a stain—namely, heads of men, different animals, battles, rocks, seas, clouds, woods, and similar things; it acts like the sound of bells, in which you can...

<summary>Leonardo emphasizes that mastery comes from diligent, part-by-part study rather than haste, and argues that a true painter must be "universal," observing and reflecting all aspects of nature rather than specializing in a single subject.</summary>
<keywords>diligence, universal painter, Botticelli, artistic observation, study methods, nature as mirror, landscape painting</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s instructions on the development of the artist’s mind and hand, emphasizing the need for universal skill, the value of self-criticism, and the importance of sketching before finishing.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

one can understand it to say whatever you please. Thus, even though these stains give you invention, they do not teach you how to finish any particular detail, and such a painter <note>referring to those who rely solely on chance patterns</note> produced the most dismal landscapes.

### *How the painter must be universal.* CAP. X.

YOU, painter, who wish to be universal and please various judgments, shall ensure that in one and the same composition there are things of great darkness and of great softness of shadows, while making known the causes of such shadows and softness.

### *Precept for the painter.* CAP. XI.

THAT painter who has no doubts gains little. When the work surpasses the judgment of the practitioner, that worker gains little; and when the judgment surpasses the work, that work never ceases to improve, unless greed <note>original: "auaritia," implying a rush to finish for profit</note> prevents it.

### *Precept as above.* CAP. XII.

THE painter must first accustom his hand by copying drawings by good masters; and having acquired this habit, under the judgment of his teacher, he must then accustom himself to copying good things in relief <note>three-dimensional objects or sculptures</note>, following those rules of drawing from relief which shall be discussed.

### *Precept on sketching stories and figures.* CAP. XIII.

THE sketching of stories <note>original: "historie," referring to narrative or historical compositions</note> should be quick, and the arrangement of limbs should not be too finished. Pay attention only to the positions of said limbs, which you may then finish at your leisure as it pleases you.

### *On correcting the errors you discover.* CAP. XIV.

I REMIND you, painter, that when by your own judgment or by the advice of others you discover any error in your works, you should correct it, so that in publishing such works, you do not publish your foolishness along with them. And do not excuse yourself, persuading yourself that you will redeem your disgrace in your next work; because painting does not die upon its creation, as music does, but lasts a long time, and time will bear witness to your ignorance. And if you excuse yourself by saying you must struggle with necessity, and lack the time to study and become a true painter, blame no one but yourself; for only the study of virtue <note>here meaning excellence or artistic power</note> is food for the soul and the body. How many philosophers were born rich, and yet gave up their wealth so that it would not hinder them!

### *On judgment.* CAP. XV.

THERE is nothing that deceives us more than our own judgment when giving sentence on our own works; the rebukes of enemies will be more useful to you than the opinions of friends, because friends are one and the same thing with you, and thus they may deceive you just as your own judgment does.

<sig>A ij</sig>

<vocab>painter, universal, judgment, shadows, relief, stories, virtue, study, philosophers</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo advises painters to embrace self-doubt as a tool for growth and warns against the permanence of artistic errors. He emphasizes that a painter must be "universal," mastering both light and shadow, and should prioritize the study of "virtue" over material wealth.</summary>
<keywords>universality, self-criticism, sketching technique, artistic discipline, light and shadow, narrative composition</keywords>

<page-num>4</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

# A way to stimulate the mind to various inventions. Cap. XVI.

I shall not hesitate to include among these precepts a new method of speculation, which, although it may seem small and almost worthy of laughter, is nonetheless of great utility in stimulating the mind to various inventions. And it is this: if you look at certain walls stained with damp, or stones of uneven colors, you may see there the invention and likeness of diverse landscapes, various battles, swift actions of figures, strange expressions of faces, and costumes, and infinite other things; because in confused things the mind is stirred to new inventions. <note>This is Leonardo's famous "stain" method, encouraging the artist to find patterns in chaos, much like seeing shapes in clouds.</note>

# On studying even when you wake, or before you fall asleep in the dark.
->Cap. XVII.<-

I have also found it to be of no small benefit, when you find yourself in the dark in bed, to go over in the imagination the superficial outlines of the forms previously studied, or other notable things of subtle speculation: and in this way, the things grasped in the memory are confirmed.

# That one must first learn diligence rather than fast practice. Cap. XVIII.

When you wish to make a good and useful study, use your drawing to work slowly, and judge among the lights, which and how many hold the first degree of brightness; and likewise among the shadows, which are those that are darker than the others, and in what way they blend together, and their quality. Compare one 
<margin>*</margin> with the other, and see in which direction the outlines are aimed, and in the lines, how much of the part should be in one direction or the other, and where it is more or less evident, and whether broad or thin. And finally, let your shadows and lights be united without strokes or marks, in the manner of smoke: <note>original: "a vso di fumo," referring to Leonardo's famous <term>sfumato</term> technique, where colors and tones blend so softly they appear like smoke without harsh edges.</note> and when you have brought your habit and hand to that diligence, the practice will become fast before you even realize it.

# How the painter must be eager to hear the judgment of everyone. Cap. XIX.

Certainly, the painter must not refuse, while he draws or paints, the judgment of anyone; for we know that a man, even if he is not a painter, will have knowledge of the forms of man—if he is hunchbacked, if he has a thick leg or a large hand, if he is lame, or has other defects. And if we know that men can judge the works of nature, how much more so will they be able to judge our errors.

# That a man must not trust himself so much that he does not look at nature.
->Cap. XX.<-

He who convinces himself that he can retain within himself all the effects of nature is deceived, because our memory is not of such great capacity: therefore, you shall see everything from nature. <note>original: "dal naturale," meaning drawing from life or the physical world rather than from imagination or other drawings.</note>

# On the variety of figures. Cap. XXI.

The painter must seek to be universal, because he lacks much dignity if he does one thing well and another poorly: like many who only study the

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

the nude measured and proportioned, and they do not seek its variety; for a man can be well-proportioned and yet be thick, or short, or long, or thin, or average. And he who does not take account of this variety always makes his figures as if from a mold <note>original: "in stampa" — meaning repetitive or formulaic, like a print</note>, which deserves great reprimand.

---

### *On being universal.* CAP. XXII.

It is an easy thing for the man who knows to become universal, because all terrestrial animals have a similarity of limbs—that is, muscles, nerves, and bones—and they do not vary at all except in length or thickness, as will be demonstrated in the [treatise on] anatomy. Regarding the animals of the water, which are of great variety, I will not persuade the painter to make a rule for them.

### *Of those who use practice without diligence, or science.* CAP. XXIII.

<margin>+</margin>Those who fall in love with practice without diligence, or science <note>original: "scienza" — here meaning the underlying theoretical principles or laws of nature</note>, to put it better, are like mariners who take to the sea in a ship without a rudder or compass, who never have certainty of where they are going. Practice must always be built upon good theory, of which perspective is the guide and the gateway; and without that, nothing is done well, neither in painting nor in any other profession.

### *On not imitating another painter.* CAP. XXIV.

A painter should never imitate the manner of another, because he will be called a grandson and not a son of nature; for since natural things are in such wide abundance, one should rather have recourse to nature itself than to the masters who have learned from it.

### *On drawing from life.* CAP. XXV.

When you have to draw from life <note>original: "ritrar dal naturale"</note>, stand away three times the size of the thing you are drawing; and ensure that when you draw, or when you move any beginning of a line, you look throughout the whole body you are drawing, noting whatever aligns with the direction of the principal line.

### *Warning to the painter.* CAP. XXVI.

Note well in your drawing how among the shadows there are insensible shadows of darkness and of shape. And this is proven by the third [proposition], which says that globular surfaces have as many various darknesses and brightnesses as there are varieties of darkness and brightness in the objects facing them.

### *How high the light should be for drawing from life.* CAP. XXVII.

The light for drawing from life should be from the north <note>original: "à tramontana"</note>, so that it does not change; and if you use a southern light, keep the windows covered with cloth <note>original: "impannate" — windows covered with oiled or waxed cloth to diffuse light before the common use of large glass panes</note>, so that the sun, shining all day, does not cause change. The height of the light should be situated in such a way that every body casts a shadow on the ground as long as the body is high.

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>
<sig>A 2</sig>
<vocab>Treatise on Painting, Leonardo da Vinci, lights, shadows, reliefs, nudes, sfumato (softened), distinct boundaries, redness, mist</vocab>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

## *Which lights should be chosen for portraying the figures of bodies.*
### CAP. XXVIII.

The figures of any body are compelled to take on that light in which you imagine those figures to be: that is, if you imagine such figures in the countryside, they are surrounded by a great abundance of light when the sun is not visible; and if the sun sees said figures, their shadows will be very dark compared to the illuminated parts, and will create shadows with distinct boundaries <note>original: "termini espediti" — sharp, clear edges rather than soft transitions</note>, both the primary and the derivative ones. Such shadows will harmonize little with the lights, because from that side the blue of the air illuminates and tints whatever part it sees; and this is very evident in white objects. That part which is illuminated by the sun shows itself to participate in the color of the sun; you will see this very clearly when the sun sets in the west amidst the redness of the clouds, so that those clouds are tinted with the color that illuminates them. This redness of the clouds, together with the redness of the sun, makes everything that catches their light turn reddish; and the part of the bodies that does not see this redness remains the color of the air. He who sees such bodies judges them to be of two colors. You cannot escape this: having shown the cause of such shadows and lights, you must make your work a participant in the aforementioned causes, otherwise your labor is vain and false. And if your figure is in a dark house and you see it from outside, such a figure will have softened shadows <note>original: "sfumate" — the famous Leonardo technique of smoky, gradual transitions</note> if you stand in the line of the light; such a figure will have grace and bring honor to its imitator, being of great relief <term>relief: the illusion of three-dimensionality on a flat surface</term> with sweet and smoky shadows, especially in that part where you see less of the darkness of the dwelling, for there the shadows are almost imperceptible; the reason for this will be stated in its proper place.

## *Of the qualities of light for portraying natural or artificial reliefs.*
### CAP. XXIX.

Light cut off from shadow with too much evidence is highly blamed; therefore, to avoid such an inconvenience, if you make bodies in the open countryside, do not make the figures illuminated by the sun, but imagine some quantity of mist or transparent clouds to be interposed between the object and the sun. Thus, the figure not being sharply lit by the sun, the boundaries of the shadows will not be distinct from those of the lights.

## *On portraying nudes. CAP. XXX.*

When you portray nudes, always portray them in their entirety, and then finish that limb which seems best to you, and put that into practice with the other limbs; otherwise, you would form a habit of never joining the limbs together well. Never make the head turned where the chest is, nor the arm moving like the leg; and if the head turns toward the right shoulder, make its parts lower on the left side than on the other; and if you make the chest outward, see that in turning the head to the left side, the parts of the right side are higher than the left.

<summary>Leonardo provides instructions on how atmospheric conditions like sunset or mist affect light and shadow on a figure, and advises on the harmonious posing of the nude body.</summary>
<keywords>lighting, shadows, sfumato, relief, landscape painting, anatomy, contrapposto, color theory</keywords>
