<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

## *On drawing from a simulated relief, or from nature.* CHAPTER XXXI.

HE who draws from a relief <note>A three-dimensional model, such as a sculpture or cast, used for practice.</note> must position himself in such a way that the eye of the figure being portrayed is at the same level as the eye of the person drawing.

## *A method for drawing a site accurately.* CHAPTER XXXII.

HAVE a pane of glass as large as a half-sheet of royal paper, and fix it firmly before your eyes—that is, between your eyes and the object you wish to draw. Then, position yourself so your eye is two-thirds of a <term>braccio</term> <note>A "braccio" (arm) was a common Italian unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or 58 cm.</note> away from said glass, and steady your head with an instrument so that you cannot move it at all. After this, close or cover one eye, and with a brush or a <term>lapis</term> <note>A drawing tool, often a graphite or lead point.</note>, mark on the glass what appears on the other side. Then, trace that glass onto paper, and by pouncing it <note>A method of transferring an image by pricking holes in the outline and dusting it with charcoal powder.</note> onto good paper, paint it if you like, making good use thereafter of aerial perspective <note>The technique of creating depth by making distant objects paler, bluer, and less distinct.</note>.

## *How landscapes should be portrayed.* CHAPTER XXXIII.

LANDSCAPES should be portrayed in such a way that the trees are half-illuminated and half-shaded. But it is better to do them when the sun is partially obscured by clouds, for then the trees are illuminated by the universal light of the sky and the universal shadow of the earth; and these are darker in their parts the closer those parts are to the ground.

## *On drawing by candlelight.* CHAPTER XXXIV.

TO this light at night, let a frame or tracing paper be interposed—either un-oiled or simply a sheet of thin <term>chancery paper</term> <note>A high-quality, thin writing paper.</note>—and you will see that your shadows are not harsh or sharply defined.

## *In what manner one should portray a face, giving it grace, shadow, and light.* CHAPTER XXXV.

A VERY great grace of shadows and lights is added to the faces of those who sit in the darker parts of dwellings. The eyes of the observer see the shaded part of such a face darkened by the shadows of the aforementioned dwelling, and see the illuminated part of the same face joined with the brightness given to it by the radiance of the air. Through this increase of shadows and lights, the face has great <term>relief</term> <note>The illusion of three-dimensionality on a flat surface.</note>, and in the illuminated part, the shadows are almost imperceptible. From this representation and increase of shadows and lights, the face acquires much beauty.

## *On the light where one portrays the flesh tones of faces and nudes.* CHAPTER XXXVI.

THIS dwelling should be open to the air, with the walls colored in flesh tones. Portraits should be made in summer when clouds cover the sun; or truly, you should make the southern walls so high that the sun's rays do not strike the northern walls, so that the reflected rays do not spoil the shadows.

<summary>Leonardo provides technical advice on using glass as a perspective tool, capturing the soft lighting of overcast days for landscapes, and utilizing indoor "dwelling" light to enhance the beauty and three-dimensionality of portraits.</summary>
<keywords>perspective glass, aerial perspective, landscape painting, candlelight, portraiture, relief, flesh tones, lighting conditions</keywords>

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### *On portraying figures for narrative paintings.* Chapter XXXVII.

**T**he painter must always consider, on the wall which he is to paint with a narrative <note>original: "historiare" – the creation of a "history painting," which depicts a specific story or event.</note>, the height of the location where he wishes to place his figures. Whatever he draws from nature for this purpose, he must keep his eye as much lower than the object he is drawing as that object will be placed higher than the eye of the viewer; otherwise, his work will be blameworthy.

### *To draw a nude from nature, or other things.* Chapter XXXVIII.

**M**ake a habit of holding a string with a hanging lead weight <note>A plumb line, used to ensure vertical accuracy.</note> in your hand, to see how different parts of the body align with one another.

### *Measurements and divisions of the statue.* Chapter XXXIX.

**D**ivide the head into twelve degrees, and divide each degree into 12 points, and each point into 12 minutes, and the minutes into minimums, and the minimums into semi-minimums. <note>Leonardo is proposing a mathematical system of proportions for the human body, similar to those used by ancient architects.</note>

### *How the painter should position himself relative to the light and his model.* Chapter XL.

**A. B.** shall be the window. **M.** shall be the point of the light source. I say that in whatever part the painter stands, he will be well-positioned, provided that his eye is between the shaded part and the illuminated part of the body being portrayed: which place you will find by positioning yourself between point **M.** and the division that the shadow makes from the light upon the body being portrayed.

---

### *On the quality of light.* Chapter XLI.

**A** large and high light, and one that is not too powerful, will be that which renders the details of bodies very pleasing.

### *On the deception encountered in judging limbs.* Chapter XLII.

**T**hat painter who has clumsy hands will make them similar in his works, and so <margin>it often happens with other parts of the body</margin>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

it will happen to him in any limb, unless long study prevents it. Therefore, every painter must look at that part which is most ugly in his own person, and with all study, provide a good remedy for it.

### *That one must know the internal form of man.* Chapter XLIII.

That painter who has knowledge of the nature of the nerves, muscles, and tendons <note>original: "lacerti" – a term used in early anatomy to describe the fleshy parts of muscles.</note>, will know well, when moving a limb, how many and which nerves are the cause of it, and which muscle swelling is the cause of shortening that nerve, and which cords converted into very thin cartilages wrap around and surround said muscle: and he will not do as many, who in different actions always show those same things in arms, backs, chests, and other muscles.

### *On the defect of the painter.* Chapter XLV.

It is a very great defect of the painter to portray or repeat the same movements, and the same folds of clothing in the same narrative painting <note>original: "historia"</note>, and to make all the heads resemble one another.

### *Precept, so that the painter is not deceived in the choice of the figure he creates.* Chapter XLV.

The painter must create his figure according to the rule of a natural body, which is commonly of praiseworthy proportion. Besides this, he should measure himself and see in what part his own person varies much or little from that aforementioned praiseworthy one: and having gained that knowledge, he must strive with all his study not to fall into the same failings in the figures he creates that he finds in his own person: and you must fight against this vice with all your might, since it is a failing born together with judgment: because the soul is the mistress of your body <note>Likely a printing error for 'corpo'.</note> and that of your own judgment, and she willingly delights in works similar to those she performed in composing your own body: and from here it arises, that there is no female figure so ugly that she does not find some lover, unless she were monstrous; and in all this, take the greatest care.

### *Defect of painters who portray a thing in relief in a house with one light, and then put it in the countryside with another light.* Chapter XLVI.

It is a great error of those painters who portray a thing in relief <note>A three-dimensional object or model.</note> with a particular light in their houses, and then put such a portrait into a work with the universal light of the air in the countryside, where such air embraces and illuminates all parts of the views in the same way; and thus he makes dark shadows where there can be no shadow: and if indeed there is one, it is of such brightness that it is imperceptible: and thus they make reflections where it is impossible for them to be seen.

### *Of painting, and its division.* Chapter XLVII.

Painting is divided into two principal parts, of which the first is

<sig>B</sig>

### *Figure, and its division.* CHAPTER XLVIII.

**T**he figure of bodies is divided into two other parts: namely, the proportionality of the parts to one another, which must correspond to the whole, and the movement appropriate to the mental state <note>original: "accidente mentale" — referring to the internal emotions or thoughts that trigger a physical reaction.</note> of the living thing that moves.

### *Proportion of limbs.* CHAPTER XLIX.

**T**he proportion of limbs is divided into two other parts: namely, equality and motion. By equality, it is meant—beyond the measurements corresponding to the whole—that you should not mix the limbs of youths with those of the old, nor those of the fat with those of the lean, nor graceful limbs with those that are clumsy and sluggish. Furthermore, do not give feminine limbs to males, so that the attitudes or movements of the elderly are not made with that same vivacity as those of the young, nor those of a woman like those of a man. Ensure that the movements and limbs of a strong man are such that the limbs themselves demonstrate that health and vigor.

### *On movements, and various actions.* CHAPTER L.

**L**et the figures of men have an action appropriate to their activity, so that in seeing them you understand what they are thinking or saying. These will be well learned by whoever imitates the motions of the mute, who speak with the movements of their hands, eyes, eyebrows, and their whole person, when wishing to express the concept of their mind. Do not laugh at me because I propose a teacher without a tongue to you, who must teach you an art he cannot perform; for he will teach you better through deeds than all others will with words. Therefore, you, painter of one school or another, pay attention—as the occasion arises—to the quality of those who speak and to the nature of the thing being spoken of.

### *That one must avoid sharp boundaries.* CHAPTER LI.

**D**o not make the boundaries <note>original: "termini" — the edges or outlines of a figure.</note> of your figures a different color than the background itself against which the figures end; that is, do not make dark profiles between the background and your figure.

### *That in small things errors are not seen as in large ones.* CHAPTER LII.

**I**n things of small scale, the quality of your error cannot be understood as it can in large ones; and the reason is that if this small thing is made in the likeness of a man or another animal, its parts—due to the immense reduction—cannot be researched with that proper finish by the artist that would be appropriate. Thus, not being finished, you cannot comprehend its errors. For example, you might look from a distance at a man at a space of 300 cubits <note>original: "braccia" — a Renaissance unit of measurement, roughly the length of an arm.</note>, and you will judge with diligence whether he is handsome or ugly, whether he is monstrous or of common quality; you will see that with your greatest effort you will not be able to

<summary>Leonardo discusses the importance of consistent proportions and how physical movement must reflect a figure's internal thoughts, famously suggesting that painters study the gestures of the mute. He also warns against harsh outlines and explains why errors are less visible in small-scale works.</summary>
<keywords>proportion, gesture, expression, movement, outlines, scale, artistic training, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<page-num>II</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

persuade one to give such a judgment; and the reason is that, due to the aforementioned distance, this man diminishes so much that the quality of his parts cannot be understood. And if you wish to clearly see this diminution of the man mentioned above, place a finger near your eye at the distance of a palm, and raise and lower said finger until its upper extremity ends just below the figure you are looking at, and you will see an incredible diminution appear: and for this reason, one often doubts the form of a friend from afar.

### *Why painting can never appear as detached <note>original: "spiccata" — meaning standing out in three-dimensional relief from the background.</note> as natural things.*
->CHAPTER LIII.<-

<margin>See chapter 351.</margin>
Painters often fall into despair in their imitation of nature, seeing that their paintings do not have that relief <term>relief: the illusion of three-dimensionality on a flat surface</term> and that vivacity which things seen in a mirror have, alleging that they possess colors which far surpass the quality of the lights and shadows of the thing seen in the mirror in terms of brightness and darkness. In this case, they accuse their own ignorance and not the underlying reason, because they do not know it. It is impossible for a painted thing to appear in such relief that it resembles things in a mirror, even though both are on a flat surface, unless it is viewed with only one eye. And the reason is this: the two eyes see one thing after another, like A and B seeing M and N. M cannot entirely hide N because the base of the visual lines is so wide that it sees the second body behind the first. But if you close one eye, like S, the body F will hide R, because the visual line originates from a single point and forms its base on the first body, whereby the second body of equal size will never be seen.

---

### *Why the arrangement of figures one above the other is a thing to be avoided.*
->CHAPTER LIIII.<-

**THIS** universal practice which is used by painters on the walls of chapels is much to be reasonably blamed, for they make 

<summary>Leonardo explains why paintings lack the perfect three-dimensional depth of reality due to binocular vision and critiques the common practice of stacking figures vertically on chapel walls.</summary>
<keywords>optics, binocular vision, relief, perspective, monocular vision, chapel decoration, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s instructions on perspective and the technical execution of painting, following his explanation of why binocular vision prevents a painting from appearing as three-dimensionally "detached" as a real object.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

...cause the visual line starts at a single point.

<note>This paragraph continues the critique of artists who ignore the laws of perspective.</note>
There are those who place a narrative <note>original: "istoria" — a multi-figure historical or biblical scene</note> on a plane with its landscape and buildings, then they rise another level and make another narrative, changing the vanishing point from the first one, and then a third and a fourth, so that a single wall is seen made with four vanishing points, which is the height of stupidity for such masters. We know that the vanishing point is placed at the eye of the viewer of the narrative. And if you were to say: "How am I to depict the life of a saint divided into many scenes on the same wall?" To this I answer you, that you must place the first foreground with the vanishing point at the eye level of the viewers of that narrative; and on said plane, depict the first narrative on a large scale. Then, by gradually diminishing the figures and buildings upon various hills and plains, you will create the entire setting of that narrative. For the rest of the wall, in its upper part, you shall make trees that are large in comparison to the figures, or angels if they are appropriate to the story, or birds, or clouds, or similar things; otherwise, do not bother with it, for all your work will be false.

### *Which style of painting should be used to make things appear more detached.* Chapter LV.

Figures illuminated by a particular light <note>a focused or directional light source, like a lamp or a sunbeam</note> are those that show more relief than those illuminated by universal light <note>diffuse, ambient light, like an overcast sky</note>, because particular light creates reflected lights, which detach the figures from their backgrounds. These reflections arise from the lights of one figure bouncing into the shadow of the one standing in front of it, illuminating it in part. But a figure placed before a particular light in a large and dark place receives no reflection, and of this figure, nothing is seen but the illuminated part; and this is only to be used in imitations of the night, with a small, particular light.

### *Which is of greater reasoning and utility: the light and shadows of bodies, or their outlines.* CHAPTER LVI.

The boundaries of bodies are of greater reasoning and ingenuity than the shadows and lights, for the reason that the outlines of the limbs, which are not flexible, are immutable and are always the same; but the locations, quality, and quantity of shadows are infinite.

### *A reminder made by the author.* CHAPTER LVII.

Describe which are the muscles and which are the tendons that, through the various movements of each limb, are revealed or hidden, or do neither; and remember that such an action is most important among painters and sculptors who make a profession of studying muscles. You shall do the same for a child, from his birth until the time of his old age, through all the stages of his life, and in all of them, you shall describe the changes of the limbs and joints, and which ones grow fat or grow thin.

### *Precepts of painting.* CHAPTER LVIII.

The painter must always seek out the readiness <note>original: "prontitudine" — the spontaneous, natural quality of a gesture</note> in natural actions made by men unexpectedly, born from the powerful emotion of their feelings; and of those, he should make brief notes in his little books, and then use them for his purposes.

<vocab>painting, narrative, perspective, relief, muscles, precepts, outlines, anatomy</vocab>

<keywords>perspective, vanishing point, chiaroscuro, reflected light, anatomy, muscle movement, artistic observation, life stages</keywords>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

by having a man stand in that same pose, in order to see the quality and appearance of the limbs that are employed in such an action.

### *How a painting must be seen from a single window.* CHAPTER LIX.

A painting must be seen from a single window <note>Leonardo uses "window" here as a metaphor for the fixed viewpoint of the observer</note>, as is evident by the reason of bodies made in such a way. And if you wish to depict a round ball at a great height, you must make it long in the likeness of an egg, and stand so far back that, through foreshortening <term>foreshortening: the visual distortion of an object to show depth</term>, it appears round.

### *On shadows.* CHAPTER LX.

Those shadows which you discern with difficulty, and whose boundaries you cannot recognize—but rather perceive and transfer into your work with a confused judgment—you shall not make finished or truly defined, so that your work may possess an ingenious resolution <note>meaning a soft, masterful blending rather than harsh, amateurish lines</note>.

### *How children should be depicted.* CHAPTER LXI.

Small children should be depicted with quick and twisted movements when they sit, and when standing, with timid and fearful gestures.

### *How old men should be depicted.* CHAPTER LXII.

Old men must be made with sluggish and slow movements, their legs bent at the knees when they stand still; their feet should be parallel and spaced apart from one another, their bodies inclined downward, the head leaning forward, and the arms not too extended.

### *How old women should be depicted.* CHAPTER LXIII.

Old women should be depicted as bold and quick, with angry movements like infernal furies; the movements should appear quicker in the arms and head than in the legs.

### *How women should be depicted.* CHAPTER LXIV.

Women must be depicted with modest gestures, their legs drawn close together, their arms gathered together, and their heads low and tilted to the side.

### *How a night scene should be depicted.* CHAPTER LXV.

That thing which is entirely deprived of light is all darkness. Night being in such a condition, if you wish to depict a narrative <note>original: "historia"</note> within it, you shall arrange it so that, there being a great fire, the thing which is closest to said fire is most tinted by its color. For that thing which is nearest to an object participates most in its nature; and making the fire lean toward a red color, you shall make all things illuminated by it also reddish; and those which are further from said fire shall be more tinted by the black color of the night. The figures that are placed in front of the fire appear dark against the brightness of that fire, because that part of the object which you see is tinted by the darkness of the night and not by the brightness of the fire; and those who are at the sides should be half-obscured,

<sig>B iij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo provides specific instructions on the physical decorum and characteristic movements of different social groups—children, the elderly, and women—while also explaining the optical principles of painting night scenes illuminated by firelight.</summary>
<keywords>artistic decorum, figure drawing, perspective, chiaroscuro, night scenes, firelight, physiological characterization</keywords>

and half-reddened: and those things that can be seen beyond the edges of the flame will all be illuminated by a reddish light against a black background. As for the actions, you shall make those who are nearby shielding themselves with their hands and using their cloaks as protection from the excessive heat, turning their faces in the opposite direction to show they are fleeing: those further away, you shall make a great part of them shielding their eyes with their hands, as they are pained by the excessive brightness.

### *How one must represent a storm. Chapter LXVI.*

If you wish to represent a storm well, consider and weigh its effects carefully: when the wind, blowing over the surface of the sea or the land, removes and carries with it those things that are not firmly fixed to the general mass. And to represent that storm, you shall first make the clouds broken and shattered, driven along the course of the wind, accompanied by sandy dust raised from the seashores; and branches and leaves, lifted by the power of the wind, scattered through the air in the company of many other light things. The trees and plants should be bent to the ground, almost appearing to want to follow the course of the winds, with their branches twisted out of their natural growth and their leaves ruffled and turned inside out. And the men who are found there, some fallen and entangled in their clothes and the dust so that they are almost unrecognizable; and those who remain standing, let them be behind some tree, embracing it so that the wind does not drag them away; others with their hands over their eyes because of the dust, stooped toward the ground, with their clothes and hair streaming in the direction of the wind. Let the sea be troubled and tempestuous, full of swirling foam between the raised waves, and let the wind cause the finest foam to rise into the embattled air, like a thick and tangled mist. Of the ships that are within, make some with broken sails, the shreds of which flutter in the air along with some broken ropes; some with broken masts fallen, and the ship itself turned sideways and shattered among the tempestuous waves, with men screaming and clinging to what remains of the vessel. You shall make the clouds, driven by impetuous winds, beaten against the high peaks of the mountains, making them wrap and twist themselves in the likeness of waves striking against rocks; the air should be terrifying because of the dark gloom created by the dust, mist, and thick clouds.

### *How one must represent a battle. Chapter LXVII.*

You shall first make the smoke of the artillery mixed into the air together with the dust raised by the movement of the combatants' horses; this mixture you shall treat in this way: the dust, because it is an earthy and heavy thing, although by its fineness it easily rises and mixes into the air, nevertheless willingly returns downward, and its highest ascent is made by its finest part. Therefore, let it be seen the least, and it will appear almost the color of the air. The smoke that mixes into the dusty air, when it rises to a certain height, will look like dark clouds, and at the summit, the smoke will be seen more clearly than the dust; the smoke will lean toward a somewhat bluish color, while the dust will keep its own color. From the side where the light comes, this mixture of air, smoke, and dust will appear much brighter than on the opposite side. The more the combatants are within said turbulence, the less they will be seen, and the less difference there will be

from their lights to their shadows. And this redness, the further it moves from its cause, the more it should vanish. The figures located between you and the light, being far away, will appear dark against a bright field; and the closer their legs are to the ground, the less they should be seen, because the dust there is thicker and denser. And if you depict horses running outside the throng, make the little clouds of dust distant from one another, according to the interval of the horse's leaps; and the cloud furthest from the horse should be the least visible—rather, let it be high, scattered, and thin—while the nearest one should be the most evident, smaller, and more dense. Let the air be full of arrows <note>original: "saettume"</note> in various directions: some mounting, some descending, some on a flat line; and the bullets of the marksmen <note>original: "scoppetieri"</note> should be accompanied by a bit of smoke following their flight. The foremost figures you shall make dusty in their hair, eyebrows, and other places suited to holding dust. 

You shall make the victors running with their hair and other light things fluttering in the wind, with their brows lowered, and thrusting forward opposite limbs—that is, if they move the right foot forward, the left arm should also come forward. And if you depict someone who has fallen, you shall show the mark of his sliding through the dust turned into bloody mud; and around the half-liquid earth, you shall show the imprinted footprints of the men and horses that have passed by. You shall make some horses dragging their dead masters, leaving behind them in the dust and mud the trail of the dragged body. You shall make the conquered and beaten pale, with their brows raised and knitted, and the flesh above them full of sorrowful creases. The sides of the nose should have several wrinkles forming an arc from the nostrils and ending at the start of the eye. The nostrils should be high, causing these folds, and the arched lips should reveal the upper teeth. The teeth should be parted as if crying out in lamentation. One of the hands should shield the fearful eyes, turning the palm toward the enemy; the other should be on the ground to support the wounded torso. 

Others you shall make screaming with their mouths wide open, and fleeing; you shall place many kinds of weapons beneath the feet of the combatants, such as broken shields, lances, swords, and similar things. You shall make dead men, some half-covered by dust and others entirely. The dust that mixes with the flowing blood turns into red mud, and you will see the blood, in its own color, running in a winding course from the body to the dust. Others, dying, shall grind their teeth, roll their eyes, clench their fists against their bodies, and have their legs contorted. One might see someone disarmed and beaten down by the enemy, turning toward said enemy with bites and scratches to take a cruel and bitter revenge. One might see a riderless, light horse running with its mane scattered in the wind among the enemies, doing great damage with its hooves. One might see a crippled man fallen to the ground, making a cover of his shield, while the enemy, leaning low, struggles to deal him death. One might see a great group of men fallen together under a dead horse. You will see some victors leaving the combat and exiting the multitude, wiping their eyes and cheeks with their hands, which are covered in mud formed by the tearing of their eyes caused by the dust. You will see the reserve squads standing full of hope and suspicion, with their brows sharp <note>knitted in concentration</note>, shading them with their hands and watching...

### *On the method of rendering distant things in painting.* Chapter LXVIII.

It is clearly seen that one layer of air is thicker than another, specifically that which borders the flat earth; the higher it rises, the thinner and more transparent it becomes. Regarding elevated and large things that are far from you, their lower parts will be little seen, because you see them through a line that passes through the continuous thicker air. The summit of said height is proven to be seen through a line which, although it originates from your eye in the thick air, nevertheless, ending at the highest point of the object seen, ends in air much thinner than that of its base. For this reason, the further this line moves from you point by point, it constantly changes quality from thin to even thinner air. Therefore, you, the painter, when you make mountains, ensure that from hill to hill the summits are always lighter than the bases; and as you make them more distant from one another, make the summits lighter; and the higher they rise, the more they should show the variety of their form and color. <note>Leonardo is describing "aerial perspective," the effect of the atmosphere on the appearance of distant objects.</note>

---

### *How the air must be made lighter the lower you make it end.* Chapter LXIX.

Because this air is thick near the earth, and the higher it rises, the more it thins, when the sun is in the east and you look toward the west—including parts of the south and north—you will see that thick air receives more light from the sun than the thin air, because the rays find more resistance. And if the sky, to your sight, ends at the low plain, that furthest part of the sky will be seen through that thicker and whiter air, which will corrupt the truth of the color seen through it; and the sky will appear whiter there than above you, because the visual line passes through a smaller quantity of air corrupted by thick vapors. And if you look toward the east, the air will appear darker to you the lower it gets, because in said low air the luminous rays pass through less.

---

### *To make figures stand out from their background.* Chapter LXX.

The figures of any body will appear to be more in relief and stand out from their backgrounds when those backgrounds are of colors ranging from light to dark, with as much variety as possible at the edges of the aforementioned figures, as will be demonstrated in its proper place; and in said colors, the decrease of brightness in the whites and of darkness in the dark colors should be observed. <note>This refers to "chiaroscuro," the use of strong contrasts between light and dark to give the illusion of three-dimensional volume.</note>

<meta>catchword: CHAPTER.</meta>

<sig>C</sig>

## *On representing the sizes of painted things.* CHAPTER LXXI.

In the representation of the sizes that objects naturally possess when placed before the eye, the foremost figures—being small like the works of <term>miniaturists</term><note>Artists who painted highly detailed, small-scale illustrations in manuscripts.</note>—must be rendered with as much finish as the large figures of painters. However, the small works of miniaturists must be viewed from up close, and those of the painter from a distance. By doing so, these figures should correspond to the eye with equal thickness; and this occurs because they come to the eye with an equal angle of vision. This is proven as follows: let the object be B.C. and the eye be A., and let D.E. be a sheet of glass through which the <term>species</term><note>In Renaissance optics, "species" were the visual images or "likenesses" that objects were thought to shed and transmit through the air to the eye.</note> of B.C. penetrate. I say that while the eye A. remains stationary, the size of the painting made in imitation of B.C. must be of a smaller figure the closer the glass D.E. is to the eye A., and it must be equally finished. And if you finish that figure B.C. on the glass D.E., your figure must be less finished than the actual object B.C., and more finished than the figure M.N. made on the glass F.G. Because if the figure P.O. were as finished as the natural object B.C., the perspective of said O.P. would be false; for while the reduction of the figure would be correct (B.C. being diminished in P.O.), the level of finish would not agree with the distance. In seeking the perfection of the finish of the natural object B.C., then B.C. would appear to be at the proximity of O.P.; but if you wish to seek the reduction of O.P., then O.P. appears to be at the distance of B.C., and in the diminishing of the finish at the glass F.G.

---

## *On finished things, and blurred things.* CHAPTER LXXII.

Things that are finished and distinct should be made near at hand, and blurred things—that is, with blurred boundaries—are feigned in remote parts.

## *On figures that are separate, so that they do not appear joined.* CHAPTER LXXIII.

The colors with which you clothe your figures should be such that they grant grace to one another; and, when one color serves as the field <note>The background or surrounding area of a specific element.</note> for another, let it be such that they do not appear joined together...

<page-num>18</page-num>

joined together, even if they were of the same nature of color, but let them vary in brightness according to what the interposition of the distance requires, and the thickness of the air that is placed between them; and with the same rule should go the definition of their boundaries, that is, more or less distinct or blurred, according to what their proximity or remoteness requires.

### *Whether the light should be taken from the front, or from the side, and which gives more grace.*
->C H A P T E R  L X X I V.<-

**THE** light taken from the front on faces placed against side walls that are dark will be the reason that such faces will have great <term>relief</term><note>The illusion of three-dimensionality or "popping out" from the flat surface of the painting.</note>, and especially when having the light from above. And this relief occurs because the front parts of such a face are illuminated by the universal light of the air placed before it, whereby such an illuminated part has almost imperceptible shadows; and after these front parts of the face follow the side parts, darkened by the aforementioned side walls of the rooms, which darken the face all the more as that face enters between them with its parts. And besides this, it follows that the light descending from above deprives all those parts of itself to which a shield is made by the reliefs of the face, such as the eyebrows which subtract the light from the sockets of the eyes, and the nose which takes it from a great part of the mouth, and the chin from the throat, and other similar reliefs.

### *On reverberation.*
->C H A P T E R  L X X V.<-

**REVERBERATIONS** <note>Reflected light bouncing off one surface onto another.</note> are caused by bodies of a bright quality, with a flat and semi-dense surface, which, when struck by light, strike it back into the first object, similar to the bounce of a ball.

### *Where there cannot be luminous reverberation.*
->C H A P T E R  L X X V I.<-

**ALL** dense bodies clothe their surfaces with various qualities of lights and shadows. Lights are of two natures: one is called original, and the other derivative. I say original is that which derives from a flame of fire, or from the light of the sun, or the air. Derivative light shall be reflected light. But to return to the promised definition, I say that luminous reverberation will not come from that part of the body which is turned toward shadowy bodies, such as dark places of roofs of various heights, of grass, green or dry woods; which, although the part of each branch turned toward the original light clothes itself with the quality of that light, nevertheless the shadows made by each branch upon the other are so many that in short such darkness results that the light there is as nothing. Therefore, such objects cannot give any reflected light to the opposite bodies.

### *On reflections.*
->C H A P T E R  L X X V I I.<-

**REFLECTIONS** shall participate more or less in the thing where they are generated than in the thing that generates them, according to whether the thing where they are generated has a cleaner <note>Meaning smoother or more polished.</note> surface than that which generates them.

<summary>Leonardo explains how atmospheric thickness blurs distant objects and describes how high, frontal lighting creates dramatic three-dimensional relief on faces. He also defines the physics of reflected light, noting that dark or cluttered surfaces like foliage fail to produce reflections.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric perspective, relief, lighting, shadows, reverberation, reflected light, optics, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

## *On the reflections of lights that surround shadows. Chapter LXXVIII.*

The reflections of illuminated parts that bounce back into the opposing shadows, illuminating or lightening their darkness more or less according to whether they are more or less near, and with more or less clarity—this consideration is put into practice by many, while there are many others who avoid it, and these two groups mock one another. But you, to avoid the criticisms of both, should put both into practice where they are necessary; however, ensure that their causes are known—that is, that a manifest cause for the reflections and their colors is seen, and likewise a manifest cause for the things that do not reflect. By doing so, you will not be entirely blamed nor praised by the various judgments, which, unless they are of total ignorance, will necessarily have to praise you in everything, satisfying both sects.

## *Where the reflections of lights are of greater or lesser clarity. Chapter LXXIX.*

The reflections of lights are of so much less or greater clarity and evidence as they are seen against fields of greater or lesser darkness. This happens because if the field is darker than the reflection, then that reflection will be strong and evident due to the great difference those colors have between them; but if the reflection is seen against a field lighter than itself, then such a reflection will show itself to be dark in respect to the whiteness with which it borders, and thus such a reflection will be <term>insensible</term><note>In this context, "insensible" means "imperceptible" or "unnoticeable" to the eye.</note>.

## *Which part of the reflection will be clearest. Chapter LXXX.*

**T**hat part will be clearest or most illuminated by the reflection which receives the light between more equal angles. Let the luminous source be N. and let A.B. be the illuminated part of the body, which bounces throughout the opposite concavity, which is in shadow. And let it be that such light, which reflects at F., is struck between equal angles. E. will not be a reflection from a base of equal angles, as is shown by the angle E.A.B., which is more <term>obtuse</term><note>An angle greater than 90 degrees.</note> than the angle E.B.A.; but the angle A.F.B., even though it is among angles of lesser quality than angle E., has base B.A. which is between more equal angles than that angle E., and therefore it will be clearer at F. than at E. And it will also be clearer because it will be closer to the thing that illuminates it, according to the sixth [rule] which says: That part of the shaded body will be more illuminated which is closer to its luminous source.

<summary>Leonardo discusses the importance of painting reflected light (reverberations) within shadows and explains how the intensity of these reflections depends on background contrast and the geometry of angles.</summary>
<keywords>reflected light, shadows, contrast, optics, geometry, angles, painting technique, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

### *On the reflected colors of the flesh.* Chapter LXXXI.

The reflections of the flesh that receive light from other flesh are redder and of a more excellent <term>incarnation</term><note>From the Italian 'incarnatione,' referring to the depiction of flesh tones in painting.</note> than any other part of the flesh found in man. This occurs because of the 3rd [proposition] of the 2nd book, which says: The surface of every opaque body participates in the color of its object; and so much more as such an object is closer to it, and so much less as it is more remote. And [this is also true] as the opaque body is larger, because being large, it blocks the <term>species</term><note>In Renaissance optics, 'species' (spetie) were the formal images or qualities emitted by objects that travel through the air to the eye or other surfaces.</note> of the surrounding objects, which are often of various colors that corrupt the primary species that are closer when the bodies are small. However, it does not fail to happen that a small nearby color tints a reflection more than a large remote color, according to the 6th [proposition] of perspective, which says: Large things can be at such a distance that they appear much smaller than small things nearby.

### *Where reflections are most sensible.* Chapter LXXXII.

That reflection will be of more ready evidence which is seen against a field of greater darkness, and that will be less <term>sensible</term><note>Meaning less perceptible or noticeable.</note> which is seen against a lighter field. This arises because when things of various darkness are placed in contrast, the less dark one makes the one that is darker seem like deep gloom; and when things of various whiteness are placed in contrast, the whiter one makes the other seem less white than it truly is.

### *On doubled and tripled reflections.* Chapter LXXXIII.

Doubled reflections are of greater power than simple reflections, and the shadows that are interposed between the <term>incident light</term><note>The primary light falling directly from the source onto the object.</note> and these reflections are of little darkness. For example, let A be the luminous source, A-N and A-S be the direct [rays], and let S and N be... <note>The text cuts off here as "sian" is a catchword for the following page.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains how flesh tones reflect onto one another to create vibrant colors and details how the visibility of a reflection depends entirely on the darkness of its background.</summary>
<keywords>flesh tones, optics, reflected light, perspective, contrast, incarnation, species, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

the parts of these bodies illuminated at **O.** and **E.** are the parts of these bodies illuminated by reflections: and the reflection **A. N. E.** is the simple reflection; **A. N. O.** and **AS. O.** is the double reflection. That is called a simple reflection which is seen by only one illuminated body, and the double is seen by two illuminated bodies, as well as the simple. **E.** is created by the illuminated body **B. D.**; the double reflection **O.** is composed from the illuminated body **B. D.** and from the illuminated body **D. R.**; and its shadow is of little darkness, which is interposed between the incident light **N.** and the reflected light **NO. SO.** <note>Leonardo is describing how light bouncing from multiple sources reduces the intensity of a shadow.</note>

## *How no reflected color is simple, but is mixed with the species of other colors.*
### CHAPTER LXXXIV.

NO color that reflects onto the surface of another body tints that surface with its own pure color, but it will be mixed with the concurrence of the other reflected colors that bounce back to the same place: as will happen with the color yellow **A.** which reflects onto the part of the sphere **C. O. E.**; and in the same place the color blue **B.** reflects. I say that because of this mixed reflection of yellow and blue, the strike of their concurrence will tint the sphere; and if it was white in itself, it will make it green, because it is proven that yellow and blue mixed together make a most beautiful green. <note>Leonardo applies the principles of pigment mixing to the physics of light, showing that reflections behave like paints when they overlap on a surface.</note>

<summary>Leonardo distinguishes between simple and double reflections and explains how overlapping reflections of different colors (such as yellow and blue) combine to create a new color (green) on a surface.</summary>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

## *How reflected colors are very rarely the same color as the body where they join.*
### CHAPTER LXXXV.

**I**t very rarely happens that reflections are of the same color as the body they strike, or of the same color as the object that produces them. For example, let the sphere **D. F. G. E.** be yellow, and the object that reflects its color upon it be **B. C.**, which is blue. I say that the part of the sphere which is struck by such a reflection will be tinted a green color, provided that **B. C.** is illuminated by the air or by the sun. <note>Leonardo explains that light behaves like pigments; when blue light reflects onto a yellow surface, the eye perceives green.</note>

---

## *Where the reflection will be most visible.* CHAPTER LXXXVI.

**A**mong reflections of the same shape, size, and strength, that part will appear more or less powerful which ends against a field <note>The "field" refers to the background color or surrounding area.</note> that is more or less dark.

**T**he surfaces of bodies participate more in the color of those objects which reflect their likeness <note>original: "similitudine" - in Renaissance optics, this refers to the visual image or 'species' of an object traveling through light.</note> into them at more equal angles.

**O**f the colors of objects that reflect their likenesses onto the surfaces of opposite bodies at equal angles, that one will be most powerful whose reflected ray has the shortest length.

**A**mong the colors of objects reflected at equal angles and with some distance onto the surface of opposite bodies, that one will be most powerful which is of a lighter color.

**T**hat object reflects its color most intensely onto the opposite body when it has no other colors around itself except those of its own kind. But that reflection will be of a more confused color when it is generated by various colors of different objects.

**T**hat color which is closest to the reflection will tint that reflection most strongly with itself, and vice versa.

**T**herefore you, painter, should make use of the color of the parts of the clothing in the reflections of the faces of your figures, specifically those parts which are nearest to the flesh tones closest to them; but do not separate them with too much definition unless necessary. <note>This is a practical tip for "sfumato" or soft blending: skin should subtly take on the hue of nearby fabric to look realistic.</note>

<vocab>reflections, color, spherical, object, reflection, illuminated, equal angles, reflected ray, painter, face/image</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains the optical physics of color mixing through reflection, specifically how blue light on a yellow surface creates green. He provides rules for painters on how distance, angle, and background contrast affect the intensity of these reflections on the human form.</summary>
<keywords>optics, color theory, reflections, geometry of light, painting technique, Leonardo da Vinci, color mixing, light rays</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

## On the colors of reflections. Chapter LXXXVII.

All reflected colors are of lesser luminosity than the direct light, and the same proportion exists between the incident light <note>The primary light source striking an object.</note> and the reflected light as exists between the luminosity of their causes.

## On the boundaries of reflections in their field. Chapter LXXXVIII.

The boundary of a reflection placed against a field <note>The background or surrounding surface.</note> lighter than the reflection itself will cause that reflection to be imperceptible; but if such a reflection ends against a field darker than itself, then that reflection will be perceptible, and it will become more evident the darker that field is, and likewise conversely.

## On the placement of figures. Chapter LXXXIX.

By as much as the side of the nude figure **D. A.** diminishes by resting <note>Referring to the weight-bearing side of the body.</note>, the opposite side increases by the same amount: that is, as much as the side **D. A.** diminishes from its measure, the opposite side grows beyond its measure. The navel <note>original: "bellico"</note> never leaves its height, nor does the virile member <note>The penis; Leonardo uses these as fixed anatomical center points.</note>. This lowering occurs because when a figure rests upon one foot, that foot becomes the center of the weight placed above it. This being so, the center of the shoulders aligns itself directly above it, moving out of its perpendicular line—the line which passes through the superficial centers of the body. This line becomes more twisted at its upper extremity over the foot that supports the weight; the transverse lineaments <note>The horizontal lines of the hips and shoulders.</note>, constrained to equal angles, result in their ends being lower on the side that supports the weight, as appears in **A. B. C.**

<note>This chapter describes "contrapposto," a classical pose where the weight is shifted onto one leg, causing a tilt in the hips and shoulders that creates a more dynamic and naturalistic appearance in art.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains how the visibility of reflected light depends on the darkness of the background and provides a detailed anatomical rule for the "contrapposto" pose in figure drawing.</summary>
<keywords>reflected light, luminosity, figure drawing, anatomy, contrapposto, proportions, weight distribution, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

### *How reflections very rarely share the color of the body where they join.*
### CHAPTER LXXXV.

**VERY RARELY** does it happen that reflections are of the same color as the body they strike, or the same as the object from which they originate. For example, let the spherical body **D. F. G. E.** be yellow, and the object that reflects its color upon it be **B. C.**, which is blue. I say that the part of the sphere struck by such a reflection will be tinted green, provided that **B. C.** is illuminated <note>original: "alluminato"; meaning lit by a light source.</note> by the air or the sun.

### *Where the reflection will be most visible.*
### CHAPTER LXXXVI.

**AMONG** reflections of the same shape, size, and strength, that part will appear more or less powerful which ends against a field <note>The background or adjacent surface.</note> that is more or less dark.

**THE** surfaces of bodies participate more in the color of those objects which reflect their likeness onto them at more equal angles <note>Referring to the law of reflection where the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection.</note>.

**OF** the colors of objects that reflect their likenesses onto the surfaces of opposing bodies at equal angles, that one will be most powerful whose reflected ray <note>original: "razzo reflesso"</note> has the shortest length.

**AMONG** the colors of objects reflected at equal angles and from some distance onto the surface of opposite bodies, that one will be most powerful which is of a lighter color.

**THAT** object reflects its color most intensely onto the opposing body when it has no other colors around it except those of its own kind. However, that reflection will be of a more confused color when it is generated by various colors from different objects.

**THAT** color which is closest to the reflection will tint that reflection most strongly with itself, and vice versa.

**THEREFORE** you, painter <note>Leonardo frequently addresses the reader directly as a practitioner.</note>, in the reflections of the faces of your figures, make use of the color of the parts of the garments that are near the parts of the flesh closest to them; but do not separate them with too much definition <note>original: "pronuntiatione"; referring to the sharpness or "pronouncement" of the edge.</note> unless necessary.

### On the colors of reflections. Chapter LXXXVII.

All reflected colors are of less luminosity than direct light, and the same proportion exists between the incident light and the reflected light as that which exists between the luminosities of their sources. <note>Leonardo is applying a mathematical logic to light: the reflection is always weaker than the original light source in a predictable ratio.</note>

### On the boundaries of reflections in their field. Chapter LXXXVIII.

The boundary of a reflection placed against a field <note>The background or surrounding area.</note> lighter than the reflection itself will cause that reflection to be imperceptible; but if such a reflection ends against a field darker than itself, then the reflection will be perceptible, and it will become more evident the darker that field is, and likewise conversely.

### On the placement of figures. Chapter LXXXIX.

By as much as the part of the nude figure **D. A.** decreases by resting <note>original: "posare"; referring to the side of the body supporting the weight.</note>, the opposite part increases: that is, by as much as the part **D. A.** decreases from its measure, the opposite part exceeds its measure, while the navel or the virile member <note>The penis.</note> never leaves its height. This lowering occurs because when a figure rests upon one foot, that foot becomes the center of the superimposed weight. This being so, the middle of the shoulders aligns itself above it, moving out of its perpendicular line—the line which passes through the superficial centers of the body. And as this line's upper extremity comes to twist more over the resting foot, the transverse lineaments <note>The horizontal lines of the body, such as the hips and shoulders.</note>, constrained to equal angles, become lower at their ends on the side that rests, as appears in **A. B. C.** <note>This describes "contrapposto," a classical posture where the weight is shifted onto one leg, causing a natural tilt in the hips and shoulders to maintain balance.</note>

### *On the way to learn well how to compose figures together in narrative scenes.*
->C H A P. LXXXX.<-

**When** you have learned perspective <note>original: "prospettiva"</note> well, and have committed to memory all the limbs and bodies of things, be eager frequently in your walks to see and consider the positions of men in speaking, or in contending, or in laughing, or fighting together; what actions are in them, and what actions the bystanders, the peacemakers <note>original: "spartitori"; those who step in to separate fighters.</note>, and the onlookers of these things perform. Note these with brief marks in a small notebook of yours, which you ought always to carry with you. And let it be of tinted paper, so that you do not have to erase, but rather change from the old to the new; for these are not things to be erased, but rather preserved with the greatest diligence. Because the infinite forms and actions of things are so many that the memory is not capable of retaining them; therefore, you shall keep these as your helpers and masters.

### *On first placing a figure in the narrative scene.*
->C A P. LXXXI.<-

**The** first figure in the narrative scene <note>original: "historia"; a complex composition telling a story, often historical or biblical.</note> you shall make as much smaller than life-size as the number of cubits <note>original: "braccia"; a traditional unit of measurement based on the length of an arm.</note> you imagine it to be distant from the front line; and then make the others in comparison to that one, using the rule mentioned above.

### *The method of composing narrative scenes.*
->C A P. LXXXII.<-

**Of** the figures that compose the narrative scenes, that one will show the greatest relief <note>original: "rilievo"; the illusion of three-dimensional depth and solidity.</note> which is feigned to be closest to the eye. This happens because of the 2nd [rule] of the 3rd [book], which says: That color shows itself in greater perfection which has a lesser quantity of air interposed between itself and the eye that judges it. For this reason, the shadows, which show the bodies to be in relief, show themselves even darker nearby than from a distance, where they are corrupted by the air interposed between the eye and those shadows. This does not happen in shadows near the eye, where they show the bodies with so much greater relief as they are of greater darkness.

### *On composing narrative scenes.*
->C A P. LXXXIII.<-

**Remember**, painter, when you make a single figure, to avoid foreshortenings <note>original: "scorci"; the technique of depicting an object as shorter than it is to create the illusion of depth.</note> of it, both of the parts and of the whole, because you would have to struggle with the ignorance of those unlearned in such art; but in narrative scenes, do them in all the ways that occur to you, and especially in battles, where by necessity there occur infinite foreshortenings and bendings of the participants in such discord, or one might say, most beastly madness <note>original: "pazzia bestialissima"; a famous phrase used by Leonardo to describe the horrors of war.</note>.

### *Variety of men in narrative scenes.*
->C A P. LXXXIV.<-

**In** narrative scenes, there ought to be men of various temperaments, statures, complexions, attitudes, fatness, thinness, thick, slender, large, small, fat, lean, fierce, civil, old, young, strong and muscular, weak and with few muscles, cheerful, melancholy, and with hair curly and straight, short and long, movements prompt and languid, and likewise various garments, and colors, and whatever thing is required in that narrative scene.

### *On learning the movements of man.*
->C A P. LXXXV.<-

**The** movements of man are to be learned after the knowledge
<meta>catchword: of</meta>

the proportion of the limbs, and of the whole, in all the movements of the limbs and joints; and then, with the brief notation of a few signs, observe the attitudes of men in their various accidents <note>In this context, "accidents" refers to spontaneous events, emotions, or sudden changes in physical state.</note>, without them realizing that you are watching them. For if they notice you, they will have their minds occupied by you, and will abandon the ferocity of their natural action, upon which they were previously intent—just as when two angry men contend together, each believing himself to be in the right, moving their brows, arms, and other limbs with great ferocity and with actions appropriate to their intention and their words. You could not achieve this if you tried to make them feign such anger, or any other state, such as laughter, weeping, pain, wonder, fear, and the like. Therefore, be eager to carry with you a small notebook of prepared paper <note>original: "libretto di carte ingessate"; paper coated with a ground of bone ash or gesso to allow for metalpoint drawing.</note>, and with a silver stylus <note>original: "stile d’argento"; a metalpoint tool that leaves a delicate, permanent mark on prepared paper.</note> briefly note such movements, and similarly note the actions of the bystanders and their distribution. This will teach you how to compose narrative scenes <note>original: "historie"; complex multi-figure compositions that tell a story.</note>; and when you have filled your notebook, set it aside and keep it for your purposes. A good painter has two principal things to observe: man, and the concept of his mind <note>original: "concetto suo della mente"; the internal thought or emotion that drives a physical action.</note> which he holds within, which is of the utmost importance.

# On the composition of narrative scenes. Chap. LXXXVI.

The study of the composition of narrative scenes must involve placing the figures roughly—that is, sketched out—and first knowing how to execute them well in all their turns, bends, and extensions of their limbs. Then, take the description of two who are boldly fighting together, and let this invention be examined in various actions and from various aspects. Afterward, follow with the combat of the bold man against the cowardly and fearful one; and let these actions, and many other states of the soul, be speculated upon with great examination and study.

# On variety in narrative scenes. Chap. LXXXVII.

Let the painter delight in the abundance and variety of the compositions of narrative scenes, and let him avoid repeating any part that has already been done, so that novelty and abundance may attract and delight the eye of the viewer. I say, therefore, that in a narrative scene it is required, according to the setting, to mix men of different appearances, with different ages and clothing, mingled together with women, children, dogs, horses, and buildings, fields, and hills. Let dignity and decorum <note>original: "decoro"; the artistic principle of suitability and appropriateness of style to the subject matter.</note> be observed for the prince and the wise man, separating them from the common folk. Nor should you mix the melancholy and weeping with the cheerful and laughing; for nature dictates that the cheerful stay with the cheerful, and the laughing with the laughing, and so on for the contrary.

# On diversifying the expressions of faces in narrative scenes. Chap. LXXXVIII.

It is a common defect among Italian painters that the expression and figure of the Emperor <note>original: "l’aria e figura dell’ Imperatore"; likely a reference to a specific model or a stereotypical "noble" face used repeatedly by artists.</note> can be recognized throughout many painted figures. To avoid such an error, the same figures should never be made or repeated, neither in whole nor in part, nor should one face be seen in another narrative scene. And the more you observe in a narrative scene that the ugly is near the beautiful, and the old near the young, and the weak near the strong, the more charming your narrative will be, and one figure by the other in- <note>The text cuts off mid-word to match the original catchword "aç-".</note>

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to use small notebooks and silver styli to capture spontaneous human emotions and movements from life. He emphasizes the importance of variety, decorum, and the juxtaposition of opposites (like beauty and ugliness) to create engaging narrative compositions.</summary>

will increase in beauty. And because it often happens that painters, when drawing whatever thing they wish, want every tiny charcoal mark to be valid, they deceive themselves in this; for many are the times when the figured animal does not have the movements of its limbs appropriate to its mental state <note>original: "moto mentale"; the internal intention or emotion that drives physical action.</note>. And having made a beautiful and pleasing arrangement of limbs <note>original: "membrificatione"; the anatomical structure or assembly of the body parts.</note>, well-finished, it will seem to him an injurious thing to change those limbs.

->*On matching colors with one another, and how one gives grace to the other.*<-
->CHAPTER LXXXIX.<-

**IF** you wish to ensure that the proximity of one color gives grace to the other that borders it, use that rule which is seen to occur with the rays of the sun in the composition of the celestial arch <note>The rainbow.</note>; those colors are generated in the movement of the rain, because each droplet is transformed in its descent into each of the colors of such an arch, as has been demonstrated in its proper place.

Now take heed: if you wish to create an excellent darkness, give it an excellent whiteness for comparison; and likewise, you will create an excellent whiteness with the greatest darkness. And pale colors will make red appear of a more fiery redness than it would appear by itself in comparison with peacock-blue <note>original: "pauonazzo"; a deep purple or violet-blue similar to a peacock's neck.</note>. There is another rule which does not aim to make the colors in themselves of a more supreme beauty than they naturally are, but rather that their company gives grace to one another, as green does to red, and likewise the opposite, such as green with azure-blue. And there is a second rule generating a graceless company, such as azure with yellow that tends toward white, or with white, and similar pairings, which shall be discussed in their proper place.

->*On making vivid and beautiful colors on their surfaces.* CHAPTER C.<-

**ALWAYS** for those colors that you wish to have beauty, you shall first prepare a field of the purest white; and I say this of colors that are transparent, because for those that are not transparent, a light field is of no use. The example of this is taught to us by the colors of glass, which, when they are placed between the eye and the luminous air, show themselves with excellent beauty, which they cannot do if they have behind them the dark air or other obscurity.

->*On the colors of the shadows of any color.* CHAPTER CI.<-

**THE** color of the shadow of any color always participates in the color of its object <note>The object casting the shadow or reflecting light into it.</note>, and more or less so as it is nearer or more remote from that shadow, and as it is more or less luminous.

->*On the variety that the colors of remote and near things make.*<-
->CHAPTER CII.<-

**OF** things darker than the air, that which is more remote will demonstrate less darkness; and of things lighter than the air, that which is more remote from the eye will demonstrate less whiteness. For among things lighter and darker than the air, when changing color over a long distance, the light thing acquires darkness, and the dark thing acquires lightness <note>This describes "atmospheric perspective," where the intervening air softens contrasts as objects recede.</note>.

### *At what distance the colors of things are entirely lost.*
->CHAPTER CIII.<-

The colors of objects are lost entirely at a greater or lesser distance, according to whether the eyes and the object seen are at a greater or lesser height. This is proven by the 7th [proposition] of this [treatise], which says: The air is more or less thick <note>original: "grossa"; referring to atmospheric density or "body," which obscures clarity.</note> the closer or further it is from the earth. Therefore, if the eye and the object seen by it are close to the earth, then the thickness of the air interposed between the eye and the object will greatly impede the color of the object seen by that eye. But if such an eye, together with the object seen by it, are remote from the earth, then such air will occupy <note>Obscure or veil.</note> little of the color of the aforementioned object; and there are as many varieties of distances in which the colors of objects are lost as there are varieties of the day, and as there are varieties of the thicknesses or thinness of the air through which the species <note>original: "specie"; a technical term in optics referring to the visual image or "likeness" of an object traveling through a medium to the eye.</note> of the colors of the aforementioned objects penetrate to the eye.

### *Color of the shadow of white.* CHAPTER CIV.

The shadow of white seen by the sun and by the air has its shadows pulling toward blue; and this arises because white by itself is not a color, but is a recipient of whatever color, and by the 4th [proposition] of this [treatise] which says: The surface of every body participates in the color of its object <note>The "object" here refers to the surrounding environment or light source facing the surface.</note>; it is necessary that that part of the white surface participates in the color of the air, its object.

### *Which color will make the blackest shadow.* CHAPTER CV.

That shadow will participate most in black which is generated on the whitest surface, and this will have a greater propensity for variety than any other surface; and this arises because white is not numbered among the colors, and is receptive to every color, and its surface participates more intensely in the colors of its objects than any other surface of whatever color, and especially of its direct contrary, which is black (or other dark colors), from which white is most remote by nature; and for this reason, there appears to be, and is, a great difference from its principal shadows to its principal lights.

### *Of the color that shows no variety in various thicknesses of air.*
->CHAPTER CVI.<-

It is possible that one and the same color makes no change at various distances, and this will happen when the proportion of the thicknesses of the airs, and the proportions of the distances that the colors have from the eye, are the same, but inverse. It is proven thus: let **A.** be the eye, **H.** be any color you wish, placed at one degree of distance remote from the eye, in air of four degrees of thickness; but because the 2nd degree above **A. M. N. L.** has air half as thin, the air carrying in it the same color, it is necessary that such color be twice as remote from the eye as it was before: therefore we will place it at the two degrees **A. F.** & **F. G.** distant from the eye, and it will be color **G.**, which then rising into the degree of double thinness at the 2nd in **A. M.** <note>Leonardo is describing a mathematical ratio where the clarity of a color remains constant if the distance increases in exact proportion to the thinning of the atmosphere.</note>

Therefore, by first calculating the thickness of the air, and then the distances, you will see colors varied in position that will not have changed in beauty <note>original: "bellezza"; here meaning the clarity or purity of the pigment.</note>; and we shall say thus for the calculation of the thickness of the air: the color H. is placed in four degrees of air thickness; G. color is placed in air of two degrees of thickness; E. color is found in air of one degree of thickness; now let us see if the distances are in equal but inverse proportion. The color E. is found distant from the eye at two and a half degrees of distance. G. at two degrees. H. at one degree: this distance does not match the proportion of the thickness, but it is necessary to make a third calculation, and this is what you must say. The degree A. C. is worth two degrees of thickness of the air above, and the half-degree C. B. is worth a whole one of that same air above, so that we have three degrees in value of that thickness above, and one is within, that is B. E. being the fourth. It follows that A. H. has four degrees of air thickness; A. G. also has four, that is A. F. has two, and F. G. two others, which makes four. A. E. also has four,

He argues that a shorter distance through thick, low-lying air obscures a color as much as a longer distance through thinner, elevated air.</summary>

### On the perspective of colors. Chapter CVII.

Of one same color placed at various distances and equal height, such shall be the
proportion of its lightening <note>original: "rischiaramento"; the way a color appears paler or more washed out as it recedes.</note>, as will be that of the distances that
each of these colors has from the eye that sees them. To prove this, let E.B.C.D.
be one same color: the 1st, E., be placed two degrees of distance from the eye
A: the 2nd, which is B., be four degrees away: the third, which is C., be six degrees: the
4th, which is D., be eight degrees: as shown by the definitions of the circles that
intersect on the line, as seen above the line A.R. then A.R.S.P. be one
degree of thin air, S.P.E.T. be one degree of thicker air: it will follow that the
first color E. will pass to the eye through one degree of thick air E.S. and through one
degree of less thick air S.A. & the color B. will send its likeness <note>original: "similitudine"; a term used in optics to describe the visual image or "species" traveling from an object to the eye.</note> to the
eye A. through two degrees of thick air, and through two of the less thick, & the C.
will send it through three degrees of the thick, and through three of the less thick; & the color
D. through four of the thick, and through four of the less thick, and thus we have
proven here such to be the proportion of the diminution of colors, or you might
say losses <note>original: "perdimenti"; the fading or loss of detail and saturation due to atmospheric interference.</note>, as is that of their distances from the eye that sees them:
and this only happens in colors that are of equal height, because in those
that are of unequal height, the same rule is not observed, because they are
in airs of various thicknesses, which create various obstructions to those colors.

---

### Of the color that does not change in various thicknesses of air. Chapter CVIII.

The color placed in different thicknesses of air will not change, when it shall be

<sig>D iij</sig>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, perspective of colors, thin air, thick air, diminution of colors, losses, lightening</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo concludes his proof on how altitude compensates for distance in color perception and introduces a new rule: for objects at the same altitude, the fading of color is directly proportional to their distance from the eye.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric perspective, optics, air density, color theory, geometry of vision, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from page 29 regarding the mathematical relationship between atmospheric density and the perception of color at varying altitudes and distances.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

one is as much more remote from the eye than the other, as one is found in thinner air than the other. This is proved thus: If the first, low air has four degrees of thickness <note>original: "grossezza"; referring here to atmospheric density or opacity.</note>, and the color is distant one degree from the eye; and the second, higher air has three degrees of thickness (having lost one degree), this causes the color to acquire one degree of distance. And when the higher air has lost two degrees of thickness, and the color has acquired two degrees of distance, then the first color is the same as the third. To be brief, if the color rises so much that it enters air that has lost three degrees of thickness, and the color has acquired three degrees of distance, then you may be certain that the high and remote color has suffered as much loss of color as the low and near color. This is because if the high air has lost three-quarters of the thickness of the low air, the color in rising has acquired three-quarters of the total distance by which it is remote from the eye: and thus our intent is proved.

### *Whether various colors can be, or appear to be, of a uniform darkness by means of the same shadow. CHAPTER CIX.*

**IT IS POSSIBLE** that all the varieties of colors may appear transformed into the color of the shadows themselves by one same shadow. This is manifested in the darkness of a cloudy night, in which no figure or color of a body can be discerned. And because darkness is nothing other than the deprivation of incident and reflected light—by means of which all figures and colors of bodies are understood—it is necessary that when the cause of light is entirely removed, the effect and knowledge of the colors and figures of the aforementioned bodies must also fail.

### *On the cause of the loss of colors and figures of bodies by means of shadows that appear to be, but are not. CHAPTER CX.*

**MANY** are the sites that are in themselves illuminated and bright, yet appear dark and entirely deprived of any variety of colors or figures of the things found within them. This happens because of the light of the illuminated air that is interposed between the things seen and the eye—as is seen inside windows that are remote from the eye, in which one perceives only a uniform and quite deep obscurity. If you then enter into that house, you will see that it is strongly illuminated in itself, and you will be able to readily perceive every minimal part of whatever thing might be found inside such a window. And this demonstration arises from a defect of the eye, which, overcome by the excessive light of the air, greatly restricts the size of its pupil, and for this reason loses much of its power. In darker places, the pupil dilates and increases in power as much as it acquires in size. Proved in the 2nd [book] of my *Perspective*. <note>Leonardo refers to his own lost or projected treatise on optics and perspective.</note>

### *How no thing shows its true color unless it has light from another similar color. CHAPTER CXI.*

**NO** thing will ever show its own proper color if the light that illuminates it is not entirely of that same color; and this is manifested in the colors of
