<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<sig>D iiij</sig>

fabrics, of which the illuminated folds, which reflect or give light to the opposing folds, cause them to demonstrate their true color. The same occurs with gold leaf in giving light to one another, and the opposite happens when it receives light from another color.

### *On colors that appear to vary from their true being, by means of the comparison of their backgrounds. CHAPTER CXII.*

NO boundary of uniform color will appear to be equal unless it ends in a background of a color similar to itself. This is clearly seen when black borders with white, where each color appears more noble <note>In this context, "noble" refers to the purity, intensity, or vividness of the hue.</note> at the boundaries of its opposite than it will appear in its center.

### *On the change of transparent colors applied or placed over different colors, with their different relationship. CHAPTER CXIII.*

WHEN a transparent color is over another color varied from it, a mixed color is composed, different from each of the simple colors that compose it. This is seen in the smoke that comes out of the chimney, which, when it is against the black of said chimney, becomes blue, and when it rises against the blue of the air, it appears grayish <note>original: "berretino"; a dull, ash-gray or leaden color.</note> or reddish. And so purple <note>original: "pauonazzo"; specifically a deep violet or peacock-purple.</note> applied over blue becomes the color of a violet; and when blue is applied over yellow, it becomes green; and saffron <note>original: "croco"; referring to a deep yellow-orange pigment.</note> over white becomes yellow; and light over darkness becomes blue, all the more beautiful as the light and the dark are more excellent.

### *Which part of the same color will show itself most beautiful in painting. CHAPTER CXIV.*

HERE it is to be noted which part of the same color shows itself most beautiful in painting: whether that which has the luster <note>original: "lustro"; the specular highlight or "glint" on a surface.</note>, or that which has the light, or that of the middle shadows, or that of the dark ones, or indeed in transparency. Here one must understand what color is being asked about: because different colors have their beauties in different parts of themselves. This is shown to us by black, which has its beauty in the shadows; white in the light; blue, green, and tan <note>original: "tanè"; a brownish-orange or tawny hue.</note> in the middle shadows; yellow and red in the lights; gold in the reflections; and lake <note>original: "lacca"; a transparent red pigment made from organic dyes.</note> in the middle shadows.

### *How every color that has no luster is more beautiful in its luminous parts than in its shadowy parts. CHAPTER CXV.*

EVERY color is more beautiful in its illuminated part than in its shadowy part, and this arises because light vivifies and gives true knowledge of the quality of colors, and shadow dampens and obscures that same beauty, and hinders the knowledge of said color. And if, on the contrary, black is more beautiful in the shadows than in the lights, the answer is that black is not a color.

### *On the evidence of colors. CHAPTER CXVI.*

THAT thing which is lighter appears more from a distance, and the darker thing does the opposite.

<summary>Leonardo explores how colors are perceived differently based on their backgrounds, their transparency when layered, and their placement in light or shadow. He notes that different pigments reach their peak "beauty" or intensity in different lighting conditions, such as black in shadow and red in light.</summary>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

## *Which part of the color should reasonably be the most beautiful.*
### CHAPTER CXVII.

IF A. be the light, and B. be the surface illuminated directly by that light: E. which cannot see that light, sees only the illuminated wall: which wall we shall say is red. This being so, the light generated at the wall will resemble its cause, and will tinge the face E. in red; and if E. should also be red, you will see it to be much more beautiful than B. And if E. were yellow, you will see a changing color created between yellow and red.

<margin>117</margin>

---

## *How the beauty of color must be in the lights.* CHAPTER CXVIII.

IF we see that the quality of colors is known by means of light, it is to be judged that where there is more light, there one sees more of the true quality of the illuminated color; and where there is more darkness, the color is tinged with the color of that darkness. Therefore you, <term>painter</term> <note>original: "pittore"</note>, remember to show the truth of colors in such illuminated parts.

## *Of the green color made from the rust of copper.* CHAPTER CXIX.

THE green made from copper <note>Referring to verdigris, a common but unstable pigment in the Renaissance.</note>, even if such color is applied in oil, its beauty vanishes into thin air <note>original: "sene và in fumo"; literally "goes up in smoke," meaning it fades or loses its vibrancy.</note> if it is not immediately <term>varnished</term> <note>original: "inuernicato"</note>: and not only does it vanish, but if it is washed with a sponge soaked in simple common water, it will lift off from the panel where it is painted, especially if the weather is humid. This happens because such <term>verdigris</term> <note>original: "verderame"; a green pigment formed by the action of acetic acid on copper plates.</note> is made by the force of salt, which salt easily dissolves in rainy weather, and especially when being soaked and washed with the aforementioned sponge.

## *Increase of beauty in verdigris.* CHAPTER CXX.

IF <term>aloe caballino</term> <note>A lower grade of aloe resin, often used as a transparent glaze or binder.</note> is mixed with the <term>verdigris</term>, that <term>verdigris</term> will acquire great beauty, and it would acquire even more from <term>saffron</term> <note>original: "zaffaranno"</note>, if it did not vanish away.

And the quality of this <term>horse aloe</term> <note>original: "aloe cauallino"; a lower grade of aloe resin used as a warm, transparent glaze.</note> is recognized when it dissolves in <term>aqua vitae</term> <note>original: "acquauite"; high-proof distilled alcohol.</note> while it is hot; for it dissolves better than when it is cold. And if you had finished a work with that simple green, and then thinly glazed it with this aloe dissolved in water, then that work would become a most beautiful color. Furthermore, this aloe can be ground in oil by itself, and also together with <term>verdigris</term> <note>original: "verde-rame"</note>, and with any other color that might please you.

### *Of the mixture of colors with one another. Chap. CXXI.*

Even though the mixture of colors with one another extends toward infinity, I shall not refrain from making a brief discourse on it. Setting down first several <term>simple colors</term> <note>original: "colori semplici"; what we would now call primary or basic pigments.</note>, I will mix each of those with every other one by one, then two by two, and three by three, and so continuing until the full number of all the colors. Then I will begin again to mix the colors two by two, and three by three, and then by fours, thus continuing to the end. Upon these two simple colors three will be placed, and with those three I will accompany another three, and then six, and I will follow such mixing in all proportions. I call "simple colors" those which are not composed, nor can they be composed by means of mixing other colors: black and white—although these are not placed among the colors, because one is darkness and the other is light, that is, one is privation and the other is generative. But I do not wish to leave them behind for this reason, because in painting they are the principal ones, since painting is composed of shadows and lights, that is, of <term>chiaroscuro</term> <note>original: "chiaro e oscuro"; the contrast between light and dark.</note>. After black and white follow blue and yellow, then green and <term>lion-color</term> <note>original: "lionato"; a tawny or brownish-yellow.</note>, that is <term>tan</term> <note>original: "tanè"</note>, or if you prefer, <term>ochre</term> <note>original: "ocria"</note>; then <term>mulberry</term> <note>original: "morello"</note>, that is <term>peacock-purple</term> <note>original: "pauonazzo"</note>, and red. And these are eight colors, and there are no more in nature, with which I begin the mixing. And let it be first black and white, then black and yellow, and black and red, then yellow and black, and yellow and red. And because I lack paper here, says the author, I shall leave off making such distinctions in my work for a long treatise, which will be of great utility, indeed most necessary; and this description will be placed between the theory and the practice.

### *Of the surface of every shaded body. Chap. CXXII.*

The surface of every shaded body participates in the color of its object <note>Meaning the color of the surrounding objects or the light source reflecting onto it.</note>. Shaded bodies demonstrate this with certainty, since none of the aforementioned bodies shows its shape or color if the medium interposed between the body and the light source is not illuminated. We shall say, therefore, that if the opaque body is yellow, and the light source is blue, the illuminated part will be green, which green is composed of yellow and blue.

### *Which surface is receptive to the most colors. Chap. CXXIII.*

White is more receptive to any color than any other surface of any body that is not a mirror. This is proved by saying that every vacant body is capable of receiving what bodies that are not vacant cannot receive; we shall say for this reason that white is "vacant," or if you prefer, deprived of any color, and being illuminated by the color of any light source, it participates more in that light than would

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

black, which is like a broken vessel that is deprived of any capacity for anything.

### *Which body will be most tinted by the color of its object. CHAP. CXXIV.*

The surface of every body will participate more fully in the color of that object which is closest to it. This happens because the nearby object occupies a greater multitude of the variety of <term>species</term> <note>original: "spetie"; in Renaissance optics, these were the "images" or "likenesses" shed by objects that travel through the air to the eye.</note>, which, coming to the surface of the bodies, would corrupt the surface of such an object more than that color would if it were remote; and by occupying such species, that color demonstrates its nature more completely in that opaque body.

### *Which body will show the most beautiful color. CHAP. CXXV.*

The surface of that opaque body will show a more perfect color when it has a color similar to its own as a nearby object.

### *Of the flesh tones of faces. CHAP. CXXVI.*

That among bodies which is of greater quantity is better preserved at a long distance. This proposition shows us that the face becomes dark in the distance, because shadow is the largest part that the face has, and the lights are minimal; and therefore they fail at a short distance. And the smallest of all are their <term>highlights</term> <note>original: "lustri"; the bright points of reflected light on a surface.</note>, and this is the cause why, the darkest part remaining, the face becomes and appears dark. And it will seem to draw closer to black the more that face has something whiter upon the back or the head.

### *Method of portraying relief, and of preparing papers for this. CHAP. CXXVII.*

Painters, to portray things in <term>relief</term> <note>original: "rilieuo"; the illusion of three-dimensionality on a flat surface.</note>, must tint the surface of the papers with a medium darkness, and then give the darkest shadows, and finally the principal lights in small areas, which are those that are the first to be lost to the eye at a short distance.

### *Of the variety of the same color at various distances from the eye. CHAP. CXXVIII.*

Among colors of the same nature, that one varies the least which is moved the least from the eye. This is proven because the air that is interposed between the eye and the thing seen somewhat occupies the said thing; and if the interposed air is of a great amount, then the thing seen is strongly tinted with the color of such air, and if the air is of a thin quantity, then the object will be little hindered.

### *Of the greenery seen in the countryside. CHAP. CXXIX.*

Of the greenery seen in the countryside of equal quality, that which is in the plants of the trees will appear to be darker, and that of the meadows will show itself to be lighter.

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

### *Which greenery will appear more blue. Chap. CXXX.*

Those green areas will show themselves to participate more in blue which possess a darker shadowiness; and this is proven by the 7th [proposition], which says that blue is composed of light and dark over a long distance. <note>Leonardo believed that the blue of the sky was not an inherent color, but the result of white light (from the sun) shining through the "darkness" of the void of space.</note>

### *Which surface shows its true color less than others. Chap. CXXXI.*

That surface will show its true color the least which is most polished and smooth. This we see in the grass of the meadows and in the leaves of trees, which, being of a polished and lustrous surface, take on the <term>highlight</term> <note>original: "lustro"</note> in which the sun is mirrored, or the air that illuminates it; and thus, in that part of the highlight, they are deprived of their natural color.

### *Which body will show its true color most. Chap. CXXXII.*

That body will most demonstrate its true color whose surface is less polished and smooth. This is seen in linen cloths, and in the leaves of plants and trees that are hairy <note>meaning "fuzzy" or "pubescent" leaves, like those of a sage plant</note>, in which no highlight can be generated; wherefore by necessity, being unable to mirror objects, they only render to the eye their true and natural color; that color not being corrupted by any body that illuminates it with an opposing color, such as the redness of the sun when it sets and tints the clouds with its own color.

### *Of the brightness of landscapes. Chap. CXXXIII.*

The colors, vibrancy, and brightness of painted landscapes will never have conformity with natural landscapes illuminated by the sun, unless those painted landscapes are illuminated by that same sun.

### *Common perspective of the diminution of colors at a long distance. Chap. CXXXIV.*

The air will participate so much less in the color blue as it is closer to the horizon, and will be so much darker as it is more remote from that horizon. This is proven by the 3rd [proposition] of the 9th [book], which shows that a body will be less illuminated by the sun if it is of a thinner quality. Therefore, fire—the element that clothes the air <note>referring to the Aristotelian spheres of elements, where a sphere of fire was thought to exist above the sphere of air</note>—because it is thinner and more subtle than air, occupies the darkness above it less than the air does; and consequently, the air, being a body less thin than fire, is more illuminated by the solar rays that penetrate it. And by illuminating the infinity of <term>atoms</term> <note>original: "atomi"; here referring to tiny particles or dust motes suspended in the atmosphere.</note> that are infused through it, it renders itself bright to our eyes. Wherefore, the <term>species</term> <note>the visual image or essence</note> of the aforementioned darkness penetrating through that air necessarily makes that whiteness of the air appear blue to us, as is proven in the 3rd [proposition] of the 10th [book]; and it will appear to us as a brighter blue the greater the thickness of air interposed between that darkness and our eyes. Just as if the eye of the observer were at P. and looked above itself at the thickness of the air P. R., then inclining somewhat, the eye would see the air through the line P. S., which will appear brighter to it, because...

<meta>catchword: because</meta>
<sig>E ij</sig>

This follows because the line of sight penetrates a much greater quantity of air through the straight path P.D. than through the oblique path P.S., and thus our intent is proven. <note>Leonardo is using the diagram to show that looking straight ahead toward the horizon (P.D.) involves looking through more "thick" atmosphere than looking upward (P.S.), which is why the sky looks whiter at the horizon and bluer overhead.</note>

### *Of things mirrored in the water of landscapes, and first of the air.*
### CHAPTER CXXXV.

Only that air will be the one to give an image of itself upon the surface of the water which reflects from the water's surface to the eye at equal angles; that is, that the angle of incidence be equal to the angle of reflection. <note>This is a fundamental law of optics: light hits and bounces off a surface at the same angle.</note>

### *Diminution of colors by means of the medium interposed between them and the eye.*
### CHAPTER CXXXVI.

A visible thing will demonstrate less of its natural color the more the medium <note>In this context, "medium" refers to the atmosphere or air between the viewer and the object.</note> interposed between it and the eye is of greater thickness.

### *Of the backgrounds that are suitable for shadows and for lights.* CHAPTER CXXXVII. <note>The original text lists this as XXXVII, likely a printer's error for CXXXVII.</note>

The backgrounds that are suitable for shadows and for lights, and for the illuminated and shadowed edges of any color whatsoever, will create more separation from one another if they are more varied. That is, a dark color should not end against another dark color, but against one that is very different, such as white, or something participating in white, or as much as you can, a color that is dark or drawing toward the dark. <note>Leonardo is describing the principle of "contrast" to make figures stand out from their background.</note>

### *How one must provide a remedy when white ends against white, and dark against dark.*
### CHAPTER CXXXVIII.

When the color of a white body happens to end against a white background, then the whites will either be equal or they will not. If they are equal, then the part that is closer to you will become somewhat dark at the edge where it...

do so with white: and if such a background is less white than the color which stands out against it, then the foreground color will stand out by itself from its different surroundings without any other help from a dark border.

### *Of the nature of the colors of backgrounds upon which white stands out.*
->CHAPTER CXXXIX.<-

**A** white object will appear whiter when it is against a darker background, and it will appear darker when it is against a whiter background: and this is taught to us by the falling of snow, which, when we see it against the background of the air, appears dark to us; but when we see it against the background of an open window, through which one sees the darkness of the shadow of that house, then that snow will appear very white. Furthermore, snow nearby appears to move quickly, and from a distance slowly; and the nearby snow appears as a continuous quantity, like white cords, while the remote snow appears discontinuous. <note>Leonardo is observing how motion and distance affect our perception of particles; nearby flakes blur into lines, while distant ones remain distinct dots.</note>

### *Of the backgrounds of figures.*
->CHAPTER CXL.<-

**O**f things of equal brightness, that one will appear of lesser brightness which is seen against a background of greater whiteness; and that one will appear whiter which stands out in a darker space. Likewise, flesh-tones will appear pale against a red background, and pale flesh will appear reddish when seen against a yellow background: and similarly, colors will be judged to be what they are not by means of the backgrounds that surround them. <note>This is an early description of "simultaneous contrast," where the eye perceives a color differently based on the colors adjacent to it.</note>

### *Of the backgrounds of painted things.*
->CHAPTER CXLI.<-

**O**f the greatest importance is the discussion of backgrounds in which opaque bodies <note>Objects that do not let light pass through them.</note> stand out, clothed in shadows and lights; because for those it is appropriate to have the illuminated parts against dark backgrounds, and the dark parts against light backgrounds, just as is demonstrated by the figure shown here below.

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### *Of those who falsely represent the most remote things in a landscape as being darker.*
### Chapter CXLII.

THERE are many who, in an open landscape, make the figures darker the further they are from the eye; this is actually the opposite of the truth, unless the object being imitated happens to be white, for in that case, the rule proposed below would apply. <note>Leonardo is correcting a common error where painters lose the effect of "aerial perspective"—the way air makes distant things look lighter and bluer.</note>

### *Of the colors of things remote from the eye.* Chapter CXLIII.

THE AIR tints the objects it separates from the eye with its own color in proportion to its thickness. Therefore, if the air separates a dark object by a thickness of two miles, it tints it more than air with a thickness of only one mile. Here the adversary responds and says that in landscapes, trees of the same species appear darker from afar than from nearby; this is not true if the plants are of equal size and separated by equal spaces. However, it will indeed be true if the first trees are sparse, allowing the brightness of the meadows dividing them to be seen, while the furthest trees are dense; as happens on the banks and vicinities of rivers, where one does not see the space of bright meadows, but all the trees joined together, casting shadows one upon the other. Furthermore, it happens that the shadowed part of plants is much larger than the luminous part, and because of the images <note>original: "spetie," referring to the Aristotelian concept of 'species' or visual forms traveling through the air</note> that the plant sends of itself to the eye, they are visible at a long distance; and the dark color, being present in greater quantity, maintains its image more than the less dark part. Thus, this mixture carries with it the more powerful part over a longer distance.

### *Degrees of paintings.* Chapter CXLIV.

THAT which is beautiful is not always good; and I say this for those painters who love the beauty of colors so much that, not without great [lack of] conscience, they give them very weak and almost imperceptible shadows, disregarding their relief <term>relief: the illusion of three-dimensionality and depth in a flat painting</term>. In this error are those who are fine speakers without any substance.

### *Of the reflection and color of seawater seen from different aspects.*
### Chapter CXLV.

THE wavy sea has no universal color; rather, he who sees it from the mainland sees it as a dark color, and it is darker the closer it is to the horizon, though one sees certain brightnesses or glimmers that move slowly like white sheep in a flock. But he who sees the sea while out in the deep ocean sees it as blue. This happens because from the land, the sea appears dark because you see in it the waves reflecting the darkness of the earth, while from the high seas they appear blue because you see in the waves the blue air reflected by such waves.

### *Of the nature of comparisons.* Chapter CXLVI.

BLACK garments make the flesh of human figures <note>original: "simulacri," meaning images or representations of the human form</note> appear whiter than they truly are, and white garments make the flesh appear dark, and there—

<meta>catchword: and there—</meta>

<summary>Leonardo explains how atmospheric thickness lightens distant objects and how the sea's color changes based on the viewer's position and reflections. He also warns painters against prioritizing bright colors over the realistic "relief" created by shadows.</summary>
<keywords>aerial perspective, landscape painting, optical illusion, seawater reflection, color contrast, relief in painting, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

yellow garments make them appear colorful, and red garments make them appear pale. <note>This fragment completes a thought from the previous page regarding how skin tones change based on the color of clothing worn near the face.</note>

### *Of the color of the shadow of any body.* CHAPTER CXLVII.

THE color of the shadow of any body will never be its true or proper shadow unless the object that casts the shadow is the same color as the body being shadowed. Let us say, for example, that I have a room in which the walls are green; I say that if blue is seen in such a place, illuminated by the brightness of the blue <note>likely referring to blue light from the sky</note>, then that illuminated part will be a most beautiful blue, but the shadow will be ugly, and not a true shadow of such a beautiful blue, because it is corrupted by the green that reflects into it; and it would be even worse if such a wall were tawny <note>term: tanè (tawny). A brownish-orange or dark yellowish-purple color common in Renaissance textiles.</note>.

### *Of the perspective of colors in dark places.* CHAPTER CXLVIII.

IN places that are uniformly dim, even unto total darkness, that color which is most remote from the eye will appear the darkest.

### *Perspective of colors.* CHAPTER CXLIX.

THE primary colors <note>meaning the colors in the foreground</note> must be simple, and the degrees of their diminishing must agree with the degrees of their distances; that is, the sizes of things will participate more in the nature of the "point" <note>the vanishing point</note> the closer they are to it, and the colors must participate more in the color of the horizon the closer they are to it.

### *Of colors.* CHAPTER CL.

THE color found between the shadowed part and the illuminated part of shadowed bodies will be of less beauty than that which is entirely illuminated; therefore, the primary beauty of colors is found in the principal lights.

### *From what the blue in the air arises.* CHAPTER CLI.

THE blue of the air arises from the thickness of the body of illuminated air interposed between the darkness above <note>the blackness of outer space</note> and the earth. The air in itself has no quality of smell, taste, or color, but it takes into itself the likenesses of the things placed behind it; and it will be a more beautiful blue the greater the darkness behind it, provided the air is not of too great a space nor of too great a thickness of humidity. And it is seen in mountains, which have more shadows, that the blue is more beautiful at long distances; and likewise, where it is more illuminated, it shows more the color of the mountain than the blue of the air that is interposed between it and the eye.

### *Of colors.* CHAPTER CLII.

AMONG colors that are not blue, that which at a long distance will participate most in blue is the one which is closest to black; and conversely, that which is most dissimilar to said black will maintain its own color for a longer distance. Therefore, the green of the fields will transform more into blue

<summary>Leonardo explains how reflected light from nearby surfaces "corrupts" the color of shadows and provides a scientific explanation for the blue of the sky as light filtered through illuminated air against a dark void.</summary>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

blue, which yellow or white do not do; and conversely, yellow and white change less than green and red.

### Of colors. Chapter CLIII.

COLORS placed in shadows will participate more or less in their natural beauty according to whether they are in greater or lesser darkness. But if colors are situated in a luminous space, then they will show themselves with much greater beauty as the light is of greater splendor. An opponent might say: there are as many varieties of colors in shadows as there are varieties of colors in the objects being shadowed. To this I say that colors placed in shadows will show less variety among themselves as the shadows in which they are situated become darker. Of this, those who look from the public squares into the doors of dark temples are witnesses; there, the paintings clothed in various colors appear entirely clothed in darkness.

### Of the backgrounds for the figures of painted bodies. Chapter CLIV.

The background that surrounds the figures of any painted thing should be darker than the illuminated part of those figures, and lighter than the shadowed part. <note>This is a fundamental rule of Leonardo's "chiaroscuro," ensuring that the figure stands out from the wall or backdrop through contrast.</note>

### Why white is not a color. Chapter CLV.

White is not a color, but is in a state of potential, receptive to every color. When it is in an open countryside, all its shadows are blue; and this arises from the 4th [proposition], which says: The surface of every opaque body participates in the color of its object <note>meaning the color of whatever is facing it</note>. Therefore, such white being deprived of the light of the sun by the interposition of some object between the sun and that white, all the white that sees the sun and the air remains participating in the color of the sun and the air; and that part which is not seen by the sun remains in shadow, participating in the color of the air. And if such white did not see the green of the fields all the way to the horizon, nor see the whiteness of that horizon, without doubt that white would appear to be of the simple color which the air shows itself to be.

### Of colors. Chapter CLVI.

The light of a fire tints everything in yellow; but this will not appear to be true except by comparison with things illuminated by the air <note>daylight</note>. This comparison can be seen near the end of the day, and certainly after the dawn, and also in a dark room where a sliver of air [daylight] falls upon the object through a vent, and also a sliver of candlelight; in such a place their differences will certainly be seen clearly and distinctly. But without such a comparison, their difference will never be known, except in colors that have the most similarity, yet are distinct, such as white from yellow, or light green from blue; because the light that illuminates the blue being yellowish—which is like mixing blue and yellow together—they compose a beautiful green; and if you then mix yellow with green, it becomes much more beautiful.

<meta>catchword: Of</meta>

<summary>Leonardo explains how shadows diminish color variety and argues that white is a receptive medium rather than a color, specifically noting how it turns blue in shadow due to atmospheric reflection. He also discusses how the yellow tint of firelight is only truly perceptible when contrasted directly with daylight.</summary>

## On the colors of incident and reflected lights. CHAPTER CLVII. <term>incident lights: light falling directly onto a surface; reflected lights: light bouncing off one surface onto another</term>

WHEN two lights place an opaque body between them, they can only vary in two ways: either they will be of equal power, or they will be unequal—speaking of the lights themselves. If they are equal, they can vary in two other ways: according to their brightness upon the object, which will be either equal or unequal. It will be equal when they are at an equal distance; unequal at unequal distances. At equal distances, they will vary in two other ways: that is, the object situated at an equal distance between two lights equal in color and brightness can be illuminated by these lights in two ways: either equally from every side, or unequally. It will be equally illuminated by these lights when the space remaining around the two lights is of equal color, darkness, and clarity; they will be unequal when those spaces around the two lights vary in darkness.

## On the colors of shadow. CHAPTER CLVIII.

IT often happens that the shadow of opaque bodies does not match the colors of the lights; the shadows will be greenish and the lights reddish, even though the body is of a uniform color. This happens when the light comes from the East upon the object and illuminates the object with the color of its own splendor; and from the West there is another object illuminated by that same light, which is of a different color than the first object, whereby with its reflected lights it bounces back toward the East and strikes with its rays the parts of the first object facing it; and its rays intersect and remain fixed together with their colors and brightness. I have often seen a white object with red lights and bluish shadows; and this happens in the snowy mountains when the sun sets on the horizon and appears fiery. <note>Leonardo is describing the phenomenon of "simultaneous contrast" and "colored shadows," where the cool blue of the sky fills the shadows while the warm setting sun colors the highlights.</note>

### *On things placed against a light background, and why such a practice is useful in painting.*

## Chapter CLIX.

When a shaded body ends against a background of a light and illuminated color, it will necessarily appear detached and distant from that background. This happens because bodies with curved surfaces necessarily become shaded on the opposite side from where they are not struck by luminous rays, as that place is deprived of such rays. For this reason, the body varies greatly from the background; furthermore, the illuminated part of said body will never end against that illuminated background with its primary brightness. Instead, between the background and the primary light of the body, a boundary of the body is interposed which is darker than the background or the light of the body respectively. <note>Leonardo is describing the optical phenomenon where the edge of a light object against a light background still requires a subtle "boundary" of shadow or contrast to define its form.</note>

## Chapter CLX.

Regarding the backgrounds of figures—that is, the light figure in a dark field, and the dark figure in a light field—of white with black, or black with white, one appears more powerful because of...

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

the other, and thus opposites always show themselves to be more powerful through one another.

### *On colors resulting from the mixture of other colors, which are called secondary species.* CHAPTER CLXI.

Of the simple colors, the first is white, although philosophers do not accept either white or black in the number of colors, because one is the cause of colors and the other is their deprivation. But because the painter cannot do without these, we shall place them in the number of the others, and we will say that in this order white is the first among the simple colors; yellow is the second; green the third; blue the fourth; red the fifth; and black the sixth. We shall set white for light, without which no color can be seen; yellow for the earth; green for water; blue for the air; red for fire; and black for the darkness that stands above the element of fire, because there is no matter or thickness there for the sun's rays to penetrate and strike, and consequently to illuminate. <note>Leonardo is aligning the primary colors of the palette with the classical four elements, adding white and black as the cosmic extremes of light and void.</note> If you wish to see the variety of all composite colors briefly, take colored glasses, and through them look at all the colors of the countryside that are seen beyond them; thus you will see all the colors of the things seen through such glass to be mixed with the color of the aforementioned glass. You will see which color is improved or spoiled by such a mixture: if the glass is yellow, I say that the <term>species</term> <note>In this context, "species" refers to the visual image or "likeness" of an object traveling through the air to the eye.</note> of the objects passing through it to the eye can either worsen or improve. This worsening in such a glass color will happen to blue, black, and white above all others, and the improvement will happen in yellow and green above all others. Thus you will go on scanning with your eye the mixtures of colors, which are infinite; in this way, you can make a selection of new inventions of mixed and composite colors. The same can be done with two glasses of different colors placed before the eye, and so you may continue on your own.

### *On colors.* CHAPTER CLXII.

Blue and green are not simple in themselves, because blue is composed of light and darkness, such as that of the air—that is, a most perfect black and a most pure white. Green is composed of a simple and a composite color; that is, it is composed of blue and yellow.

A reflected thing always participates in the color of the body that reflects it, and the mirror is partially tinted by the color it reflects. One participates in the other more or less depending on whether the thing being reflected is more or less powerful than the color of the mirror; and that thing will appear to be of a more powerful color in the mirror the more it participates in the color of the mirror itself.

Of the colors of bodies, that which is of a more splendid whiteness will be seen at a greater distance. Therefore, that which is of greater darkness will be seen at a lesser distance.

Among bodies of equal whiteness and distance from the eye, that which is surrounded by greater darkness will appear more pure white; and conversely, that darkness will appear more shadowy which is seen against a more pure whiteness.

<sig>F ij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo defines his primary color system, linking colors to the physical elements, and explains how colors change when viewed through a medium or reflected on a surface.</summary>
<keywords>color theory, simple colors, composite colors, optics, reflection, four elements, visual perception, contrast</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Leonardo discussed the primary colors and the effect of viewing the world through colored glass, he now explores the principles of simultaneous contrast and atmospheric perspective.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

Among colors of equal perfection, that one will demonstrate the greatest excellence which is seen in the company of its direct contrary: such as pale with red, or black with white—although neither of these latter two are strictly colors <note>Leonardo repeats the Aristotelian view that white and black are the extremes of light and dark rather than true hues.</note>—blue and yellow, or green and red. This is because every color is better recognized in its contrary than in its similar, just as darkness is better seen in light, and light in darkness.

That object which is seen in dark and murky air, if it is white, will appear to be of a larger form than it truly is. This happens because, as was said above, a light object expands within a dark field for the reasons previously assigned. <note>This refers to the optical phenomenon of irradiation, where bright objects seem to bleed into their darker surroundings.</note>

The medium that exists between the eye and the object seen transforms that object into its own color; just as the blue of the air causes distant mountains to appear blue, or red glass causes everything the eye sees through it to appear red. Furthermore, the light that the stars create around themselves is occupied by the darkness of the night that is found between the eye and the illumination of those stars.

The true color of any body will demonstrate itself in that part which is not occupied by any quality of shadow, nor by luster <note>specular highlights</note>, if it is a polished body.

I say that white which borders with darkness causes the darkness to appear blacker at those borders, and the white to appear more candid <note>original: "candido," meaning a pure, brilliant white</note>.

## *On the color of mountains.* Chapter CLXIII.

That mountain will demonstrate itself to the eye as a more beautiful blue which is in itself darker; and that one will be darker which is higher and more forested. This is because such forests cover many shrubs on their underside, so that the sky does not see them <note>meaning the sky's light cannot reach the shaded interior of the dense foliage</note>; furthermore, the wild plants of the forests are in themselves darker than domestic ones. Oaks, beeches, firs, cypresses, and pines are much darker than domestic trees and olives. That lucidity <note>The "lucidity" refers to the illuminated atmosphere or "air" between the viewer and the object.</note> which is interposed between the eye and the blackness—which will be thinner at its great summit—will be a black of a more beautiful blue, and vice versa. And that plant appears less divided from its field which borders a field of a color more similar to its own, and vice versa. That part of the white will appear more candid which is closer to the border of the black, and likewise those parts will appear less white which are more remote from that darkness. And that part of the black will appear darker which is closer to the white, and it will appear less dark the further it is from that white.

## *How the painter must put into practice the perspective of colors.* Chapter CLXIV.

To wish to put into practice this perspective of varying, losing, or indeed diminishing the proper essence of colors, you shall take objects placed in the countryside at intervals of one hundred <term>braccia</term> <note>A Florentine unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches or 58 centimeters.</note>, such as trees, houses, men, and sites. Regarding the first tree, you shall have a glass held very still, and let your eye be just as still; and on said glass, draw a tree over the form of that one. Then move it sideways until the natural tree almost borders your drawing; then color your drawing in such a way that in color and form the two stand in comparison to one another, so that both, by closing one eye, appear painted; and let this be...

<summary>Leonardo explains the principles of color contrast and atmospheric perspective, noting how the medium of air turns distant mountains blue and how painters can use glass to calibrate color changes over distance.</summary>
<keywords>color theory, simultaneous contrast, atmospheric perspective, optics, blue mountains, painting techniques, braccia</keywords>

said glass at a single distance: and this same rule applies to the second and third rows of trees, from hundred to hundred <term>braccia</term> <note>A "braccio" (plural "braccia") was a Florentine unit of measurement roughly equal to 23 inches or 58 centimeters, literally meaning an "arm's length."</note>, from space to space; and let these serve as your assistants and masters, always employing them in your works where they belong, and they will make the work recede well. But I find by the rule that the second diminishes by 1/2 from the first, when it is twenty braccia distant from the first.

### *Of aerial perspective.* CHAPTER CLXV.

There is another perspective, which is called aerial; for by the variation of the air one can recognize the different distances of various buildings terminated at their bases by a single line—as would happen when seeing many buildings beyond a wall, so that they all appear above the edge of said wall at the same size, and you wished in a painting to make one appear further away than the other. One must imagine the air to be a little thick. You know that in such air, the last things seen in it, such as mountains, appear blue—almost the color of the air—because of the great quantity of air that is found between your eye and said mountains when the sun is in the east. Therefore, you shall make the first building above the said wall in its own color; make the one further away less defined and more blue; and that which you want to be just as much further again, make it just as much bluer; and that which you want to be five times further away, make it five times bluer. This rule will ensure that buildings which are above a single line will appear to be of the same size, and it will be clearly recognized which is more distant and which is larger than the other.

---

### *Of the various accidents and movements of man, and the proportion of limbs.*
### CHAPTER CLXVI.

The measurements of a man vary in each limb as one bends them more or less, and from different aspects; diminishing or increasing as much more or less on one side as they increase or diminish on the opposite side.

### *Of the changes in the measurements of man from his birth to his final growth.*
->C H A P. CLXVII.<-

In his first infancy, a man has a shoulder width equal to the length of the face, and to the space from the joint of those shoulders to the elbow when the arm is bent; it is also similar to the space from the thumb of the hand to the said elbow, and similar to the space from the base of the penis to the middle of the knee, and similar to the space from that knee joint to the ankle joint. But when a man has reached his final height, every aforementioned space doubles its length, except for the length of the face, which, together with the size of the whole head, undergoes little change. For this reason, a man who has finished his growth and is well-proportioned is ten of his own faces tall; the width of his shoulders is two of those faces, and likewise all the other lengths mentioned above are two of those faces. The rest shall be discussed in the general measurement of man.

### *How children have joints contrary to men in their thickness.*
->C H A P. CLXVIII.<-

Small children all have thin joints, while the spaces located between one joint and another are thick. This happens because the skin over the joints lies directly over the bone without other flesh, being of the nature of a sinew <note>original: "neruo" — in Leonardo's time, this term referred to tendons, ligaments, or nerves interchangeably</note> that encircles and binds the bones together; the moist fleshiness is found between one joint and another, enclosed between the skin and the bone. But because bones are thicker at the joints than between the joints, as a man grows, the flesh loses that superfluity that stayed between the skin and the bone; thus the skin draws closer to the bone and the limbs become thinner. But over the joints, there being nothing but the cartilaginous and sinewy skin, it cannot dry out, and not drying, it does not diminish. For these reasons, children are thin at the joints and thick between them, as seen in the joints of the fingers, arms, and shoulders, which are thin and concave; a man, on the contrary, is thick in all the joints of the arms and legs, and where children have hollows, men have relief.

### *Of the difference in measurement between children and men.*
->C H A P. CLXIX.<-

Between men and children, I find a great difference in the length from one joint to another; for a man has two heads' length from the shoulder joint to the elbow, and from the elbow to the tip of the thumb, and from one shoulder blade to the other; whereas the child has only one, because nature composes the size of the house of the intellect <note>A poetic reference to the skull/head</note> before that of the vital spirits <note>In Renaissance physiology, "vital spirits" were believed to be substances in the blood that provided life and movement to the body</note>.

### *Of the joints of the fingers.*
->C H A P. CLXX.<-

The fingers of the hand thicken at their joints on all sides when they bend; the more they bend, the more they thicken, and likewise

<meta>catchword: likewise</meta>

<summary>Leonardo describes the proportional shifts that occur as a human grows from infancy to adulthood, noting that while limbs double in length, the head remains relatively constant. He also explains the anatomical reasons why children's joints appear as dimples while adults' joints protrude.</summary>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

diminish the more they are straightened; the same happens with the toes of the feet, and they will vary all the more as they are more fleshy.

### *Of the joints of the shoulders, and their increases.* Chap. CLXXI.

The joints of the shoulders, and of the other bendable limbs, will be discussed in their proper place in the treatise on <term>anatomy</term> <note>original: "notomia" — a common Renaissance spelling for anatomy</note>, where the causes of the movements of all the parts of which man is composed are shown.

### *Of the shoulders.* Chap. CLXXII.

The principal simple movements of the bending performed by the shoulder joint are those when the arm attached to it moves upward, or downward, or backward; although one could say such movements are infinite, because if the shoulder is turned toward a wall and one marks a circular figure with the arm, one will have performed all the movements that are in that shoulder. Since every continuous quantity is infinitely divisible, and such a circle is a continuous quantity made by the movement of the arm, which movement does not produce a continuous quantity unless the continuation leads it. Therefore, the movement of that arm has passed through all parts of the circle, and the circle being infinitely divisible, the variations of the shoulders are infinite.

### *Of the universal measurements of bodies.* Chap. CLXXIII.

I say that the universal measurements of bodies must be observed in the lengths of the figures, and not in their thicknesses, because among the laudable and marvelous things that appear in the works of nature is that never, in any species, does one individual precisely resemble another. Therefore, you, as an imitator of such nature, look and attend to the variety of <term>features</term> <note>original: "lineamenti" — referring to the outlines or characteristic features of a person</note>. It pleases me well that you avoid monstrous things, such as long legs with short torsos, narrow chests and long arms; take, therefore, the measurements of the joints and the thicknesses in which nature varies greatly, and you shall vary them too.

### *Of the measurements of the human body, and the bending of limbs.* Chap. CLXXIV.

Necessity compels the painter to have knowledge of the supporting bones, the framework for the flesh that rests upon them, and of the joints which increase and diminish in their bending; for which reason the measurement of the extended arm does not match the measurement of the bent arm. The arm increases and diminishes between the variety of its ultimate extension and bending by one-eighth part of its length. The increase and shortening of the arm comes from the bone that projects out of the joint of the arm, which, as you see in figure A. B. <note>This likely refers to an illustration on a different page or section.</note> makes the length from the shoulder to the elbow, when the angle of that elbow is less than a right angle; and it increases all the more as that angle diminishes, and diminishes all the more as the aforesaid angle becomes greater: and the space from the shoulder to the elbow increases as the angle of the bend of that elbow becomes less than a right angle, and diminishes as it becomes greater than a right angle.

<sig>F iiij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of the shoulder joint's infinite range of motion and the necessity for painters to understand how limb measurements change during movement. He emphasizes that while proportions should follow nature, artists must capture the unique variations found in individual bodies rather than following a rigid, uniform thickness.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, shoulder joint, proportions, artistic variety, limb measurements, skeletal structure, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Leonardo discussed the infinite variety of human movement and the importance of observing universal measurements in the length of figures rather than their thickness.</meta>

### *On the proportionality of the limbs.* CHAPTER CLXXV.

**A L L** the parts of any animal should be corresponding to its whole; that is, he who is short and thick must have every limb in itself short and thick, and he who is long and thin should have long and thin limbs, and the medium person should have limbs of the same medium quality. I intend the same to be said of plants that have not been mangled by man or by winds; for these put forth youth upon old age <note>This refers to new growth appearing on old branches</note>, and thus their natural proportionality is destroyed.

### *On the joint of the hands with the arm.* CHAPTER CLXXVI.

**T H E** joint of the arm with its hand diminishes when clenching, and thickens when the hand comes to open; and the arm between the elbow and the hand does the opposite in all its aspects. This arises because in opening the hand, the <term>internal muscles</term> <note>original: "muscoli domestici" — Leonardo uses 'domestic' to mean the inner/medial muscles and 'sylvan' for the outer/lateral muscles</note> extend and make the arm thinner between the elbow and the hand; and when the hand is clenched, the internal and <term>external muscles</term> <note>original: "muscoli siluestri" — literally 'wild' or 'of the woods', referring here to the outer muscles</note> retract and thicken, but only the external ones move away from the bone, being pulled by the bending of the hand.

### *On the joints of the feet, and their thickening and diminution.* CHAPTER CLXXVII.

**O N L Y** the diminution and increase of the foot joint is made in the aspect of its external part D. E. F., which increases when the angle of such a joint becomes more acute, and diminishes as much as it becomes more obtuse; that is, we are speaking of the front joints A. C. B.

<meta>catchword: Of the</meta>

### *On the limbs that diminish when they bend, and grow when they extend. Chapter CLXXVIII.*

**AMONG** the limbs that have flexible joints, only the knee is the one that diminishes in its thickness when bending, and thickens when extending.

### *On the limbs that thicken at their joint when they bend. Chapter CLXXIX.*

**ALL** the limbs of a man thicken at the bends of their joints, except for the joint of the leg <note>Leonardo is referring specifically to the knee joint mentioned in the previous chapter.</note>.

### *On the limbs of naked men. Chapter CLXXX.*

**THE** limbs of naked men who exert themselves in various actions should only reveal their muscles on that side where those muscles move the limb in that operation; and the other limbs should have their muscles more or less pronounced according to whether they are exerting themselves more or less.

### *On the powerful motions of the human limbs. Chapter CLXXXI.*

**THAT** arm will perform a more powerful and longer motion which, having been removed from its natural position, will have a more powerful adherence of the other limbs to pull it back into the position where it desires to move. As with the man A, who moves the arm with the stroke E, and carries it to the opposite position by moving with his whole person toward B.

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### On the movement of man. CHAPTER CLXXXII.

**THE** highest and principal part of the art is the investigation of the compositions of any thing; and the second part of movements is that they should pay attention to their operations, which should be performed with promptness, according to the degrees of their operators, as much in sluggishness as in diligence. And the promptness of ferocity should be of the highest quality required by the operator of that action. For instance, when one must throw darts, or stones, or other similar things, the figure should demonstrate its highest disposition in such an action, of which there are two figures here in various modes of action and potential. The first in strength is figure A; the second is the movement B. But A will remove the thrown object further from itself than B will; because even though both show a desire to throw their weight toward the same aspect, A, having turned its feet toward that aspect when it twists or bends, and moves away from it to the opposite position where it prepares the disposition of power, returns with speed and ease to the position where it lets the weight go from its hands. But in this same case, figure B, having the tips of its feet turned in the opposite direction to the place where it wants to throw its weight, twists toward that place with great discomfort, and consequently the effect is weak. The motion participates in its cause, because the preparation of force in each movement must be with twists and bends of great violence, and the return should be with ease and comfort, and thus the operation has a good effect. For the crossbow <note>original: "baleſtro"</note> that does not have a violent disposition, the motion <unclear>is weak</unclear>...

of the object removed by him will be brief, or nothing: because where there is no undoing of violence <note>Leonardo uses "violence" (violenza) to mean physical tension or potential energy stored in a body</note> there is no motion, and where there is no violence, it cannot be destroyed; and for this reason the bow that has no violence cannot create motion unless it acquires that violence, and in acquiring it, it will vary from itself. So the man who does not twist or bend has not acquired power. Therefore, when A has thrown his dart, he will find himself twisted and weak in the direction toward which he threw the object, and having acquired a power which is only useful for returning in the opposite motion.

---

### On attitudes, movements, and their limbs. Chapter CLXXXIII.

The same movements should not be repeated in the limbs, hands, or fingers of a single figure; nor should the same attitudes be repeated in a narrative painting <note>original: "hiſtoria" — a term for a complex multi-figure composition depicting a specific event</note>. And if the narrative were very large, such as a battle or a slaughter of soldiers, where there are only three ways of striking—namely a thrust <note>original: "punta"</note>, a backhand <note>original: "roueſcio"</note>, and a downward cut <note>original: "fendente"</note>—in this case you must teach yourself that all the downward cuts be made from various views. For example, let one be seen from behind, another from the side, and another from the front, and likewise for all the other perspectives of the same three attitudes; and by this method we shall account for all the others as participating in one of these. But composite motions <note>moti compoſti</note> in battles are of great artifice, great liveliness, and movement; and those are called "composite" which a single figure demonstrates to you, such as if it is seen with the legs facing forward, and part of the body seen through the profile of the shoulder. And of these, we shall speak in another place.

### On the movement of man. CHAPTER CLXXXII.

The highest and principal part of art is the investigation of the compositions of any thing; and the second part of movements is that they should pay attention to their actions, which should be performed with readiness <note>original: "prontitudine" — referring to the physical alertness and preparation of a body in motion</note>, according to the degrees of their performers, whether in sluggishness or in diligence. And the readiness of ferocity should be of the highest quality required by the performer of that action. For instance, when one must throw darts, or stones, or other similar things, the figure should demonstrate its highest disposition in such an action. Of this, there are two figures here in various modes of action and potential: the first in strength <note>original: "valetudine" — here meaning physical vigor or health</note> is figure A; the second is the movement B. But A will move the thrown object further from itself than B will, because even though both show a desire to throw their weight toward the same aspect, A, having turned his feet toward that aspect when he twists or bends, moves away from it in the opposite direction where he prepares the disposition of power. He then returns with speed and ease to the position where he lets the weight leave his hands. But in this same case, figure B, having the tips of his feet turned in the opposite direction to the place where he wants to throw his weight, twists toward that place with great discomfort, and consequently the effect is weak. The motion participates in its cause, because the preparation of force in every movement must be with twists and bends of great violence <note>Leonardo uses "violence" to describe the accumulation of potential energy through physical tension</note>, and the return should be with ease and convenience, and thus the operation has a good effect. ### *On attitudes, movements, and their limbs.* Chapter CLXXXIII.

The same movements should not be repeated in the limbs, hands, or fingers of a single figure; nor should the same attitudes be repeated within a historical scene <note>original: "historia" — a narrative painting depicting a specific event, often biblical or classical.</note>. And if the scene were very large, such as a battle or a slaughter of soldiers, where there are only three ways of striking—namely, the thrust <note>original: "punta" — a stabbing motion with the tip of a weapon.</note>, the backhand <note>original: "rovescio" — a transverse strike delivered with the back of the hand or blade.</note>, and the downward cut <note>original: "fendente" — a vertical, cleaving blow.</note>—in this case, you must teach yourself that all the downward cuts be shown from various views. For example, let one be seen from behind, another from the side, and another from the front, and likewise for all the other aspects of these same three attitudes; by this method, we shall account for all the others as participants in one of these three. But "composite movements" in battles are of great artistry, great liveliness, and motion; those are called composite which a single figure demonstrates to you, such as when it is seen with the legs facing forward while part of the shoulder is seen in profile. And of these, we shall speak in another place.

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

## *On the joints of the limbs.* Chapter CLXXXIV.

In the joints of the limbs and the variety of their bends, one must consider how, as flesh increases on one side, it must decrease on the other. This should be studied in the necks of animals, because their movements are of three natures, of which two are simple and one is a compound that partakes of both simple ones. Of these simple movements, the first is when the neck bends toward one or the other shoulder, or when it raises or lowers the head that rests upon it. The second is when the neck twists to the right or left without curving, remaining straight instead, with the face turned toward one of the shoulders. The third movement, which is called compound, is when its bending is joined by its twisting, such as when the ear leans toward one of the shoulders and the face turns toward that same side, or toward the opposite shoulder with the face turned toward the sky.

## *On the proportioning of the human limbs.* Chapter CLXXXV.

Measure the proportions of your limb-structure <note>original: "membrificatione" — the way the limbs are formed and joined together.</note> on the canvas, and if you find it discordant in any part, take note of it, and be very careful not to use it in the figures you compose. For it is a common vice among painters to delight in making things similar to themselves <note>Leonardo frequently warned that artists tend to paint figures that resemble their own physical appearance or temperament.</note>.

## *On the movements of human limbs.* Chapter CLXXXVI.

Let all limbs perform that office for which they were intended; that is, in the dead or the sleeping, no limb should appear alive or awake. Likewise, the foot that receives the weight of the man should be flattened, and not with playful toes, unless it is resting only on the heel.

## *On the movements of the parts of the face.* Chapter CLXXXVII.

The movements of the parts of the face, caused by mental accidents <note>original: "accidenti mentali" — emotional states or sudden changes in the mind that manifest physically.</note>, are many; the principal ones are laughing, crying, shouting, singing in various high and low voices, wonder, anger, joy, melancholy, fear, pain, and the like. Mention will be made of these, and first of laughter and crying, which are very similar in the mouth, the cheeks, and the closing of the eyes, but vary only in the eyebrows and the space between them. We shall discuss all of this in its proper place—that is, the variations taken by the face, the hands, and the whole person for each of the "accidents." Knowledge of these is necessary for you, the painter, otherwise your art will truly show bodies that are twice dead. Furthermore, I remind you that movements should not be so erratic <note>original: "sbalestrati" — literally "shot from a crossbow," meaning exaggerated or uncontrolled.</note> or so agitated that peace appears like a battle or a drunken Morris dance <note>original: "moresca" — a lively, rhythmic pantomime dance.</note>. Above all, ensure that those surrounding the event for which the narrative scene <note>original: "historia"</note> is made are intent with gestures that show wonder, reverence, sorrow, suspicion, fear, or joy, according to what the event requires for the gathering or assembly of your figures. And see to it that your narrative scenes are not placed one above the other on the same wall with different horizons, so that it looks like a merchant's shop with its boxes made into little squares.

<summary>Leonardo explains the three types of neck movement, warns painters against projecting their own likeness onto their work, and emphasizes the importance of depicting emotions (mental accidents) accurately to avoid lifeless figures.</summary>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

### *On the members and the description of features.* Chapter CLXXXVIII.

<term>The parts</term> <note>Referring here to the bridge or the sides of the nose.</note> that flank the bulb of the nose vary in eight ways: that is, they are either equally straight, equally concave, or equally convex (1st). Or they are unequally straight, concave, and convex (2nd). Or they are straight in the upper parts and concave below (3rd). Or straight above and convex below (4th). Or concave above and straight below (5th). Or concave above and convex below (6th). Or convex above and straight below (7th). Or convex above and concave below (8th).

The <term>attachment</term> <note>original: "applicatvra" — the point where the nose meets the brow.</note> of the nose to the brow is of two types: either it is concave or it is straight.

<term>The forehead</term> has three varieties: it is either flat, concave, or rounded <note>original: "colma" — full or bulging.</note>. The flat forehead is divided into two parts: that is, it is either convex in the upper part, or in the lower part, or both above and below, or flat both above and below.

### *How to keep in mind, and how to make a human likeness in profile, with only a single glance.* Chapter CLXXXIX.

In this case, you must commit to memory the variety of the four different members in profile, such as the nose, mouth, chin, and forehead. First, we shall speak of noses, which are of three kinds: straight, concave, and convex. Of the straight ones, there are no more than four varieties: long, short, high-tipped, and low. Concave noses are of three kinds, of which some have the concavity in the upper part, some in the middle, and some in the lower part. Convex noses also vary in three ways: some have the bridge <note>original: "gobbo" — literally "hump."</note> in the upper part, some in the middle, and some below. The protrusions that flank the bridge of the nose vary in three ways: they are either straight, concave, or convex.

### *How to keep in mind the form of a face.* Chapter CLXXXX.

If you wish to easily keep the character of a face in mind, first learn many types of heads, mouths, eyes, noses, chins, throats, necks, and shoulders. Let us take an example. Noses are of ten types: straight, humped, hollow, with the relief higher or lower than the middle, aquiline, snub, round, and sharp. These are good as far as the profile is concerned. From the front, they are of eleven types: even, thick in the middle, thin in the middle, the tip thick and thin at the attachment, thin at the tip and thick at the attachment, with wide nostrils, narrow ones, high ones, low ones, with the openings exposed, or with the openings hidden by the tip. And so you will find diversity in the other features; these things you must draw from nature and commit to memory. Or, when you must compose a face from memory, carry with you a small notebook where such features are noted, and when you have glanced at the face of the person you wish to portray, look afterward in private at which nose or mouth resembles it, and make a small mark to recognize it later at home and put it all together.

<sig>G iij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo provides a taxonomic breakdown of facial features, particularly the nose and forehead, to help artists categorize what they see. He also offers a practical mnemonic technique for portraitists to record a likeness using a "reference kit" of feature types in a notebook.</summary>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

## *On the beauty of faces.* Chapter CLXXXI.

Do not create muscles with harsh definitions, but let the soft lights end imperceptibly into pleasant and delightful shadows; from this, grace and beauty are born.

## *On posture.* Chapter CLXXXII.

The <term>pit of the throat</term> <note>original: "fontanella della gola" — the suprasternal notch.</note> falls directly over the foot <note>meaning the weight-bearing foot</note>; if one arm is thrown forward, the pit of the throat leaves the vertical line of the feet; and if the leg is thrown backward, the pit of the throat moves forward, and thus it shifts with every change in posture.

## *On the movements of the limbs: that they should be appropriate actions when depicting man.* Chapter CLXXXIII.

That figure whose movement does not match the <term>mental state</term> <note>original: "accidente... nella mente" — the internal emotion or intention.</note> feigned to be in the mind of the figure, shows that its limbs are not obedient to its own judgment, and that the judgment of the artist is worth little. Therefore, such a figure must show great affection and fervor, and demonstrate that its motions cannot signify anything other than the purpose for which they were made.

## *On the arrangement of limbs in nudes.* Chapter CLXXXIV.

The limbs of nude figures must be more or less evident in the uncovering of the muscles according to the greater or lesser labor of said limbs; show only those limbs that are most employed in the motion or action, and let the one most used be the most manifest; that which is not used at all should remain slack and soft.

## *On the motion and gait of man and other animals.* Chapter CLXXXV.

When a man moves with speed or slowness, that part which is above the leg sustaining the body will always be lower than the other side.

## *When there is a greater difference in the height of shoulders in human actions.* Chapter CLXXXVI.

Those shoulders or sides of a man (or other animals) will have a greater difference in height when the movement of the whole body is slower; the opposite follows—that is, those parts of the animal will have less difference in their heights when the movement of the whole is faster. This is proven by the ninth rule of <term>local motion</term> <note>A principle of Aristotelian physics regarding the movement of a body from one place to another.</note>, which says: "Every heavy body weighs along the line of its motion." Therefore, the whole moving toward a certain place, the part united to it follows the shortest line of the motion of the whole, without exerting its weight upon the lateral parts of said whole.

## *A response against [an objection].* Chapter CLXXXVII.

The adversary says, regarding the first part above, that it is not necessary for a man who stands still, or who walks with slow motion, to continually use the aforementioned <term>balancing</term> <note>original: "ponderatione" — the distribution of weight or contrapposto.</note> of the limbs over the center of gra-

He emphasizes that a figure's physical movement must convincingly reflect its internal psychological state.</summary>

This page deepens the study of "ponderation" (balance), explaining how the body compensates for shifted weight and how the speed of movement affects the center of gravity.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

...the center <note>referring to the vertical axis of balance</note> that sustains the weight of the whole, because many times a man does not use or observe such a rule; rather, he does quite the opposite. This is because sometimes he leans sideways while standing on a single foot, and sometimes he discharges part of his weight onto the leg that is not straight—that is, the one that is bent at the knee, as shown in the two figures B and C. The answer is that what is not done by the shoulders in figure C is done in the hip, as has been demonstrated in its proper place.

### *How the retracted arm changes the whole man from his initial balance when that arm is extended.* Chapter CLXXXXVIII.

The extension of a retracted arm moves the entire <term>balance</term> <note>original: "ponderatione" — the distribution of weight in a figure to maintain equilibrium.</note> of the man over the foot that serves as the <term>support</term> <note>original: "sostentacolo" — the base or point of contact with the ground.</note> for the whole, as is seen in one who walks upon a rope with open arms without any other balancing pole.

### *Of man and other animals who, in moving slowly, do not have their center of gravity too far from the center of their supports.* Chapter CLXXXXIX.

That animal will have the center of the legs (its support) so much closer to the <term>perpendicular line</term> <note>original: "perpendicolo" — the vertical line dropping straight down from the center of gravity.</note> of the center of gravity the slower its movements are; and conversely, that animal will have the center of its supports further from the perpendicular of its center of gravity the faster its motion shall be.

## *On the beauty of faces.* Chapter CLXXXXI.

One should not create muscles with harsh definitions, but let the soft lights end imperceptibly into pleasant and delightful shadows, and from this arises grace and beauty.

## *On attitude.* Chapter CLXXXXII.

The pit of the throat <note>original: "fontanella della gola" — the hollow at the base of the neck.</note> falls directly over the foot; if one arm is thrown forward, the pit of the throat leaves the vertical line of those feet, and if the leg is thrown backward, the pit of the throat moves forward, and thus it changes in every attitude.

## *On the movements of the limbs, ensuring they are appropriate actions when depicting a man.* Chapter CLXXXXIII.

That figure whose movement is not a companion to the emotion <note>original: "accidente" — here referring to the mental state or intent.</note> feigned to be in the mind of the figure, shows that the limbs are not obedient to the judgment of that figure, and that the judgment of the artist is worth little; therefore, such a figure must show great affection and fervor, and show that such motions could signify nothing else but that for which they were made.

## *On the arrangement of limbs in nudes.* Chapter CLXXXXIV.

The limbs of nudes <note>original: "ignudi" — a standard term for undraped figures in art.</note> must be more or less evident in the uncovering of the muscles according to the greater or lesser labor of said limbs; show only those limbs that are most used in the motion or action, and let the one that is most used be most manifest, while that which is not used at all remains relaxed and soft.

## *On the motion and running of man and other animals.* Chapter CLXXXXV.

When a man moves with speed or slowness, that part which is above the leg sustaining the body will always be lower than the other.

## *When there is a greater difference in the height of shoulders in the actions of man.* Chapter CLXXXXVI.

Those shoulders or sides of a man, or of other animals, will have a greater difference in height between them when the whole body moves more slowly; the opposite follows, that is, those parts of the animal will have less difference in their heights when the whole body moves more quickly. And this is proven by the 9th rule of local motion <note>A reference to Leonardo's own studies on mechanics and physics.</note>, which says: Every heavy body weighs along the line of its motion; therefore, the whole moving toward a certain place, the part united to it follows the shortest line of the motion of its whole, without adding weight to the lateral parts of said whole.

---

### *How the retracted arm changes the whole man from his initial balance when that arm is extended. CHAPTER CLXXXXVIII.*

**THE EXTENSION** of the retracted arm moves the entire balance <term>ponderatione: the distribution of weight in a figure to maintain balance</term> of the man over his foot, which is the support of the whole, as is shown in one who walks upon a rope with open arms without any other balancing pole.

### *On man and other animals who, in moving slowly, do not have the center of gravity too far from the center of their supports. CHAPTER CLXXXXIX.*

**THAT** animal will have the center of the legs (its support) so much closer to the perpendicular line of the center of gravity the slower its movements are; and conversely, that one will have the center of the supports further from the perpendicular of its center of gravity which moves with greater speed.

<sig>G iiij</sig>

<vocab>balance, gravity, support, perpendicular, Leonardo da Vinci, counterpose</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains how the human body maintains equilibrium by shifting its weight (ponderation) through the hips and arms, and notes that the distance between the center of gravity and the feet determines the speed of movement.</summary>
<keywords>balance, center of gravity, ponderation, human motion, anatomy, equilibrium, speed of movement</keywords>
