## *Of the man who carries a weight upon his shoulders. Chapter CC.*

**ALWAYS** the shoulder of the man who sustains a weight is higher than the shoulder without the weight, and this is shown in the figure placed below, through which passes the central line of the entire weight of the man and of the weight carried by him. If this combined weight were not divided with an equal load over the center of the leg that rests on the ground, it would be necessary for the entire composition to collapse. But necessity provides that as much of the natural weight of the man is thrown to one side as there is a quantity of accidental weight <note>original: "peſo accidentale" — referring to an external load added to the body's natural weight</note> added to the opposite side. This cannot be done unless the man bends and lowers himself on his lighter side with enough of a lean that he shares in the accidental weight he carries; and this cannot be done unless the shoulder carrying the weight rises, and the light shoulder lowers. And this is the means that ingenious necessity has found in such an action.

---

## *Of the balance of a man upon his feet. Chapter CCI.*

**ALWAYS** the weight of a man who rests upon a single leg will be divided with an equal opposite part over the center of gravity that sustains him.

### *On the balancing of weight in any animal standing still upon its legs.*

#### CHAPTER CCIII.

The absence of motion in any animal that rests upon its feet arises from the absence of inequality between the opposing weights supported by its center of gravity. <note>Leonardo uses the term "inequality" to describe an imbalance; if the weights on either side of the supporting axis are equal, the body remains still and stable.</note>

<term>bilicatione</term> <note>An archaic term referring to the act of balancing or equilibrium.</note>, weight, motion, animal, inequality, Treatise on Painting

<note>The engraving depicts a muscular male figure in a dynamic contrapposto stance, carrying a heavy stone block. This illustrates how the body must shift its weight to compensate for an external load to maintain its center of gravity.</note>

<note>The decorative woodcut at the bottom of the page features a central figure with serpents and floral scrolls, a common ornamental flourish in 17th-century printing to mark the end of a section.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains that physical stillness is the result of perfectly balanced weights around an animal's center of gravity. This principle of equilibrium is essential for artists to understand how to depict a figure in a state of rest.</summary>

<keywords>equilibrium, center of gravity, biomechanics, stationary figures, Leonardo da Vinci, anatomy, physics of art</keywords>

### *On the bendings and turnings of man.* CHAPTER CCIV.

A man decreases as much in the bending of one of his sides as he increases in his other opposite side; and such a fold will ultimately be half the size <note>original: "subdupla"</note> of the part that extends. And of this, a particular treatise shall be made.

### *On bendings.* CHAPTER CCV.

By as much as one of the sides of the bendable members becomes longer, by so much will its opposite part be diminished. The extrinsic central line of the sides that do not bend, in bendable members, never diminishes or increases in its length. <note>Leonardo is observing that while the skin and outer contours stretch or compress, the structural "center line" of a limb—like the bone or the axis of the joint—remains a constant length.</note>

### *On counterbalance.* CHAPTER CCVI.

The figure that sustains a weight outside of itself and outside the central line of its mass must always throw as much weight—either natural or accidental—from the opposite side, so that it creates an equilibrium <term>equiponderantia</term> <note>The state of being equally balanced in weight or force.</note> of weights around the central line. This line starts from the center of the part of the foot that rests on the ground and passes through the entire sum of the weight supported above that part of the feet placed on the earth. One naturally sees a person who takes a weight in one of their arms throw out the opposite arm; and if this is not enough to create the counterbalance, they provide so much more of their own weight by leaning that it becomes sufficient to resist the applied weight. One also sees in a person who is about to fall over on one of their sides that they always throw out the arm of the opposite side.

<sig>H ij</sig> <note>A printer's signature mark used to keep the pages in the correct order during binding.</note>

<summary>Leonardo describes the geometric changes in the body during movement, noting how one side compresses as the other stretches, and explains the physics of counterbalance required to maintain stability when carrying a load.</summary>

<keywords>biomechanics, counterbalance, equilibrium, anatomical proportions, center of gravity, Leonardo da Vinci, physics of motion</keywords>

->CHAPTER CCIII.<-

**T**he absence of motion in any animal that rests upon its feet arises from the absence of inequality between the opposing weights that are supported upon their centers of gravity. <note>Leonardo is explaining that stability is a state of perfect equilibrium; if the weights were unequal or shifted, the body would be forced to move to regain balance.</note>

### *On the bendings and turnings of man. Chapter CCIV.*

**A** man decreases as much in the bending of one of his sides as he increases in his other opposite side; and such a fold will ultimately be half the size <note>original: "subdupla," a mathematical term meaning a ratio of 1:2</note> of the part that extends. And of this, a particular treatise shall be made.

### *On bendings. Chapter CCV.*

**B**y as much as one of the sides of the bendable members becomes longer, by so much will its opposite part be diminished. The extrinsic central line of the sides that do not bend, in bendable members, never diminishes or increases in its length. <note>Leonardo is observing that while the skin and outer contours stretch or compress during movement, the structural "center line" or axis of the limb remains a constant length.</note>

### *On counterbalance. Chapter CCVI.*

**T**he figure that sustains a weight outside of itself and outside the central line of its mass must always throw as much weight—either natural or accidental <note>Natural weight refers to the body's own limbs; accidental weight refers to an external load being carried.</note>—from the opposite side, so that it creates an equilibrium <term>equiponderance: the state of being equally balanced</term> of weights around the central line. This line starts from the center of the part of the foot that rests on the ground and passes through the entire sum of the weight supported above that part of the feet. One naturally sees a person who takes a weight in one of their arms throw out the opposite arm; and if this is not enough to create the counterbalance, they contribute so much more of their own weight by bending that it becomes sufficient to resist the applied weight. One also sees in a person who is about to fall over on one of their sides that they always throw out the arm of the opposite side.

### *On human motion. Chapter CCVII.*

When you wish to represent a man as the mover of some weight, consider that the motions must be made along different lines: that is, either from bottom to top with a simple motion, as does one who, stooping down, takes up a weight which he intends to lift by straightening himself; or when he wishes to drag something behind him, or push it forward; or when he wishes to pull downward with a rope passing through a pulley. Here it is remembered that the weight of the man pulls as much as the center of his gravity is outside the center of his support <note>original: "sostentacolo," referring to the base of support, such as the feet</note>. To this is added the force exerted by the legs or the bent back as they straighten.

One never descends or ascends, nor ever walks along any line, without the heel of the back foot being raised.

### *On motion created by the destruction of balance. Chapter CCVIII.*

Motion is created by the destruction of balance <term>bilico: the state of equilibrium or physical balance</term>, that is, by inequality: for nothing moves by itself that does not depart from its balance, and that motion becomes swifter the further it moves from its said balance.

### *On the balance of figures. Chapter CCIX.*

If the figure rests upon one of its feet, the shoulder of that side which rests shall always be lower than the other, and the pit of the throat <note>the "fontanella della gola," or the jugular notch at the base of the neck</note> will be positioned over the middle of the leg that supports the weight. The same will happen along whatever line we view that figure, provided it is without arms extending far out from the body, or without a weight on its back, or in its hand, or on its shoulder, or the extension of the leg that does not support the weight either forward or backward. <note>This describes the classic "contrapposto" pose, where the shifting of weight creates a naturalistic, asymmetrical curve in the torso.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains the physics of human movement, noting that all motion requires a shift in the center of gravity and describing the anatomical alignment of a body resting its weight on a single leg.</summary>
<keywords>human motion, balance, center of gravity, support, painting, Leonardo da Vinci, contrapposto, anatomy, equilibrium</keywords>

### *On the grace of the limbs. Chapter CCX.*

**T H E** limbs in the body ought to be arranged with grace in accordance with the purpose of the effect you wish the figure to produce. If you wish to create a figure that displays elegance <note>original: "leggiadria," implying a light, airy charm</note>, you must make the limbs delicate and extended, without showing too many muscles; and those few that you do find necessary to show, make them soft—that is, with little prominence and with shadows that are not harsh. The limbs, and especially the arms, should be "un-knotted" <note>original: "disnodate," meaning the joints should not appear stiff or locked</note>; that is, no limb should be in a straight line with the part that is joined to it. And if the pole of the man's hip is found, due to the posture taken, to have the right side higher than the left, you shall make the joint of the upper shoulder drop in a perpendicular line over the most prominent point of the hip; and let that right shoulder be lower than the left. The pit of the throat <note>the jugular notch</note> should always be higher than the center of the joint of the foot that supports the leg. The leg that does not support the weight should have its knee lower than the other, and close to the other leg.

**T H E  A T T I T U D E S** of the head and arms are infinite; therefore, I will not expand upon giving any specific rule for them. I will say, however, that they should be easy and pleasing, with various twists, so that they do not look like pieces of wood.

---

### *On the convenience of the limbs. Chapter CCXI.*

**R E G A R D I N G** the convenience <note>original: "commodità," referring to the natural ease or functional comfort of movement</note> of these limbs, you must consider that when you wish to represent someone who, by some accident, has to turn backward or to the side, you should not make them move their feet and all their limbs in the same direction where the head turns. Rather, you shall effect this by dividing the turn among four joints: namely, those of the foot, the knee, the hip, and the neck. And if you rest upon the right leg, make the left knee bend inward, and let its foot be slightly raised on the outside; and let the left shoulder be somewhat lower than the right. The nape of the neck should align with the same place where the outer ankle-bone of the left foot is turned, and the left shoulder will be over the tip of the right foot in a perpendicular line. Always ensure that where the figures turn their heads, the chest does not turn there as well, for nature, for our convenience, has given us the neck, which can easily serve various directions when the eye wishes to turn to different places; and the other joints are partly obedient to this same purpose. And if you make a man sitting, and his arms have to be used in some way for something across him, make the chest turn over the hip joint.

---

### *On a single figure outside of a narrative. Chapter CCXII.*

**F U R T H E R M O R E,** do not repeat the limbs in the same motion in a figure which you represent as being alone. That is, if the figure is shown running alone, do not make both hands forward, but one forward and the other behind, because otherwise one cannot run. And if the right foot is forward, the right arm should be behind and the left forward, because without such a disposition one cannot run well. And if another figure is made following him, who has one leg thrown somewhat forward, make the other return under the head, and

### *On human motion.* CHAPTER CCVII.

WHEN you wish to represent a man as the mover of some weight, consider that the motions must be made along different lines: that is, either from low to high with a simple motion, as one does who stoops to take up a weight that he intends to lift by straightening up; or when he wishes to drag something behind him, or push it forward; or when he wishes to pull downward with a rope passing through a pulley. Here it is remembered that the weight of the man pulls as much as the center of his gravity <term>center of gravity: the point at which the body's weight is perfectly balanced</term> is outside the center of his support. To this is added the force exerted by the legs or the back, bent as they straighten.

ONE never descends or ascends, nor ever walks along any line, without the back foot raising its heel <note>original: "calcagno"</note>.

### *On motion created by the destruction of balance.* CHAPTER CCVIII.

MOTION is created by the destruction of balance <note>original: "bilico," referring to equilibrium or poise</note>, that is, by inequality; for nothing moves by itself unless it leaves its balance, and that motion becomes swifter the further it is removed from its said balance.

### *On the balance of figures.* CHAPTER CCIX.

IF the figure rests upon one of its feet, the shoulder of that side which supports the weight will always be lower than the other, and the pit of the throat <note>original: "fontanella della gola," the hollow at the base of the neck</note> will be directly above the middle of the leg that supports the weight. The same will happen along whatever line we view the figure, provided it is without arms extending very far out from the body, or without a weight on its back, or in its hand, or on its shoulder, or the extension of the leg that is not supporting the weight either forward or backward.

### *On the grace of the limbs.* CHAPTER CCX.

The limbs in the body must be arranged with grace according to the purpose of the effect you wish the figure to produce. If you wish to create a figure that displays elegance <note>original: "leggiadria"</note>, you must make the limbs delicate and extended, without showing too many muscles; and those few that you do show for the purpose, make them soft—that is, with little prominence and with shadows that are not harsh. The limbs, and especially the arms, should be "un-knotted" <note>original: "disnodate," meaning fluid or relaxed</note>; that is, no limb should be in a straight line with the limb that joins it. And if the pole of the man's hip is found, due to the pose taken, to have the right side higher than the left, you shall make the joint of the upper shoulder drop in a perpendicular line over the most prominent point of the hip; and let this right shoulder be lower than the left. The pit of the throat <note>original: "fontanella," the hollow at the base of the neck</note> should always be higher than the middle of the joint of the foot that supports the leg. The leg that does not support the weight should have its knee lower than the other, and close to the other leg.

The attitudes of the head and arms are infinite; therefore, I will not expand upon giving any specific rule for them. I will say, however, that they should be easy and pleasing, with various twists so that they do not look like pieces of wood.

---

### *On the convenience of the limbs.* CHAPTER CCXI.

Regarding the convenience <note>original: "commodità," referring to the natural ease or functional logic of movement</note> of said limbs, you must consider that when you wish to depict someone who, by some accident, has to turn backward or to the side, you should not make the feet and all the limbs move in the same direction as the head. Rather, you should effect this turning by distributing it across four joints: namely, those of the foot, the knee, the hip, and the neck. And if the figure rests on the right leg, make the left knee bend inward, and let its foot be slightly raised on the outside. The left shoulder should be somewhat lower than the right, and the nape of the neck should align with the same place where the outer ankle-bone <note>original: "noce di fuori," literally the "outer nut" of the foot</note> of the left foot is turned. The left shoulder will be perpendicularly above the tip of the right foot. Always ensure that where the figures turn their heads, the chest does not turn with them, for nature, for our convenience, has given us a neck that can easily serve different directions when the eye wishes to turn to various places; and the other joints are partly obedient to this same principle. If you depict a man sitting, and his arms must be used for something off to the side, make the chest turn above the hip joint.

---

### *On a single figure outside of a narrative scene.* CHAPTER CCXII.

Furthermore, do not repeat the limbs in the same motion in a figure that you represent as being alone. That is, if the figure is shown running alone, do not put both hands forward, but one forward and the other behind, because otherwise one cannot run. And if the right foot is forward, the right arm should be behind and the left forward, because without such a disposition one cannot run well. And if another figure is made following him, who has one leg thrown somewhat forward, make the other return under the head, and

### *What are the principal matters of importance belonging to the figure.*
-> Chap. CCXIII. <-

Among the principal things of importance required in the representation of animals <note>In Leonardo's time, "animals" often included humans as part of the biological category.</note>, is to situate the head well upon the shoulders, the torso upon the hips, and the hips and shoulders upon the feet.

### *On balancing the weight around the center of gravity of bodies.*
-> Chap. CCXIV. <-

A figure that sustains itself upon its feet without motion will distribute equal opposite weights around the center of its support. I say that if the motionless figure is poised upon its feet, and it throws one arm forward in front of its chest, it must throw as much natural weight backward as it throws of natural and accidental <note>Natural weight refers to the body itself; accidental weight refers to anything the body is carrying or an external force acting upon it.</note> weight forward: and I say the same of every part that projects out from its whole beyond the usual.

### *Of figures that have to handle and carry weights.*
-> Chap. CCXV. <-

A man will never lift or carry a weight without shifting more than an equal amount of his own weight to the opposite side from where he intends to lift said weight.

### *On the attitudes of men.*
-> Chap. CCXVI. <-

Let the attitudes of men be disposed with their limbs in such a way that through them the intention of their soul is demonstrated.

### *Variety of attitudes.*
-> Chap. CCXVII. <-

Let the actions in men be expressed according to their age and dignity, and let them vary according to their kind, that is, of males and of females.

### *On the attitudes of figures.*
-> Chap. CCXVIII. <-

I say that the painter must note the attitudes and the motions of men born of any sudden accident <note>original: "accidente," meaning an unexpected event or spontaneous occurrence</note>, and let them be noted or fixed in the mind, and not wait for the act of weeping to be performed by someone as a rehearsal without a great cause for weeping, and then portray it; because such an act, not being born from a true case, will be neither prompt nor natural: but it is very good to have first noted it from a natural case, and then have someone stand in that pose to see some part relevant to the purpose, and then portray it.

### *On the actions of bystanders at a noteworthy event.*
-> Chap. CCXIX. <-

All the bystanders of any event worthy of being noted stand in various admiring acts to consider said act, as when justice punishes wrongdoers: and if the case is of a devout thing, all the bystanders

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanical laws of balance and gravity in the human body while emphasizing that a painter must capture genuine emotional expressions observed from real life.</summary>
<keywords>center of gravity, balance, anatomy, expression of the soul, sketching from life, human motion, artistic observation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Leonardo’s instructions on the narrative and physical representation of the human figure, moving from the psychological reaction of crowds to the specific anatomical rendering of muscles based on body type and age.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<sig>H iiij</sig>

direct their eyes with various acts of devotion toward that event, such as the showing of the Host during the sacrifice <note>A reference to the elevation of the bread during the Catholic Mass.</note>, and the like: and if it is an event worthy of laughter or of weeping, in this it is not necessary that all the bystanders turn their eyes toward that event, but rather with diverse movements, and that a great part of them rejoice or grieve together: and if the event is fearful, let the terrified faces of those who flee show a great demonstration of fear and of flight, with various movements, as will be said in the book of motions.

### *The quality of nudes.* Chap. CCXX.

Never make a figure that is slender with muscles of too much relief; because slender men never have too much flesh over the bones, but are slender due to the scarcity of flesh, and where there is little flesh, there cannot be thickness of muscles.

### *How muscles are short and thick.* Chap. CCXXI.

The <term>muscular</term> <note>original: "mvscolosi"</note> have large bones, and are thick and short men, and have a lack of fat, because the fleshiness of the muscles, through their growth, draws together, and the fat that is usually interspersed between them has no place; and the muscles in such lean men, being entirely compressed among themselves and unable to dilate, grow in thickness, and they grow most in that part which is most remote from their extremities, that is, toward the middle of their width and length.

### *How fat people do not have thick muscles.* Chap. CCXXII.

Although fat people are in themselves short and thick, like the aforementioned muscular ones, they have thin muscles, but their skin covers a great deal of spongy and hollow thickness, that is, full of air; therefore, these fat people stay afloat on the water better than the muscular ones do, who have a smaller quantity of air enclosed in their skin.

### *Which are the muscles that disappear in the various movements of man.* Chap. CCXXIII.

In the raising and lowering of the arms, the breasts disappear, or they become of greater relief: the same is done by the relief of the hips in bending outward or inward at their sides; and the shoulders show more variety, as do the hips and the neck, than any other joint, because they have more variable motions: and of this a particular book will be made.

### *Of muscles.* Chap. CCXXIV.

The limbs in youth ought not to have a pronounced definition of muscles, because it is a sign of aged strength, and in youths it is not the time, nor is there mature strength: but let the feelings of the limbs be pronounced more or less evidently, according to whether they are more or less fatigued: and always the muscles that are fatigued are higher and thicker than those that are at rest.

<summary>Leonardo discusses the psychological reactions of crowds in painting and provides anatomical rules for depicting different body types—slender, muscular, and fat—as well as how muscles change during movement and age.</summary>

repose, and the central intrinsic lines of the members that bend never remain at their natural length.

### *That the nude figured with great prominence of muscles should be without motion.*
->CHAP. CCXXV.<-

**THE NUDE** depicted with great prominence of all its muscles should be without motion, because it cannot move unless one part of the muscles relaxes while the opposing muscles pull: and those that relax lose their definition, while those that pull reveal themselves strongly and become evident.

### *That nude figures should not have their muscles overly defined.*
->CHAP. CCXXVI.<-

**THE** nude figures should not have their muscles entirely defined, because they turn out difficult and ungraceful. According to the aspect in which the limb turns toward its operation, let its muscles be more frequently pronounced in that same way. The muscle in itself often pronounces its parts through action, in a way that without such action they were not previously demonstrated in it.

### *Of the widening and shortening of the muscles. CHAP. CCXXVII.*

**THE** muscle of the back of the thigh makes greater variety in its extension and contraction than any other muscle in man. The second is that which composes the buttock. The third is that of the back. The fourth is that of the throat. The fifth is that of the shoulders. The sixth is that of the stomach, which originates under the <term>pomegranate</term> <note>original: "pomo granato" — referring to the xiphoid process at the base of the breastbone</note>, and ends under the pubic bone, as will be said of all of them.

### *Where a cord is found in men without muscles.*
->CHAP. CCXXVIII.<-

**WHERE** the arm ends at the palm of the hand, about four fingers away, one finds a <term>cord</term> <note>original: "corda" — here meaning a ligament or tendon</note>, the largest in man, which is without muscle, and originates in the middle of one of the <term>fucili</term> <note>original: "fucili del braccio" — an archaic anatomical term for the bones of the forearm, the radius and ulna</note> of the arm, and ends in the middle of the other bone, and has a square shape, and is about three fingers wide and half a finger thick, and this serves only to hold the two said bones of the arm tightly together, so that they do not spread apart.

### *Of the eight pieces that originate in the middle of the cords in various joints of man. CHAP. CCXXIX.*

**THERE ORIGINATE** in the joints of man certain pieces of bone, which are stable in the middle of the cords that bind certain joints, such as the kneecaps, and those of the shoulders, and of the feet, which are eight in total: one for each shoulder, one for each knee, and two for each foot under the first joint of the big toes toward the heel, and these become very hard toward a man's old age.

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

## *Of the muscle that is between the pomegranate and the pubic bone.* <note>original: "pomo granato" and "pettignone." The "pomegranate" refers to the xiphoid process at the base of the sternum; the "pettignone" refers to the pubic region.</note> Chap. CCXXX.

A muscle originates between the <term>pomegranate</term> and the <term>pubic bone</term> (I say it terminates in the pubic bone) which is of three powers, because it is divided along its length by three cords <note>Leonardo is describing the *rectus abdominis* muscle, which is divided by "cords" or tendinous intersections, creating the "six-pack" appearance.</note>; that is, first the upper muscle, and then follows a cord as wide as the muscle itself, then follows the second muscle lower than this, to which the second cord is joined, and finally follows the third muscle with the third cord, which cord is joined to the pubic bone. And these three repetitions of three muscles with three cords are made by nature for the great motion that man has in his bending and extending with such a muscle. If it were made of a single piece, it would create too much variation in its stretching and shrinking during the bending and extending of the man. It creates greater beauty in the man to have little variation of such a muscle in his actions; for if the muscle has to extend nine inches, and then retract as many, it does not amount to three inches for each muscle segment, which creates little variation in their shape and little deforms the beauty of the body.

## *Of the extreme turning that a man can make to see behind him.* Chap. CCXXXI.

The extreme turning that a man can make will be in showing his heels behind him while his face is looking forward <note>Leonardo describes a complex torsion where the torso is twisted so far that the head and feet face opposite directions.</note>: and this will not be done without difficulty, and only if the leg is bent and the shoulder facing the <term>nape</term> <note>original: "nucca" — the back of the neck.</note> is lowered. The cause of such turning should be demonstrated in anatomy, showing which muscles move first and which move last.

### *Of the muscle that is between the pomegranate and the pubic bone.* Chap. CCXXX.

A muscle originates between the <term>pomegranate</term> <note>original: "pomo granato," the xiphoid process or the pit of the stomach.</note> and the <term>pubic bone</term> <note>original: "pettignone," the mons pubis or pubic region.</note> (I say it terminates in the pubic bone) which is of three powers, because it is divided along its length by three <term>cords</term> <note>original: "corde," referring here to the transverse tendons or tendinous intersections of the rectus abdominis.</note>. That is: first the upper muscle, and then follows a cord as wide as said muscle; then follows the second muscle lower than this, to which the second cord is joined; finally follows the third muscle with the third cord, which cord is joined to the bone of the pubic region. These three repetitions of three muscles with three cords are made by nature because of the great motion man has in his bending and extending with such a muscle. If it were made of a single piece, it would create too much variation in its stretching and contracting during the bending and extending of the man. It creates greater beauty in the man to have little variation of such a muscle in his actions; for if the muscle has to extend nine inches, and then retract as many, it only amounts to three inches for each [segment of] muscle, which creates little variation in their shape and little deforms the beauty of the body.

### *Of the extreme turn a man can make to see behind himself.* Chap. CCXXXI.

The extreme turn a man can make will be in showing his heels behind him while his face is looking forward. This will not be done without difficulty, and only if the leg is bent and the shoulder facing the <term>nape</term> <note>original: "nucca," the back of the neck.</note> is lowered. The cause of such a turn shall be demonstrated in the anatomy, showing which muscles move first and which move last.

## *How closely one arm can be brought to the other from behind.*

### CHAPTER CCXXXII.

Regarding the arms when they are sent behind the back, the elbows will never be closer together than the distance required for the longest fingers to pass the elbows of the opposite hands. That is to say, the ultimate proximity that the elbows can have behind the small of the back will be equal to the space from one's elbow to the tip of the middle finger <note>original: "maggior dito," the longest finger.</note>. These arms thus positioned form a perfect square. Furthermore, the extent to which the arms can be crossed over the chest is such that the elbows reach the center of the chest; these elbows, together with the shoulders and arms, then form an equilateral triangle.

## *On the preparation of force in a man who wishes to generate a great strike.*

### CHAPTER CCXXXIII.

When a man prepares himself to create motion with force, he bends and twists as much as he can in the direction opposite to that where he intends to deliver the strike <note>original: "percussione," a blow or impact.</note>. There, he readies himself with all the strength possible for him, which he then directs and releases upon the object he strikes through the combined motion of the whole body <note>original: "moto del composto," the composite motion of all body parts working together.</note>.

<summary>Leonardo defines the geometric limits of arm movement, noting that crossed arms form squares or triangles, and explains how physical force is generated through a preparatory counter-twist of the body.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, arm proportions, geometry in art, kinetics, physical force, percussion, Trattato della Pittura</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

### *On the force composed by man, and first we shall speak of the arms.*

->CHAPTER CCXXXIV.<-

The muscles that move the larger bone of the arm <note>original: "maggior fucile," referring here to the humerus or the main lever of the arm.</note> in the extension and retraction of the arm, arise near the middle of the bone called the *adjutorium* <note>original: "adiutorio," a medieval anatomical term for the humerus.</note>, one behind the other; at the back is born the one that extends the arm, and in front the one that bends it.

Whether man is more powerful in pulling than in pushing is proven by the 9th proposition of *On Weights* <note>original: "de ponderibus," likely referring to the medieval "Liber de ponderibus" or Jordanus de Nemore’s works on statics, which Leonardo studied.</note>, where it says: Among weights of equal power, that one will show itself more powerful which is more remote from the pole of their balance. It follows that if N. B. is a muscle and N. C. is a muscle of equal power between them, the front muscle N. C. is more powerful than the back muscle N. B. because it is fixed to the arm at C., a site more remote from the pole of the elbow A. than is B., which is on the other side of that pole; and thus the intent is concluded. 

But this is simple force, and not "composed" force, as I proposed to treat, and we must go further. Composed force is that when, in performing an operation with the arms, a second power is added from the weight of the person and the legs—as in pulling and pushing—where, beyond the power of the arms, the weight of the person and the strength of the back and legs are added. This occurs in the desire to extend oneself, as two men might do to a column: where one pushes it and the other pulls it.

<sig>I ij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo applies the laws of the lever to human anatomy, proving that pulling is more powerful than pushing due to the muscle's attachment point on the bone, and introduces the concept of "composed force" involving the whole body's weight.</summary>

### *How much one arm can be brought near the other from behind.*

## CHAPTER CCXXXII.

Regarding the arms when they are sent behind the back, the elbows will never be closer than the distance where the longest fingers pass the elbows of the opposite hands; that is, the ultimate proximity that the elbows can have behind the kidneys <note>The author uses "kidneys" (reni) to refer to the small of the back or the lumbar region.</note> will be equal to the space from one's elbow to the tip of the middle finger <note>original: "maggior dito," the longest finger.</note> of the hand. These arms thus form a perfect square. And as for how much the arms can be crossed over the chest, it is so that the elbows meet in the middle of the chest; these elbows, together with the shoulders and arms, form an equilateral triangle.

### *On the preparation of force in a man who wishes to generate a great strike.*

## CHAPTER CCXXXIII.

When a man disposes himself to the creation of motion with force, he bends and twists as much as he can in the direction opposite to that where he wishes to generate the strike <note>original: "percussione," meaning a blow, impact, or forceful hit.</note>. There, he prepares himself with all the force possible to him, which he then conducts and releases upon the object he has struck by means of the "composed motion" <note>A term used by Leonardo to describe the combined momentum of the body's weight and muscular action.</note>.

# *On the force composed by man, and first we shall speak of the arms.*

## CHAPTER CCXXXIV.

The muscles that move the larger bone of the arm <note>The "maggior fucile" refers to the humerus, the long bone of the upper arm.</note> in the extension and retraction of the arm, originate near the middle of the bone called the *adjutorium* <note>A medieval anatomical term for the humerus bone.</note>, one behind the other; at the back is born the one that extends the arm, and in front the one that bends it.

Whether man is more powerful in pulling than in pushing is proven by the 9th proposition of *On Weights* <note>original: "de ponderibus," likely referring to the medieval "Liber de ponderibus" or similar works on statics attributed to Jordanus de Nemore, which Leonardo studied extensively.</note>, where it says: Among weights of equal power, that one will show itself more powerful which is more remote from the pole of their balance. It follows that if N. B. is a muscle and N. C. is a muscle of equal power between them, the front muscle N. C. is more powerful than the back muscle N. B. because it is fixed to the arm at C, a site more remote from the pole of the elbow A than is B, which is on the other side of said pole; and thus the intent is concluded. 

But this is simple force, and not composed force, as I propose to treat, and we must move further ahead; for composed force is that when, in performing an operation with the arms, a second power is added from the weight of the person and the legs—as in pulling and pushing. In these cases, beyond the power of the arms, there is added the weight of the person and the force of the back and the legs, which occurs in the desire to extend oneself; as would happen with two people at a column, where one pushes it and the other pulls it.

<sig>I ij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanical advantage of pulling over pushing by comparing the arm's muscles to a lever and balance, while introducing the concept of "composed force" where body weight and leg strength augment the arms.</summary>

## *Which is the greater power of man: that of pulling, or that of pushing.*

### CHAPTER CCXXXV.

A man has much greater power in pulling than in pushing, because in pulling there is added the power of the muscles of the arms, which are created solely for pulling and not for pushing. This is because when the arm is straight, the muscles that move the elbow cannot have any action <note>original: "attione," referring here to mechanical work or movement</note> in pushing, any more than a man would have by simply leaning his shoulder against the object he wishes to move from its place. In that case, only the nerves <note>In Leonardo's time, "nerui" (nerves) often referred generally to tendons or the "sinews" that transmit force from the muscles to the bones.</note> are employed which straighten the curved back, and those which straighten the bent leg—which are located under the thigh and in the calf behind the leg. 

Thus, it is concluded that to pulling is added the power of the arms and the powerful extension of the legs and the back, together with the man's chest, in the quality required by his tilt <note>original: "obliquità," referring to the angle of the body relative to the ground to leverage weight.</note>. In pushing, the same elements concur, but the power of the arms is lacking; for pushing with a straight arm without motion is exactly the same as having a piece of wood interposed between the shoulder and the object being pushed.

<note>The accompanying diagram illustrates the mechanical leverage of the arm. Leonardo argues that because the arm's muscles are designed to contract (pull), a locked, straight arm in a pushing maneuver acts merely as a rigid strut, contributing no additional muscular force beyond what the legs and torso provide.</note>

<summary>Leonardo explains that pulling is a more powerful action than pushing because it utilizes the contractile strength of the arm muscles in addition to the force of the legs and back, whereas pushing with a straight arm relies solely on the lower body and torso.</summary>
<keywords>biomechanics, muscular force, pulling vs pushing, anatomy, leverage, kinetics</keywords>

<page-num>69</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

# BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

## *Of the limbs that bend, and the function of the flesh that covers them in said bending.* Chapter CCXXXVI.

**T**he flesh that covers the joints of the bones <note>original: "osfa," an archaic plural for bones</note>, and the other parts near the bone, increases and decreases in thickness according to the bending or extension of the aforementioned limbs; that is, it increases on the inside of the angle generated by the bending of the limbs, and it thins and extends on the outside of the exterior angle. The middle part that lies between the convex and concave angles participates in such increase or decrease, but more or less so as the parts are closer to or further from the angles of the said bent joints.

## *Of turning the leg without the thigh.* Chapter CCXXXVII.

**I**t is impossible to turn the leg from the knee down without turning the thigh with an equal amount of motion. This arises because the joint of the knee bone has its contact with the thigh bone deeply set and joined <note>original: "commesso," meaning fitted or articulated together</note> with the leg bone, and such a joint can only move forward or backward, in the manner required for walking and kneeling. It can never move laterally, because the contacts that compose the knee joint do not allow it. Indeed, if such a joint were bendable and rotatable, like the bone of the "assistant" <term>adjutorium: an archaic anatomical term for the humerus or upper arm bone</term> which is joined to the shoulder, or like that of the thigh which is joined to the hips, a man would always have legs that could bend sideways just as they do from the front to the...

<sig>I iij</sig>

<summary>Leonardo explains how muscle tissue compresses or stretches around a joint during movement and describes the mechanical restriction of the knee, which prevents lateral rotation independent of the thigh.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, biomechanics, knee joint, muscle contraction, Leonardo da Vinci, Trattato della Pittura, leg movement</keywords>

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion from the previous page regarding the mechanical constraints of the knee joint, specifically why the leg cannot rotate independently of the thigh.</meta>

[page 70]
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

behind, and such legs would always be crooked. Furthermore, such a joint cannot exceed the straightness of the leg, and is only bendable forward, and not backward; for if it bent backward, a man would not be able to rise to his feet when he was kneeling. This is because in rising from a kneeling position, of the two knees, one first places the load of the torso upon one of the knees, and the weight is discharged from the other. At that time, the other leg feels no weight other than its own, whereby it easily lifts the knee from the ground and places the sole of the foot firmly on the earth. Then, it returns the whole weight upon that planted foot, leaning the hand upon its knee, and at the same time extends the arm which carries the chest and the head upward; thus it extends and straightens the thigh with the chest, and makes itself upright upon that planted foot until it has lifted the other leg.

### *Of the folding of the flesh.* CHAPTER CCXXXVIII.

**T**he folded flesh is always wrinkled on the side opposite to that where it is pulled tight.

### *Of the simple motion of man.* CHAPTER CCXXXIX.

**S**imple motion is that which is made in bending simply, either forward or backward.

### *Compound motion.* CHAPTER CCXL.

**C**ompound motion is that when, for some operation, it is required to bend downward and sideways at the same time. Thus the painter must take care to make compound movements which are entirely suited to their compositions; that is, if one makes a compound act necessitated by such an action, you must not imitate it in the contrary way by making him perform a simple act, which would be more remote from that action.

### *Of motions appropriate to the effects of men.* CHAPTER CCXLI.

**T**he motions of your figures ought to be demonstrative of the amount of force which it is appropriate for them to use in various actions; that is, you should not show the same force in one who lifts a small rod as would be suitable for the lifting of a beam. Therefore, make the demonstrations of force diverse, according to the quality of the weights handled by them.

### *Of the motions of figures.* CHAPTER CCXLII.

**Y**ou shall never make heads straight upon the shoulders, but turned crosswise, to the right or to the left, even though they look up or down, or straight ahead; because it is necessary to make their motions show a wakeful liveliness, and not a sleeping one. And do not make the midpoints of the whole person, from the front or the back, show their straightness directly above or below the other upper or lower midpoints <note>Leonardo is advising against a stiff, perfectly vertical alignment of the body's center line, suggesting a "contrapposto" or natural twist instead</note>. And if you must use it, do so for old men. And do not repeat the movements of the arms or the legs, not only in the same figure, but not even in those surrounding and nearby, unless the necessity of the case you are depicting constrains you.

<summary>Leonardo describes the mechanics of rising from a kneeling position and defines the difference between simple and compound bodily motions. He advises painters to vary the intensity of physical exertion and to avoid stiff, symmetrical poses to ensure figures appear lively.</summary>
<keywords>anatomy, kneeling, simple motion, compound motion, physical force, artistic decorum, liveliness, contrapposto, figure drawing</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the mechanics of the human body, specifically how physical movements must align with the intended action and composition of a figure.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

### *On demonstrative gestures.* <term>Chapter CCXLIII.</term>

<term>In</term> affectionate demonstrative gestures, things that are near in time or location must be shown with the hand not too far from the person demonstrating them. If the aforementioned things are remote, the hand of the demonstrator must also be remote, and the face should be turned toward what is being shown.

### *On the variety of faces.* <term>Chapter CCXLIV.</term>

<term>Let</term> the expression of the faces be varied according to the accidents <note>In this context, "accidents" refers to temporary states or emotions affecting a person.</note> of the man: in fatigue, in rest, in weeping, in laughter, in shouting, in fear, and similar things; and furthermore, the limbs of the person, together with their entire attitude, must correspond to the altered countenance.

### *On motions appropriate to the mind of the moving subject.* <term>Chapter CCXLV.</term>

<term>There</term> are some mental motions without the motion of the body, and some with the motion of the body. Mental motions without the motion of the body let the arms, hands, and every other part that shows life fall limp; but mental motions with the motion of the body hold the body and its limbs with the motion appropriate to the motion of the mind. Regarding this discourse, many things will be said. There is a third motion which participates in both; and a fourth which is neither one nor the other; and these last are senseless, or rather "out of one's senses," and are placed in the chapter on madness or of buffoons in their Morris dances <note>original: "moreſche" — a stylized folk dance often involving grotesque or rhythmic movements.</note>.

### *How mental acts move the person in the first degree of ease and convenience.* <term>Chapter CCXLVI.</term>

<term>The</term> mental motion moves the body with simple and easy acts, not here and there, because its object is in the mind, which does not move the senses when it is occupied within itself.

### *On motion born from the mind by means of an object.* <term>Chapter CCXLVII.</term>

<term>When</term> the motion of a man is caused by means of an object, either such an object arises immediately or not. If it arises immediately, the one who moves first turns the most necessary sense—which is the eye—toward the object, leaving the feet in their original place, and only moves the thighs together with the hips and knees toward that part where the eye is turned; and thus, in such occurrences, a great discourse will be made.

### *On common motions.* <term>Chapter CCXLVIII.</term>

<term>The</term> motions of men are as varied as the variety of accidents that pass through their minds; and each accident in itself moves these men more or less, according to whether they are of greater power, and according to their age; because a young man will make a different motion regarding the same case than an old man.

<sig>I iiij</sig>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, mental motions, demonstrative acts, Treatise on Painting, madness, Morris dances, object</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains how a figure's physical gestures and facial expressions must be dictated by their internal mental state and the proximity of the objects they are reacting to. He categorizes movements into mental, physical, and those resulting from external stimuli, emphasizing that age and emotion change how a body moves.</summary>

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### *On the movement of animals.* <term>Chapter CCXLIX.</term>

EVERY two-footed animal lowers that part which is above the lifted foot more than the part above the foot resting on the ground during its movement; and its uppermost part does the opposite. This is seen in the hips and shoulders of a man when he walks, and the same in birds with their heads and their rumps.

### *That every limb should be proportioned to its whole body.* <term>Chapter CCL.</term>

MAKE sure that a part of a whole is proportioned to its whole; for example, if a man is of a thick and short figure, make sure that every one of his limbs is the same in itself—that is, short and thick arms, wide and thick hands, and short fingers, with their joints in the manner mentioned above. And likewise for the rest.

### *On the observance of decorum.* <term>Chapter CCLI.</term>

OBSERVE <term>decorum</term> <note>original: "decoro" — the artistic principle of suitability, ensuring that characters and settings match their social status and the narrative context.</note>, that is, the appropriateness of the action, clothing, setting, and surroundings to the dignity or lowliness of the things you wish to depict. That is, a king should have a beard, an air, and dress of gravity, and the setting should be ornate, and the bystanders should stand with reverence and admiration, in dignified clothing suitable to the gravity of a royal court. Conversely, the lowly should be unadorned and abject, and their bystanders should bear a resemblance to them with lowly and presumptuous actions, and all the limbs should correspond to such a composition. Let the actions of an old man not be similar to those of a young man, nor those of a woman to those of a male, nor those of a man to those of a child.

### *On the age of the figures.* <term>Chapter CCLII.</term>

DO NOT mix a quantity of children with as many old men, nor young men with infants, nor women with men, unless the event you wish to depict happens to bind them together.

### *The quality of men in historical compositions.* <term>Chapter CCLIII.</term>

ORDINARILY, in common <term>historical compositions</term> <note>original: "componimenti dell' hiſtorie" — referring to 'istoria' or narrative painting, considered the highest form of art in the Renaissance.</note>, make old men rare and separated from the young, because old men are rare, and their customs do not agree with the customs of the young; and where there is no conformity of customs, friendship is not made, and where there is no friendship, there is separation. And where you make historical compositions appearing to have gravity and counsel, make few young men, because young men willingly flee from counsel; and other similar things.

### *On depicting one who speaks with several people.* <term>Chapter CCLIV.</term>

YOU SHALL use to make the one whom you want to speak among many people in the act of considering the matter he has to treat, and to adapt in him the actions belonging to that matter; that is, if the matter is persuasive, let the actions be

be similar to the purpose; and if the subject is an explanation of various reasons, make the one who speaks take a finger of the left hand with two fingers of the right, having closed the two smaller ones <note>This gesture—holding the ring and pinky fingers down while counting points on the other hand—was a standard rhetorical gesture for making a logical argument.</note>; and with his face alert toward the people, with the mouth somewhat open, so that he appears to be speaking. And if he is sitting, let him appear to raise himself somewhat upright, with his head forward. And if you make him standing, make him lean somewhat with his chest and head toward the people, whom you shall depict silent and all attentive to look at the orator's face with admiring gestures. And make the mouth of some old man closed in wonder at the heard sentences, and at the lower ends let many folds of the cheeks pull back, and with the eyebrows high at the junction, which create many folds across the forehead. Some should be sitting with the fingers of their hands interwoven, holding their tired knee; others with one knee over the other, upon which they hold a hand that receives the elbow within itself, the hand of which goes to support the bearded chin of some old man.

### How an angry figure should be made. <term>Chapter CCLV.</term>

FOR the angry figure, you will make him hold someone by the hair with their head twisted to the ground, and with one of his knees on the ribs, and with the right arm raising his fist high. This man should have his hair standing up, the eyebrows low and narrow, and the teeth clenched at the side of the arched mouth, the neck thick, and in front—due to leaning toward the enemy—full of wrinkles.

### How a desperate man is depicted. <term>Chapter CCLVI.</term>

FOR the desperate man, you will make him strike himself with a knife, and with his hands having torn his garments, and let one of these hands be at work tearing the wound; and you shall make him with his feet standing, and the legs somewhat bent, and the person similarly toward the ground, with torn hair.

### On laughing and crying, and their difference. <term>Chapter CCLVII.</term>

FROM the one who laughs to the one who cries, neither the eyes, nor the mouth, nor the cheeks vary, but only the rigidity of the eyebrows which join together in the one who cries, and are raised in the one who laughs. To the one who cries is also added the hands tearing the clothes; and it varies in the various causes of crying, because some cry with anger, some with fear, some for tenderness and joy, some for suspicion, and some for pain and torment, some for pity and grief for lost relatives or friends. Of these mourners, some show themselves desperate, some moderate; some weep, some scream, some stay with their face to the heaven and with their hands down, having their fingers interwoven; others fearful with their shoulders raised to their ears, and so they follow according to the aforesaid causes. He who sheds tears raises his eyebrows at their junction and squeezes them together, creating wrinkles above, and turns the corners of the mouth downward; but he who laughs has them high, and the eyebrows open and wide.

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### *On the posing of children.* <term>Chapter CCLVIII.</term>

In children and the elderly, there should be no ready or agile actions performed by means of their legs. <note>Leonardo advises against showing the very young or very old in poses requiring great athletic agility, as it contradicts their natural physical limitations.</note>

### *On the posing of women and youths.* <term>Chapter CCLX.</term>

In women and youths, there should be no actions with legs splayed or too wide apart, because this demonstrates boldness or a total lack of shame, whereas narrow poses demonstrate modesty.

### *Of those who jump.* <term>Chapter CCLIX.</term>

Nature operates and teaches, without any conscious thought from the jumper, that when he wishes to jump, he raises his arms and shoulders with impetus. These, following the momentum, move together with a large part of the body and rise upward until their impetus is consumed. This impetus is accompanied by the sudden extension of the body, which was curved at the back and at the joints of the thighs, knees, and feet. This extension is made obliquely—that is, forward and upward. Thus, the motion dedicated to moving forward carries the jumper's body ahead, and the upward motion raises the body, making it describe a great arc, which increases the jump.

### *Of a man who wishes to throw something with great force.*
### <term>Chapter CCLXI.</term>

A man who wishes to throw a dart, a stone, or any other thing with impetuous motion can be depicted in two principal ways: either he can be shown when he is preparing for the creation of the motion, or truly when the motion itself is finished. But if you depict him at the creation of the motion, then the inside of the foot will be on the same line as the chest, but he will have the opposite shoulder over that foot. That is, if the right foot is supporting the weight of the man, the left shoulder will be directly over the point of that right foot. <note>This describes the "wind-up" phase of a throw, where the body twists to create potential energy.</note>

<vocab>children, old men, women, youths, jumper, impetus, extension, dart, motion</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo describes the decorum of posture for different ages and genders, alongside a mechanical analysis of the physics involved in jumping and throwing.</summary>
<keywords>posture, decorum, jumping, throwing, anatomy, physics of motion, children, women, elderly</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

### *Why he who wishes to pull, or to drive an iron tool into the ground while pulling, raises the opposite curved leg.* <term>Chapter CCLXII.</term>

He who, by pulling, wishes to drive or drag a heavy object <note>original: "cannone," which can mean a large tube, pipe, or even a piece of artillery</note> into the ground, raises the leg opposite to the arm that pulls, and bends that leg at the knee. He does this to balance himself <term>belicarsi</term> <note>from "bilico," meaning to find one's center of gravity or equilibrium</note> over the foot that rests on the ground; without such bending or twisting of the legs, he could not perform the action, nor could he pull if that leg did not eventually extend itself.

### *The balancing of bodies that do not move.* <term>Chapter CCLXIII.</term>

The <term>ponderationi</term> <note>a term used in art to describe the distribution of weight in a figure</note> or balances of men are divided into two parts: namely, simple and composite. Simple balance is that which is performed by a man upon his own immobile feet. In this state, if the man opens his arms at different distances from his center, or stoops while standing on one of his feet, the center of his gravity always remains in a perpendicular line over the center of the foot that supports him. And if he rests equally upon both feet, then the center of the man's chest will be perpendicularly over the middle of the line that measures the space between the centers of those feet.

Composite balance is understood to be that which a man performs when he supports a weight upon himself through various motions. This is seen in the figure of Hercules crushing Antaeus <note>In Greek mythology, Hercules defeated the giant Antaeus by lifting him off the ground, as Antaeus drew strength from his mother, the Earth</note>: as he suspends him from the ground between his chest and arms, you must place as much of Hercules's figure behind the central line of his feet as Antaeus has the center of his gravity in front of those same feet.

<sig>K ij</sig>

### *On the posing of infants.* <term>Chapter CCLVIII.</term>

In infants <note>original: "putti," referring to young children or the cherubic figures common in Renaissance art</note> and in the elderly, there should not be any ready or agile actions performed by means of their legs.

### *On the posing of women and youths.* <term>Chapter CCLX.</term>

In women and young men, there should not be poses with legs spread wide or too far apart, because this demonstrates boldness or a total lack of shame, whereas legs kept close together demonstrate modesty.

### *Of those who jump.* <term>Chapter CCLIX.</term>

Nature operates and teaches, without any conscious reasoning from the jumper, that when he wishes to jump, he raises his arms and shoulders with force. These, following the momentum, move together with a great part of the body and lift themselves upward until their momentum is spent. This momentum is accompanied by the sudden extension of the body, which was curved in the back and at the joints of the thighs, knees, and feet. This extension is made diagonally—that is, forward and upward. Thus, the motion dedicated to moving forward carries the jumper's body ahead, and the upward motion lifts the body, making it describe a great arc, which increases the distance of the jump.

### *Of a man who wishes to throw something with great force.*
### <term>Chapter CCLXI.</term>

The man who wishes to throw a dart, a stone, or another object with impetuous motion can be depicted in two principal ways: that is, he can be depicted when he is preparing for the creation of the motion, or truly when that motion is finished. But if you represent him during the creation of the motion, then the inner side of the foot will be on the same line as the chest, but he will have the opposite shoulder over that foot. That is, if the right foot is under the weight of the man, the left shoulder will be positioned over the point of that same right foot.

### Why he who wishes to pull, or to drive an iron into the ground by pulling, raises the opposite curved leg. Chapter CCLXII.

He who, by pulling, wishes to drive or draw a <term>cannone</term> <note>original: "cannone"; here referring to a heavy iron rod or stake rather than a piece of artillery</note> into the ground, raises the leg opposite to the arm that pulls, and bends that leg at the knee; and this he does to <term>balance himself</term> <note>original: "belicarſi," a technical term for finding one's center of gravity</note> over the foot that rests on the ground. Without such bending or twisting of the legs, he could not perform the action, nor could he pull if that leg did not then extend itself.

### *The weight distribution of bodies that do not move. Chapter CCLXIII.*

The <term>weight distributions</term> <note>original: "ponderationi"</note> or <term>balances</term> <note>original: "bilichi"</note> of men are divided into two parts: that is, simple and composite. Simple is that which is performed by a man upon his own immobile feet; while standing upon these, by opening his arms at various distances from his center, or by leaning while standing on one of his feet, the center of his gravity always remains in a perpendicular line over the center of the foot that rests on the ground. And if he rests equally upon both feet, then the man's chest will have its perpendicular center in the middle of the line that measures the space between the centers of those feet.

The composite <term>balance</term> <note>original: "bilico"</note> is understood to be that which a man performs when he supports a weight upon himself through various motions: as in the figure of Hercules crushing Antaeus <note>A reference to the Greek myth where Hercules defeats the giant Antaeus by lifting him off the ground, as Antaeus drew strength from his mother, the Earth</note>. In suspending him from the ground between his chest and arms, you must place as much of Hercules's figure behind the central line of his feet as Antaeus has his center of gravity in front of those same feet.

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanical necessity of counterbalancing the body's weight when performing heavy labor or lifting an external load. He distinguishes between simple balance (a body at rest) and composite balance (a body supporting an external weight, like Hercules lifting Antaeus).</summary>

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

->*Of the man who rests upon both feet, and who gives more of his weight to one than to the other. Chapter CCLXIV.*<-

**W**hen, by standing for a long time, a man has tired the leg upon which he rests, he shifts part of the weight onto the other leg; but this type of resting should be used for those of a decrepit age, or in infancy, or truly in one who is exhausted, because it shows weariness or a lack of <term>vigor</term> <note>original: "valetudine"; referring here to the health or strength of the limbs</note> in the limbs. Therefore, one always sees a young man who is healthy and robust resting upon only one of his legs; and if he places some weight upon the other leg, he does so only when he wishes to initiate a necessary movement, without which all motion is denied, because motion is generated by <term>inequality</term> <note>The principle that movement requires a shift in balance or an asymmetrical distribution of weight</note>.

->*Of the posing of figures. Chapter CCLXV.*<-

**ALWAYS** the figures that are posed must vary their <term>limbs</term> <note>original: "membra"</note>; that is, if one arm goes forward, the other should remain still or go backward; and if the figure rests upon one leg, the shoulder that is above that leg must be lower than the other. This is observed by men of good sense, who always attend by nature to <term>balancing</term> <note>original: "bilicare"; the act of finding the center of gravity</note> the man over his feet, so that he does not collapse. Because when resting upon one foot, the opposite leg does not support the man while it is bent—it is as if it were dead. Thus, necessity dictates that the weight from the legs upward must send its center of <term>gravity</term> <note>original: "gravità"</note> over the <term>joint</term> <note>original: "giuntura"</note> of the leg that supports it.

<summary>Leonardo explains the mechanics of posture, noting that shifting weight between both legs signals weakness or age, whereas a healthy youth balances on one leg to prepare for movement. He emphasizes the principle of 'contrapposto,' where the body must counterbalance its parts to maintain equilibrium.</summary>
<keywords>posture, balance, gravity, contrapposto, anatomy, movement, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

## *Of the balance of a man when standing upon his feet.*
### Chap. CCLXVI.

**The man** who stands upon his feet will either load himself <term>equally</term> <note>distributed evenly across both supports</note> upon those feet, or he will load himself with unequal weights. If he loads himself equally upon those feet, he will be loaded with <term>natural weight</term> <note>the weight of the body itself</note> mixed with <term>accidental weight</term> <note>external weight, such as a burden being carried</note>, or he will be loaded with simple natural weight. If he is loaded with natural weight mixed with accidental weight, then the opposite ends of the limbs are not equally distant from the centers of the joints of the feet; but if he is loaded with simple natural weight, then such ends of opposite limbs will be equally distant from the joints of the feet; and thus regarding this <term>balance</term> <note>original: "ponderatione"; the scientific study of weight and equilibrium</note> a <term>particular book</term> <note>Leonardo often referred to planned but unorganized treatises on specific mechanical principles</note> shall be made.

## *Of local motion, more or less fast.*
### Chap. CCLXVII.

**Local motion** <note>movement from one place to another</note> made by a man, or by any other animal, will be of greater or lesser speed as the <term>center of their gravity</term> is more remote from or near to the center of the foot where they are supported.

## *Of four-footed animals, and how they move.*
### Chap. CCLXVIII.

**The total height** of four-footed animals varies more in animals that are walking than in those that stand still; and it varies more or less according to whether those animals are of greater or lesser size. This is caused by the <term>obliquity</term> <note>the slant or angle of the limbs relative to the ground</note> of the legs that touch the earth, which raise the figure of that animal when such legs lose their <term>obliquity</term> and place themselves perpendicularly over the ground.

<summary>Leonardo analyzes the physics of balance in humans standing still and the mechanics of motion in quadrupeds, noting how the angle of the limbs affects an animal's height during movement.</summary>

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### *On the correspondence of one half of a man's thickness with the other half. Chap. CCLXIX.*

**The one half** of the thickness and width of a man will be equal to the other, provided that the limbs joined to it do not perform equal and similar movements. <note>Leonardo is noting that while the body is symmetrical at rest, movement creates an appearance of asymmetry that the artist must account for.</note>

### *How three movements are found in a man jumping upwards. Chap. CCLXX.*

**When a man jumps** upwards, the head is three times faster than the heel of the foot before the tip of the toe leaves the ground, and twice as fast as the hips. This happens because three angles are undone <note>original: "si disfanno"; meaning the joints are straightened or extended</note> at the same time: the first is where the torso joins the thighs in front, the second is where the thighs join the legs at the back <note>the knee</note>, and the third is where the leg joins the bone of the foot <note>the ankle</note>.

### *That it is impossible for memory to preserve all the aspects and changes of the limbs. Chap. CCLXXI.*

**It is impossible** for any memory to be able to retain all the aspects or changes of any limb of any animal whatsoever. We shall exemplify this case with the demonstration of a hand. And because every <term>continuous quantity</term> <note>a mathematical term for something that can be divided without limit, like space or time</note> is infinitely divisible, the motion of the eye that looks at the hand, moving from point A to point B, moves through a space A-B which is also a continuous quantity, and consequently infinitely divisible. In every part of that motion, the aspect and figure of the hand varies in one's sight, and it will do the same while moving through the entire circle. The same will be done by the hand that is raised during its motion; that is, it will pass through space which is a quantity.

<meta>Note: The number 271 is inscribed in the upper right corner of the diagram frame.</meta>

### *On the practice sought with great diligence by the painter. Chap. CCLXXII.*

**And you, painter**, who desire the greatest practice, you must understand that if you...

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the limitations of memory and the infinite complexity of physical movement, Leonardo now addresses the foundational necessity of natural science and the practical methods for self-criticism in art.</meta>

BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.

If you do not base your knowledge on the firm foundation of natural things, you will produce works with very little honor and even less profit; but if you build it well, your works will be many and good, to your great honor and utility.

### *On the painter judging his own works and those of others. Chap. CCLXXIII.*

<margin>See above, chapter II.</margin>
When the work is equal to the judgment <note>meaning the artist's critical standard or mental ideal</note>, that is a poor sign for such judgment; and when the work exceeds the judgment, that is the worst sign, as happens to those who marvel at having performed so well. But when the judgment exceeds the work, that is a perfect sign. If a young man is in such a disposition, he will without doubt be an excellent practitioner; he will be a composer of few works, but they will be of such quality that they will stop men in admiration to contemplate them.

### *On the painter judging his own painting. Chap. CCLXXIV.*

We know that errors are recognized more easily in the works of others than in our own; therefore, ensure that you are first a good master of <term>perspective</term> <note>the science of representing three-dimensional depth on a flat surface</note>, then have a complete knowledge of the measurements of man, and be a good architect—that is, in what pertains to the form of buildings and other things. Where you lack experience, do not refuse to draw them from nature. But you must hold a flat mirror when you paint, and often look at your work within it, where it will be seen in reverse and will seem to be by the hand of another master; thus you will better judge your errors. It will also be good to rise often and take some recreation, because upon returning, your judgment is improved; staying fixed upon the work makes you easily deceived.

### *How the mirror is the master of painters. Chap. CCLXXV.*

When you want to see if your painting as a whole conforms with the things portrayed from nature, take a mirror and reflect the living thing within it, and compare the reflected object with your painting, and consider well your subject in both. You see a flat mirror demonstrate things that appear <term>relief-like</term> <note>original: "rilevate"; meaning they appear three-dimensional or standing out from the surface</note>, and painting does the same. Painting has only one surface, and the mirror is the same. Both the mirror and the painting show the likeness of things surrounded by shadow and light, and both appear to be far beyond their actual surface. And if you recognize that the mirror, by means of lines and shadows, makes things appear to stand out—and since you have among your colors shadows and lights more powerful than those in the mirror—certainly if you know how to compose them well together, your painting will also appear like a natural thing seen in a large mirror. Your master <note>referring to the mirror</note> shows you the highlights and shadows of any object; your colors include one that is brighter than the illuminated parts of the image of such an object, and similarly among those colors one is found that is darker than any darkness of that object. From this it follows that you, the painter, shall make your paintings similar to those in such a mirror when viewed with a single eye; for two eyes surround an object that is smaller than the eye <note>Leonardo is noting that binocular vision allows us to see "around" small objects, a depth cue that a flat painting cannot replicate, hence the mirror's utility for checking 2D accuracy</note>.

<sig>K iiij</sig>

<vocab>Leonardo da Vinci, painting, judgment, mirror, relief, shadow, light, perspective, architect</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to maintain a critical judgment that exceeds their technical skill and suggests using a mirror as a "master" to gain an objective perspective on their work. He explains that because both mirrors and paintings represent 3D depth on a 2D plane, the mirror is the perfect tool for identifying errors in proportion and lighting.</summary>
<keywords>artistic judgment, mirror technique, perspective, self-criticism, three-dimensionality, light and shadow, natural science</keywords>
