<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

### *Which painting is most praiseworthy. CHAP. CCLXXVI.*

That painting is most praiseworthy which has the greatest conformity with the thing imitated. This comparison is to the confusion of those painters who wish to "improve" the things of nature, such as those who imitate a one-year-old child, whose head fits five times into his height, yet they make it <margin>See above, chapter 167.</margin> fit eight times; and while the width of the shoulders should be equal to the head, they make it double, thus reducing a small child of one year to the proportions of a thirty-year-old man. They have used and seen this error used so many times that they have turned it into a habit, which habit has so penetrated and established itself in their corrupted judgment that they make themselves believe that nature—or those who imitate nature—make great errors by not doing as they do.

### *What is the primary object and intention of the painter. CHAP. CCLXXVII.*

The first intention of the painter is to make a simple flat surface appear as a body in relief and detached from that plane; and he who exceeds others in such art deserves the greatest praise. This investigation—or rather, the crown of such science—is born from shadows and lights, or what you might call <term>chiaroscuro</term> <note>the treatment of light and shade in drawing and painting</note>. Therefore, if you flee from shadows, you flee from the glory of the art among noble minds, and you acquire it among the ignorant common folk, who desire nothing more than the beauty of colors, not recognizing the quality of relief.

### *Which is more important in painting: the shadow, or its outlines. CHAP. CCLXXVIII.*

Shadows in painting require much greater investigation and speculation than their outlines. The proof of this is shown by the fact that outlines can be traced <note>original: "lucidare," referring to the practice of tracing through a transparent medium</note> using veils or flat glass placed between the eye and the object to be traced; but shadows are not captured by such a rule, because of the "insensibility" <note>meaning the lack of sharp, perceptible edges</note> of their boundaries, which are most often blurred, as is demonstrated in the book on shadows and lights.

### *How one must apply light to figures. CHAP. CCLXXIX.*

Light must be used according to the natural site where you imagine your figure to be: that is, if you imagine it in the sun, make the shadows dark and the areas of light large, and let the shadows of all surrounding bodies be imprinted on the ground. And if the figure is in gloomy weather, make little difference between the lights and the shadows, and without giving it any shadow at its feet. And if the figure is indoors, make a great difference between the lights and the shadows, with a shadow on the ground. And if you depict a window with a cloth screen and a white dwelling, make little difference between lights and shadows; and if it is illuminated by fire, make the lights reddish and powerful, and the shadows dark, and let the casting of the shadows against the walls or the ground be sharply defined; and the further they get from the body, the wider they should become. And if said figure were illuminated partly by the air <note>meaning natural daylight</note> and partly by fire, make the light caused by the air more powerful, and that of the fire almost red, in the likeness of fire.

<meta>catchword: And above</meta>

<summary>Leonardo argues that the highest goal of painting is the perfect imitation of nature through the mastery of light and shadow (chiaroscuro) to create the illusion of three-dimensional relief. He warns against distorting natural proportions and provides specific rules for rendering light based on different environmental conditions like sunlight, fire, or overcast skies.</summary>

<keywords>imitation of nature, proportions, relief, chiaroscuro, shadows, light sources, artistic judgment, anatomy of children</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

And above all, ensure that your painted figures have a large light source from above—that is, the living model you are portraying—because the people you see in the streets all have their light from above. Know that even your closest acquaintance, if lit from below, would be difficult for you to recognize.

### *Where the viewer of a painting should stand.* CAP. CCLXXX.

**L**et us suppose that A. B. is the painting being viewed, and that D. is the light source. I say that if you place yourself between C. and E., you will perceive the painting poorly, especially if it is done in oil or is heavily varnished, because it will have a luster and act almost like a mirror. For these reasons, the closer you get to point C., the less you will see, because that is where the rays of light sent from the window to the painting reflect back. But if you place yourself between E. and D., your vision will be well-served, especially the closer you get to point D., because that location participates less in the aforementioned striking of the reflected rays.

<gloss>280</gloss>
(Diagram illustrating perspective and light reflection on a plane A-B from points C, E, and D)

### *How high the vanishing point should be placed.* C A P. CCLXXXI.

**T**he <term>point</term> <note>the vanishing point in perspective</note> should be at the eye level of an average man, and the furthest edge of the plain that borders the sky should be made at the height of that boundary where the flat earth meets the sky, except for mountains, which are free from this rule.

### *That small figures should not, by reason, be finished.* C A P. CCLXXXII.

**I** say that when things appear to be of minute form, it arises from those things being far from the eye. This being the case, there must be much air between the eye and the object, and this abundance of air hinders the clarity of the object's forms, whereby the minute parts of those bodies become indiscernible and unrecognizable. Therefore, you, painter, shall make small figures only hinted at and not <term>finished</term> <note>highly detailed or polished</note>; if you do otherwise, it will be against the effects of Nature, your teacher. The thing remains small because of the great distance between the eye and the object; the great distance encloses much air within itself; and the abundance of air creates a thick body in itself, which obstructs and takes away the minute details of the objects from the eye.

<summary>Leonardo advises on the optimal placement of light sources and viewers to avoid glare, and explains why distant figures should lack detail due to atmospheric perspective.</summary>

### *What background the painter should use for his figures.*
#### C A P. CCLXXXIII.

<margin>See above, chapter 141.</margin>
Since experience shows that all bodies are surrounded by shadows and lights, I want you, painter, to arrange the illuminated part so that it ends against a dark object, and likewise, let the shadowed part of the body end against light objects. This rule will be of great help in giving <term>relief</term> <note>the illusion of three-dimensionality or "popping out" from the surface</note> to your figures.

### *Precept of painting.* C A P. CCLXXXIV.

Where the shadow borders the light, pay attention to where it is lighter or darker, and where it is more or less <term>smoky</term> <note>original: "sfumosa," referring to Leonardo's famous "sfumato" technique of soft, blurred transitions</note> toward the light. Above all, I remind you that in young people you should not make the shadows defined like stone, because flesh has a bit of transparency. This is seen by looking at a hand placed between the eye and the sun, for it appears reddish and transparently luminous. And if you want to see what kind of shadow is required for your flesh, cast a shadow there with your finger; according to whether you want it lighter or darker, hold your finger closer or further from your painting, and imitate that.

### *On depicting a wild site.* C A P. CCLXXXV.

Trees and grasses that are more branched with thin twigs should have less subtle shadows; and those trees and grasses that have larger leaves shall be the cause of larger shadows.

### *How to make a fictional animal appear natural.*
#### C A P. CCLXXXVI.

You know that it is impossible to create any animal which does not have its own limbs, and that each limb by itself must resemble those of some other animal. Therefore, if you want to make a fictional animal appear natural—let us say, for example, a serpent—take for the head that of a mastiff or hound, and give it the eyes of a cat, the ears of a porcupine, the nose of a greyhound, the eyebrows of a lion, the temples of an old rooster, and the neck of a water turtle.

### *Of faces that should be made, so they have relief with grace.*
#### C A P. CCLXXXVII.

In streets facing west, when the sun is at midday, let the walls be high enough so that the wall facing the sun does not reflect light onto the shadowed bodies. It would be good if the air were without glare, at the time when the sides of the faces are seen to participate in the darkness of the walls opposite them. Thus, the sides of the nose and the whole face turned toward the mouth of the street will be illuminated; for this reason, the eye positioned in the middle of the mouth of such a street will see that face, with all the parts turned toward it, being illuminated, and those sides turned toward the walls of the buildings being in shadow.

To this will be added the grace of shadows with a pleasing "lost" quality <note>referring to "perdimento," where edges softly vanish into the background</note>, entirely deprived of any sharp boundaries. And this will arise because of the...

### *On dividing and separating figures from their backgrounds. CAP. CCLXXXVIII.*

You must place your figure against a light background if it is dark; and if it is light, place it against a dark background; and if it is both light and dark, place the dark part against the light background, and the light part against the dark background.

### *On the difference of lights placed in various positions. CAP. CCLXXXIX.*

A small light creates large and sharply defined shadows upon shadowed bodies. Large lights create small shadows with blurred edges upon shadowed bodies. When a small and powerful light is enclosed within a large and less powerful one, such as the sun in the atmosphere, then the less powerful light will remain in the place of shadow upon the bodies illuminated by it. <note>Leonardo is explaining how the ambient light of the sky acts as a "shadow" when compared to the intense, direct light of the sun.</note>

### *On avoiding the disproportion of surroundings. CAP. CCXC.*

A very great vice is demonstrated by many painters: that is, making the dwellings of men and other surroundings in such a way that the doors do not reach the knees of their inhabitants, even though they are closer to the eye of the viewer than the man who shows he wants to enter therein. We have seen porticos crowded with men, and one of the...

<page-num>84</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

...columns supporting them appear to be in the fist of a man who leans on them as if using a thin stick, and similar things which are to be avoided with every possible study. <note>Leonardo is warning against errors in scale where massive architectural elements are painted so poorly they look like flimsy canes.</note>

### *On the boundaries of bodies called lineaments, or contours. Chap. CCXCI.*

**T**he boundaries of bodies are of such minimal evidence that at any small interval interposed between the object and the eye, the eye does not grasp the features of a friend or relative, and does not recognize them except by their clothing; thus the mind receives notice of the whole together with the parts. <note>Leonardo argues that outlines are not sharp lines in nature, but rather the eye perceives a "total" impression of a form from a distance.</note>

### *On the superficial accidents that are first lost when shadowed bodies move away. Chap. CCXCII.*

**T**he first things that are lost when shadowed bodies move away are their boundaries <term>termini</term><note>The outermost edges or outlines of an object.</note>. Secondly, at a greater distance, the shadows that divide the parts of the bodies that touch each other are lost. Third, the thickness of the legs and feet, and so successively the smaller parts are lost, so that at a long distance only a mass of confused shape remains.

### *On the superficial accidents that are first lost due to distance. Chap. CCXCIII.*

**T**he first thing among colors that is lost in the distance is the luster <term>lustro</term><note>The specular highlight or the brightest point of reflection on a surface.</note>, their smallest part and the light of lights. Second is the light, because it is smaller than the shadow. Third are the principal shadows, and in the end there remains a mediocre, confused darkness.

### *On the nature of the boundaries of bodies against other bodies. Chap. CCXCIV.*

**W**hen bodies with a convex surface end against other bodies of equal color, the boundary of the convex part will appear darker than that which terminates against the convex boundary. The boundary of horizontal shafts <note>original: "haste equigiacenti"</note> will appear very dark against a white field, and against a dark field it will appear brighter than any other of its parts, even though the light descending upon the shafts is of equal brightness across them.

<summary>This page discusses the optical effects of distance on the perception of outlines, highlights, and shadows, emphasizing how details vanish progressively as an object recedes. It also provides rules for rendering the contrast of convex surfaces and shafts against different colored backgrounds.</summary>
<keywords>contours, distance, luster, highlights, optical perspective, convex surfaces, shadows, visual perception</keywords>

### *On the figure that moves against the wind. Chap. CCXCV.*

**A** figure that moves against the wind, along any line, never maintains its center of gravity with the proper alignment over the center of its support <term>sostentacolo</term><note>The point or base upon which the body's weight rests, such as the feet.</note>.

![Fine engraving of a muscular male figure leaning forward with determination against a strong headwind, his classical robes billowing dramatically behind him. The figure is set in a sparse landscape with a gnarled, broken tree stump, illustrating physiological and physical principles of motion.]

### *On the window where the figure is portrayed. Chap. CCXCVI.*

**L**et the window of the painters' rooms be made of cloth frames <term>impannate</term><note>Frames covered with oiled cloth or paper, used before glass was common or to provide a softer, more diffused light.</note> without crossbars, and darkened degree by degree toward its edges with shades of black, so that the edge of the light is not joined directly with the edge of the window. <note>Leonardo suggests this to create a "soft box" effect, preventing harsh, abrupt transitions in the light falling on the subject.</note>

### *Why measuring a face, and then painting it in that size, it will appear larger than life. Chap. CCXCVII.*

**A. B.** is the width of the site, and it is placed at the distance of the paper **C. F.** where the cheeks are, and it would have to stay back as far as **A. C.**; and then the temples would be brought to the distance **O. R.** of the lines **A. F. B. F.** so that there is the difference **C. O.** and **R. D.**; and it is concluded that the line **C. F.** and the line **D. F.**, by being shorter, must go to find the paper where the entire height is drawn, that is, the lines **F. A.** and **F. B.** where the truth is, and the difference is made, as I have said, of **C. O.** and **R. D.** <note>This technical passage explains a common optical illusion in portraiture: because a flat drawing surface is closer to the eye than the receding sides of a three-dimensional head, a direct measurement transferred to paper often results in a portrait that feels unnaturally wide or large.</note>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

[Diagram showing a human head with geometric lines originating from a focal point F below, passing through points O, R, C, D, A, and B to demonstrate the projection of light and shadow on a spherical surface.]

->Whether the surface of every opaque body partakes of the color of its object.<-

## Chap. CCXCVIII.

You must understand that if a white object is placed between two walls, one of which is white and the other black, you will find the same proportion between the shaded and luminous parts of said object as exists between the aforementioned walls. And if the object is blue <term>azzurro</term><note>Likely referring to lapis lazuli or azurite pigments common in the Renaissance.</note>, it will be the same; therefore, when you have to paint, proceed as follows. Take black to shade the blue object that is similar to the black or the shadow of the wall that you imagine is reflecting into your object. 

If you wish to work with certain and true science, you should act in this manner: When you make your walls of whatever color you please, take a small spoon—slightly larger than those used to clean the ears—larger or smaller according to the size of the works in which such an operation is to be practiced. This spoon should have its edges of equal height, and with it, you will measure the degrees of the quantities of colors that you use in your mixtures. For example, if on the said walls you had made the first shadows with three degrees of darkness and one degree of lightness—that is, three level spoonfuls, as one measures grain—and these three spoonfuls were of simple black and one spoonful of white lead <term>biacca</term><note>A common white pigment made of basic lead carbonate, prized for its opacity.</note>, you would have made a composition of certain quality without any doubt. 

Now, you have made one white wall and one dark one, and you must place a blue object between them. If you want this object to have the true shadow and light that befits such a blue, place on one side that blue which you want to remain without shadow, and place the black beside it. Then take three spoonfuls of black and mix them with one spoonful of luminous blue, and apply with it the darkest shadow. Having done this, see if the object is spherical, columnar, or square, or whatever it may be; and if it is spherical, draw lines from the edges of the dark walls to the center of this spherical object, and where these lines intersect on the surface of such an object, there the greatest shadows terminate...

<summary>Leonardo explains a scientific method for mixing pigments using a measuring spoon to ensure the correct proportions of light and shadow, specifically how an object's color is influenced by its surroundings.</summary>

between equal angles, then it begins to brighten as it would in N. O., which leaves as much of the dark as it partakes of the upper wall A. D. This color you shall mix with the first shadow of A. B. with the same distinctions.

### ->On the motion of animals. Chap. CCXCIX.<-

**THAT** figure will demonstrate a greater run which is more inclined toward falling forward.
<margin>See above, chapter 269.</margin>
**THE** body that moves by itself will be so much faster as the center of its gravity is more distant from the center of its support. This is said for the motion of birds, which move by themselves without the beating of wings or the favor of the wind; and this happens when the center of their gravity is outside the center of their support, that is, outside the middle of their residence between the two wings. For if the middle of the wings is further back than the middle or center of the said gravity of the whole bird, then that bird will move forward and downward; but so much more or less forward than downward as the center of the said gravity is more remote or near to the middle of its wings. That is to say, the center of gravity remote from the middle of the wings makes the bird's descent very oblique, and if that center is near the middle of the wings, the descent of such a bird will have little obliquity.

For the eye does not have to attend to the flatness or the curvature of those parts, but only to the things

### *To make a figure on a wall of 12 cubits <term>braccia</term><note>A 'braccio' was a Florentine unit of measurement, approximately 23 inches.</note> that appears to be 24 cubits in height.*

### CHAP. CCCI.

**I**f you wish to make a figure or other object that appears to be 24 cubits high, you shall do it this way. First, depict on the wall M. N. half of the man you wish to make; then you shall make the other half on the vaulted ceiling M. R. But first, on the floor of a hall, draw the wall in the same shape as the wall and vault where you are to make your figure. Then, behind that wall, draw the figure in profile at whatever size you please, and draw all your lines to the point F. In the same way that they intersect on the wall N. R., you shall depict it on the actual wall that resembles your drawing. You will then have all the heights and projections of the figure, as well as the widths or thicknesses found on the straight wall M. N. You shall give it its proper form, because as the wall recedes, the figure diminishes by itself. The part of the figure that goes onto the vault must be diminished as if it were straight; this diminution you must perform on a very flat floor, and there create the figure that you will take from the wall N. R. with its true thicknesses, and then re-diminish them onto a relief wall <note>A 'parete di rilievo' refers to the actual physical three-dimensional surface of the architecture.</note>, and this will be a good method.

<meta>catchword: Warning</meta>

<summary>Leonardo explains a technique for anamorphosis, showing how to paint a figure across a flat wall and a curved vault so that it appears upright and double its actual height from a specific perspective point.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, anamorphosis, vault painting, optical illusion, braccia, geometry, architectural painting</keywords>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

### *A warning regarding shadows and lights.* CHAP. CCCII.

**TAKE NOTE** that light and shadow are always mixed at the boundaries of shadows; and the derivative shadow <note>Leonardo distinguishes between the 'original shadow' on the object itself and the 'derivative shadow' cast through space or onto another surface.</note> mixes with light all the more as it is more distant from the shaded body. But color will never be seen in its simple state: this is proven by the ninth proposition, which says: The surface of every body partakes of the color of its object <note>The 'object' here refers to whatever is facing or reflecting onto the surface.</note>, even if it is the surface of a transparent body, such as air, water, and the like; because the air takes its light from the sun, and its darkness from the deprivation of that same sun. Therefore, it tints itself in as many various colors as there are things placed between the eye and itself, because the air in itself has no more color than water has. But the moisture that mixes with it from the middle region <note>In Renaissance meteorology, the atmosphere was divided into three 'regions'; the 'middle region' was thought to be where clouds and moisture gathered.</note> downwards is what thickens it; and as it thickens, the solar rays that strike it illuminate it. The air that is from the middle region upwards remains dark; and because light and darkness together compose the color blue, this is the blue with which the air is tinted, with greater or lesser obscurity as the air is mixed with greater or lesser humidity.

### *Painting, and universal light.* CHAP. CCCIII.

**MAKE IT A HABIT** always, in a multitude of men and animals, to make the parts of their figures or bodies darker as they are lower down, and as they are closer to the center of their gathering, even if they are of a uniform color in themselves. This is necessary because a smaller quantity of the sky—the illuminator of bodies—is visible in the low spaces interposed between the said bodies.

<sig>M</sig>

# *To make a figure on a wall of 12 cubits that appears to be 24 in height.*

## CHAPTER CCCI.

**IF** you wish to make a figure or other thing that appears to be 24 cubits <note>original: "braccia" — a Renaissance unit of measurement roughly equivalent to an arm's length or two feet.</note> in height, you shall do as follows. First, depict the wall M. N. with half of the man you wish to make; then you shall make the other half on the vault M. R. But first, on the floor of a hall, make the wall of the same shape as the wall with the vault where you have to make your figure; then, behind that wall, you shall make the figure drawn in profile of whatever size pleases you, and draw all your lines to the point F. And in the manner that they intersect on the wall N. R., so you shall depict it on the wall that resembles the wall [in the hall], and you will have all the heights and projections of the figure. And for the widths, or thicknesses, that are found on the straight wall M. N., you shall make its proper form, because in the receding of the wall the figure diminishes by itself. The figure that goes on the vault you must diminish as if it were straight; which diminution you must do on a very flat hall floor, and there the figure will be made, which you will take from the wall N. R. with its true thicknesses, and re-diminish them on a wall in relief <note>This refers to a three-dimensional or architectural surface rather than a flat canvas.</note>, and this will be a good method.

<meta>catchword: Warning</meta>

### Warning regarding shadows and lights. CHAPTER CCCII.

**Take note** that at the boundaries of shadows, light and shadow are always mixed: and the derivative shadow <note>original: "ombra deriuatiua" — the shadow cast by an object onto another surface, as opposed to the "primary shadow" on the object itself.</note> mixes with the light more as it becomes more distant from the shaded body. But color will never be seen in its simple state: this is proven by the ninth [proposition], which says: The surface of every body partakes of the color of its object <note>The "object" here refers to whatever is facing or reflecting onto the surface.</note>, even if it is the surface of a transparent body, such as air, water, and the like; because the air takes its light from the sun, and its darkness from the deprivation of that same sun. Therefore, it tints itself in as many various colors as there are things placed between the eye and itself, because air in itself has no more color than water has. However, the moisture that mixes with it from the middle region <note>The "middle region" refers to the lower atmosphere where vapors and mists collect.</note> downwards is what thickens it; and as it thickens, the solar rays that strike it illuminate it, while the air from the middle region upwards remains dark. And because light and darkness composed together create a blue color, this is the blue with which the air is tinted, with more or less darkness as the air is mixed with more or less moisture.

### Painting, and universal light. CHAPTER CCCIII.

**Make it a habit** always to make the parts of figures or bodies within a multitude of men and animals darker as they are lower down, and as they are closer to the center of their gathering, even if they are of a uniform color in themselves. This is necessary because a smaller quantity of the sky—the illuminator of bodies—is seen in the low spaces placed between the said

### On backgrounds proportioned to the bodies that stand out against them, and first of flat
### surfaces of uniform color. Chapter CCCIV.

**The** backgrounds of any flat surface of uniform color and light will not appear
separated from that surface if they are of the same color and light. Therefore,
by the converse, they will appear separated if they are of different color and light.

### Painting of figure and body. Chapter CCCV.

**Regular** bodies are of two sorts, one of which is clothed in a curved,
oval, or spherical surface; the other is surrounded by sided surfaces, regular or
irregular. Spherical or oval bodies always appear separated from their backgrounds,
even if the body is the same color as its background, and the same will happen with
sided bodies <note>original: "corpi laterati" — polyhedral or multi-sided geometric solids.</note>: and this happens because they are disposed to the generation of shadows
from some of their sides, which cannot happen on a flat surface.

### In painting, the part of a body that is of
### smaller quantity will lose its definition first. Chapter CCCVI.

**Of** the parts of those bodies that move away from the eye, that which is of
smaller shape will lose its definition <note>original: "notitia" — literally 'notice' or 'recognition'; the point at which the eye can no longer distinguish the form.</note> first. From which it follows that the part of
greater quantity will be the last to lose its definition. Therefore, you, painter,
do not finish the tiny members of those things that are very remote, but
follow the rule given in the sixth <note>Likely a reference to the sixth book or section of his notes on perspective.</note>.

<summary>Leonardo explains how the geometry of the sky affects the intensity of light on the ground and discusses how shadows and size influence our ability to distinguish objects from their backgrounds.</summary>
<keywords>optics, illumination, background separation, geometric solids, visual perception, aerial perspective, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on how smaller parts of bodies lose their definition first as they recede into the distance.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

**HOW** many there are who, in depicting cities and other things remote from the eye, make the edges of the buildings very distinct, no differently than if they were in very close proximity; and this is impossible in nature, because no sight is so powerful that at such a very far distance it can see the aforementioned edges with true recognition. This is because the edges of these bodies are the boundaries of their surfaces, and the boundaries of surfaces are lines, which lines are not any part of the quantity of that surface, nor even of the air that clothes such a surface. Therefore, that which is not part of any thing is invisible, as is proven in geometry. Thus you, painter, if you make these edges sharp and distinct, as is the custom, you will not be depicting a remote distance, for by such a defect it will appear very close. Furthermore, the angles of buildings are those which, in distant cities, should not be depicted, because from afar it is impossible to see them, since these angles are the meeting of two lines in a point, and the point has no part <note>original: "il punto non hà parte" — a reference to Euclidean geometry where a point is defined as having no dimensions.</note>; therefore it is invisible.

---

### Why the same countryside appears at times larger or smaller than it is. Chapter CCCVII.

**COUNTRYSIDES** appear at times larger or smaller than they are due to the interposition of air that is thicker or thinner than usual, which comes between the horizon and the eye that sees it.

**BETWEEN** horizons of equal distance from the eye, that one will appear more remote which is seen through thicker air, and that one will appear closer which is seen in thinner air.

<margin>See above chapter 106.</margin>
**THINGS** seen unequally at equal distances will appear equal if the thickness of the air interposed between the eye and those things is unequal—that is, the thick air being interposed before the smaller thing. This is proven by means of the perspective of colors <note>original: "prospettiua de’ colori" — often referred to as aerial or atmospheric perspective.</note>, which causes a great mountain appearing small by measurement to seem larger than a small thing near the eye; just as one sees that a finger near the eye covers a great mountain far from the eye.

---

### Diverse observations. Chapter CCCVIII.

**AMONG** things of equal darkness, magnitude, figure, and distance from the eye, that one will appear smaller which is seen against a field of greater brightness or whiteness. This is taught by the sun seen behind trees without leaves, where all their branches that are positioned against the solar body are so diminished that they remain invisible. The same will happen with a spear <note>original: "hasta" — a staff or pike.</note> interposed between the eye and the solar body.

**PARALLEL** bodies placed upright, when seen through fog, must appear thicker at the top than at the bottom. This is proven by the ninth [proposition], which says: Fog, or thick air, when penetrated by solar rays, will appear so much whiter the lower it is.

**THINGS** seen from afar are disproportionate; and this arises because the lighter part sends its image <note>original: "simulacro" — a technical term in optics for the visual species or likeness of an object traveling to the eye.</note> to the eye with a more vigorous ray than the darker part does. And I saw a woman dressed in black with a cloth

### *Of cities and other things seen in thick air.* CHAPTER CCCIX.

**BUILDINGS** of cities seen from above in times of fog, or when the air is thickened by the smoke of their fires or other vapors, will always be less distinct the lower they are. Conversely, they will be clearer and more distinct the higher they are seen. This is proven by the fourth [proposition] of this [treatise], which says: The air is thicker the lower it is, and thinner the higher it is. And this is demonstrated by the same fourth proposition placed below: and we shall say the tower A.F. is seen by the eye N. in thick air, which is divided into four levels, each thicker the lower it is.

**THE** smaller the quantity of air interposed between the eye and the object seen, the less the color of that object will participate in the color of that air. It follows that the greater the quantity of air interposed between the eye and the object seen, the more that object participates in the color of the interposed air. This is demonstrated: The eye being at N., to which the five <term>species</term> <note>original: "spetie" — in Renaissance optics, these were the "images" or "likenesses" of objects that traveled through the air to the eye.</note> of the five parts of the tower A.F. converge—namely A.B.C.D.E. I say that if the air were of uniform thickness, the proportion of the color of the air acquired by the foot of the tower F. compared to the color of the air acquired by the part of the tower B. would be the same as the proportion of the length of the line M.F. to the line B.S. But according to the previous rule, which proves the air is not uniform in its thickness, but thicker the lower it is, it is necessary that the proportion of the colors with which the air tints the parts of the tower B. and F. be of a greater proportion than the proportion mentioned above; for the line M.F., besides being longer than the line S.B., passes through air that has a <term>uniformly varying</term> <note>original: "vniformemente difforme" — a scholastic term used by Leonardo to describe a gradient that changes at a constant rate.</note> thickness.

## *Of the solar rays that penetrate the openings of the clouds.*

### CHAPTER CCCX.

**THE** solar rays penetrating the openings <note>original: "spiracoli" — literally "breathing holes" or vents in the cloud cover.</note> interspersed among the various densities and spherical shapes of the clouds, illuminate all the sites where they intersect; they also illuminate the darkness and tint with their own color all the obscure places that lie behind them, which shadows are revealed within the intervals between these solar rays.

## *Of things that the eye sees below itself mixed within fog and thick air.*

### CHAPTER CCCXI.

**THE** closer the air is to the water or the earth, the thicker it becomes. This is proven by the 19th [proposition] of the second [book], which says: That thing which has in itself the greatest weight rises the least; it follows that the lighter thing rises higher than the heavy one. <note>Leonardo applies a principle of physics to explain why moisture and dust settle in the lower atmosphere, creating "thick air."</note>

## *Of buildings seen in thick air.* CHAPTER CCCXII.

**THAT** part of the building will be less evident which is seen in air of greater thickness; and conversely, that which is seen in thinner air will be more distinct. Therefore, the eye N., seeing the tower A. D., will see in every degree of lowliness a part that is less distinct and lighter in color, and in every degree of height a part that is more distinct and less light.

---

## *Of the object that shows itself from a distance.* CHAPTER CCCXIII.

**THAT** dark object will appear lighter which is more remote from the eye. It follows by the converse that the dark object will show a greater darkness when it is found closer to the eye. Therefore, the lower parts of any object placed in thick air will seem more remote from the feet than their summits; for this reason, the low base <note>original: "radice" — literally "root."</note> of the mountain will appear more distant than the peak of the same mountain, which in itself is actually more remote.

<summary>Leonardo explains how atmospheric density (thick air) affects the perception of light and shadow, noting that objects appear lighter and less distinct as they descend into the denser air near the ground.</summary>

## *Of the view of a city in thick air. CHAPTER CCCXIV.*

**THE EYE** that sees a city below itself in thick air <note>original: "aria grossa" — refers to air filled with moisture, dust, or haze, which creates atmospheric perspective.</note>, sees the tops of the buildings darker and more distinct than their bases; it sees these tops against a light background because it views them against the low, thick air. This occurs for the reasons previously stated.

## *Of the lower boundaries of remote objects. CHAPTER CCCXV.*

**THE** lower boundaries of remote objects will be less perceptible than their upper boundaries. This happens frequently with mountains and hills, where the peaks are set against the sides of other mountains located behind them. In these cases, the upper boundaries are seen more clearly than their bases, because the upper boundary is darker, being less obscured by the thick air that settles in low places. It is this air that blurs the boundaries of the bases of the hills. The same occurs with trees, buildings, and other things that rise up through the air. From this it arises that often high towers seen at a great distance appear wide at the top and thin at the bottom; this is because the upper part reveals the angle of the sides where they meet the front, as the thin air does not hide them from you as the thick air does. This happens according to the 7th [proposition] of the first [book], which says that the thick air interposed between the eye and the sun is brighter at the bottom than at the top; and where the air is whiter, it obscures dark objects more from the eye than if such air were blue, as is seen at a long distance. The battlements <note>original: "merli" — the rectangular parapets on top of a fortress wall.</note> of fortresses have spaces between them equal to the width of the battlements themselves, and yet the space always appears much larger than the battlement; at a more remote distance, the space occupies and covers the entire battlement, and such a fortress usually appears as a straight wall without any battlements at all.

## *Of things seen from afar. CHAPTER CCCXVI.*

**THE** boundaries of an object will be less distinct the further the distance from which they are viewed.

## *Of the blue color that appears in distant landscapes. CHAPTER CCCXVII.*

**OF** remote things seen by the eye, whatever their color may be, that which is of the greatest darkness—whether natural or accidental—will appear the most blue. Natural darkness is that which is dark in itself; accidental darkness is that which is dark due to the shadow cast upon it by other objects.

<margin>
See above, chapters 292 and 306.
</margin>

## *Which parts of bodies are lost to sight due to distance. CHAPTER CCCXVIII.*

**THOSE** parts of bodies that are of smaller size will be the first to be lost to sight over a long distance. This happens because the images <note>original: "spetie" — the visual 'species' or rays that Leonardo believed traveled from an object to the eye.</note> of smaller things at an equal distance reach the eye at a smaller angle than larger things, and the perception of remote things diminishes as their size diminishes. It follows, therefore, that when

### *Why things are less recognizable the further they are removed from the eye.*
### CHAPTER CCCXIX.

That object will be less known which is more remote from the eye. This happens because those parts that are most minute are lost first; and the second-most minute parts are also lost at a greater distance, and so on successively, with the parts being consumed little by little until the recognition of the remote thing is consumed. In such a way, eventually, all the parts are lost together with the whole; and the color also fails because of the thickness of the air that is interposed between the eye and the thing seen.

### *Why faces appear dark from a distance.* CHAPTER CCCXX.

We see clearly that all the images <note>original: "similitudini" — the visual species or likenesses of objects that Leonardo believed traveled through the air to the eye.</note> of evident things that are before us as objects, both large and small, enter the sense through the small light <note>original: "luce dell'occhio" — likely referring to the pupil.</note> of the eye. If through such a small entrance passes the image of the greatness of the sky and the earth—the face of a man being almost nothing among such large images of things—it occupies so little of that light due to the distance that diminishes it, that it remains incomprehensible. Furthermore, having to pass from the surface to the impression-receiver <term>impressiva: a hypothetical part of the brain where Leonardo believed sensory data was processed</term> through a dark medium, that is, the hollow nerve <note>original: "nervo uoto" — the optic nerve, which Leonardo believed was a hollow tube for transmitting visual spirits.</note> which appears dark, that image, not being of a powerful color, is tinged by that darkness of the path, and having reached the impression-receiver, it appears dark. No other cause can in any way be taught regarding that point and the nerve that stands in the light; and because it is full of a transparent humor like air, it performs the same function as a hole made in a board, which appears black when looked at, and things seen through clear and dark air are confused in the darkness.

### *Which parts of bodies removed from the eye are lost to notice first, and which are preserved longer.* CHAPTER CCCXXI.

That part of the body which is removed from the eye is the one that least preserves its evidence, and which is of a smaller shape. This happens in the highlights <note>original: "lustri"</note> of spherical or columnar bodies, and in the thinner limbs of bodies; as with the stag <note>original: "cervo"</note>, which ceases to send to the eye the species or images of its legs and horns before those of its torso, which, being thicker, is preserved longer in its image. But the first thing lost in the distance are the outlines <note>original: "lineamenti"</note> that terminate the surface and shape.

### *Of linear perspective.* CHAPTER CCCXXII.

Linear perspective <note>original: "prospettiva lineale"</note> extends its duty through visual lines to prove by measurement how much the second object is smaller than the first, and the third than the second, and so on by degrees until the end of the things seen. I find by experience that if the second object is as distant from the first as the first is distant from your eye, even though they are of equal size...

He also introduces the principles of linear perspective and the proportional reduction of objects in the distance.</summary>

size, the second shall be half the size of the first; and if the third object is at an equal distance from the second as the second is from the first, it shall be smaller by two-thirds; and so, degree by degree at equal distances, they will always undergo a proportionate diminution, provided that the interval does not exceed the number of 20 <term>braccia: an Italian unit of measurement, roughly a "cubit" or an arm's length, approximately 23 inches</term>. Within said 20 braccia, a figure similar to you will lose 2/3 of its size; within 40, it will lose 3/4; and then 5/6 at 60 braccia; and so, step by step, it will diminish. This assumes the wall <note>The "parete" or picture plane upon which the artist projects the image.</note> is distant from you by twice your own height; for if it were only one height away, there would be a great difference between the first braccia and the second.

### *Of bodies seen in the fog.* Chapter CCCXXIII.

Those things which are seen in the fog will appear much larger than their true size. This arises because the perspective of the medium interposed between the eye and such an object does not match its color with the magnitude of said object; because such fog is similar to the hazy air interposed between the eye and the horizon in clear weather. A body near the eye, seen through the proximity of the fog, appears to be at the distance of the horizon, where a very great tower would appear smaller than the aforementioned man standing nearby.

### *Of the height of buildings seen in the fog.* Chapter CCCXXIV.

and 315.
</margin>
That part of a nearby building appears more blurred which is more remote from the ground; and this arises because there is more fog between the eye and the top of the building than there is from the eye to its base. And a parallel tower seen at a long distance through the fog will appear so much thinner as it is closer to its base. This arises from the previous rule, which says: The fog will appear so much whiter and thicker as it is closer to the earth; and by the second rule of this [subject], which says: A dark object will appear of a smaller shape the more it is seen against a field of more powerful whiteness. Therefore, the fog being whiter at the foot than at the top, it is necessary that the darkness of such a tower appears narrower at the foot than at the top.

### *Of cities and other buildings seen in the evening or morning in the fog.* Chapter CCCXXV.

In buildings seen at a long distance in the evening or morning in the fog or thick air, only the brightness of their parts illuminated by the sun is shown, which are found toward the horizon; and the parts of said buildings that are not seen by the sun remain almost the color of the medium darkness of the fog.

### *Why higher things placed in the distance are darker than low things, even though the fog is uniform in thickness.* Chapter CCCXXVI.

Of things placed in the fog, or other thick air, or in vapor, or smoke, or in the distance, that one will be so much more recognizable which is higher. And of things of equal height, that one appears darker which stands out against darker fog, as happens to the eye H, which, seeing A, B, and C—towers of equal height among themselves—sees C, the summit of the first tower, at R, a depth of two degrees of profundity...

->*Of the patches of shadows that appear in bodies from a distance.*<-

### CHAPTER CCCXXVII.

The throat, or any other perpendicular upright that has an overhang above it, will always be darker than the perpendicular face of that overhang. It follows that a body will appear more illuminated when it is seen by a greater amount of the same light. See in A. that no part of the sky F. K. illuminates it; and in B. the sky H. K. illuminates it; and in C. the sky G. K.; and in D. the sky F. K. entirely. Therefore, the chest will be of equal brightness to the forehead, nose, and chin. 

But what I must remind you regarding faces is that you should consider how, at various distances, different qualities of shadows are lost, and only those primary patches remain—namely, the hollows of the eye and other similar features. Ultimately, the face remains dark because the highlights are consumed <note>original: "si consumano," meaning they vanish or are overwhelmed by the surrounding tones</note>, which are small things compared to the <term>middle shadows: the transitional tones between light and deep shadow</term>. For this reason, over a long distance, the quality and quantity of the principal lights and shadows are consumed, and every quality is confounded into a middle shadow. 

And this is the reason why trees, and every body at a certain distance, appear to become darker in themselves than when those same objects are near the eye. But then the air that is interposed between the eye and the object causes that object to brighten and lean toward blue; but it turns blue more in the shadows than in the luminous parts, where the truth of the colors is more apparent.

### Why at the approach of evening the shadows of bodies produced on a white wall are *blue*. CHAPTER CCCXXVIII.

**THE SHADOWS** of bodies produced by the redness of the sun near the horizon will always be blue. This arises from the eleventh [rule], which states: The surface of every opaque body partakes of the color of its object <note>In this context, "object" refers to the source of light or the surrounding environment reflecting onto the surface</note>. Therefore, since the whiteness of the wall is entirely deprived of any color of its own, it is tinted by the colors of its objects, which in this case are the sun and the sky. And because the sun turns reddish toward evening, and the sky appears blue, the place where the shadow does not see the sun <note>meaning the area blocked from direct sunlight</note>—according to the eighth [rule] of shadow, which says: No luminous body ever saw the shadows of the body it illuminates—will instead be seen by the sky. Therefore, by the aforementioned eleventh rule, the <term>derivative shadow: the shadow cast by an object onto another surface</term> will strike the white wall with a blue color, while the field of that shadow <note>the surrounding illuminated area</note> seen by the redness of the sun will partake of the color red.

<margin>shadows, blue, eleventh rule, eighth rule of shadow, opaque body, color of its object, derivative shadow, percussion <note>original: "percussione," referring to the point where a ray of light or a shadow strikes a surface</note></margin>

<summary>Leonardo explains the optical phenomenon where shadows appear blue at sunset because they are shielded from the red sunlight and instead reflect the blue light of the sky. This demonstrates his principle that opaque surfaces take on the colors of the light sources facing them.</summary>
<keywords>optics, blue shadows, sunset, atmospheric perspective, color theory, Leonardo da Vinci, light and shadow</keywords>

<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

### *Where smoke is brightest. Chapter CCCXXIX.*

**SMOKE** seen between the sun and the eye will appear brighter and more lucid than in any other part of the landscape where it originates. The same occurs with dust and mist; however, if you place yourself between the sun and these elements <note>meaning the sun is behind the painter, shining onto the smoke</note>, they will appear dark to you.

### *Of dust. Chapter CCCXXX.*

**THE DUST** that is raised by the movement of any animal appears brighter the higher it rises; conversely, it appears darker the less it is elevated, provided it remains between the sun and the eye.

### *Of smoke. Chapter CCCXXXI.*

**SMOKE** is more transparent and dark toward the edges of its <term>globules: the rounded, billowing clusters of smoke</term> than toward its center.

**SMOKE** moves with greater slant or obliquity the more powerful the wind is that drives it.

**SMOKES** are of as many various colors as there are varieties of things that generate them.

**SMOKES** do not create distinct or sharp shadows; their boundaries are less discernible the further they are from their source. Objects placed behind them are less evident the denser the knots of smoke are; the smoke is whiter the closer it is to its beginning, and more blue toward its end.

**FIRE** will appear darker to us the greater the amount of smoke that is interposed between the eye and said fire.

**WHERE** the smoke is more remote, objects are less obscured by it.

**REPRESENT** the landscape confused as if by a thick mist, in which smoke is seen in various places with flames at their base—the illuminators of the densest <term>globules</term> of said smoke. Let the highest mountains be more evident than their bases, as is seen to happen in mists.

### *Various precepts of painting. Chapter CCCXXXII.*

**THE SURFACE** of every opaque body partakes of the color of the transparent medium interposed between the eye and said surface; it does so more intensely the denser that medium is, and the greater the space that exists between the eye and the said surface.

**THE BOUNDARIES** of opaque bodies will be less distinct the further they are from the eye that sees them.

**THAT PART** of an opaque body will be more shadowed or illuminated which is closer to the shadowy thing that obscures it, or to the luminous thing that illuminates it.

**THE SURFACE** of every opaque body partakes of the color of its object <note>the surrounding environment or light source</note>, but with a greater or lesser impression depending on whether that object is near or remote, or of greater or lesser power.

**THINGS** seen between light and shadow will show greater <term>relief: the illusion of three-dimensionality and depth in a painting</term> than those which are entirely in the light or in the shadows.

**WHEN** you represent known and distinct things at great distances, they...

<meta>Continuing from page 99, which discussed the visual properties of smoke, dust, and mist. This page transitions into the principles of aerial perspective, specifically how distance and the atmosphere affect the clarity, color, and boundaries of objects.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

things that are not distant but close will appear so. Therefore, in your imitations <note>referring to the artist's painted copies of nature</note>, ensure that things possess that degree of definition that their distances demonstrate. And if the object before you has confused and doubtful boundaries, you shall do the same in your representation.

**A DISTANT** thing appears with confused and doubtful boundaries for two different reasons. The first is that it comes to the eye at such a small angle and diminishes so much that it functions like minute things; even if these are close to the eye, the eye cannot comprehend what the shape of such a body might be—such as fingernails, ants, or similar things. The second is that between the eye and distant things, so much air is interposed that it becomes thick and dense; by its whiteness, it tints the shadows and veils them with its own brightness, changing them from dark into a color between black and white, which is blue <note>Leonardo is describing the phenomenon of Rayleigh scattering, which makes distant mountains look blue</note>.

**ALTHOUGH** through long distances the recognition of the nature of many things is lost, nonetheless those illuminated by the sun will present a more certain appearance, while others will seem wrapped in confused shadows. And because at every lower level the air acquires more density, things that are lower will appear more confused, and vice versa.

**WHEN** the sun makes the clouds on the horizon turn red, things that were clothed in blue by distance will participate in that redness; thus, a mixture will be created between the blue and the red, which will make the countryside very cheerful and pleasant. All dense things illuminated by such redness will be very evident and will turn reddish; and the air, being transparent, will have such redness infused throughout it, so that it will appear the color of the lily flower <note>likely referring to the *Lilium candidum* or a pale purple iris, suggesting a soft violet or mauve hue</note>.

**THAT** air which stays between the sun and the earth, when it rises or sets, will always obscure the things behind it more than any other part of the air; this happens because it is more whitening.

**NO BOUNDARIES** or profiles should be made of one body standing out against another; rather, the body should stand out by itself.

**IF THE BOUNDARY** of a white object meets other white objects, if it is curved, it will naturally create a dark boundary, and it will be the darkest part of the illuminated side; and if it stands out against a dark place, that boundary will seem the brightest part of the illuminated side.

**THAT THING** will appear more remote and detached from another which stands out against a field most different from itself.

**IN DISTANCES**, the boundaries of bodies that have similar colors are lost first, where the boundary of one is against the other—such as the boundary of one oak tree against another similar oak. Secondly, at a greater distance, the boundaries of bodies of medium colors placed one against the other will be lost—such as trees, plowed land, walls, or other ruins of mountains or stones. Finally, the boundaries of bodies where light meets dark and dark meets light will be lost.

**AMONG THINGS** of equal height situated above the eye, that which is more remote from the eye will be lower; and if it is situated below the eye,

<page-num>101</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

the part closest to the eye will appear lower, and the parallel sides will converge at a single point.

**THINGS** located near rivers are less evident in distant places than those that are remote from such rivers and marshes.

**AMONG** things of equal thickness, those that are closer to the eye will appear more sparse, and those more remote will appear denser.

**THE EYE** that has a larger pupil will see the object as a larger figure. This is demonstrated by looking at a celestial body <note>original: "corpo celeste"; likely referring to the sun or moon</note> through a tiny hole <term>spiracolo: a small aperture or pinhole</term> made with a needle in paper; because only a small part of its light can operate, that body seems to diminish in size by as much as the portion of light seeing it is lacking from its whole.

**THE AIR** that is thickened and interposed between the eye and the object renders the boundaries of that object uncertain and confused, and makes the object appear larger than it is. This happens because linear perspective <term>prospettiva lineare: the mathematical system of creating depth through converging lines</term> does not diminish the angle that carries its images to the eye, but the perspective of colors <term>prospettiva de' colori: also known as aerial perspective, where color and clarity change with distance</term> pushes and removes it to a greater distance than it actually is; thus, one removes it from the eye, and the other preserves its magnitude.

**WHEN** the sun is in the west, the falling mists thicken the air, and things not seen by the sun remain dark and confused, while those illuminated by the sun turn reddish and yellowish, according to how the sun appears on the horizon. Furthermore, the things illuminated by this light are very evident, especially the buildings and houses of cities and villas, because their shadows are dark; it seems that their clear appearance arises from confused and uncertain backgrounds, because everything is of one color unless it is seen by the sun.

**AN OBJECT** illuminated by the sun is also illuminated by the air, so that two shadows are created; of these, the one that has its central line pointing directly to the center of the sun will be darker. The central line of the primary and derivative light will always be aligned with the central line of the primary or derivative shadows. <note>Leonardo distinguishes between "primary" shadows on the object itself and "derivative" shadows cast onto other surfaces.</note>

**THE SUN** makes a beautiful spectacle when it is in the west, illuminating all the high buildings of cities and castles, and the tall trees of the countryside, tinting them with its color. Everything else from there downwards remains with little relief, because being illuminated only by the air, there is little difference between the shadows and the lights, and for this reason, they do not stand out much. Those things that rise highest among them are touched by the solar rays and, as has been said, are tinted in their color; therefore, you must take the color with which you make your sun, and mix it into whatever light color you use to illuminate those bodies.

Furthermore, it often happens that a cloud will appear dark without having a shadow cast upon it by another separate cloud; this happens according to the position of the eye, because from one nearby cloud only the shaded part is visible, while of the others, both the shaded and the luminous parts are seen.

Among things of equal height, that which is more distant from the eye will appear lower. You see that the first cloud, even though it is lower than the second, appears higher than it, as is demonstrated on the wall <note>original: "parete"; referring to the vertical plane of the drawing or the artist's canvas</note> by the intersection of the <term>pyramid: the cone of vision or visual rays extending from the eye to the object</term> of the first low cloud at M. A. and of the second higher cloud at N. M. This occurs when you seem to see a dark cloud higher than a bright cloud due to the rays of the sun in the east or the west.

### On backgrounds. CHAPTER CCCXXXIV.

**THE MOST PRINCIPAL** part of painting are the backgrounds of the painted things. In these backgrounds, the boundaries <note>original: "termini"; the edges or outlines</note> of natural objects that possess a convex curvature are always distinguishable, as are the figures of such bodies against these backgrounds, even if the colors of the bodies are the same color as the aforementioned background. This arises because the convex boundaries of bodies are not illuminated in the same way that the background is illuminated by the same light; because such a boundary will often be lighter or darker than that background. But if such a boundary is the same color as the background, without a doubt that part of the painting will prevent the recognition of the figures' outlines; and such a choice in painting is to be avoided by the minds of good painters, since the intention of the painter is to make his bodies appear to stand out in front of the backgrounds—and in the case mentioned above, the opposite happens, not only in painting, but in objects in <term>relief: three-dimensional sculpture or high-contrast modeling</term>.

### *On the judgment that must be made regarding a painter's work. CHAPTER CCCXXXV.*

**FIRST** is that you consider the figures, whether they have the relief required by their position and the light that illuminates them; that the shadows are not the same at the edges of the <term>narrative: "historia," the overall composition or scene</term> as they are in the middle, because it is one thing to be surrounded by shadow, and another to have shadow on only one side. Those figures are surrounded by shadow which are toward the middle of the scene, because they are shaded by the figures placed between them and the light; and those are shaded on only one side which are placed between the light and the scene, because where they do not see the light, they see the scene, and the darkness of that scene is represented there; and where they do not see the scene, they see the splendor of the light, and its brightness is represented there.

<summary>Leonardo discusses the importance of backgrounds in defining the outlines of objects and provides criteria for judging a painting based on the realistic distribution of light and shadow within a composition.</summary>
<keywords>backgrounds, outlines, relief, composition, light and shadow, visual perception, artistic judgment, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on how backgrounds and outlines affect the perception of three-dimensional depth and the painter's duty to make figures stand out.</meta>

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>
<meta>catchword: in various</meta>

**SECONDLY**, the distribution, or rather the arrangement <note>original: "compartitione"</note> of the figures, should be according to the subject <note>original: "historia"; the narrative or scene being depicted</note> which you wish to represent.

**THIRDLY**, the figures should be intently focused on their particular actions with readiness.

### *On the relief of figures remote from the eye.* CHAPTER CCCXXXVI.

That opaque body will show itself to be of less relief <note>relief here refers to the illusion of three-dimensionality</note> which is more distant from the eye. This happens because the air interposed between the eye and that opaque body—being a thing clearer than the shadow of such a body—corrupts that shadow and brightens it, taking away the power of its darkness. This loss of darkness is the cause of the body losing its appearance of relief.

### *On the boundaries of illuminated members.* CHAPTER CCCXXXVII.

The boundary <note>original: "termine"; the edge or outline</note> of an illuminated limb will appear darker when seen against a lighter background, and similarly, it will appear lighter when seen against a darker background. And if such a boundary is flat and seen against a light background similar to its own brightness, the boundary will be imperceptible.

### *On boundaries.* CHAPTER CCCXXXVIII.

The boundaries of secondary objects will never be as recognizable as the primary ones. Therefore, you, painter, do not define the fourth-level objects as sharply against the fifth-level ones as you do the first against the second. For the boundary of one thing against another is of the nature of a mathematical line, but is not a line; because the boundary of one color is the beginning of another color, and it should not be called a line, because nothing intervenes between the boundary of one color placed before another color except the boundary itself, which is an imperceptible thing when viewed closely. Therefore, you, painter, do not emphasize it in distant things.

### *On flesh tones and things remote from the eye.* CHAPTER CCCXXXIX.

The painter must place in figures and things remote from the eye only unfinished spots <note>original: "macchie"; patches of color or "sfumato" effects</note> with confused boundaries. Such figures should be chosen when it is cloudy or toward evening. Above all, one must guard, as I have said, against defined lights and shadows, because they appear like mere tints when seen from afar, and result in works that are difficult and without grace. And you must remember that shadows should never be of such a quality that, through their darkness, you lose the color where they are caused—unless the place where the bodies are situated is already pitch black. Do not make profiles, do not separate individual hairs, do not apply white highlights except on white things, and let those highlights demonstrate the primary beauty of the color where they rest.

### *Various precepts of painting.* CHAPTER CCCXXXX.

The boundaries and shape of any part of shaded bodies are poorly recognized in their shadows and their lights; but in the parts interposed between the lights and the shadows of these bodies, they are in the first degree of clarity.

Perspective, as it extends into painting, is divided into three principal parts: the first of which concerns the diminution <note>the gradual shrinking of size</note> of the quantities of bodies...

<summary>Leonardo explains how atmospheric perspective and light affect the clarity of outlines and the perception of three-dimensional relief in distant objects. He advises painters to avoid sharp edges and harsh highlights on remote figures to maintain a natural, graceful appearance.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric perspective, relief, boundaries, shadows, flesh tones, diminution, light and shade, artistic grace</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the boundaries of objects and how they should be rendered with decreasing sharpness as they recede into the distance.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

at various distances. The second part is that which treats of the diminution <note>the fading or lessening</note> of the colors of such bodies. The third is that which diminishes the perception of the figures and the boundaries that these bodies have at various distances.

The **Azure** <note>the blue color of the sky</note> of the air is a color composed of light and darkness. I say light because of the air illuminated within the particles of moisture infused within said air. By darkness, I mean the pure air, which is not divided into atoms—that is, particles of moisture—upon which the solar rays might strike. And an example of this is seen in the air that is interposed between the eye and the shaded mountains, due to the shadows of the great abundance of trees found upon them; or rather, the air is shaded in that part which is not struck by the solar rays. This air becomes blue, and it does not become blue in its luminous part, and much less so in the part covered with snow.

Among things equally dark and at an equal distance, that one will appear to be darker which terminates against a whiter field, and so conversely.

That thing which is most painted with white and black will appear in better relief <note>three-dimensional depth</note> than any other. Therefore, remember, painter, to dress your figures in the lightest colors you can: for if you make them of a dark color, they will have little relief and little evidence from a distance. This is because the shadows of all things are dark, and if you make a dark garment, there will be little variation between the light and the shadow; whereas in light colors, there will be a great difference.

### *Why things portrayed perfectly from nature do not appear with the same relief as that which the natural object itself appears to have. Chapter CCCXLI.*

<margin>See above, chapter 53.</margin>
It is impossible that a painting, though imitated with the highest perfection of outlines, shadows, light, and color, can appear with the same relief as the natural object itself, unless that natural object is viewed from a long distance with only a single eye. This is proven: Let the eyes be A and B, which see the object C with the convergence of the central lines of the eyes A-C and B-C. I say that the lateral lines of these central ones see behind said object the space G-D; and eye A sees the entire space F-D, and eye B sees the entire space G-E. Therefore, the two eyes see behind the object C the entire space F-E. For this reason, the object C remains transparent—according to the definition of transparency, behind which nothing is hidden—which cannot happen to one who sees with a single eye an object larger than the eye itself. And from what has been said, we can conclude our inquiry: because a painted thing occupies all the space behind itself, and by no means is it possible to see any part of the background which its circumferential line has behind it.

# On making things appear detached from their backgrounds, that is, from the wall where they are painted. CHAPTER CCCXLII.

**Much** more relief <note>the illusion of three-dimensionality</note> will objects show against a light and illuminated background than against a dark one. The reason is that if you wish to give relief to your figure, you ensure that the part of the body furthest from the light participates less in that light, whereby it remains darker; and then ending against a dark background, it falls into confused boundaries. For this reason, if a reflection does not occur there, the work remains without grace, and from a distance nothing appears except the luminous parts. Thus, it is inevitable that the dark parts seem to be of the same background, and so things appear cut out, and stand out much less than they ought to, in proportion to how dark the background is.

# Precept. CHAPTER CCCXLIII.

**Figures** have more grace placed in universal lights <note>broad, diffused lighting like an overcast sky</note> than in particular and small ones <note>sharp, direct light sources like a single candle or window</note>; because great and powerful lights embrace the reliefs of bodies, and works made in such lights appear from a distance with grace. Those that are portrayed in small lights take on a great amount of shadow, and similar works made with such shadows never appear from distant places as anything other than tinted <note>flat or stained in appearance</note>.

# On depicting the parts of the world. CHAPTER CCCXLIV.

**You** shall be advised that in maritime places, or those near them, facing southern regions, you should not depict winter in the trees or meadows as you would in northern parts remote from those seas—except in the case of trees which shed their leaves every year.

# On depicting the four parts of the seasons of the year, or those participating in them. CHAPTER CCCXLV.

**In** autumn you shall make things according to the age of such a time; that is, in the beginning the leaves of the trees start to turn pale on the oldest branches, more or less <unclear>...</unclear>

<keywords>relief, lighting, universal light, landscape painting, seasons, autumn, maritime climate, artistic precepts</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on depicting the seasons and the effects of climate on vegetation, specifically how southern regions differ from northern ones in winter.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<sig>O ij</sig>

lesser according to whether the plant is in a sterile or fertile place: and do not do as many, who make all types of trees—even if they are equally distant from one another—of the same quality of green. Likewise, the color of the meadows, stones, and the trunks <term>pedali: the lower part of the tree trunk</term> of the aforementioned plants always varies, because nature is infinitely variable.

### *On painted wind.* Chapter CCCXLVI.

In the depiction of wind, besides the bending of branches and the turning over of leaves toward the direction the wind is coming from, one must depict the clouding of fine dust mixed with the turbid air.

### *On the beginning of a rain.* Chapter CCCXLVII.

Rain falls through the air, darkening it with a glossy tint, catching the light of the sun from one side and shadow from the opposite side, as is seen to happen with mists; and the earth is darkened, for the splendor of the sun is taken from it by such rain. Things seen beyond the rain have confused and unintelligible boundaries, and those things that are closer to the eye will be more distinct. Things seen in the shaded part of the rain will be more distinct than those seen in the illuminated rain. This happens because things seen in the shaded rain lose only their principal lights, but things seen in the luminous parts lose both light and shadow, because their luminous parts mix with the luminosity of the illuminated air, and their shaded parts are brightened by that same clarity of the said illuminated air.

### *On shadows cast by bridges upon their waters.* Chapter CCCXLVIII.

The shadows of bridges will never be seen upon their waters unless the water first loses its function of mirroring on account of turbidity <note>muddiness or lack of clarity</note>. This is proven because clear water has a lustrous and polished surface, and mirrors the bridge in all the places interposed at equal angles between the eye and the bridge; it also mirrors the air under the bridge, where the shadow of such a bridge ought to be. This, turbid water cannot do, because it does not mirror, but rather receives the shadow, just as a dusty road would.

### *Precepts of painting.* Chapter CCCXLIX.

Perspective is the bridle and the rudder of painting.
The size of the painted figure should show at what distance it is seen.
If you see a figure at life-size, know that it will show itself to be near the eye.

### *Precepts.* Chapter CCCL.

The balance <note>center of gravity</note> is always in the central line of the chest from the navel upwards; thus, one must take into account the accidental weight of a man as well as his natural weight. This is demonstrated in the extending of an arm, where the fist placed at its extremity performs the same function seen in a counterweight placed at the end of a steelyard <note>a type of balance scale</note>; whereby of necessity as much weight is thrown beyond the navel as the accidental weight of the fist, and the heel must consequently be raised.

<summary>Leonardo discusses the atmospheric effects of wind and rain on visibility and light, the conditions required for water to show shadows versus reflections, and the mechanical principles of human balance in art.</summary>
<keywords>atmospheric perspective, painting rain, reflections in water, center of gravity, artistic precepts, Leonardo da Vinci, optics</keywords>
