<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

## *On the statue.* CHAPTER CCCLI.

**I**f you wish to make a figure of marble, first make one of clay. Once it is finished and dry, place it in a box large enough to eventually receive the marble block you wish to carve in the likeness of that clay model. After placing the clay figure inside this box, take small rods that fit exactly through holes in the box. Push them through each hole until each white rod touches the figure at various points. Tint the part of the rods remaining outside the box with black, and mark each rod and its corresponding hole so they can be matched at will. Then, remove the clay figure from the box and place your piece of marble in it. Remove enough from the marble so that all your rods are hidden up to their marks in said holes. To do this better, arrange it so the entire box can be lifted up while the bottom remains under the marble; in this way, you can use your carving tools with great ease. <note>Leonardo is describing a "pointing machine," a mechanical method for accurately transferring the dimensions of a model to a block of stone.</note>

<margin>*</margin>
## *On making a painting with eternal varnish.* CHAPTER CCCLII.

**P**aint your picture upon delicate, flat paper stretched on a frame. Then apply a good, thick priming of pitch and well-pounded brick. Afterward, apply a priming of white lead and <term>giallolino: a lead-based yellow pigment, often called Naples Yellow</term>. Then color it, and varnish it with old oil that is clear and thick, and fix it flatly to the glass. But it is better to make a panel of well-glazed clay, with a priming of white lead and yellow; then color and varnish it. Then attach the crystal glass to it with very clear varnish. However, first let the coloring dry well in a dark stove <note>A heated drying chamber</note>, and then varnish it with nut oil and amber, or nut oil thickened in the sun.

<margin>*</margin>
## *Method of coloring on canvas.* CHAPTER CCCLIII.

**P**lace your canvas in a frame and treat it with weak glue. Let it dry, draw your design, and apply the <term>incarnatione: flesh tones</term> with bristle brushes. While it is still fresh, you shall create the <term>sfumato: the "smoky" blending of tones without harsh outlines</term> shadows as you like. The flesh tone will be made of white lead, <term>lacca: a red lake pigment derived from organic dyes</term>, and yellow. The shadow will be black, <term>majorica: likely a dark earth or burnt pigment</term>, and a little red lake, or if you prefer, <term>lapis duro: a hard stone or mineral pigment</term>. Once you have blended it, let it dry. Then retouch it when dry with red lake and gum <note>gum arabic</note> that has been liquid for a long time in gum-water; this is better because it performs its function without becoming glossy.

**F**urthermore, to make the shadows darker, take the aforementioned gummed red lake and ink. With this shadow, you can shade many colors because it is transparent. You can shade blue, red lake, and various other shadows; I say this because you can shade various highlights with simple gummed red lake over the lake without tempera, or over <term>cinabro: cinnabar or vermilion</term> that is tempered and dry.

## *Precept of perspective in painting.* CHAPTER CCCLIV.

**W**hen you do not perceive a variety of brightness or darkness within the air, then the perspective of shadows should be excluded from your imitation. You must then rely only on the perspective of the diminution of bodies, the diminution of colors, and the loss of definition of things to the eye con- <note>The text cuts off mid-word "con-", likely leading to "concerning distance" on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page details a mechanical method for transferring clay models to marble and provides specific recipes for durable "eternal" paintings on glass and canvas, emphasizing the use of transparent glazes and sfumato.</summary>
<keywords>sculpture techniques, pointing machine, varnish, flesh tones, sfumato, pigments, red lake, perspective of diminution</keywords>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>
<sig>O iij</sig>

interposed: and this makes a thing appear more remote, namely the loss of the recognition of the shape of any object.

The eye will never, through linear perspective <note>original: "prospettiua lineale"</note> and without its own movement, have knowledge of the distance between one object and another, except by means of the perspective of colors <note>original: "prospettiua de' colori," also known as atmospheric or aerial perspective</note>.

### *On objects. Chapter CCCLV.*

**T**hat part of the object will be more illuminated which is closer to the light source that illuminates it.

**T**he likeness of things at every degree of distance loses degrees of power; that is, the more remote the thing is from the eye, the less its likeness will be able to penetrate through the air.

### *On the diminution of colors and bodies. Chapter CCCLVI.*

**L**et the diminution of the quality of colors be observed together with the diminution of the bodies to which they are applied.

### *On the interposition of transparent bodies between the eye and the object. Chapter CCCLVII.*

**T**he greater the transparent interposition between the eye and the object, the more the color of the object changes into the color of the interposed transparent medium.

**W**hen the object is placed between the eye and the light, along the central line that extends between the center of the light and the eye, then such an object shall be totally deprived of light.

### *On the cloths that clothe figures, and their folds. Chapter CCCLVIII.*

**T**he cloths that clothe figures ought to have their folds arranged to encircle the limbs they cover, in such a way that in the illuminated parts no folds of dark shadow are placed, and in the shaded parts no folds of too much brightness are made. The outlines of these folds should go in some parts surrounding the limbs they cover, and not with lines that cut across the limbs, nor with shadows that sink deeper than the surface of the clothed body. In effect, the cloth should be so adapted that it does not appear uninhabited—that is, it should not look like a bundle of cloth stripped from a man, as many are seen to do. These artists fall so in love with various groupings of various folds that they fill an entire figure with them, forgetting the purpose for which such cloth is made: which is to clothe and surround the limbs with grace where they rest, and not to fill them all with winds or blown-up bladders over the illuminated reliefs of the limbs. I do not deny that one should make some beautiful drapes, but let them be made in the part of the figure where the limbs and the body gather and collect such cloth. Above all, vary the cloths in narrative paintings <note>original: "historie"</note>; for instance, making the folds of some with sharp breaks <note>original: "rotture a facciate," referring to the angular, stiff folds found in heavy fabrics</note>, which occurs in thick cloths; let other cloths have soft folds, with their turns not squared, and others twisted.

<vocab>linear perspective, perspective of colors, object, diminution of colors, cloths, folds, limbs, illuminated, luminous</vocab>

<summary>Leonardo explains how atmospheric perspective and the "diminution of colors" create the illusion of distance, and provides detailed instructions on painting drapery that respects the underlying anatomy of the human figure.</summary>
<keywords>linear perspective, aerial perspective, optics, drapery, anatomy, painting techniques, light and shadow</keywords>

<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING</header>

# TREATISE ON PAINTING

### *On the nature of the folds of drapery.* CHAPTER CCCLIX.

**M**any love the folds of the edges of drapery to have sharp, harsh, and brisk angles; others prefer angles that are almost imperceptible; others still prefer no angles at all, but in their place certain curvatures.

### *How the folds of drapery ought to be made.* CHAPTER CCCLX. <note>The original text lists this as CCCL, likely a typographical error for 360.</note>

**T**hat part of the folds which is found furthest from its constrained ends will return most to its original nature. Naturally, everything desires to maintain itself in its own state of being. Cloth, because it is of equal density and thickness on both its back and its front, desires to remain flat; hence, when it is constrained by some fold or pleat to leave that flatness, it observes the nature of the force in that part of itself where it is most constrained. You will find that the part furthest from those constraints returns most to its original nature—that is, being extended and wide. Let the example be A. B. C. for the fold of the cloth mentioned above. Let A. B. be the place where the cloth is folded and constrained. I proposed to you that the part of the cloth furthest from the constrained ends would return most to its original nature; therefore, C. being found furthest away, the fold at C. shall be wider than in any other of its parts.

---

### *How one ought to make the folds in drapery.* CHAPTER CCCLXI.

**O**ne should not give a cloth a confusion of many folds; rather, make them only where they are held by the hands or arms, and let the rest fall naturally <note>original: "lasciar cadere," literally 'let fall,' referring to the natural gravitational hang of the fabric</note>.

<summary>Leonardo explains the physical mechanics of drapery, noting that fabric naturally seeks to remain flat and that folds are widest where they are furthest from the point of tension or constraint.</summary>
<keywords>drapery, folds, physics of fabric, artistic composition, tension and release, Leonardo da Vinci, Renaissance painting theory</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>Continuing from the discussion on the physical properties of cloth and the necessity of allowing fabric to fall naturally rather than forcing artificial patterns.</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

simply, and they must be portrayed from nature; that is, if you wish to make wool cloth, use folds according to that material, and if it be silk, or fine cloth, or that of a peasant <note>original: "da villano," referring to coarse, rustic fabric</note>, go about diversifying the folds for each, and do not make garments, as many do, based on models covered with paper or thin leather <note>A common studio practice where artists draped stiffened materials over small mannequins</note>, for you would deceive yourself greatly.

### *On the folds of drapery in foreshortening. CHAPTER CCCLXII.*

**W**here the figure foreshortens <term>foreshortening: the visual compression of an object to show depth</term>, make a greater number of folds appear than where it does not foreshorten, and let its limbs be surrounded by thick folds that wind around those same limbs. And where the eye is positioned: M. N. shows the middle of each circle further from the eye than their ends. N. O. shows them straight, because it is found directly opposite. P. Q. shows them in the opposite direction.

### *On the eye that sees the folds of drapery surrounding a person. CHAPTER CCCLXIII.*

**T**he shadows placed between the folds of drapery surrounding human bodies will be darker the more they are directly opposite the eye within the concavities where such shadows are generated; and I mean this to have been said when the eye is situated between the shaded part and the luminous part of the aforementioned figure.

### *On the folds of drapery. CHAPTER CCCLXIV.*

**A**lways, the folds of drapery situated in any action of the figures must—

<summary>Leonardo advises artists to study specific fabric types from life rather than using artificial models and explains how perspective and the viewer's eye level affect the appearance of folds and shadows on the body.</summary>
<keywords>drapery, wool, silk, foreshortening, perspective, shadows, artistic observation, Leonardo da Vinci</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the visual perception of shadows within the folds of drapery and the necessity of aligning fabric movement with the figure's action.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>TREATISE ON PAINTING BY LEONARDO DA VINCI.</header>

—be good at showing the action of such a figure through its outlines, in a way that they do not give ambiguity or confusion regarding the true posture to whoever considers it. Furthermore, no fold should, by its shadow, take away from any limb; that is, the depth of the fold should not appear to go deeper than the surface of the clothed limb. And if you depict figures dressed in multiple garments, it should not appear that the outermost garment encloses only the simple bones of such figures, but rather the flesh together with them, and the cloths as a covering for the flesh, with as much thickness as is required by the multiplication of its layers.

**T**he folds of the cloths that surround the limbs must decrease in their thickness toward the extremities of the part they surround.

**T**he length of the folds that are tighter to the limbs must wrinkle on that side where the limb diminishes due to its bending, and stretch on the opposite side of its bend.

### On the horizon reflected in the waves. CHAPTER CCCLXV.

**T**he horizon will be mirrored, according to the sixth [rule] of this [treatise], on the side seen by the horizon and by the eye, as is demonstrated by the horizon F. seen from the side B. C., which side is also seen by the eye. Therefore you, painter, who have to depict the flooding of waters, remember that the color of the water will not be seen by you to be any lighter or darker than the lightness or darkness of the site where you are, mixed together with the color of the other things that are behind you.

<summary>Leonardo provides technical instructions on ensuring drapery reflects the underlying anatomy and thickness of the body, followed by a rule on how the horizon and surroundings are reflected in moving water.</summary>

# LEON BATTISTA

# ALBERTI

## ON PAINTING.

## TO MY MOST ILLUSTRIOUS AND MOST REVERED LORD, LORD
# CHARLES ERRARD
## PAINTER TO THE MOST CHRISTIAN KING.

->MOST ILLUSTRIOUS LORD,<-

D edications are always well employed, and are especially suited to those who profess the same arts and sciences taught in the books themselves; for if they value their own virtue and reputation, it follows that they must also prize the gift being made to them. Furthermore, being of sufficient strength to resist the slanders of the envious and the quibbles of the ignorant, they preserve the dedicated works from every enchanting and venomous tongue by the mere character of their names. Driven by these considerations, but much more by the desire I have always had to reveal, through some public act, the great affection of my service toward Your Lordship, I offer you these two treatises, revised and almost resurrected by me. To these I have added the life of their author, <term>Leon Battista Alberti</term> <note>A foundational Renaissance humanist, architect, and principal art theorist (1404–1472).</note>, whom you deemed worthy to accompany his countryman <term>Leonardo da Vinci</term> <note>The famous High Renaissance polymath (1452–1519); the translator is linking these two giants of art theory.</note> even to the royal throne of the most virtuous Queen of <term>Sweden</term> <note>Refers to Queen Christina of Sweden (1626–1689), a famed patron of the arts and philosophy who abdicated and moved to Rome.</note>. Both writers owe much to the—

<summary>This page contains the opening of the formal dedication to Charles Errard, painter to the King of France, linking the work of Alberti with that of Leonardo da Vinci under the patronage of the Queen of Sweden.</summary>
<keywords>Charles Errard, Leon Battista Alberti, Leonardo da Vinci, Queen Christina of Sweden, art theory, dedication, Renaissance treatises</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication to Charles Errard by Raphael Trichet du Fresne, following the previous page's introduction of the treatises by Alberti and Leonardo.</meta>

care and labors of Your Lordship, who, in order to make them appear more magnificent before such a great Princess <note>Referring again to Queen Christina of Sweden.</note>, wished to embroider them from head to foot with that number of rare ornaments that are now seen. We expect from you on other occasions more solid things of greater importance; for, because of your profound and universal knowledge of all parts of <term>design</term> <note>original: "disegno" — a term encompassing both the physical act of drawing and the intellectual concept of artistic structure.</note>, the abundance and richness of your <term>inventions</term> <note>original: "inventioni" — the creative capacity to devise new subjects and compositions.</note>, and that <term>silent eloquence</term> <note>original: "muta eloquenza" — a classic rhetorical trope describing painting as "silent poetry."</note> with which you so perfectly express the internal movements of the soul, we know that nothing is impossible for your hand. Meanwhile, I kiss your hands a thousand times.

->Of Your Most Illustrious Lordship,<-

# L I F E

## OF LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI

### DESCRIBED BY RAPHAEL DU FRESNE.

**H**ow ancient, noble, and powerful the Alberti family was in Florence is attested to by history. Scipione Ammirato, who for certain reasons wished to elevate the name of the Concini family, found no more beautiful or expedient device than to place them in company with the Alberti, assigning one and the same origin to both families. For us, it will suffice to say that in 1304 they were already of great authority in Florence, and that they favored the faction of the **Whites** <note>The "Bianchi" or White Guelphs, a political faction in Florence famously including Dante Alighieri, who favored more independence from Papal influence.</note>. In the year 1384, during the festivals held in Florence for the acquisition of Arezzo, the displays and pageantry of the Alberti were of such magnificence that they seemed more appropriate for any great prince than for private individuals. In the annals of the Republic, it is found that the Alberti held the office of <term>Gonfalonier</term> <note>original: "gonfalonerato" — the "Standard-bearer of Justice," the highest executive office in the Florentine government.</note> nine times, which was the supreme rank in honor and power to which Florentines could aspire. But in the frequent storms and upheavals of public affairs, they did not always have favorable fortune. In the year 1387, Cipriano and Benedetto degli Alberti were driven from their homeland, and later, in the year 1411, even the children of that house were banished. But in 1428, the ban was lifted, and an order was given that everyone could come and stay freely in Florence. The aforementioned Cipriano was the father of Alberto, Lorenzo, and Giovanni. Alberto Alberti was first a canon, and then in the year 1437, Bishop of Camerino. Pope Eugenius—who was welcomed by the Florentines into their city with such pomp and demonstration of affection—having tested the virtues of that prelate during the time he celebrated the Council there, honored him with the cardinalate as a sign of gratitude toward his nation and as a reward due to his merits. Lorenzo, brother of Alberto, left several sons: Bernardo, Carlo, and Leon Battista, whose rare qualities will provide ample material for praise in this brief discourse. With what care and discipline these brothers were raised in their youth by their father is read in the treatise that Leon Battista himself wrote *On the Advantages and Disadvantages of Letters* <note>original: "De commodis litterarum atque incommodis," one of Alberti's early humanist works.</note>, where he recounts that all the hours of the day were distributed among their various studies in such a manner that

<summary>This page begins the biography of Leon Battista Alberti, detailing his family's noble lineage in Florence, their political history, and his father Lorenzo's dedication to the education of Leon Battista and his brothers.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, Alberti family, Florence, Scipione Ammirato, White Guelphs, Gonfalonier, exile, Pope Eugenius IV, humanist education</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>The previous page established the noble Florentine lineage of the Alberti family and their political exile. This page transitions to the personal education and early intellectual triumphs of Leon Battista Alberti, specifically his literary forgery and his participation in the Platonic circles of the Medici.</meta>

->LIFE<-

they never remained idle. Having reached a more mature age, in addition to the study of letters, Carlo took up the care of domestic business, but Leon Battista, taking no account of anything but books, gave himself entirely to the cultivation of his intellect. He made such progress in the sciences that he left behind all the men of talent who lived with fame in his time.

The first proof he gave of the vivacity and sharpness of his genius was in deceiving the judgment of the learned men of his time with a scholarly and ingenious prank—and with more success than Sigonio <note>Carlo Sigonio (c. 1524–1584), a famous humanist scholar who also attempted to pass off his own work as an ancient text by Cicero.</note> later achieved. While he was twenty years old at the University of Bologna, he secretly composed a play called *Philodoxios* under the name of "Lepidus the Comedian." He later published it as an ancient work, as if it had newly come into his hands from an old manuscript. And truly, Alberti imitated the ancient style of the Latin comic playwrights with such felicity that when it came into the hands of Aldo Manuzio <note>The famous Venetian printer and scholar, known for his "Aldine" editions of classics.</note>—who was held by everyone as the standard for true and purest Latinity—he had it printed in Lucca in the year 1588, dedicating it to Ascanio Persio (himself a person of profound erudition), as if it were the work of an ancient writer:
> I send to you, most learned Persio, this charming play by Lepidus, an ancient comedian, whoever he may be; since it came into my hands, I did not wish it to perish, and I felt that regard should be shown for its antiquity. There are many things in it worthy of observation which will not displease you, a most skillful seeker of all antiquity, as they certainly pleased me, etc. <note>original Latin: "Lepidam Lepidi, antiqui comici... mihi certè cum placuerint, &c."</note>

But that Alberti composed this play in the twentieth year of his age, he himself wished to indicate in the prologue:
> I do not indeed desire, nor do I seek to be praised, because at this twentieth year of my age I have written this play somewhat foolishly. But I expect to instill this persuasion in you: that I have not spent my years in idleness, nor entirely without care. <note>original Latin: "Non quidem cupio... non exundique incure meos obiuisse annos."</note>

Alberti having thus tested his strengths at that age, there was no science that he did not acquire through study, not letting a single day pass without reading or composing something, as he himself asserts. He possessed a genius so versatile that he seemed equally born for every sort of discipline; nor is it known whether he was a better orator or poet, whether a more excellent writer in Latin or Tuscan <note>The Italian vernacular.</note>, whether he was more capable in the practical or speculative sciences, or whether he reasoned with more gravity on elevated matters or with more grace and urbanity on ordinary and lowly ones.

One reads that once Lorenzo de' Medici, the true Maecenas <note>A term for a generous patron of the arts, named after the advisor to the Roman Emperor Augustus.</note> of his century, in order to pass the great heat of summer with less discomfort, held a gathering of illustrious figures in every branch of literature in the forest of Camaldoli. Among these, Marsilio Ficino, Donato Acciaioli, Leon Battista Alberti, Alamanno Rinuccini, and Cristoforo Landino were the principals. Everyone can imagine what the conversation of such learned people was like. But more than any other, Alberti made himself admired; with elevated discourses full of sublime doctrine, he showed fully that within the *Aeneid*, beneath the skin of various and charming fictions, the highest secrets of philosophy were hidden, and that Virgil was a true and real philosopher, but dressed fantastically as a poet. Such solid reasonings made such an impression on the minds of the listeners that Cristoforo Landino (who on that occasion chose to be the secretary of the assembly) recorded them all in a book, and

<summary>This page details Leon Battista Alberti's early intellectual brilliance, specifically his successful literary forgery of a Latin play and his influential philosophical interpretations of Virgil's Aeneid within the Medici circle.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, Philodoxios, literary forgery, Aldo Manuzio, Lorenzo de' Medici, Camaldoli, Marsilio Ficino, Cristoforo Landino, Virgil, Aeneid, humanism</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion of Alberti's early literary success with the play Philodoxios, this page explores his broader literary output and his transition from a man of letters to a master of architecture and the arts.</meta>

# BY LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI.

...he then formed from it that work which is seen printed in the Latin language under the name of *Camaldolese Disputations*: in which, toward the end, Landino <note>Cristoforo Landino (1424–1498), a central figure of the Florentine Renaissance and tutor to the Medici.</note> writes thus: *These are the things which, out of many and far more excellent matters, Leon Battista Alberti discussed from memory, clearly and copiously, in the assembly of such great men, which I wished to record.* <note>original: "Hæc sunt quæ de plurimis longeque eccellentioribus, quæ Leo Baptista Albertus memoriter, dilucide, ac copiose, in tantorum virorum concessu disputauit, meminisse volui."</note>

Alberti left many beautiful compositions in Latin and in Tuscan <note>The dialect of Florence, which became the basis for modern Italian.</note>, of which a copious index will be seen below. Among the Latin works, eternal praise is deserved by—and it can be compared with all of antiquity—the one titled *Momus*, which for its excellence was printed twice in Rome in the same year, 1520. And truly in that work, with extraordinary charm and unexpected skill, joking, laughing, and jesting, he explains in four books those things which others wrote of moral philosophy in grave and severe manners; he primarily proposed, however, to touch upon those things expected to form a perfect and excellent prince, and to understand the customs of those who surround him. Also beautiful is the short work called *Trivia*, or *On the Causes Pertaining to Senators*, and that which he titled *De Iure*, that is, *On the Administration of Justice*. I do not know for what reason Cosimo Bartoli <note>A diplomat and mathematician who translated Alberti’s works into the vernacular to make them accessible to craftsmen.</note>, who translated many of Leon Battista Alberti’s pamphlets into the Italian language and had them printed in one volume, made them the fifth and sixth books of *Momus*, or *Of the Prince*. He wrote a little book of fables, in which it is said that in the whimsy of his concepts he surpassed Aesop. He also composed a treatise on the life and habits of his dog, and another on the fly, being able to jest about significant and grave things in a skillful manner, and to philosophize about the low and abject. In the Italian language, he left three books on *Economy*, and some amorous things in prose and verse; he was the first (as Giorgio Vasari writes in his *Life*) who attempted to reduce vernacular verses to the meter of the Latins, as is seen in that epistle of his:

->This most extreme miserable epistle I send<-
->To you who miserably despise us, etc.<-

But in speaking of the singular genius of Alberti in every genre of polite letters, and of the place he holds among men of letters, I feel pulled by people of another profession—that is, by painters and architects—who claim him as their own. Showing me how much he achieved in painting and architecture, they call me back; as if I were to write the virtues of another Alberti, they force me to make the passage from the speculative sciences to the practical and mechanical arts. And truly, such was the capacity and vastness of our Alberti’s intellect that he could not only embrace all disciplines with general knowledge, but also descend to the particulars of each. Applying himself to whatever thing, he made men believe that his most noble intellect had never been employed in anything else, equaling or even advancing beyond those who were esteemed the best in that profession. In his time, the study of architecture was entirely extinguished, or if some knowledge of it was held, it was so corrupt and far from the refinement and nobility of the ancient Roman century that it produced crude effects in practice. Leon Battista Alberti was the first who attempted to reduce that art to its original purity, and by chasing away the barbarism of the Gothic centuries, introduced order and proportion into it, so that he was universally called by everyone the—

<summary>This page details Alberti's diverse literary contributions, including his satirical work Momus and his fables, before transitioning to his revolutionary impact on architecture and the restoration of classical Roman standards.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, Cristoforo Landino, Momus, Cosimo Bartoli, Giorgio Vasari, architecture, Gothic vs. Roman style, Renaissance humanism, Italian vernacular poetry</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>Continuing the biography of Leon Battista Alberti, transitioning from his literary achievements to his career as an architect under the patronage of Popes and Princes.</meta>

# LIFE

*The Florentine Vitruvius <note>A nickname for Leon Battista Alberti, comparing him to the ancient Roman architect Vitruvius.</note>. The fame of his name induced Pope Nicholas V to make use of him in the ordering of many buildings in Rome, and to trust so much more in his advice, as he was particularly informed of his rare qualities by Biondo of Forlì <note>Flavio Biondo (1392–1463), a famous humanist and historian.</note>, a personage of high merit and his close associate.*

*He created for Sigismondo Pandolfo Malatesta, Lord of Rimini, the design for the church of St. Francis <note>Now known as the Tempio Malatestiano.</note>, which was begun in the year 1447 and turned out to be one of the most superb and sumptuous in Italy. It was brought to the state seen today in the year 1550. And because Vasari <note>Giorgio Vasari, the famous biographer of Italian artists.</note>, who is often quite prolix on matters of minor importance, showed himself very sparse with words in the description of this temple—even though during his stay in Rimini, where he painted the St. Francis seen on the high altar of said building, he could have minutely observed all its parts—we, to partially supply for his negligence and to further honor the memory of the architect, shall write what seemed to us worthy of observation after considering it many times. And beginning from the facade, we say that one sees a most beautiful base, entirely of Istrian marble <note>A dense, white limestone from the Istrian peninsula, prized for its durability.</note>, which runs around the entire building and has as its crowning a fine ornament of foliage and Malatesta coats of arms, intertwined together with charming invention. Above this rise four fluted columns of the Composite order <note>A Roman architectural order combining Ionic and Corinthian elements.</note> in half-relief. The three interstices are occupied by three niches, of which the middle one forms the main door, which goes somewhat inward with a most beautiful foliage pattern. Then follows the architrave, the frieze, and the cornice, above which, opposite the door, there were to be two pilasters of the same order with a niche in the middle; if this had been completed, it would have served to give light to the central nave and to house the statue of the Lord. On the exterior side of the temple, with superb and noble invention, seven large arches are seen, and beneath them as many sepulchers, made specifically to serve as the resting places of illustrious men of Rimini. The interior part of the building yields nothing to the exterior, neither in the greatness of the design nor in the delicacy of the ornaments, which, although they have a certain touch of the Gothic, are nevertheless not without praise if one considers the rudeness of that century. Marbles of various sorts, both inside and out, have been used with profusion; and one reads in the life of Sigismondo that as he passed with his people near Ravenna, he took the opportunity to strip the ancient churches of St. Severus and Classis <note>The Basilica of Sant'Apollinare in Classe.</note>, removing the encrustations and bringing to Rimini everything that seemed most suitable to complete his work, to such a point that he was deservedly blamed by Pius II and called sacrilegious.* <note>Pope Pius II famously excommunicated Sigismondo Malatesta, partly for his perceived paganism and "theft" of church materials.</note> *In one of the chapels, of which there are six, are seen the quite beautiful and rich tombs of Sigismondo and Isotta his wife, and above one (as Vasari writes) is the portrait of said Lord, and in another part of the same work that of Leon Battista.*

*In the year 1451 <note>The text says 1551, likely a printer's error for 1451, as Alberti died in 1472.</note>, Ludovico Gonzaga, Marquess of Mantua, who was devotedly affectionate toward the Annunziata of Florence because of a vow made to her by his consort for a successful childbirth, had the choir—or tribune—that is presently seen in that church built according to the design of Leon Battista, with the arms of the Gonzaga family around it. Just as this bears witness to the magnificence of that Lord, so it shows the value of the architect, who with a whimsical and very difficult manner ordered that building in the guise of a round temple with nine chapels around it. And because there are certain things that do not respond to the eye with total...*

<summary>This page describes Leon Battista Alberti's architectural commissions for Pope Nicholas V, Sigismondo Malatesta in Rimini, and Ludovico Gonzaga in Florence, highlighting the Tempio Malatestiano's construction and materials.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, Tempio Malatestiano, Sigismondo Pandolfo Malatesta, Rimini, Sant'Annunziata Florence, Ludovico Gonzaga, Renaissance architecture, Istrian marble, Pope Nicholas V</keywords>

--- Page 187 ---
<meta>Continuing the description of Alberti's architectural works, specifically the Tempio Malatestiano in Rimini, before moving to his projects in Mantua and Florence.</meta>

# BY LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI.

that grace which is required—it appearing, due to the curve of the structure, that the arches of the chapels fall backward when viewed in profile—we refer the reader to what *Vasari* writes of it.

The same Marquis <note>Ludovico III Gonzaga, Marquis of Mantua.</note>, wishing to rebuild from the foundations in his own city the church of *St. Andrew*, venerable for the blood of *Christ* which is preserved there <note>The Basilica of Sant'Andrea in Mantua houses a relic believed to be the Precious Blood of Christ.</note>, called *Alberti* to him in the year 1472. Having signified to him the thought he had to illustrate *Mantua* with a most noble and superb temple, he had him make the model of the new temple which is seen today. It is built entirely of terracotta in the form of a *Cross*, with a single vault forming the lower part of it, placed over the main body of the church, 104 cubits <note>original: "braccia" – a traditional Italian unit of measurement, roughly 23 inches.</note> long and 40 cubits wide, without any chain of iron or wood to support it; it is entirely of the Composite order, with three large chapels on each side, and as many small ones. In the arms of the cross there are two chapels for each, opposite one another. The center of the square, where the dome is to be built, is nearly forty cubits wide. Beyond the square of the dome is the oval-shaped choir, 52 cubits long and as wide as the body of the church; this, along with the aforementioned square, was finished up to the final cornices in the year of our Savior 1600, in accordance with the ancient model by *Alberti*. The facade is divided into three doors, the largest of which is in the middle, adorned with white marbles and most beautiful foliage diligently carved; the side doors are of gray marble, also finely worked. Whoever wishes to see everything described more particularly should read *Donesmondi* in the sixth book of the *Ecclesiastical History of Mantua*, from which we have drawn what has been mentioned above. *Mario Equicola* in his *Mantuan History* teaches us that the same *Alberti* began the church of *St. Sebastian* in that same city. He had as a helper and faithful executor of his designs in *Mantua* one *Luca of Florence* <note>Luca Fancelli (c. 1430–1494).</note>, who had already worked for him in *Florence* on the construction of the choir of the *Annunziata*.

But if *Rome*, *Rimini*, and *Mantua* owe much to the industry of *Leon Battista*, his homeland feels no less obliged to his virtue, he having contributed greatly to its beauty. The facade of the church of *Santa Maria Novella* was ordered in *Florence* according to his design, and artfully adorned with a charming mixture of black and white marbles, corresponding to the greatness of the entire body of the building. To *Cosimo Rucellai* he gave the design for the palace he had built on the street called *The Vineyard* <note>The Palazzo Rucellai on Via della Vigna Nuova.</note>, and in the church of *St. Pancratius* <note>original: "S. Brancacio"</note> one sees a chapel of his invention. He did many other things which are omitted for brevity. He left very few works of painting. *Paolo Giovio*, who composed his eulogy and gave him a place among illustrious men of letters, praises the portrait he made of himself: which, at the time *Vasari* wrote, was found in the house of *Palla Rucellai*, with other paintings by the same *Alberti*.

It is seen, therefore, from what we have written above, that through the study of letters and the knowledge of design, *Leon Battista Alberti* can with excellent reason be registered among the famous men of both professions. Indeed, to marry them more closely together, he wished that the discourses of the one should serve to illustrate the operations of the other, making those arts speak which in the past had remained almost mute, leaving their precepts written in a most beautiful style in lan-

*as much in composition as in drawing and coloring, which are the three things to*
*which all considerations that can be made in painting are reduced. In the*

*in painting.*

# COSIMO BARTOLI
## TO THE VIRTUOUS GIORGIO VASARI
### MOST EXCELLENT PAINTER AND ARCHITECT.

*What could I say of you, my dear Master Giorgio, more than what is already said by your infinite and praiseworthy works—as much in painting as in writing and in architecture? These works speak so clearly of your praises without a tongue, that the tongues or pens of others yield to your brushes, your styluses, and your designs. And most recently, the most stupendous and praiseworthy Great Hall <note>This refers to the Salone dei Cinquecento in the Palazzo Vecchio, Florence, which Vasari extensively renovated and decorated for Duke Cosimo I de' Medici.</note> of the royal palace of their Highnesses, executed with such art and with such admirable judgment by your most refined genius, has left all men who see it so marveled that they stand for a very long time like marble statues, almost senseless, to consider it. Thus, I judge it much better to leave this wonder in the minds of the public, rather than wishing to seek with words to increase that which I would certainly diminish.*

*But I have not taken up the pen for this purpose; let men of judgment marvel at your works, and let your rivals follow you and imitate you without envy. Let the youth continually run to learn from your drawings, from your coloring, and from your teachings. And you, meanwhile, do not disdain that this little work on painting by the most virtuous Leon Battista Alberti should come forth, translated by me into this our tongue <note>The "Tuscan" or Italian vernacular.</note> under your name. I do this so that beginners in painting may take from the said little work, as they say, the first elements, and then from your marvelous works, the perfections of fine painting. Love me as you are accustomed to do, and live happily.*

<column-break/>

<note>Cosimo Bartoli (1503–1572) was a diplomat and humanist who translated Alberti's Latin works into Italian to make them accessible to practicing artists like Vasari who might not have been fluent in Latin.</note>

<summary>A formal dedication by the translator Cosimo Bartoli to the artist Giorgio Vasari, praising Vasari's work in the Palazzo Vecchio and presenting this translation of Alberti's "On Painting" as a foundational text for students.</summary>
<keywords>Cosimo Bartoli, Giorgio Vasari, Leon Battista Alberti, Renaissance art theory, Palazzo Vecchio, translation, painting, architecture</keywords>

# LEON
# BATTISTA ALBERTI
## ON PAINTING.

---

### BOOK ONE.

<insert>H</insert> **AVING** to write about painting in these very brief commentaries, so that my speech may be clearer, I will first take from the mathematicians those things which seem to me relevant to the subject. Once these are understood, I will declare (to the extent that my talent serves me) from these principles of nature what painting is. But in all my reasoning, I want it to be noted that I will speak of these things not as a mathematician, but as a painter: for mathematicians, with their intellect alone, consider the species and forms of things separated from any matter whatsoever. But because I want the thing to be placed before our eyes, I will make use in my writing of—as the saying goes—a "fatter Minerva" <note>original: "più grassa Minerva." This is a Latin idiom (crassa Minerva) meaning a more practical, down-to-earth, or common-sense approach, rather than abstract or subtle academic theory.</note>. And truly, it will seem to me that I have done enough if painters, in reading this, understand in some way this truly difficult matter, about which, as far as I have seen, no one has yet written. I ask, therefore, as a favor, that these writings of mine be interpreted not as by a pure mathematician, but as by a painter.

Therefore, one must first know that the point is a sign (so to speak) that cannot be divided into parts. I call a "sign" in this place anything that is on a surface in such a way that it can be perceived by the eye. For those things that are not perceived by the eye, there is no one who would not confess that they have nothing to do with the painter, since the painter strives to imitate only those things that can be seen by means of light.

These points, if they are placed continually one after the other in order, will extend into a line. And for us, the line will be a sign whose length can be divided into parts, but it will be so very thin that it can never be split <note>Alberti is distinguishing between the mathematical definition of a line (which has no width) and the painter's practical line, which is visible but treated as having only length.</note>.

<summary>Alberti begins his treatise by establishing a practical, painterly approach to geometry, defining the point and the line as the fundamental visible elements of art.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, On Painting, geometry for painters, definition of a point, definition of a line, visual perception, practical art theory</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical definitions of geometry as applied to painting, following the definition of a point. Alberti is establishing the foundational vocabulary for perspective.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>

# ON PAINTING

Of lines, some are straight and some are curved. A straight line is a mark drawn directly along its length from one point to another. A curved line is that which is drawn not directly from one point to another, but instead forming an arc. If many lines, like threads in a cloth, are joined together, they will create a surface. Indeed, a surface is that outermost part of a body which is considered not in terms of any depth, but only in terms of width and length, which are its proper qualities. Of these qualities, some are so inherent to the surface that, unless the surface is completely altered, they cannot be moved or separated from it in any way. And some other qualities are such that, while the face of the surface remains the same, they fall under the view in such a way that the surface appears altered to those looking at it. The permanent qualities of surfaces are two: one is truly that which comes to our knowledge by means of that outer circuit by which the surface is enclosed, which circuit some call the horizon. We, if it is permitted, by way of a certain similarity, will call it by a Latin term, *Ora* <note>original: "Ora." Meaning a border, edge, or rim.</note>, or if it pleases us more, the **Outline** <note>original: "Il d’intorno." Literally 'the around,' referring to the contour or perimeter of a shape.</note>. And this outline will be bounded by a single line or by several. By a single line, such as a circular one; by several, such as by one curved and one straight, or even by several straight lines, or several curved ones. A circular line is that which embraces and contains within itself the entire space of a circle. And the circle is a type of surface surrounded by a line in the manner of a crown. In the middle of which, if there is a point, all the rays that depart in length from this point and go directly to the crown or circumference will be equal to one another.

---

And this same point is called the center of the circle. The straight line that cuts the circumference twice and passes through the center is called by mathematicians the diameter of the circle. We shall call this same line the **centric line**. And let us be persuaded in this place by what the mathematicians say: that no line which cuts the circumference—

<vocab>straight line, curved line, surface, horizon, edge, outline, circle, center, diameter, centric line</vocab>

<summary>Alberti defines the basic geometric elements of art, including straight and curved lines, surfaces, and the properties of a circle. He introduces the concept of the 'outline' (d'intorno) as the defining boundary of a shape.</summary>

<keywords>geometry, lines, surface, outline, circle, center, diameter, perspective theory, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 2, Alberti is further defining the geometric properties of surfaces and angles, moving from the concept of the outline (the "d'intorno") to the classification of angles and the texture or "skin" of a surface.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<header>BOOK ONE.</header>

circumference cannot make equal angles within that circumference, except for the one that touches the center.

---

But let us return to surfaces. For from those things I have said above, it can be easily understood how, by changing the drawing of the outer lines, or of the **outline** <note>original: "d'intorno"</note> of a surface, that surface immediately loses its name and its original face; and that which perhaps was called triangular may now be called quadrangular, or perhaps many-angled. The outline will be called changed every time the lines or the angles are made not only more numerous, but more obtuse, or longer, or more acute, or shorter. This passage warns us that something should be said regarding angles. Truly, the angle is that which is made by two lines that intersect together upon the extremity of a surface. There are three kinds of angles: **right** <note>original: "a squadra," literally 'at the square.'</note>, **acute** <note>original: "sotto squadra," literally 'under the square.'</note>, and **obtuse** <note>original: "sopra squadra," literally 'above the square.'</note>. The right angle—or we might say the straight angle—is one of those four angles made by two straight lines that mutually intersect each other in such a way that it is equal to any of the other three that remain. And from this it follows that they say all right angles are equal to one another.

An **obtuse angle** <note>original: "sopra squadra"</note> is that which is greater than a right angle.
An **acute angle** <note>original: "sotto squadra"</note> is that which is smaller than a right angle.

Let us return again to the surface. We have said in what manner, by means of an outline, a quality was impressed upon the surface; it remains for us to speak of the other quality of surfaces, which is (so to speak) almost like a **skin** stretched over the entire face of the surface. And this is divided into three types. For some are flat and uniform, others are spherical and swollen, and others are hollowed and concave. To these, let a fourth be added: those surfaces composed of the aforementioned types. We shall treat these later; let us now speak of the first.

A **flat surface** is that upon which, if a ruler is placed, it touches

    The spherical surface imitates the **outline** <note>original: "d'intorno"</note> of a sphere. They say the sphere is
a round body, movable in every direction, in the middle of which is a point,
from which all the outermost parts of said body are equally distant.
    The concave surface is that which has its extremity on the inside,
which is beneath, so to speak, the "skin" of the sphere, such as are the inner sur-
faces inside the shells of eggs.
    But the composite surface is that which has one part of itself flat, and
the other either concave or round, such as are the inner surfaces of reeds, or
the outer surfaces of columns, or of pyramids.

    Therefore, the qualities that are found to be either in the circuit or in the faces
of the surfaces have imposed different names, as was said, upon the surfaces.
But the qualities which, without the surface being altered, vary their appearances, are
likewise two: because when the place or the lights are changed, they appear va-
ried to those who look at them. We shall speak of the place first, and then of the lights. And
it is certainly necessary first to consider in what way, when the place is changed, those
qualities that are in the surface appear to be changed. These things ve-
rily belong to the force and power of the eyes: because it is of ne-
cessity that the outlines, either by moving away or by changing position, appear to us either smaller, or
larger, or entirely dissimilar to how they first appeared to us. Or likewise-
ly that the surfaces appear to us either increased or diminished in color. All of which
things are those that we measure or traverse with the **visual triangle** <note>original: "squadro." Alberti uses this term to refer to the pyramid of vision or the geometric tool used to measure sight.</note>. And
how this triangle or sight is made, we shall now go on investigating. And let us be-

sual, that is, that through them the images of things are imprinted upon the sense. Because
these same rays between the eye and the surface seen, stretched by their...

by their own nature, and by a certain wonderful subtlety of theirs, they run together most
splendidly, penetrating the air and other similar rare or transparent bodies, having
light as their guide, until they encounter some dense body that is
not entirely dark; at which place, striking it with their points, they immediately stop.
But there was no small dispute among the ancients as to whether these rays issued from
the eyes or from the surface. This dispute, truly very difficult and
not necessary for our purposes, we shall set aside. And let it be permitted us to imagine
that these rays are like very fine threads bound together at one end, perfectly straight,
as if made into a bundle, and that they are received within the eye where
the sight is formed or created; and there they remain not otherwise than a trunk of
rays: from which place the tireless rays, issuing forth at length like very straight
worms, run to the surface that is opposite them. But among these
rays there is some difference, which it is well to know, for they are
different in both power and function: since some of them, touching the
outlines <note>original: "d'intorni"</note> of the surfaces, encompass all the quantities of the surface. And
these, because they go flying and barely touch the outermost parts of the
surfaces, we shall call extreme or ultimate rays.

Other rays, either received or issued from the whole face of the surface, per-

shortly below: for they are filled with the same colors and
lights with which that surface shines. And therefore we call these middle or
median rays.

---

The entire painting is a single surface, but when one wishes to paint an octagon <note>original: "ottangolo"</note> within it, the rays shown are those called median <term>median rays: rays that fall within the outline of an object, filling the interior of the visual pyramid</term>, which travel from the eye to the points within the division of the octagon.
   Among these rays, one is found that is formed in the likeness of that central line we mentioned; it can be called the centric ray, or the ray of the center, because it is positioned within the surface in such a way that it creates equal angles around itself on every side.

---

Thus we have found that there are three types of rays: the ultimate, the median, and the centric. Let us now investigate what each of these types of rays contributes to the act of seeing. First, we shall speak of the ultimate rays, then the median, and finally the centric. With the ultimate rays, quantities are perceived; and quantity is truly that space between two separate points on the outline <note>original: "d'intorno"</note> which passes through the su—

He begins to explain how these rays allow the eye to perceive the "quantity" or dimensions of an object's surface.</summary>
<keywords>linear perspective, visual pyramid, centric ray, geometry of vision, Leon Battista Alberti, optics in art</keywords>

--- Page 199 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 6, where Alberti began explaining how the "ultimate" or "extreme" rays define the boundaries and dimensions of an object's surface.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>BOOK ONE.</header>

—perfice <note>original: "superficie"</note>, which space is grasped by the eye with these ultimate rays, almost, so to speak, as if with a pair of compasses <note>original: "seste"</note>. And there are as many quantities in a surface as there are separate points on an outline that face one another. For we, with our sight, recognize length by means of its height or lowness; width by means of the right or left side; thickness by means of the near or far; or indeed, we understand all other measurements as well, whatever they may be, solely with these ultimate rays. Hence it is customary to say that sight occurs by means of a triangle, the base of which is the quantity seen, and the sides of which are those same rays that exit from the points of the quantity and come all the way to the eye. And it is a most certain thing that no quantity is seen except by means of this triangle. The sides of the visual triangle are therefore manifest. But the angles in this same triangle are two—namely, both those at the ends of the quantity. But the third and principal angle is that which is formed in the eye opposite the base.

---

->A B C. can be called the pyramid.<-

---

Nor is it necessary in this place to dispute whether sight itself rests, as they say, in the junction of the inner nerve <note>A reference to the optic nerve and the "sensus communis" or common sense where medieval science believed images were processed</note>, or whether the images are formed on the surface of the eye itself, almost as if in a living mirror. But one should not recount in this place all the functions of the eyes regarding seeing, since it will be enough to put briefly into these commentaries those things that seem necessary to us. The principal visual angle consisting, therefore, in the eye, this rule has been derived from it: namely, that the more acute <term>acute: a sharp angle less than 90 degrees</term> the angle in the eye is, the smaller the seen quantity will appear to us. From this it is clearly seen why it happens that from a long distance, the seen quantity seems to thin out, as if it were coming to a point. But even though things are in this manner, it nevertheless happens with some surfaces that the closer the eye of the viewer approaches them, the smaller they seem to him; and the further the eye moves away from them, the larger they seem to him.

<vocab>surface, rays, triangle, visual, pyramid, eye, perspective, length, height, thickness</vocab>

<summary>Alberti explains the "visual triangle" (or pyramid), asserting that we perceive dimensions like length and thickness through the angles formed by light rays entering the eye. He establishes the fundamental rule of perspective: that the size of an object appears to change based on the acuteness of the angle at the eye.</summary>
<keywords>visual pyramid, geometry of vision, optics, perspective theory, visual angle, Leon Battista Alberti, Renaissance art theory</keywords>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>
<meta>catchword: for</meta>

that part of the surface: which is seen clearly in spherical surfaces.
Therefore, quantities appear either larger or smaller to the viewer depending on the distance. Whoever understands the reason for this will not doubt for a moment that the middle rays sometimes become the ultimate rays, and the ultimate rays, when the distance is changed, become middle rays. And for this reason, one must know that when the middle rays have become ultimate, the quantities will immediately appear smaller to him. And on the contrary, when the ultimate rays are gathered within the outline; the further they are from the outline, the larger that quantity will appear. Here, then, I wish to give my close friends a rule: that the more rays we occupy with our sight, the larger we should think the seen quantity is, and the fewer we occupy, the smaller. Finally, these ultimate rays, embracing part by part and universally the entire outline of a surface, circle all around that surface, almost like a moat. Whence they say that sight is achieved by means of a pyramid of rays.

It is necessary, then, to say what the pyramid is. The pyramid is a long-bodied figure, from the base of which all straight lines drawn upwards terminate in a point. The base of the pyramid is the surface seen; the sides of the pyramid are those visual rays which we call the ultimate rays. The point of the pyramid rests here within the eye, where the angles of the quantity join together: and let this suffice for the ultimate rays, from which the pyramid is made, by means of which it is seen for every reason that it matters greatly what and how great the distances are between the eye and the surface.

It remains for us to treat the middle rays. The middle rays are that multitude of rays which, surrounded by the ultimate rays, are found to be inside the pyramid. And these rays do what they say the chameleon does, and similar beasts terrified by fear, which are accustomed to take on the colors of the things closest to them so as not to be found by hunters. This is what the middle rays do. For, from their contact with the surface all the way to the point of the pyramid, finding throughout this stretch a variety of colors and lights, they become so stained by them that in whatever place you might cut them, they would project from themselves in that same place that same light and that same color with which they have been soaked. And these middle rays, by the very fact itself, it has been seen first that they fail over a long distance and cause the sight to be weaker; finally, the reason why this happens has been found. Since these same rays, and all the other visual rays, being filled and heavy with lights and colors, passing through the air—and the air itself being also filled with some thickness—it happens that because of the great part of the weight, while they run through the air, they are pulled down as if exhausted. And therefore they say rightly that the greater the distance, the darker and more blurred the surface appears.

It remains for us to treat the centric ray. We call that the centric ray which alone strikes the quantity in such a way that the equal angles on both sides correspond to the angles that are beside them. And truly,

As far as it pertains to this centric ray <note>The "centric ray" is the single ray of light that strikes the surface at a right angle; in modern perspective, we might call this the central axis of vision.</note>, it is a most certain thing that this ray, of all the rays, is the fiercest and the most vivid of all. Nor can it be denied that no quantity will ever appear larger to the sight than when the centric ray is upon it. Many things could be recounted concerning the power and the office of the centric ray, but let this one thing not be left behind: that this ray alone is fostered by all the other rays that have placed it in their midst, as if they had made a certain united congregation to favor it, so much so that it can rightly be called the head and the prince of the rays. Let us leave behind those other things that would seem to belong more to the ostentation of wit than being suitable to those things we have ordained to say; many things will yet be said of the rays more conveniently in their proper places.

The middle rays of the octagon can be called a pyramid of eight faces within a pyramid of four faces. <note>The author is describing how complex shapes are nested within the primary visual field defined by the four corners of a rectangular view.</note>

And let it suffice in this place to have recounted these things, as far as the brevity of these commentaries allows, so that there is no one who doubts that the matter stands in this way—which I believe has been sufficiently shown—namely, that when the interval is changed, and the position of the centric ray is changed, it immediately appears that the surface has been altered. For it will appear either smaller, or larger, or changed, according to the order that the lines or the angles have among themselves. Therefore, the position of the centric ray and the distance contribute greatly to the true certainty of the sight.

There is also another certain third thing, by means of which surfaces appear deformed and varied to the one who looks at them: this is the reception of lights. For it can be seen in a spherical surface and in a concave one, that if there is only one light, the surface will appear somewhat dark on one side and will appear brighter on the other. And from the same primary interval, and with the primary position of the centric ray remaining fixed, provided that the surface itself...

But this point warns us that some things ought to be said concerning lights and colors. That colors vary by means of lights is a manifest thing, since any color whatsoever does not appear to our sight in the shadow as it appears when it is placed in the rays of the lights. For the shadow shows the color obscured, and the light makes it bright and open. The philosophers <note>Likely referring to Aristotelian optics or medieval perspectivists who argued that light is the "act" of color.</note> say that nothing can be seen if it is not clothed in light and color; therefore, there is a great kinship between colors and lights in creating sight. # BOOK ONE.

colors also, gradually becoming dark, they too fail; and when the light or the illumination returns, the colors return along with it to our sight, through the power of the lights. This being the case, it will be well first to treat colors, and afterward we shall go on investigating in what way the said colors vary by means of lights. Let us set aside that philosophical dispute through which they investigate the births and the first origins of colors. For what does it matter to the painter to have known in what way color is generated from the mixing of the rare and the dense, or from that of the hot and the dry, or from that of the cold and the moist? <note>Alberti is dismissing the Aristotelian "Four Qualities" theory of physics in favor of a practical manual for artists.</note> Nor do I despise those who, philosophizing, dispute about colors in such a way that they affirm that the species of colors are seven—that is, that white and black are the two extremes, between which there is one in the middle, and that between each of these two extremes and the one in the middle, on each side there are two others; and because one of these two approaches the extreme more than the other, they place them in a way that it seems they doubt where to put them. To the painter, it is enough to know what the colors are, and in what way they have to make use of them in painting. I would not wish to be reproved by those who know more, who, while following the philosophers, say that in the nature of things there are found only two true colors, namely white and black, and that all the others are born from the mixing of these. I truly, as a painter, understand it in this way regarding colors: that through the mixing of colors, other almost infinite colors are born. But among painters, there are four true kinds of colors, just as there are also four elements <note>The classical elements: Fire, Air, Water, and Earth.</note>, from which many, many species are drawn. For there is that which seems like fire, so to speak, which is **red**; and then that of the air, which is called **blue**; that of the water is **green**; and that of the earth has the **ash-gray**. All the other colors we see are made of mixtures, no differently than it seems to us with jasper and porphyry. There are, then, four kinds of colors, from which, by means of the mixing of white and black, innumerable species are generated; for we see green leaves lose so much of their greenness little by little, until they become white. We see the same thing also in the air itself, which sometimes, having taken on the quality of some white vapor toward the horizon, returns to regain its own color little by little. Beyond this, we see this same thing in roses, some of which are sometimes so bright in color that they imitate crimson <note>original: "chermiſì"</note>, others appear the color of the cheeks of young girls, and others appear white like ivory. The color of the earth also, through the mixture of white and black, has its species. Therefore, the mixing of white does not change the kinds of colors, but generates and creates those species. And the black color similarly has the same power; for through the mixing of black, many species are generated. This is very fitting, because that color is altered by means of the shadow, where before it was seen clearly: for as the shadow increases, the brightness and whiteness of the color fails, and as the light increases, it becomes clearer and more candid. And therefore the painter can be sufficiently persuaded that white and black are not true colors, but the alterers, so to speak, of the—

colors. For the painter has found nothing more than white by which he can express that ultimate brightness of light, nor anything with which he can represent the obscurity of shadows more than with black. Add to these things that you will never find white or black in any place that does not itself fall under some genus of colors.

Let us now treat the power of lights. Lights come either from the stars—that is, from the Sun, the Moon, and the star of Venus—or from material lights and fire. And among these there is a great difference. For the lights of heaven render shadows almost equal to the bodies; but fire makes them larger than the bodies are, and the shadow is caused by the interception of the rays of light. The intercepted rays are either bent elsewhere, or they double back on themselves. They bend as when the rays of the sun strike the surface of water, and then rise up to the ceilings: and every bending of the rays is made, as the mathematicians say, with equal angles between them. But these things belong to another part of painting. The rays that bend are soaked <note>original: "s’inzuppano" - a vivid metaphor suggesting the light absorbs the color of the surface it hits.</note> in some part of that color which they find on that surface from which they are bent or reflected. And we see this happen when the faces of those walking through meadows appear green to us. I have treated, then, surfaces; I have treated rays; I have treated in what way the pyramid is made from triangles in the act of seeing. I have proved how greatly it matters that the interval, the position of the centric ray, and the reception of the lights be determined and certain. But since with a single glance we see not just one surface alone, but many surfaces at once; and since we have treated, and not poorly, each surface by itself, it now remains for us to investigate in what way many surfaces joined together present themselves to the eyes. Each surface certainly enjoys being particularly filled with its own lights and its own colors, just as was said of its own pyramid. And since bodies are covered by surfaces, all the quantities of bodies that we see, and all the surfaces, create a single pyramid, pregnant, so to speak, with as many smaller pyramids as there are surfaces which, by means of that view, are encompassed by the rays <note>original: "razzi" - an archaic spelling of "raggi" (rays).</note> of said view. And things being so, someone will perhaps say: Why does the painter need such consideration? Or what utility will it give him in painting? This is certainly done so that he may know that he is to become an excellent master whenever he knows perfectly the differences of surfaces and notices their proportions; which has been known by very few. For if they are asked what thing they seek to achieve in tinting that surface, they can answer much better to any other thing than to know how to tell the reason for what they labor to do. For which reason I pray that studious painters listen to me. For learning those things that are useful was never bad from any master you please. And let them truly learn while they circumscribe a surface with lines, and while they cover the drawn and finished places with colors, that nothing more is sought than that in this one single surface there be represented to us many...

forms of surfaces: no differently than if this surface, which they cover with colors, were as if made of glass, or some other similar transparent thing, such that through it passed the entire visual pyramid to see the real bodies, with a determined and fixed interval, and with a fixed position of the centric ray, and of the lights placed in the air distant in their places. And that this is so is demonstrated by painters when they move backward from the thing they are painting to consider it from afar; for, guided by nature, they go seeking in this way the point of that very pyramid. From which they realize that from that place they consider and judge all things better. But since this is a single surface, either of a panel or of a wall, in which the painter labors to paint many and diverse surfaces and pyramids contained by a single pyramid, it will be necessary that in some of its places, this visual pyramid be cut, so that in this place the painter, with both lines and painting, can express the outlines and the colors that the cut <note>original: "taglio" - Alberti's technical term for the "section" or "intersection" of the visual pyramid, which we now call the picture plane.</note> will give him. This being the case, those who look at the painted surface see a certain cut of the pyramid. Painting will therefore be the cut of the visual pyramid according to a determined space or interval with its center, and with determined lights, represented with lines and colors upon a given surface. Now, since we have said that painting is a cut of the pyramid, we must therefore go investigating all those things by which all the parts of such a cut may become very well known to you. We must, then, speak again of surfaces, from which it has been shown that the pyramids come which are to be cut by the painting. Of surfaces, some are lying on the ground, such as floors and the spaces of buildings; and some others there are that are equally distant from those spaces. Some surfaces are upright, such as walls, and other surfaces that have the same kinds of lines as walls: those surfaces are said to stand equally distant from each other when the distance between them is everywhere the same. Surfaces that have the same kinds of lines are those that are touched on every side by a continuous straight line, such as the surfaces of square columns that are set in a row in a loggia. These are the things that must be added to what was said above regarding surfaces. But to those things we said of rays—both the outer ones and those inside, and the centric one—and to the things recounted above regarding the visual pyramid, one must add that theorem of the mathematicians <note>Alberti is referring to Thales's theorem or the principle of similar triangles.</note> with which it is proved that if a straight line cuts the two sides of any triangle, and this cutting line is such that it ultimately makes another triangle, and is equally distant <note>meaning "parallel"</note> from the other line which is the base of the first triangle, then certainly that larger triangle will be proportional in its sides to this smaller one. This is what the mathematicians say. But we, so that our speech may be more open to painters, will explain the matter more clearly. It is necessary that we know what that thing is which we wish to call "proportional" in this place. We say that those are proportional triangles whose sides and angles have the same correspondence among themselves. For if one of the sides of the triangle is longer than the base by...

<meta>Continuing from page 13, where Alberti defined painting as a "cut" (intersection) of the visual pyramid. He now begins a geometric explanation of how different sizes of triangles remain proportional within that pyramid.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>

two and a half times, or another by three; all triangles made in this way, whether they be larger or smaller than this one, provided they have the same correspondence of the sides to the base, so to speak, will be proportional to one another. Because whatever relationship a part has to its corresponding part in the larger triangle, that same part will have to its corresponding part in the smaller one. All triangles, therefore, that are made in this way, shall be called proportional among us. And so that this may be understood more clearly, we will provide a comparison. A small man will be proportional to a very large one by means of the cubit <note>term: cubit - an ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger</note>, provided that the same proportion of the palm and the foot is maintained to measure the other parts of the body. In this man, so to speak—that is, in Evander <note>note: A hero of Roman mythology, often depicted as smaller or more humble compared to the giants</note>—the same was observed as in that other—that is, in Hercules, of whom Gellius <note>note: Aulus Gellius, a 2nd-century Roman author who wrote about Hercules' stature in his "Attic Nights"</note> said that he was of a stature much larger than all other men. Nor was there any other proportion in the limbs of Hercules than there was in the body of Antaeus the giant <note>note: A giant in Greek mythology defeated by Hercules</note>. For just as the hand corresponded in each one in proportion to the cubit, and the cubit in proportion to the head and to the other limbs with equal measure among themselves, the same will occur in our triangles; for there will be some sort of measure among the triangles, by means of which the smaller ones will correspond to the larger ones in all things, except for their size. And if these things are understood sufficiently, we determine—by the judgment of mathematicians, as much as serves our purpose—that every cut of any triangle, equally distant from the base, generates and makes a triangle similar (as they say) to their larger triangle, and (as we say) proportional. And because in all those things that are proportional to one another, their parts are also corresponding, it follows that in those things where the parts are diverse and not corresponding, they are not proportional. The parts of the visual triangle, besides the lines, are also the rays themselves, which will certainly be equal in number to the true ones when looking at the proportional quantities of the painting, and in those that are not proportional, they will not be equal. Because one of these non-proportional quantities will occupy either more rays or fewer. You have learned, therefore, in what way any smaller triangle is called proportional to the larger one, and you remember that the visual pyramid is made of triangles. Therefore, let all our reasoning that we have had regarding triangles be referred to the pyramid, and let us persuade ourselves that none of the seen quantities of the surface, which are equally distant from the cut, make any alteration in the painting. Because they are truly quantities equally distant, proportional in every equally distant cut from their corresponding parts. This being the case, it follows that no alteration of the outlines occurs in the painting, and that the quantities are not altered, of which the field or the space is filled, and by which the outlines are measured or understood. And it is manifest that every cut of the visual pyramid, which is equally distant from the viewed surface, is likewise proportional to that viewed surface.

We have spoken of the surfaces proportional to the cut, that is, those equally distant from the painted surface. But because we will have to paint many diverse surfaces that will not be equally distant, we must...

<summary>Alberti explains the geometric principle of proportionality using the analogy of human bodies (Hercules and Antaeus) to show how a small "cut" of the visual pyramid maintains the same ratios as the larger original scene. He concludes that any cross-section parallel to the base of the pyramid remains proportional to the object being viewed.</summary>
