<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>BOOK ONE.</header>
<meta>image quality: good; catchword: lu- (leading into "luminosissima" on the next page)</meta>

We must make a more diligent investigation of these matters, so that any reason for the cut <note>term: cut - Alberti's term for the "intersection" or the picture plane that cuts through the visual pyramid</note> may be explained. And because it would be a long, very difficult, and most obscure thing to narrate everything regarding these cuts of triangles and the pyramid according to the rules of mathematicians; therefore, speaking according to our custom, we shall proceed as painters. Let us recount very briefly some things concerning quantities that are not equally distant, which once known, it will be easy for us to understand every consideration of surfaces that are not equally distant. Of those quantities, then, that are not equally distant, some are lines entirely similar to the visual rays, and some are equally distant from certain visual rays. Quantities that are entirely similar to the visual rays, because they do not form a triangle and do not occupy a number of rays, do not gain any place for themselves in the cut. But in quantities equally distant from the visual rays, the more obtuse that angle at the base of the triangle is, the fewer rays that quantity will receive, and therefore it will have less space in the cut. We have said that the surface is covered with quantities, and because it often happens in surfaces that there will be some quantity that is equally distant from the cut, while other qualities of the same surface will not be equally distant, it follows from this that only those quantities that are equally distant on the surface suffer no alteration in the painting. But those quantities that are not equally distant, the more obtuse their angle is—which will be the largest in the triangle at the base—the more alteration they will receive. Finally, to all these things one must add that opinion of the philosophers, by which they affirm that if the heavens, the stars, the seas, the mountains, and the animals themselves, and then all bodies, should become by the will of God half as small as they are now, it would happen that all these things would not appear to us to be diminished in any part from what they are now. For greatness, smallness, length, shortness, height, lowness, narrowness, and breadth, darkness, brightness, and all other such things that can be found or not found in things, the philosophers called accidents <note>term: accidents - in Aristotelian philosophy, qualities that do not belong to the essence of a thing, such as its size or color</note>; and they are of such a kind that the entire knowledge of them is achieved through comparison. *Virgil* said that *Aeneas* surpassed all other men by his shoulders. But if a comparison were made between him and *Polyphemus* <note>note: The giant Cyclops from the Odyssey</note>, he would seem to us a *Pygmy*. They say that *Euryalus* <note>note: A character in Virgil's Aeneid known for his great beauty</note> was most beautiful, who if compared to *Ganymede*—snatched away by *Jove* <note>note: Jupiter/Zeus, who took the beautiful youth Ganymede to Olympus</note>—would seem ugly. In *Spain* some girls are considered fair-skinned, who in *Germany* would be considered swarthy and black. Ivory and silver are white in color, and nonetheless if a comparison is made with swans, or with white linen cloths, they will appear somewhat more pale. For this reason, surfaces in painting appear most beautiful and most radiant to us when one sees in them that proportion from white to black that exists in the things themselves from the lights to the shadows. So all these things are learned by making comparisons. Since in making a comparison of things, there is a certain power by which one knows what is more, or less, or equal. For which reason we call "great" that thing which is larger than a smaller one, "greatest" that which is larger than the great, "luminous" that which is brighter than the dark, lu-

<summary>Alberti explains how the perception of size and quality is entirely relative, based on the philosophical concept of "accidents" and the power of comparison. He argues that painting relies on these proportional relationships—such as light to shadow—rather than absolute measurements.</summary>
<keywords>visual rays, perspective, proportionality, accidents, relativity of size, Aeneas, Polyphemus, light and shadow, comparison</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 15, where Alberti explained how the "cut" (the picture plane) captures visual rays. He now moves from the geometry of vision to the practical application of scale and the invention of the "perspective window."</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>
<meta>catchword: Who (referring to the next page's start)</meta>

...most luminous is that which is clearer than the bright. And truly, the comparison of things is made against those things that are already most manifest to us. But since man is the most known of all things to man, perhaps Protagoras <note>Protagoras of Abdera (c. 490–420 BC), a Pre-Socratic philosopher famous for the dictum "Man is the measure of all things."</note> said that man was the model and the measure of all things; and he meant by this that the accidents <note>term: accidents - in Aristotelian philosophy, these are non-essential qualities like size, color, or position that can change without changing the essence of the thing.</note> of all things could be well known and compared with the accidents of man. These things instruct us in this: that we understand that whatever sort of bodies we depict in a painting, they will appear large or small according to the measure of the men who are painted therein. And I seem to see that Timanthes <note>A celebrated Greek painter of the 4th century BC.</note> understood this power of comparison much more excellently than any other of the ancients; for this painter, depicting the sleeping Cyclops <note>The one-eyed giant of Greek myth.</note> on a small panel, painted Satyrs <note>Mythological forest spirits, usually depicted as small and mischievous.</note> next to him who were embracing the sleeper's thumb, so that by means of the measure of the Satyrs, the one who was sleeping appeared infinitely larger.

Up to this point, we have said almost all those things that pertain to the power of sight and to knowing the cut; but because it serves our purpose to know not only what the cut is and of what things it consists, but also how it is actually made, it remains for us to say with what art this cut is expressed in painting. Of this, therefore (leaving other things aside), I will recount what I do while I am painting. The first thing, when painting a surface, I draw a quadrangle of right angles, as large as pleases me, which serves me as an open window through which the story <note>term: historia - for Alberti, this is the "narrative" or "history," the highest form of painting involving multiple figures in significant action.</note> is to be seen; and there I determine the sizes of the men I wish to make in the painting, and I divide the height of this man into three parts; which to me are proportional to that measure which the common people call the *braccio* <note>term: braccio - literally "arm," a traditional Italian unit of measurement roughly equal to 23 inches or 58 cm.</note>. For he is three *braccia* tall, as is clearly seen from the proportion of the members of man, because such is the common length for the most part of the human body. With this measure, therefore, I divide the bottom line that lies at the base of the drawn quadrangle, and I see how many of such parts fit into it. And this same base line of the quadrangle is proportional to me to the nearest transverse quantity seen at an equal distance on the floor. After this, I place a single point where the sight must run within the quadrangle; which point occupies that place where the centric ray <note>term: centric ray - the single visual ray that strikes the picture plane at a right angle, connecting the eye directly to the vanishing point.</note> must arrive, and therefore I call it the point of the center <note>note: This is the first formal description of the "vanishing point" in art history.</note>. This point shall be placed appropriately, no higher from the base line than the height of the man who is to be painted there; for in this way, both those who look at it and the things painted appear to be on the same level plane. Having placed the point of the center, I draw straight lines from that point to each of the divisions of the base line; which lines truly demonstrate to me the way in which, having to proceed to the infinite and ultimate distance, the transverse quantities narrow in relation to my aspect and sight.

<summary>Alberti explains how human scale determines the perceived size of objects and introduces his revolutionary method for linear perspective: the "perspective window" and the vanishing point.</summary>
<keywords>Protagoras, Timanthes, Cyclops, Satyrs, braccio, quadrangle, historia, centric ray, center point, Leon Battista Alberti, perspective, vanishing point</keywords>

# BOOK ONE.
<page-num>17</page-num>

There are some who would draw within the <term>quadrangle</term> <note>A square or rectangular frame representing the picture plane.</note> a line equally distant from the already divided base line, and would divide the space between these two lines into three parts. Then, by this rule, they would draw another line equally distant from this second line, such that the space between the first divided line and this second line parallel to it (or equally distant) exceeds by one of its own third parts that space which is between the second and the third line. They would then add the other lines in such a way that each subsequent space between the lines would always be reduced by half <note>original: "per la metà più" - Alberti is describing a mathematical progression used by some artists to simulate depth without a true vanishing point.</note>, to speak as the <term>mathematicians</term> do. In this manner proceed those who, although they say they follow an excellent path in painting, I nonetheless think err not a little. For having placed the first parallel line at random, even if the other parallels are placed with rule and order, they have no certain or determined location for the point of the <term>pyramid</term> <note>The "visual pyramid" formed by rays of light traveling from the object to the eye.</note> from which to properly view the thing; from this, no small errors easily occur in the painting. Add to this that their rule would be very false whenever the central point <note>The vanishing point.</note> was placed either higher or lower than the height of the painted man; for all those who are knowledgeable will say that none of the things painted—if they are to conform to the truth—can be looked at or discerned unless they are placed with a certain rule at a distance from the eye. Concerning which matter...

we shall explain the reasoning, if ever we write of these demonstrations of painting, which, having already been made by us, our friends called the "miracles of painting" while they looked upon them with wonder. For all these things that I have said belong principally to that part. Let us return, then, to our purpose. These things being so, I have therefore found this excellent method. In all other things I follow the same line, and the central point, and the division of the base line, and the drawing of lines from the point to each of the divisions of the base line. But in the transverse quantities <note>The horizontal lines that recede into the distance, also called transversals.</note> I hold to this order. I have a small space in which I draw a straight line; this I divide into the same parts as the base line of the quadrangle is divided. Then I place a point high above this line, as high as the height of the central point in the quadrangle is from the divided base line, and I draw lines from this point to each division of that line. Then I determine how much distance I want there to be between the eye of the viewer and the painting, and there, having established the location of the cut with a straight vertical line <note>original: "linea ritta a piombo" — literally a "plumb line," used here to represent the intersection of the visual field.</note>, I make the cutting of all the lines that it finds. A vertical line is that which, falling upon another straight line, will cause right angles on every side.

<page-num>61</page-num>
<header>BOOK ONE.</header>

goes to the other opposite to it, which divides the quadrangle into two parts, in such a way that it makes two triangles out of said quadrangle. Having then diligently finished these things, I draw again from above another line across, equally distant from the others below, which intersects the two upright sides of the large quadrangle and passes through the central point. And this line serves me as a limit and boundary, by means of which no quantity exceeds the height of the eye of the beholder. And because it passes through the central point, let it therefore be called the **centric line**. From this it follows that those men who shall be painted between the two further parallel lines will be much smaller than those who shall be between the forward parallel lines. It is not because of this that they are smaller than the others; but, because they are further away, they appear smaller, a thing which nature truly demonstrates clearly to us to be so. For we see in churches that the heads of men walking about seem to go almost always at one same equal height, but the feet of those who are quite far away appear to us to correspond to the knees of those who are in front of us.

This entire rule for dividing the pavement belongs principally to that part of painting which we, in its proper place, shall call **composition**. And it is such that I fear that, because it is a new thing and because of the brevity of these my commentaries, it may be little understood by the reader; for, as we easily recognize through ancient works, it was not known among our ancestors because it was obscure and difficult. Consequently, among the ancients you will endure great labor to find any history <note>original: "historia" — in this context, a narrative or multi-figure scene, considered the highest form of painting.</note> that is well composed, well painted, well formed, or well sculpted. For which reason I have said these things with brevity, and as I think, not yet obscurely. But I know what and of what sort they are: that I shall not be able to acquire any praise for eloquence through them, and those who do not understand them at first sight will endure the greatest labor to ever be able to comprehend them. These things are very easy and very beautiful to the most subtle minds and those inclined toward painting, in whatever way they are told; but to coarse men, and those little suited or inclined by nature to these most noble arts, even if one spoke of them most eloquently, they would be little pleasing, and perhaps these same things recited by us very briefly without any eloquence will be read not without boredom. 

But I would wish to be forgiven if, while I have principally desired to be understood, I have attended to making my writing clear rather than composed or ornate; and those things that follow will bring, as much as I hope, less tedium to those who shall read. We have therefore treated of triangles, of the pyramid, of the cut <note>original: "taglio" — the intersection of the visual pyramid by the picture plane.</note>, and of those things that seemed to us necessary to say: of which things, nonetheless, I was accustomed to reason with my friends much more at length with a certain rule of geometry, and to show them the reasons why it happened so, which I have thought to leave behind for brevity in these my commentaries: because in this place I have recounted only the first principles of painting, and I have wished to call them the first principles because they are the first foundations of the art for painters who do not know. But they are such that those who understand them well, will recognize—

...will find that they will help them not a little, as much regarding their intellect as to understanding the definition of painting, and also regarding those things which we have yet to say. And let no one doubt that he will never become a good painter who does not excellently understand what he seeks to do in his painting. For in vain is the bow drawn if you have not first designated the place where you wish to direct the arrow. And I would certainly wish that we persuade ourselves that he alone is destined to become an excellent painter who has now learned to perfectly place all the outlines and all the qualities of the surfaces. On the contrary, I affirm that he will never succeed as a good painter who does not know exactly and most diligently the things we have discussed. And therefore, all that has been said regarding surfaces and the "cut" <note>The "cut" refers to the intersection of the visual pyramid, the geometric basis for perspective.</note> was necessary. It remains now for the painter to be instructed in the method he must hold in imitating with his hand the things he has first imagined in his mind.

# LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI
## ON PAINTING.
### BOOK TWO.

**BUT** because this study of learning may perhaps seem too laborious to the young, it therefore seems right to me to show in this place how painting is not unworthy of our every study and every diligence. For it possesses within itself a certain divine power, such that it not only does what they say friendship does—representing to us as present those who are far away—but it also sets before our eyes those who have been dead for many, many years, so that they are seen with the greatest wonder for the painter and delight for the viewer. Plutarch relates that Cassander, one of Alexander’s captains, upon seeing the image of the now-deceased Alexander and recognizing in it that royal majesty, began to tremble with his whole body. They say also that Agesilaus the Lacedaemonian <note>A King of Sparta (r. c. 399–360 BC).</note>, knowing himself to be very ugly, did not want his image to be seen by his descendants, and therefore it never pleased him to be either painted or sculpted by anyone: thus the faces of the dead live a long life, in a certain way, by means of painting. And that painting has expressed for us the gods who are revered by the nations must be thought of as a very great gift granted to mortals: for painting has greatly aided piety, through which we are principally joined to the gods, and in retaining our souls within a certain complete religion. They say that Phidias made a Jupiter <note>The colossal Statue of Zeus at Olympia, one of the Seven Wonders of the Ancient World.</note> in Elis, the beauty of which added much to the already conceived religion. But how much painting aids the most honorable pleasures of the mind, and how much ornament it brings to things, can be seen elsewhere and principally from this: that you will find almost no thing, however precious, which does not become much dearer and more prized through the accompaniment of painting.

<summary>Alberti concludes the theoretical first book on perspective and begins Book Two by praising the "divine power" of painting to make the absent present and the dead seem alive. He cites classical examples like Alexander the Great and Phidias to argue that painting enhances both religious devotion and the value of material objects.</summary>
<keywords>perspective, Book Two, divinity of art, Alexander the Great, Phidias, Jupiter, portraiture, memory, piety</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Alberti began to praise the "divine power" of painting to make the absent present and the dead seem alive.</meta>

# BOOK SECOND.
<page-num>21</page-num>

Ivory, gems, and such valued things become, by the hand of the painter, more precious. Even gold itself, when adorned by painting, is esteemed much more than gold. Indeed, even lead—the most base of all metals—if Phidias or Praxiteles <note>Famous ancient Greek sculptors; Alberti uses them here to show how artistic skill elevates raw material.</note> had made a statue of it with their hands, it would perhaps be held in higher regard than an equal amount of raw and unworked silver. Zeuxis the painter had begun to give away his works because, as he said, they could not be paid for with any price: for he judged that no price could be found that could satisfy one who, in painting or sculpting animals, was almost like another god among mortals. Painting, then, possesses these praises: that those who are masters of it not only marvel at their own works, but realize they are most similar to the gods. What shall I say? Is not painting either the mistress of all the arts, or at least their principal ornament? For the architect, if I am not mistaken, has taken from the painter alone the moldings, the capitals, the bases, the columns, the cornices, and all the other such praised parts of buildings; for the painter, by means of his rule and art, has taught and given the method to the stonecutters, the sculptors, and to all the workshops of the smiths, the carpenters, and all those who work with manual construction; such that finally no art will be found, however lowly, that does not have regard for painting, whence I will dare to say that everything that is of ornament in things is drawn from painting. But painting was honored by the ancients principally with this honor: that while almost the majority of other craftsmen were called *Fabri* <note>Latin: "smiths" or "craftsmen/laborers."</note> among the Latins, the painter alone was not numbered among the *fabbri*. These things being so, I am accustomed to say among my friends that the inventor of painting was, according to the opinion of the poets, that Narcissus who was turned into a flower. For since painting is the flower of all arts, it will well seem that the whole fable of Narcissus is most perfectly suited to this thing: for what else is painting than to embrace and capture with art that surface of the fountain? <note>Alberti famously reinterprets the myth of Narcissus falling in love with his reflection as the origin of painting—the attempt to capture a fleeting image on a surface.</note> Quintilian thought that the ancient painters were accustomed to draw shadows according to how the sun cast them, and that then the art was increased little by little with additions. There are some who relate that a certain Philocles the Egyptian, and a Cleanthes (I know not which one), were the first inventors of this art. The Egyptians affirm that painting had been in use among them six thousand years before it was transported into Greece, and our people say that it came from Greece to Italy after the victories of Marcellus in Sicily. But it does not matter much to know the first painters or the inventors of painting: since we do not wish to recount the history of painting, like Pliny, but to treat anew of the art: of which until this age there is no memory left to us (that I have seen) by the ancient writers: even though they say that Euphranor of Isthmus wrote something about measures and colors, and that Antigonus and Xenocrates wrote some things about painting, and that Apelles also put together some things about painting and sent them to Perseus. Diogenes Laertius relates that Demetrius the philosopher also wrote some commentaries on painting. Besides this, I also estimate that since [it was practiced] by our...

<summary>Alberti argues for the nobility of painting, claiming it elevates base materials and serves as the foundation for all other arts, including architecture. He famously identifies the myth of Narcissus as the poetic origin of the art and distinguishes his work as a technical manual rather than a historical chronicle like Pliny's.</summary>

# ON PAINTING
<page-num>22</page-num>

written down in times past, painting was not left behind by our Italian writers: for in Italy the Etruscans <note>The ancient civilization of central Italy, predecessors to the Romans, known for their sophisticated tomb paintings.</note> were most ancient, and more valiant than all others in the art of painting. Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Trimegisto"; a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with wisdom and the origins of art and religion.</note>, a most ancient writer, believes that painting and sculpture were born together with religion; for he spoke thus to Asclepius: "Humanity, mindful of its nature and its origin, fashioned the gods in the likeness of its own face." And who is there who would deny that painting has claimed for itself the most honored role in all things, both public and private, both secular and religious? So much so, that I shall find no artifice among mortals that is held in greater account by everyone. Almost incredible prices are recounted for painted panels. Aristides of Thebes sold a single painting for one hundred talents, which is sixty thousand florins <note>Alberti converts ancient Greek currency into the contemporary Florentine gold florin to help his readers grasp the immense value.</note>. They recount that the panel by Protogenes was the reason that Rhodes was not burned by King Demetrius, because he did not want said panel to burn. We can therefore affirm that Rhodes was ransomed from its enemies by a single painting. Many other similar things have been gathered together, besides these, through which you may easily understand that good painters have always been greatly praised and held in esteem by everyone: so much so that the most noble and excellent citizens, and philosophers, and kings, have delighted not only in painted things, but in painting itself. Lucius Manilius, a Roman citizen, and Fabius—a most noble man in Rome—were painters. Turpilius, a Roman knight, painted in Verona. Pacuvius the tragic poet, grandson of the poet Ennius by his daughter, painted Hercules in the square. Socrates, Plato, Metrodorus, and Pyrrho, all philosophers, were excellent in painting. Nero, Valentinian, and Alexander Severus, emperors, were most studious of painting. It would be a long matter to recount how many princes and how many kings have been inclined toward this most noble art.

And it is not yet reasonable to stop to recount the entire infinite multitude of ancient painters; how great it was can be seen from this: that in less than four hundred days, three hundred and sixty statues were completely finished for Demetrius Valerius <note>Demetrius of Phalerum, a statesman in Athens.</note>, son of Phanostratus—some upon their horses, some upon chariots, and some upon coaches. And if in that city the number of sculptors was so great, shall we remain in doubt that there were not infinite painters there? Truly, painting and sculpture are arts joined together by kinship, and nourished by the same intellect. But I will always prefer the intellect of the painter, as one who labors in a much more difficult thing. But let us return to the subject. Infinite was the multitude of painters and sculptors in those times, since princes and commoners, the learned and the ignorant, delighted in painting. And it being the custom among the first spoils they brought from the provinces to put panels and statues on public display in the theater, the matter went so far that Lucius Paullus <note>original: "Paolo Emilio"; the Roman general who defeated Macedon.</note> and not a few other Roman citizens had their children taught painting, along with the liberal arts, in order to live well and blessedly. This excellent custom was most greatly observed among the Greeks, so that well-bred noble and free youths learned geometry and music along with letters, and also the art of painting. Indeed, the faculty of painting was also an honorable thing—

# BOOK TWO.
<page-num>23</page-num>

honored among women. Martia, daughter of Varro <note>original: "Martia figliuola di Varrone"; likely referring to Iaia of Cyzicus, whom Varro called Martia, a famous female painter of antiquity.</note>, is celebrated by writers because she knew how to paint. And painting was certainly held in such high esteem and worthy of so much praise among the Greeks that they forbade by public decree that it should be lawful for slaves to learn painting. Nor was this truly without reason, because the art of painting is truly most worthy of liberal <note>term: "liberali" (liberal arts); those studies considered suitable for a free person, as opposed to manual or "servile" labor.</note> and most noble minds; and as for me, it has always seemed a sign of an excellent and superior intellect in anyone whom I have known to take great delight in painting. And this art alone is that which equally delights both the learned and the ignorant, a thing which never happens in any other art: that what delights those who practice it also moves those who are unlearned. You will find no one who would not easily and greatly desire to have made progress in painting. And it is manifest that Nature herself delights in painting, since we see that Nature shapes centaurs and the faces of kings with beards in marble. Indeed, they say that in a gem belonging to Pyrrhus <note>King of Epirus, known for his legendary agate.</note>, the nine Muses with their emblems were painted by Nature herself. Add to these things that there is almost no art in which men—both those who practice it and those who do not—toil with such delight throughout their whole lives in learning and exercising it, more than in this one.

Let me be permitted to say what happens to me: if it ever occurs that for my own pleasure and delight I set myself to painting, which I do very often when I have time left over from other business, I remain fixed with such pleasure in doing that work that I can hardly believe I have been there long enough for three or four hours to have already passed. Thus, this art brings delight with it while you honor it, and praise, riches, and perpetual fame while you practice it excellently. This being the case, since painting is a most excellent and ancient ornament of things, worthy of free men, pleasing to the learned and the unlearned, I encourage student youths as much as I possibly can to apply themselves greatly to painting as far as they are able.

Next, I warn those who are most studious of painting that they should pursue the learning of this perfect art of painting, sparing neither labor nor any diligence. Let it be your care, you who seek to be excellent in painting, first of all to consider what names and what fame the ancients acquired. And it will benefit you to remember that avarice has always been an enemy to praise and to virtue; for the mind intent on gain will rarely acquire the fruit of posterity. I have seen some who, almost in the beauty of their learning <note>i.e., just as they were beginning to master the art.</note>, immediately gave themselves over to gain, and therefore they subsequently acquired neither riches nor any fame. If these men had accustomed their intellect to study, they would easily have become famous, from which they would have drawn both riches and delight. Therefore, let enough be said of them up to this point.

Now let us return to the subject. We divide painting into three parts, which division we have drawn from Nature herself: for as painting strives to represent to us things seen, we consider in what way those things come to our sight. Principally, when we examine something, we see that thing to be a certain "something" that occupies space; and the painter will circumscribe the space of this place, and this method of drawing...

<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Alberti discussed the inherent delight and nobility of painting, noting how even Nature seems to practice the art through patterns in stone and gems.</meta>

->ON PAINTING<-

...the outlines, with a suitable term, he shall call **circumscription** <note>original: "circonscrittione"; the act of defining the edges of a form.</note>. After this, in looking, we consider in what way the various surfaces of the seen body join together among themselves; and the painter, by drawing these joinings of the surfaces in their proper places, may well call this **composition**. Lastly, in looking, we discern more distinctly the colors of the surfaces; and because the representation of this thing in painting almost always receives all its variations from the lights, we may conveniently call this the **reception of light**. The outlines, therefore, the composition, and the reception of light make painting perfect.

It remains for us, then, to treat these things very briefly, and first of the outlines, or circumscription, which is that drawing made with lines around the edges, called **design** <note>original: "disegno"; in this context, it refers to the foundational linear drawing or draftsmanship.</note> by the moderns. In this, they say that the painter Parrhasius—the one whom Xenophon introduces speaking with Socrates—was most excellent: for they say that he considered the lines most subtly. And in this design, I think one must principally take care that it be done with the thinnest possible lines, such that they are not at all discernible to the eye, just as they say the painter Apelles used to do when practicing and competing with Protogenes to see who could make them thinner. For design is nothing other than the drawing of the outlines; if this is done with lines that appear too prominent, they will not seem to be the margins of the surfaces in that painting, but will look like cracks. Furthermore, I would desire that in the design one should follow nothing other than the circuit of the outlines; in which design I affirm that it is necessary to exercise oneself vehemently: since no composition and no reception of light will ever be praised if design is not present. Indeed, design alone is most pleasing most of the time.

Let us therefore give effort to design; and to learn this perfectly, I do not believe anything more suitable can be found than that **veil** which I am accustomed to call among my friends the **intersection** <note>original: "taglio"; literally "the cut," referring to the intersection of the visual pyramid.</note>. The method of using this, I was the first to have discovered, and it is made thus: I take a veil of very fine thread, loosely woven, of whatever color you please. This I then divide with somewhat thicker threads, making as many equal squares as I like upon a frame, and I place it between the eye and the object to be seen, so that the **visual pyramid** <note>term: "piramide uisiua"; the geometric concept that light travels from an object to the eye in the shape of a pyramid or cone.</note> penetrates and passes through the openings of the veil. Truly, this intersection of the veil has in itself no small advantages. First, it always represents the same surfaces to you as immobile; for once the boundaries are placed, you will immediately find the original point of the pyramid with which you began, which without this intersection of the veil is truly a most difficult thing. And you know how impossible it is in painting to correctly change anything, because the same aspect and view does not remain perpetually for the one who paints. From this it follows that it is easier to copy those things which are portrayed from paintings than those portrayed from sculptures. You know also, besides this, how much the seen object appears altered by the change of the distance or the position of the center. Therefore, the veil or the net will bring you this...

# BOOK TWO.

little utility, for the object will always appear the same to your sight.
The other utility is that you will be able to easily place the locations of the outlines and the boundaries of the surfaces in very certain spots when painting your panel. Because, seeing the forehead in one mesh of the net, the nose in the one next to it, the cheeks in the closest one after that, the chin in the one below, and all other such things arranged in their proper places, you will likewise be able to place them perfectly on your panel or on the wall, which you have also divided with a grid equal to that one. Finally, this net or veil <note>original: "velo"; Alberti's famous perspective tool consisting of a thin, gridded cloth placed between the artist and the subject.</note> provides the greatest convenience and help in bringing the painting to perfection: for you will see that three-dimensional and rounded object drawn and painted upon the flat surface of the net. By means of these things, we can easily know through both judgment and experience how much utility this net provides for painting well and perfectly. Nor do I like those who say it is not good for painters to accustom themselves to these things; for even if they bring the greatest help to painting, they are nonetheless such that without them a painter will hardly ever be able to do anything on his own. Since we do not require that the painter—if I am not mistaken—must endure infinite labor; rather, we praise that painting which has great relief <note>original: "rilieuo"; the illusion of three-dimensionality on a flat surface.</note>, and which appears to us very similar to the bodies it is meant to represent. Which thing, certainly, I do not see how it could succeed for anyone even moderately well without the help of the net. Therefore, let those who strive to make progress use this "cut"—that is, this net. And if there are some who delight in testing their talent without the net, let them procure for themselves this same rule of the meshes with their sight, so that they always imagine a horizontal line being cut by a vertical one at the point where they establish the boundary observed in the painting. But because most of the time the outlines of surfaces appear doubtful and uncertain to inexperienced painters—as happens in faces, where they sometimes do not discern in which place the temples are primarily terminated by the forehead—it is therefore necessary to teach them how they may learn to recognize this thing. Nature truly teaches it to us very well. For, just as we see in flat surfaces that they are beautiful when they have their own lights and their own shadows, so in spherical and concave surfaces it seems to us that they look right when, as if divided into several surfaces, they have various patches of shadows and lights. All the parts, therefore, each by itself, which have different lights and different shadows, are to be considered as so many different surfaces. And if a visible surface continues from its shadow, fading little by little until its greatest light, one must then mark with a line the midpoint between one space and the other, so that there is less doubt regarding the rule you must follow in coloring the space.

It remains for us to treat something further regarding design <note>original: "disegno"</note>, which truly pertains not a little to composition; therefore, it is well to know what composition is in painting. Composition is truly that method or rule in painting by which all the parts are composed together

<page-num>26</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>

together in the work of painting. The greatest work of the painter is the **narrative** <note>original: "historia"; for Alberti, this refers to the complex arrangement of figures to tell a story, the highest goal of art.</note>. The parts of the narrative are the bodies; the parts of the body are the limbs; the parts of the limbs are the surfaces. And since **design** <note>original: "disegno"; meaning both drawing and the conceptual plan.</note> is that rule or method of painting by which the outlines are drawn around each of the surfaces—and of surfaces, some are small, like those of animals, and some are very large, like those of colossi and buildings—for drawing small surfaces, those instructions that have been said up to here shall suffice; since it has been demonstrated how they are drawn well with the net. But in drawing larger surfaces, we must find another rule.

For which reason, it is necessary to recall to memory all those things that were taught above regarding surfaces, rays, the pyramid, and the cross-section <note>original: "taglio"; the intersection of the visual pyramid with the picture plane.</note>. Finally, you remember what I said about the parallel lines of the space or **floor** <note>original: "pauimento"; the perspective grid on the ground plane.</note>, and of the central point, and of the line. Upon the floor, therefore, drawn with parallel lines, one must raise the wings of the walls, and whatever other similar things are desired, which we call upright surfaces. I will therefore say briefly what I do in raising these things.

The first thing I do is begin from the foundations themselves, and I draw on the floor the width and the length of the walls. In drawing which thing, I have learned from nature that from a single view one cannot see more than two joined surfaces rising upright from the plane of any square body made with right angles. In drawing the foundations of the walls, therefore, I observe this: to draw only those faces or sides that present themselves to my view. And the first thing I do is begin with those far from the cross-section. Therefore, I draw these before the others, and I determine by means of those parallel lines drawn on the floor how long and wide I want those walls to be. For I take as many parallels as I want there to be **braccia** <note>original: "braccia"; a Renaissance unit of measurement, roughly the length of an arm (approx. 23 inches).</note>. And I find the middle of the parallels from the mutual intersection of each diameter of those parallels. So that by this measure of the parallels, I draw very well the width and the length of those walls that rise up from the plane. Then I achieve from this, without difficulty, the height of the surfaces as well: for that measure which is between the central line and that place on the floor where the mass of the building begins to rise—that entire quantity will observe the same measure. And if you want that quantity from the floor to the top to be four times the length of the painted man, and the central line is placed at the height of the man, there will truly be three braccia from the lowest part of the mass up to the central line. But you, who want this quantity to grow to twelve braccia, draw upward three times that quantity which is from the bottom to the central line. We can therefore, by means of the cited rules of painting, draw all angular surfaces well.

<summary>Alberti explains how to transition from drawing small objects with a grid to constructing large-scale architectural spaces using the principles of linear perspective. He details how to project walls and heights from a gridded floor plan (pauimento) using a consistent scale based on the human figure.</summary>

<header>BOOK TWO.</header>

->*A baseline of nine cubits.* <note>original: "braccia"; a Renaissance unit of measurement based on the length of an arm, roughly 23 inches.</note><-
->*A. B. Pillars or walls ten cubits high.*<-

It remains for us to treat the drawing of circular surfaces with their outlines. Circular surfaces are truly derived from angular ones, which I do in this manner. I draw a circle inside a quadrangle <note>a square</note> with equal sides and right angles, and I divide the sides of this quadrangle into as many parts as the baseline of the quadrangle in the painting was divided. Then, drawing the lines of the divisions from each point to the one opposite it, I fill that space with small quadrangles. Within this, I draw a circle as large as I wish, in such a way that the circle and the parallel lines intersect one another. I note the locations of all the points of intersection, which I then mark on those same parallel lines of the floor drawn in the painting or **perspective** <term>prospettiva: the mathematical system for representing three-dimensional space on a flat surface</term>. 

But because it would be an extreme labor to intersect the entire circle with very frequent and almost infinite parallels until the outline of the circle is completed by a numerous marking of points, I instead note only eight, or as many intersections as I please. Then, by means of my skill <note>original: "ingegno"; referring to the artist's practiced judgment and hand</note>, I draw the circumference or perimeter of the circle through the points already marked. Perhaps it would be a shorter path to draw this outline by the shadow of a lamp, provided that the body causing the shadow receives the light according to a certain rule.

Having finished treating every sort of <term>drawing</term> <note>original: "disegno"; in the Renaissance, this meant both the act of drawing and the underlying intellectual design or structure of a work</note>, it remains for us to treat <term>composition</term> <note>original: "componimento"; the harmonious arrangement of parts into a whole</note>. And truly, composition is that rule of painting by which the parts are composed together in the work of <term>painting</term>. The greatest work the painter performs is not a statue as large as a colossus, but a <term>history</term> <note>original: "historia"; the highest genre of art, depicting a narrative scene with multiple figures, usually from the Bible, mythology, or history</note>: for one finds greater praise for intellect in a history than in a colossus. The parts of the history are the bodies, the parts of the bodies are the limbs, and the parts of the limbs are the surfaces, because from these the limbs are made, from the limbs the bodies, and from the bodies the history, from which is made that final, truly and perfectly finished work of the painter. From the composition of the surfaces arises that elegance and that grace which they call beauty. For that face which has some large surfaces and some small ones—which in one place protrude too far and in another are hidden too deep, as is seen in the faces of old women—this will certainly be an ugly thing to see. But in that face in which the surfaces are joined together in such a way that the gentle lights turn little by little into soft shadows, and there are no harshness of angles, this we shall rightly call a beautiful face, possessing loveliness <note>original: "venustà"; a quality of aesthetic grace and charm</note>. Therefore, in this composition of surfaces, one must go about investigating grace and beauty with great care. But in what man-

<header>BOOK TWO.</header>
<sig>d iij</sig>

# BOOK TWO.

To achieve this, I have found no more certain way than to consider nature itself. Therefore, let us look most diligently, and for a very long time, at how nature—that marvelous craftswoman of all things—has composed the surfaces in the most beautiful limbs. In imitating her, we must exercise all our thoughts and diligence, and take great delight, as we said, in the <term>net</term> <note>original: "rete"; referring to the perspective grid or veil Alberti previously described as an aid for drawing</note>. And when we have extracted the surfaces from the most beautiful bodies and are ready to put them to use, we shall always first determine the boundaries, by means of which we can draw the lines in their destined places.

Let what has been said up to this point regarding the composition of surfaces suffice; it remains for us to speak of the composition of limbs. In the composition of limbs, one must first ensure that all the limbs are proportionate to one another. They are said to be well-proportioned when they correspond to beauty and majesty in terms of size, function, species, color, and any other similar qualities. For if in any figure there is a massive head, a small chest, a very large hand, a swollen foot, and a bloated body, this composition will truly be ugly to look upon. Therefore, regarding size, one must maintain a certain rule of measurement. In painting animals, it is very helpful to first examine with the mind what kind of bones they have; for these, because they do not bend, always occupy a fixed and certain place. Afterward, one must place the nerves and muscles in their proper places, and finally clothe the bones and muscles with flesh and skin. But at this point, there will perhaps be those who will criticize me because I said above that the painter should not concern himself with things that cannot be seen. These people would indeed speak correctly; but just as in dressing a figure one must first draw the <term>nude</term> <note>original: "ignudo"</note> which we then want to wrap in garments, so in painting a nude, one must first arrange and place the bones and muscles in their positions, which you then cover in order with flesh and skin, so that it is not difficult to recognize where those muscles are situated. But because nature herself has explained all these measurements and placed them before our eyes, the studious painter will find no small utility in recognizing these same things through his own labor from nature. Therefore, let the studious take up this labor, so that all the effort they spend in recognizing the proportion of limbs will help them hold firmly in their memory the things they have learned. I warn them, however, of this first: that in measuring the animal, one should take one of the limbs of that same animal by which all the other limbs are measured. *Vitruvius* <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman author of "De architectura"</note> the architect measures the length of a man by the feet; but I think it is a worthier thing if the other limbs are related to the size of the head. Although I have also considered that it is common among men that the measure of the foot is usually the same as the distance from the chin to the top of the head; so that having taken one of these members, all the others are to be accommodated to it, in such a way that there is no...

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>

no limb in the entire animal should fail to correspond to the others in length or width. Beyond this, care must be taken that all the limbs perform their functions according to what they were made for. It is appropriate for a man who runs to throw his hands no less than his feet; but for a philosopher delivering an oration, I would wish that every one of his limbs were more modest than those of a <term>pugilist</term> <note>original: "giuocator di braccia"; literally a "player of arms," referring to a wrestler or prize-fighter</note>. The painter Demon expressed <term>Hoplitides</term> <note>original: "Hoplicite"; likely referring to the "Hoplitodromos" or armored runners depicted by the ancient Greek painter Antidotus or Parrhasius</note> in a combat in such a way that you would say he was sweating, and another who was laying down his arms so that you would say he was barely catching his breath. There was also one who painted Ulysses in such a manner that you would recognize in him not true madness, but a feigned and simulated insanity. Among the Romans, the <term>history</term> <note>original: "historia"; the narrative or dramatic scene in a painting</note> is praised in which Meleager is carried away dead, and those who carry him seem to be grieving and straining with all their limbs; and in him who is dead, there is no limb that does not appear more than dead—that is, everything falls away: the hand, the fingers, the head, everything hangs limp and languid. Finally, all things work together to express the death of the body, which is the most difficult of all things. For to represent idle limbs in every part of a body is the work of a most excellent master, just as it is to make all the living limbs do something. Therefore, in every painting, this must be observed: that whatever the limbs may be, they perform the function for which they were made in such a way that no artery, however small, fails in its duty, so that the limbs of the dead appear perfectly dead, and those of the living perfectly alive. A body is said to be alive when it performs some motion of its own accord; and dead, when the limbs can no longer exercise the functions of life—that is, motion and sensation. Therefore, those images of bodies that the painter wishes to appear alive should have all their limbs put their motions into action. But in every motion, one must pursue beauty and grace; and those motions of bodies that rise toward the air are greatly lively and most pleasing. Beyond this, we said that in composing the limbs, regard must be had for the <term>species</term> <note>original: "specie"; referring to the character, type, or social standing of the figure</note>: for it would be a very unseemly thing if the hands of Helen or Iphigenia appeared to be the hands of old women or peasant women; or if Nestor were given a young man's chest or a delicate head; or if Ganymede were given a forehead full of wrinkles or the legs of a pugilist; or if Milo, the most robust of all men, were given thin and slender flanks. Furthermore, in an image that has a full and plump face, as they say, it would be an ugly thing to show the arms and hands wasted and consumed by hunger; and conversely, if one were to paint Achemenides in that manner and with that face which Virgil says was found by Aeneas on the island, if the other limbs did not correspond to that thinness, such a painter would certainly be ridiculous and foolish. Beyond this, I would wish that they also correspond to one another in color: for those images that have faces like roses, most beautiful and dewy, it is not fitting that they should have dark and fierce chests and limbs. Therefore, in the composition of limbs, we have said enough about what must be observed regarding size, function, species, and colors; since it must...

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>BOOK TWO</header>
<meta>catchword: wants</meta>

It is necessary that everything corresponds according to the truth of the matter: and it is not fitting to make a Venus or a Minerva dressed as a beggar; nor would it be appropriate to make a Jove or a Mars dressed in a woman’s garment. The ancient painters, in depicting Castor and Pollux, took care that although they appeared to be born at a single stroke <note>A reference to their birth from a single egg</note>, one nevertheless showed a more robust nature, and the other a more agile one. Beyond this, they wanted Vulcan to appear limping beneath his clothes: such was the study they placed in expressing things according to their function, their species, and their dignity.

Next follows the <term>composition</term> <note>original: "componimento"; the formal arrangement of elements in a painting</note> of bodies, in which consists all the genius and all the praise of the painter. Regarding this composition, some things have been said pertaining to the arrangement of the limbs: for it is necessary that, as far as function and size are concerned, all bodies in the <term>history</term> <note>original: "historia"; the narrative or dramatic scene</note> agree together. For if you were to paint the Centaurs rioting together at a banquet, it would be the act of a madman if, in such an unbridled and bestial tumult, there were someone lying asleep because of the wine. Furthermore, it would also be a defect if, at an equal distance, some men appeared larger than others: or if in a painting dogs were made as large as horses. And it would be no small cause for blame—as I see most of the time—to paint men in a building as if they were shut in a chest, in which they barely have room to sit, or are squeezed into a circle. All bodies, therefore, must conform, by means of their size and their function, to that thing for which they are made. But the <term>history</term> that is reasonably to be praised and looked upon with wonder must be such that, with certain charms, it shows itself to be so delightful and ornate that it holds the eyes of those who are learned and those who are not for a long time, with pleasure and delight of the soul.

The first thing in a <term>history</term> that brings and offers you pleasure is the very abundance and variety of things: for just as in food and in music novelty always delights, so in every variety of things and in every abundance the soul finds pleasure and delight. Therefore, in painting, the variety of bodies and colors is joyful. I would say that a <term>history</term> is most copious in which, in their proper places, there are mixed together old men, youths, boys, matrons, maidens, children, domestic animals, little dogs, birds, horses, sheep, buildings, and landscapes. I will praise any abundance whatsoever, provided that it suits the subject that one <insert>wants</insert> to represent there: for it happens that those who look upon it, in considering the things, spend more time there, and the abundance and richness of the painter acquires grace. But I would wish that this abundance be ornate, and provide a certain grave and moderate variety, through dignity and reverence. I do not praise those painters who, in order to appear copious and because they do not want any empty space to remain in their works, do not follow any composition, but sow everything foolishly and confusedly; because of this, it does not seem that the <term>history</term> represents what it intends to do, but rather a tumult: and perhaps for the dignity of the <term>history</term> one should have...

<summary>Alberti discusses the importance of decorum and "historia," emphasizing that figures must be depicted according to their specific character and that the overall composition should be varied yet orderly.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the proper scale and decorum of figures within a narrative scene, specifically the need for figures to fit their architectural surroundings.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>

one must primarily learn to avoid solitude <note>In this context, "solitude" refers to an emptiness or lack of figures that makes a scene appear barren</note>; for just as in a prince, speaking little brings majesty—provided the meaning of his words and commands are understood—so in a <term>history</term> <note>The narrative or dramatic scene</note> a reasonable number of bodies brings dignity, and variety brings grace. I hate solitude in a history, yet I do not praise an abundance that is unsuited to dignity. Rather, in a history, I greatly praise what I see has been observed by tragic and comic poets: they represent their story with a smaller number of characters. And truly, in my judgment, it will not be necessary to fill a history with such a variety of things that it cannot be worthily composed of nine or ten men. Just as I judge that the saying of Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, Roman scholar</note> applies here, who, wishing to avoid a tumult at a banquet, never invited more than nine guests. But while variety is pleasing in any history, that painting is nonetheless most welcome to all in which the poses and attitudes of the bodies are very different from one another. Let some, then, be seen full-face, with hands raised and fingers shining <note>Likely referring to the highlights or "splendor" of the paint on the extremities</note>, resting on one of their feet; let others be in profile, with arms lowered and feet together, and let each have his own particular bends and attitudes. Let others be sitting or kneeling, or almost lying down; let some be naked, if that is appropriate; let others, through the blending of one and the other art, be partly naked and partly clothed. But always have a care for decency and reverence: for the shameful parts of the body, and other similar parts that have little grace, should be covered either with drapery, or leaves, or with the hands. <term>Apelles</term> <note>The most famous painter of ancient Greece</note> painted only that side of the face of <term>Antigonus</term> from which the defect of his eye did not appear. And <term>Homer</term>, when he wakes <term>Ulysses</term> from sleep after the shipwreck, so as not to have him go naked through the woods toward the voices of the women, we read that he gave that man one of the leafy branches of the trees so that he might cover his private parts. They relate that <term>Pericles</term> had a long and ugly head, and therefore by painters and sculptors he was never made with a bare head like the others, but always with a helmet on his head. Beyond this, <term>Plutarch</term> relates that ancient painters, when painting kings, if they had any defect in their form, did not want it to appear that they had simply left it out, but while preserving the likeness, they corrected it as much as they could. This modesty and this reverence I desire to be observed in the whole history, so that obscene things are either left aside or corrected. Finally, as I said, I think one should strive so that in no image is the same gesture or the same attitude seen. Furthermore, the history will keep the minds of the spectators attentive when those men who are quiet therein most greatly represent the movements of their souls: for it happens by nature (than which nothing is found more rapacious, nor which draws us more than similar things) that we weep with those who weep, laugh with those who laugh, and grieve with those who lament. But these movements of the soul are known through the movements of the body: for we see how the melancholy, because they are afflicted by thoughts and weary from

## BOOK TWO.

infirmity, as if they were, so to speak, crippled in all their senses and strengths, and how they remain sluggish with pale limbs that almost fall away from them. For those who are grieving truly have a lowered brow, a weary head, and all their other members finally fall as if exhausted and abandoned. But the angry—because their souls are inflamed by wrath—have faces and eyes that swell and turn red, and the movements of all their limbs, through the fury of their anger, are extremely fast and fierce. But when we are joyful and cheerful, then we have free and graceful movements through certain <term>attitudes</term> <note>The specific physical poses or stances of a figure</note>. Euphranor <note>A celebrated Greek sculptor and painter of the 4th century BCE</note> is praised because, in his Alexander, he painted the face and countenance of Paris in such a way that you could easily recognize him as the judge of the goddesses, the lover of Helen, and at the same time the slayer of Achilles. Wonderful praise is also given to the painter Demon, in whose panels you could recognize the presence of the wrathful, the unjust, the inconstant, and at the same time the exorable, the clement, the merciful, the boastful, the humble, and the fierce. But among others, they say that Aristides of Thebes, an equal to Apelles <note>The most famous painter of antiquity</note>, greatly expressed these <term>movements of the soul</term> <note>The internal emotions or passions as expressed through external physical action</note>, which it is certain that we also will be able to do very well when we apply to this matter the study and diligence that is appropriate. It is necessary, therefore, that the painter knows excellently the attitudes and movements of the body, which I judge must be drawn from nature with infinite diligence: for the matter is most difficult because of the infinite movements of the soul, by which the movements of the body are also varied. Besides this, who would believe—except one who has experienced it—how difficult it is when you wish to paint a laughing face, to avoid that look by which it will seem rather to be crying than laughing? Furthermore, who will be able, without the greatest study and diligence, to express faces in which the mouth, the chin, the eyes, the cheeks, the forehead, and the eyebrows all correspond and unite together in both weeping and laughter? And therefore it is necessary to go about finding them most diligently from nature, and always to imitate the most vivid things. And principally, those things should be painted which leave more for the mind to think about than what is seen by the eyes.

But let us recount some things that we have fashioned with our own wit regarding attitudes, and partly also learned from nature itself. The first thing I believe we need is that all bodies among themselves should move with a certain grace and suitability toward the subject being treated. Besides this, I like there to be someone in the <term>history</term> <note>The narrative scene</note> who warns the spectators, calling them with his hand to see those things that are being done there: or, as if he wants that business to be a secret, threatens with a cruel face and fearful eyes that you should not approach, or shows you that there is some great danger or some marvelous thing: or who with his gestures invites you to laugh with him, or perhaps to weep. Finally, it is necessary that all those things they do among themselves, and also with those who watch them, concur to create and demonstrate the story. Timanthes of Cyprus is praised in that panel in which he defeated Col-

...the same, because having made Calchas <note>The Greek seer during the Trojan War</note> melancholy, he made Ulysses even more melancholy; and because in painting Menelaus <note>King of Sparta</note> as most sorrowful, he had applied all his genius and exhausted all his art. Having spent all the expressions of emotion, and finding no way to be able to paint the face of the most sorrowful father <note>Referring to Agamemnon at the sacrifice of his daughter Iphigenia</note>, he wrapped his head in a cloth, to leave more to be imagined of the grief he had in his soul than could be discerned in his face. Praise is given to the ship in Rome <note>The "Navicella," a famous mosaic by Giotto in St. Peter's Basilica</note>, in which our Tuscan painter Giotto expressed the eleven frightened and amazed disciples in such a way—due to their companion <note>St. Peter</note> walking upon the waves of the sea—that each one by himself gave a particular indication of his troubled soul, and with body attitudes such that each represents in a different way the terror they feel.

But it is appropriate to pass briefly over this entire topic of movements: for among movements, some belong to the soul, which by the learned are called <term>passions</term> <note>From the Latin 'passio', meaning suffering or being acted upon; the internal emotions</note>, such as anger, grief, joy, fear, desire, and the like. There are also others that belong to bodies: for it is said that bodies move in many ways, namely when they grow, or when they diminish, or when being healthy they fall into infirmity, or when from infirmity they return to health. When they also change place, and through other similar cases, it is said that bodies move. But we painters, who through the movements of the limbs wish to express the affections of the soul, leaving all other disputes aside, will treat only that movement which we say is made when the location is changed.

All things that move from their place have seven directions of movement: for they move either upward, or downward, or toward the right, or toward the left, or moving away, or approaching us, and the seventh direction is when they move by turning around. All these movements, therefore, I desire to be in a painting. Let there be some bodies that come toward us, some others that move away, some that go toward the right, and others toward the left. Beyond this, let some parts of these bodies be shown facing the viewer, some turning back, some rising upward, and some lowering themselves.

But because in drawing these movements one sometimes exceeds the rule and order, I am pleased in this place to recount some things about the position and movements of the limbs, which I have drawn from nature, so that it may be clearly seen with what modesty we must employ these movements. I have certainly seen in man that in every attitude he subjects the whole body to the head, the member most heavy of all others. Beyond this, if one stands with the whole body upon a single foot, that foot always—as if it were the base of a column—comes directly under the head; and almost always the face of one who stands on one foot looks in that direction toward which the foot is pointed. But I have noticed that the movements of the head are hardly ever such toward one of the sides that it does not always have under it some parts of the rest of the body by which the great weight is supported.

<sig>e ij</sig>

or else that he does not extend some other limb toward the opposite side, like a part of a **balance** <note>original: "bilancia"</note> that counterpoises it. For we see the same thing when someone, with an outstretched hand, supports a weight; with the other foot—just as the spindle of a balance is fixed—he steadies himself on the opposite side with the rest of his body to counterweight the load. I have noticed that the head of one who stands upright never turns upward more than what allows the eyes to see the middle of the sky, nor does it ever turn to either side more than until the chin strikes against the shoulder bones. And in that part of the body where we gird ourselves <note>The waist or belt-line</note>, we can hardly ever turn so much that the shoulder comes in a straight line above the navel. The movements of the legs and arms are somewhat more free, provided they do not impede the other decent parts of the body. And in these I have observed in nature that the hands, for the most part, do not rise above the head, nor the elbow above the shoulders, nor does the foot rise above the knee, nor does one foot ever move away from the other except by the space of one foot. I have seen, besides this, that if we raise one of our hands high, all the other parts of that side down to the foot follow that motion, such that even the heel of that foot is lifted from the floor by the movement of that arm. There are infinite things similar to these, which the diligent master will notice. And perhaps those I have recounted up to now are so manifest that they may seem superfluous; but I have not left them out because I have seen many err greatly in this matter. Attitudes and movements that are too forced express and show in a single image both the chest and the back in one single view, which, being impossible to do, is also most unseemly to see. But because such people feel that those images appear much more alive the more they make forced attitudes of the limbs, they despise all the dignity of painting and go about imitating the movements of jugglers <note>original: "giocolatori"; likely referring to acrobats or street performers</note>. Wherefore not only are their works naked and without any grace or elegance, but they also express the overly fervent spirit of the painter. Painting ought to have smooth and graceful movements, appropriate to what it wishes to represent. Let there appear in young girls a venerable movement and habit, a graceful and simple ornament befitting their age; let her posture be more of the sweet and quiet kind than one suited to agitation; even though **Homer**, whom **Zeuxis** <note>A famous Greek painter of the 5th century BCE</note> followed, also liked a very robust beauty in women. Let there appear in youths lighter and more joyful movements that give a sign of a brave soul and valiant strength. Let there appear in men firmer movements and beautiful attitudes, suited to a swift movement of the arms. In old men, let all movements appear slow, and let their attitudes be weary, such that they not only support themselves on both feet but lean on something with their hands; and finally, let all movements of the body be referred, according to the dignity of each person, to those affections of the soul that you wish to represent. Then, finally, it is necessary that the signs of the greatest passions of the soul appear and express themselves most greatly in those bodies. And this rule of movements and attitudes is very common in...

one might wish for strong movements in animals: for it is not fitting that an ox, which serves for plowing, should perform the same actions as the noble horse of Alexander, **Bucephalus** <note>The famous horse of Alexander the Great, known for its spirit and strength.</note>: but that much-celebrated daughter of Inachus <note>Io, a priestess of Hera who was transformed into a heifer by Zeus.</note>, who was converted into a cow, we shall perhaps paint appropriately with her head held high, her feet raised, and her tail twisted. Let it suffice to have briefly touched upon these things regarding the movements of animals.

But because I think that all these movements of which we have spoken are also necessary for inanimate things in painting, I think it is well to treat the manner in which they move. For the movements of hair, tresses, branches, leaves, and clothing, when expressed in painting, also provide delight. I certainly desire that hair should represent all seven of those movements I have recounted <note>Alberti previously categorized movement into seven directions: up, down, right, left, forward, backward, and circular.</note>: for let them wind in a circle, forming a knot; let them spread in the air, imitating flames; let them go now snaking under other hairs, now rising toward another part. Let there also be bends in the branches and their curves arched toward the top, some returning inward, some winding like a rope. And let this same thing happen in the folds of drapery, so that just as many branches grow from the trunk of a tree in different directions, so from one fold many folds should be born, like branches from the trunk: and in these likewise let all the movements be seen, such that there is no fold of cloth in which almost all the said movements are not found. But let all the movements be—as I often warn—moderate and gentle, and let them show grace rather than the wonder of great effort. 

But since we want the drapery to be suited to movement, and since cloth is by nature heavy and continually falls straight to the ground, thereby avoiding every bend, it will be well to place in the painting the face of **Zephyrus** or **Auster** <note>The West and South winds, personified as deities.</note> blowing through the clouds at one edge of the story, from which all the drapery is pushed toward the opposite side: from which will also come that grace where those sides of the bodies struck by the wind—because the cloth will press against the bodies due to the wind—will appear almost naked under the veil of the cloth; and on the other sides, the cloth agitated by the wind will make beautiful undulating folds in the air. But in this striking of the wind, one must take care that no movement of any cloth goes against the wind, and that the folds are not too sharp nor too broken.

These things, then, which have been said of the movements of animals and inanimate things, must be greatly observed by painters, and all the other things must also be diligently put into execution that were said above regarding the composition of the surfaces of members and bodies. So we have determined two parts of painting: **design** <note>original: "disegno"</note> and **composition** <note>original: "componimento"</note>. It remains for us to treat the reception of light. In the first principles, it was sufficiently shown what power lights have in varying colors: for while the types of colors remain fixed, we taught in what way they appeared now lighter and now darker, according to the application of lights or shadows, and that white and black were those colors by which we express light and shadow in painting: and that the other colors are to be es-
