<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>BOOK SECOND.</header>

formed by the material, to which the alterations of lights and shadows are added. Therefore, leaving other things aside, we must declare in what manner the painter has to make use of white and black. The ancient painters marveled that **Polygnotus** and **Timanthes** <note>Polygnotus of Thasos and Timanthes of Cythnus were celebrated Greek painters of the 5th and 4th centuries BCE.</note> used only four colors, and that **Aglaophon** delighted in a single color, as if in such a great number of colors as they thought existed, it were a small thing that those excellent painters had put so few into use: whereas they judge that it belongs to a copious master to put into work whatever multitude of colors he wishes. I truly affirm that the variety and abundance of colors brings much grace and much elegance to painting. But I would wish that talented painters judge that every industry and every art must be placed in disposing and locating white and black well, and that in locating these, and accommodating them well, all one's genius and whatever extreme diligence should be applied. For just as the occurrence of lights and shadows makes it seen in which place the surfaces rise in relief, and in which they sink, and how much each of the parts declines or bends: so the good arrangement of white and black achieves that which was attributed as praise to **Nicias** <note>Nicias of Athens, a 4th-century BCE painter known for his skill in light and shadow.</note>, the Athenian painter, and that which the master must desire as the first thing: that his paintings appear with great relief. They say that **Zeuxis** <note>A legendary Greek painter of the 5th century BCE, famous for his realism.</note>, a most noble and ancient painter, was almost the first who knew how to hold this rule of lights and shadows. But this praise is not attributed to the others. I certainly shall not think that anyone is even a mediocre painter who does not know very well what force each shadow and each light has on all surfaces. I will praise those painted faces—with the good grace of both the learned and the ignorant—which seem to emerge out of the panels in relief, and on the contrary, I will blame those in which perhaps no point of art is seen, except in the outlines. I would wish that the composition be well drawn, and excellently colored. Therefore, so that they are not reproached, and so that they deserve to be praised, the first thing they must do is mark the lights and shadows most diligently, and they must consider that on that surface upon which the rays of light strike, that color should be as clear and luminous as possible, and that beyond this, as the force of the lights gradually fails, one should gradually apply a somewhat darker color. Finally, it is necessary to observe how the shadows correspond on the side opposite to the lights, for there will never be a surface of any body that is bright with light, where you do not find the surface opposite to it on the same body covered and loaded with shadows. But as far as pertains to imitating the lights with white, and the shadows with black, I warn you that you place your principal study in knowing those surfaces that are touched either by light or by shadow. This you will learn well from nature and from things themselves: and when finally you know these things very well, you will alter the color within its outlines in its place as sparingly as you can with very little white, and in its opposite place you will likewise add in that instant a little black. For with this balancing, so to speak, of white and black, the relief appears greater. Then continue with the increases

## ON PAINTING

with the same parsimony <note>frugality or restraint in the use of materials</note>, until such time as you know yourself to have gained enough skill; and the mirror will truly be an excellent judge to make this known to you. And I do not know how it is that painted things have a certain grace in the mirror, provided they have no defect. Beyond this, it is marvelous how every defect in a painting appears more ugly in the mirror. Therefore, let things portrayed from nature be corrected by the judgment of the mirror. But let me be permitted here to recount some things that I have drawn from nature. I have truly considered how flat surfaces maintain their color uniformly in every part of themselves; but round and concave surfaces vary their colors, because on one side they are light, and on the other dark, and in another place they maintain a middle color. And this alteration of color in non-flat surfaces brings difficulty to lazy painters. But if the painter marks well, as we said, the outlines of the surfaces, and separates the areas of the lights, the method and rule of coloring will then be easy for him. For he will first go about altering that surface with either white or black, as will be necessary, up to the line of division, almost as if he were scattering a dew. Then he will scatter, so to speak, another dew beyond the line, and after this another beyond that, and after that adding another on top of it, it will happen that the place of the light will be illuminated with a clearer color, and then the same color, almost like smoke, will fade into the parts contiguous to it. But one must remember that no surface should ever be made so white that you cannot make the same even whiter. In expressing white garments, one must also withdraw far from the ultimate whiteness; for the painter has nothing except the color white with which he can imitate the ultimate splendors of the most polished surfaces, and I have found only black with which he can represent the ultimate shadows and darkness of the night. And therefore, in painting white garments, one must take one of the four kinds of colors that is open and light; and conversely, do the same in painting a black cloth, using the other extreme, because it is not far from shadow, as if we were taking from the deep and blackening sea. Finally, this composition of white and black has such power that, when done with art and rule, it demonstrates in painting the most splendid surfaces of gold, silver, and glass. Therefore, those painters are to be greatly blamed who use white intemperately and black without any diligence; and for this reason, I would wish that white color were bought by painters more dearly than the most precious gems. It would truly be well if white and black were made from those pearls of **Cleopatra** <note>Referring to the legend of Cleopatra dissolving a pearl in vinegar to win a wager regarding the most expensive meal.</note> which she softened with vinegar, so that they might become more stingy with them. For the works would become more graceful and closer to the truth, nor can it be easily said how much parsimony and moderation are needed in distributing white and black in painting. For this reason, **Zeuxis** <note>A famous Greek painter of the 5th century BCE.</note> used to reprove painters because they did not know what "too much" was; for if errors are to be forgiven, they are less to be...

<vocab>Painting, Mirror, Nature, Surface, Color, White, Black, Zeuxis, Cleopatra, Shadow, Diligence</vocab>

<summary>Alberti discusses the technical application of light and shadow, advising painters to use white and black sparingly and to use mirrors to detect flaws in their work. He emphasizes that the highest degree of white and black should be reserved for the most extreme highlights and shadows to maintain realism.</summary>
<keywords>chiaroscuro, mirror as judge, color theory, white and black, Zeuxis, Cleopatra, artistic restraint, relief in painting</keywords>

<meta>Continuing the discussion on the technical application of light and shadow, specifically the warning against overusing white and black, and transitioning into the harmony of colors and the imitation of gold through pigments.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>BOOK SECOND.</header>

I have more often rebuked those who use black too profusely than those who use white too intemperately. We have learned through the practice of painting that nature itself hates darkness and gloom more than the other <note>meaning nature prefers light over darkness</note>, and continually, the more we know, the more we make our hand inclined toward grace and elegance. Thus, we all naturally love things that are clear and open. Therefore, we must close off the path from that side where the way of sinning <note>in this context, making an artistic error</note> is most open to us.

Let these things suffice that have been said until now regarding the use of white and black. But as for the types of colors, one must still have a certain rule. It follows, then, that some things should be told concerning the types of colors. We shall not recount, as the architect <term>Vitruvius</term> <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, Roman author of 'De architectura'</note> did, where good <term>cinnabar</term> <note>a bright red mineral pigment, mercury sulfide</note> may be found, or the most praised colors, but in what way the most select and well-ground colors must be mixed to make the <term>mixtures</term> <note>original: "mestiche" — the prepared batches of oil or tempera paint mixed to specific shades on the palette</note> in painting. They say that <term>Euphranor</term> <note>a celebrated Greek painter and sculptor of the 4th century BCE</note>, an ancient painter, wrote some things about colors: but these writings are not with us. But we, who have returned this art of painting to the light—either as something already described by others and recalled by us from the infernal gods, or as something never described by anyone and brought here from heaven by our own wit—let us proceed according to our order, just as we have done until now. I would wish that the types and species of colors, as far as it can be done, be seen with a certain grace and elegance in the painting. Grace will be present when colors are placed near other colors with a certain extreme diligence; as if you were painting <term>Diana</term> <note>Roman goddess of the hunt</note> leading a dance, it would be a fitting thing to dress the nymph closest to her in green cloths or fabrics, the next in white, the next then in red, and the other in yellow. And beyond this, that through the diversity of such colors they be dressed in such a way that light colors are always joined with some dark colors of a different kind from that with which they are joined. For that joining of colors procures a greater charm through variety, and a greater beauty through comparison. And there is truly a certain friendship among colors, so that when joined with one another they increase charm and beauty. If one puts the color red between blue and green, it awakens in both a certain mutual decorum. The color white, placed not only beside ash-gray and yellow, brings a kind of joy to almost all colors. Dark colors stand not without dignity among the light ones, and likewise the light ones are well placed among the dark. The painter will therefore arrange for the <term>history</term> <note>original: "historia" — a narrative or multi-figure scene, considered the highest form of painting</note> that variety of colors which we have described. But there are some who use gold without any modesty, because they think that gold brings a certain majesty to the history: I truly do not praise them. Rather, if I should wish to paint that <term>Dido</term> of <term>Virgil</term> <note>The Queen of Carthage from the 'Aeneid'</note>, who had a quiver of gold, and her hair bound in gold, and her dress with fasteners and belts of gold, and who was carried by horses with golden bits, and that all things shone with gold, I would nonetheless strive to imitate with colors rather than with gold that great abundance of the rays of gold, which strikes the eyes of the beholders from every side. For being...

<summary>Alberti discusses the aesthetic preference for light over dark and provides a theory of color harmony, advocating for the use of pigments to simulate gold rather than using the metal itself.</summary>
<keywords>color theory, harmony, Vitruvius, Euphranor, Diana, Dido, Virgil, cinnabar, mixtures, gold imitation</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion on the use of gold in painting, Alberti argues that representing gold with pigments rather than gold leaf brings more honor to the artist's skill.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>

the praise and the wonder for the master is greater when expressed through colors. It can also be seen that when gold is placed on a flat panel—as is the case with the majority of surfaces that need to be represented as bright and shining—they appear dark to the viewers. And some others that perhaps ought to be more shaded appear more luminous to us. As for the other ornaments of the masters that are added to the painting, such as the columns, the bases, and the frames that are made of sculpture around it, I shall not find fault if they are made of silver or solid gold, or at least highly polished. For a perfect and well-executed <term>history</term> <note>original: "historia" — the narrative or central subject of a painting, considered the highest form of art by Alberti</note> will be most worthy of the adornment of gems.

Up to here, we have very briefly brought an end to the three parts of painting: we have treated the <term>circumscription</term> <note>original: "disegno" — here referring to the drawing of outlines and surfaces</note> of smaller and larger surfaces; we have spoken of the <term>composition</term> of members and bodies, and also of colors as much as we judged to belong to the use of the painter. Thus, the whole of painting has been declared, which we said above consists of these three things: in <term>circumscription</term>, in <term>composition</term>, and in the <term>reception of light</term>.

# LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI
## ON PAINTING.
### BOOK THREE.

**B**UT to form a perfect painter, so that he may acquire all those praises that have been recounted, there remain some things for us to say, which I do not think should be left behind in these commentaries of mine, and I shall recount them as briefly as possible. The office of the painter is to draw and color whatever bodies are proposed to him on a surface with lines and colors in such a manner that, by means of a certain interval and a certain determined position of the <term>centric ray</term> <note>the central line of vision in Alberti's perspective system</note>, all things seen in the painting appear in relief and most similar to the things proposed to us. The goal of the painter is to seek to acquire praise, grace, and benevolence through his works, rather than riches; and he will obtain this as long as his painting entertains and moves the eyes and the minds of the viewers. How these things can be done, and by what path, was said when we disputed composition and the reception of light. But I desire that the painter, so that he may know and understand all these things well, be a man who is both good and learned in the <term>liberal arts</term> <note>original: "buone arti" — the humanities and sciences necessary for a well-rounded education</note>. For there is no one who does not know how much more goodness can do than the wonder of any industry or art whatsoever to acquire the benevolence of the citizens. Beyond this, there is no one who doubts that benevolence helps a master greatly in acquiring praise and procuring riches; because from this benevolence it happens that sometimes the wealthy are moved to give profit primarily to this modest and good man, leaving aside another who knows more, but who

<meta>is perhaps</meta>

<summary>Alberti concludes his technical discussion on the three parts of painting—circumscription, composition, and light—and begins Book Three by defining the painter's duty to achieve realism and moral character.</summary>
<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, On Painting, Book Three, circumscription, composition, reception of light, centric ray, painter's office, moral character</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from Book Three, where Alberti begins to describe the personal qualities and education required for a perfect painter.</meta>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>BOOK THREE.</header>

is perhaps less modest. These things being so, the master must have great diligence regarding his morals and upbringing, and especially toward humanity and kindness, through which things he may procure for himself both goodwill—a firm defense against poverty—and profit, an excellent aid in being able to bring his works to perfection. I truly desire that the painter be as learned as possible in all the liberal arts, but principally I desire that he know geometry. I am pleased by what Pamphilus <note>A celebrated Greek painter of the 4th century BCE and teacher of Apelles</note>, a most ancient and noble painter, used to say—from whom noble youths first learned painting—for he said that no one could ever be a good painter who did not know geometry. Truly, our first instructions, from which the entire absolute and perfect art of painting is derived, are easily understood by the geometer; but he who has no knowledge of it, I cannot believe will understand our instructions, nor even sufficiently some rules of painting. Therefore, I affirm that painters must not make a mockery of geometry. Furthermore, it will not be out of place if we take delight in poets and rhetoricians; for these have many ornaments in common with painters. Nor truly will those copious men of letters, who have knowledge of many things, be of little help to him in excellently ordering the composition of the <term>history</term> <note>original: "historia" — the narrative or subject matter</note>, the praise of which consists principally in the <term>invention</term> <note>original: "inuentione" — the conceptualizing of the story or theme</note>, since invention has such power that it alone, even without the painting, gives delight.

One praises, while reading it, that description of Calumny which Lucian <note>Lucian of Samosata, a Greek satirist whose description of a lost painting by Apelles inspired many Renaissance artists</note> relates as having been painted by Apelles; and I do not believe that recounting it is out of place, to advise painters that they must be watchful in finding and putting together such inventions. There was truly a man who had two very large ears, around whom stood two women, Ignorance and Suspicion. From the other side arrived Calumny herself, who had the form of a beautiful young woman, but whose face appeared all too malicious and cunning; she held a lighted torch in her left hand, and with the other hand she pulled by the hair a youth, who raised his hands to heaven. His guide was a certain pale and thin man, ugly and of a cruel aspect, whom you would reasonably liken to those whom long fatigue had consumed in a feat of arms; and they deservedly called him Envy <note>original: "liuore"</note>. There were also two other women, companions of Calumny, who arranged the ornaments of their mistress: Treachery and Deceit. After these came Repentance, dressed in a dark and most filthy garment, who tore and scratched herself, followed closely by modest and shameful Truth. Although this <term>history</term> entertains the mind while it is being told, how much delight and grace do you think it gave to see it in a painting made by an excellent master? What shall we say of those three young sisters, to whom Hesiod gave names, calling them Aglaia, Euphrosyne, and Thalia, who were painted holding each other by the hands, and who laughed, adorned in transparent and loose clothing? By these they intended to signify Liberality: for one of the sisters gives, the other receives, and the third returns the benefit, which conditions truly must be found in every perfect liberality. See how much great praise they bring to the

<summary>Alberti emphasizes the importance of geometry and classical literature for painters, using the famous "Calumny of Apelles" and the Three Graces as examples of powerful narrative invention.</summary>
<keywords>geometry, liberal arts, invention, Apelles, Lucian, Calumny, Three Graces, Hesiod, liberality</keywords>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>O N  P A I N T I N G</header>

master such inventions? And therefore I advise the studious painter to give himself as much as he can to the poets and rhetoricians, and to others learned in letters, and to make himself familiar and well-disposed toward them. For from such intelligent minds he will draw excellent ornaments, and he will be truly aided by them in these inventions, which in painting hold no small praise. Phidias <note>The most famous of Greek sculptors, though Alberti here refers to him as a painter, likely following ancient sources that blurred the lines between the arts</note>, an excellent painter, confessed to having learned from Homer the manner in which he should principally paint Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus</note> with majesty. I think that our painters will become even more copious and more capable by reading the poets, provided they are more studious of learning than of profit. But most of the time, those who are no less studious than desirous of learning become weary, more because they do not know the way or the method of learning the thing than they do from the labor of learning itself. And therefore let us begin to say in what manner we may become good masters in this art.

Let the principle be this: we must draw all the stages of learning from nature itself, and the rule for making the art perfect is acquired with diligence, with study, and with assiduity. I truly would wish that those who begin to want to learn to paint would do what I see observed by the masters of writing. For these men teach first to make all the characters of the letters separately, then they teach how to make syllables, and after this they teach how to put words together. Let our students, then, keep this rule in painting. Let them first teach the outlines of the surfaces, as if they were the a. b. c. of painting; then let them teach the joining of the surfaces; after this, the forms of all the members distinctly and separately, and let them learn by heart all the differences that can exist in the members: for they are both many and notable. There will be those who will have a hooked nose, others who will have it flat, twisted, or wide; others have a mouth that projects forward as if it were falling; others appear adorned by having thin lips; and finally all the members have a certain something of their own property, which if found either a little more or a little less, will then greatly vary that entire member. Indeed, we see beyond this how the same members in children <note>original: "putti"</note> appear to us round and, so to speak, made on a lathe and polished; and having then grown through age, they appear to us harsher and more defined. All these things, then, the studious painter will draw from nature itself, and he will assiduously examine for himself how each of them is, and he will continue with his eyes and with his mind all the time of his life in this investigation. For he will consider the lap of those who sit and how the legs, bending sweetly in a certain way, fall; he will consider the face and the entire attitude of one who stands upright; finally, there will be no part of which he does not know the function and the proportion, and he should love in all parts not only the likeness, but principally the beauty of things. Demetrius, that ancient painter, was much more curious in expressing the likeness of things than he was in recognizing the beautiful. Therefore, one must go about choosing from the most beautiful bodies the most praised parts; for this reason, it is necessary to apply every study and industry princi-

<summary>Alberti encourages painters to collaborate with literary scholars for inspiration and outlines a systematic method of learning to paint by imitating nature, starting from basic elements like an alphabet.</summary>

# BOOK THREE.

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>BOOK THREE.</header>

principally in knowing, learning, and expressing the beautiful. Although this task is the most difficult of all—because all the merits of beauty are not found in a single place, being rare and dispersed—one must nonetheless expend every effort in investigating and learning it. For he who has learned the most important things, and knows how to practice them, will then be able much more easily to handle matters of lesser importance as he pleases. Finally, nothing is found so difficult that it cannot be brought to effect through both study and assiduity. 

But so that your study is not useless or in vain, one must guard against that habit or custom of many, who by themselves and with their own wit <note>original: "ingegno"; refers to innate talent or imagination without external reference</note> seek to acquire praise in painting without wishing to portray anything from nature, either with their eyes or their minds. Indeed, such men do not learn to paint well, but rather accustom themselves to errors. For that idea of beauty does not reveal itself to the ignorant; it is scarcely discerned even by those who practice the art. 

Zeuxis <note>A celebrated Greek painter of the 5th century BCE, famous for his realism</note>, a most excellent painter, and more learned and capable than all others, when he had to make the panel that was to be placed publicly in the temple of Diana in Croton, did not trust in his own wit as almost all painters do in these times. He did not set himself foolishly to paint it, but because he thought that to find all that he sought—to make it as beautiful as possible—he could not find it with his own wit, nor could he find it in a single body even when portraying from nature. Therefore, he chose five young girls from all the youth of that city, the most beautiful of all others, so that he could then put into painting that which he had extracted of womanly beauty from them; and he truly acted as a wise man. 

For it often happens to painters—when they do not place before themselves the things they wish to portray or imitate, but seek to acquire praise by finding beauty through their own wit alone—that they not only fail to acquire the praise they seek through that labor, but they accustom themselves to a bad manner of painting, which they then cannot leave behind except with great effort, even if they desire to. But he who uses the practice of portraying everything from nature will make his hand so practiced in the good that everything he strives to do will appear natural. We see how much this quality is to be desired in painting. For if in a "history" <note>term: "historia"; a narrative painting depicting a significant scene from the Bible, mythology, or history, considered the highest form of art</note> there is portrayed the head of some man whom we know, even if there are other things of greater excellence by the master, nonetheless the recognized face of someone draws to itself the eyes of all the viewers: such is the grace and the power it has within itself by being portrayed from nature.

Therefore, let us portray from nature all those things that we have to paint, and from these let us choose those that are the most beautiful and the most worthy. But one must guard against what some do: that is, that we do not paint on panels that are too small. I would wish that you accustom yourself to large images, which should approach in size as much as possible that which you wish to create. For in small figures, major defects are more easily hidden, but in large figures, errors—even small ones—are greatly visible. Galen <note>The famous Greek physician and philosopher</note> wrote of having seen carved in a ring Phaethon <note>In mythology, the son of Helios who attempted to drive the sun-chariot</note> pulled by four horses, the reins and all the feet and all the chests of which could be seen...

<sig>f ij</sig>

<summary>Alberti argues that beauty must be sought by synthesizing the best parts of natural models rather than relying on imagination alone, citing the famous example of Zeuxis. He also warns against painting on a small scale, as larger works more honestly reveal a painter's errors.</summary>

<page-num>44</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>

distinctly. Let painters concede this praise to the engravers of gems <note>original: "intagliatori delle gioie"</note>, and let them exercise themselves in greater fields of glory. For those who know how to paint or sculpt large figures will easily and with a single stroke be able to make small ones excellently. But those who have accustomed their hand and their wit <note>original: "ingegno"</note> to these small things will easily err in larger ones. There are some who copy and portray the works of other painters, and seek to acquire praise in that thing. This is what they say the sculptor Calamides <note>original: "Camalide"; likely referring to the 5th-century BCE Greek sculptor Calamis</note> did, who made two sculpted cups, imitating Zenodorus <note>A famous sculptor of the 1st century CE known for his colossal statues</note> so perfectly that no difference could be discerned between the works. But painters are in the greatest error if they do not recognize that those who were true painters strove to represent a figure exactly as we see it painted by nature on that net or veil <note>A reference to Alberti’s "intercisione" or perspective grid used to map 3D objects onto a 2D surface</note>. And if it should benefit us to portray the works of others, as they show themselves with more steady patience than living things, I would prefer that we set before ourselves a moderately well-sculpted object rather than an excellently painted one. For in portraying something from paintings, we accustom the hand to represent a mere likeness; but from sculpted things, we learn both the likeness and the true lights <note>original: "i veri lumi"; the way light naturally falls on three-dimensional relief</note>. In bringing these lights together, it is very helpful to narrow the sharpness of the sight by squinting the eyelashes, so that the lights then appear somewhat darker and as if veiled. And perhaps it will benefit us more to practice in sculpting than in using the brush; for sculpture is more certain and easier than painting. Nor will it ever happen that anyone can paint anything well who does not know all its reliefs <note>original: "rilieui"; the three-dimensional contours and projections of a form</note> well. And reliefs are more easily found in sculpture than in painting. For this fact serves our purpose not a little: that one can see how in almost every age some mediocre sculptors have been found, but almost no painters who were not laughable and ignorant. Finally, whether one attends to painting or sculpture, we must always set before us some excellent and singular example to look upon and imitate; and in portraying it, I believe it is necessary to join diligence with speed in such a way that the painter never lifts the brush or the drawing tool from the work until he has first resolved and most excellently determined in his mind what he is about to do, and in what manner he can bring it to a good end. For it is a safer thing to amend with the mind than to later erase errors from the finished work. Beyond this, when we have become accustomed to portraying everything from nature, it will happen that we become much better masters than Asclepiodorus <note>A Greek painter of the 4th century BCE praised by Apelles for his proportions</note>, who they say was the swiftest of all masters in painting. For in that thing in which we have exercised ourselves many times, the wit becomes more ready, more apt, and faster; and that hand will be swiftest which is guided by the certain rule of the wit. And if some masters are sluggish, it happens to them for no other reason than that they are late and slow in attempting that thing which they have not first clearly mastered in their mind through study. And while they exercise themselves in those shadows of errors, they go about attempting and searching like fearful and blind men for the way with their brush, just as blind men do for the paths or exits they do not know—

<summary>Alberti argues that painters should study sculpture to better understand light and three-dimensional form, and emphasizes that mental preparation must precede the physical act of painting to ensure speed and accuracy.</summary>

...with their small brushes. Therefore, let no one ever set their hand to work except under the guidance of their intellect <note>original: "ingegno"; refers to the innate talent and mental faculty of the artist</note>, and ensure that it is well-practiced and instructed. But since the principal work of the painter is the <term>narrative painting</term> <note>original: "historia"; the highest genre of painting, depicting a significant story or event with multiple figures</note>, in which one must find every abundance and excellence of things, it is necessary to ensure that we know how to paint excellently—as far as the intellect can manage—not only man, but also the horse, the dog, and other animals, and all other things most worthy of being seen; so that in our narrative painting there is no lack of variety and abundance of things, without which no work is esteemed. It is truly a great thing, and hardly granted to any of the ancients, to have been—I will not say excellent in all things—but even a mediocre master. Nevertheless, I judge it right to strive and apply every study so that, through our negligence, we do not lack that which can bring us the greatest praise, or the greatest blame if we should make light of it.

Nicias, the Athenian painter, painted women most diligently; but Zeuxis, in painting the bodies of women, they say surpassed all others. Heraclides was excellent at painting ships. Serapion did not know how to paint men, yet he painted all other things very well. Dionysius knew how to paint nothing but men. Alexander, the one who painted the portico of Pompey, made all four-legged beasts excellently, and especially dogs. Aurelius, as one who was always in love, enjoyed only painting goddesses and expressing the faces of his beloveds in his portraits. Phidias labored more to demonstrate the majesty of the gods than the beauty of men. Euphranor had such a fantasy for representing the dignity of heroes that in that matter he was more excellent than the others. And so, not everyone can do all things well, since nature distributed to each intellect the property of its own gifts. We must not rest so much upon these gifts that we leave anything untried; rather, we must revere the gifts given to us by nature and increase them with industry, study, and exercise. Furthermore, we must not appear to omit through negligence anything that pertains to praise.

Ultimately, when we have to paint a narrative <note>original: "historia"</note>, we will first spend a long time thinking about what order or what methods we can use to make the composition most beautiful; and by making <term>sketches</term> <note>original: "schizzi"</note> and <term>models</term> <note>original: "modelli"; preparatory drawings or small three-dimensional figures</note> upon paper, we will examine both the whole narrative and every part of it; and in this, we shall ask advice from all our friends. Finally, we will strive to ensure that all things are so thought out and examined by us that in our work there is nothing that we do not know very well where it should be placed. And so that we may know this more certainly, it will be helpful to draw a <term>grid</term> <note>original: "rete"; literally "net," referring to the squared grid used to transfer a small drawing to a larger surface</note> over the models, so that in executing the work, things are placed exactly as they were drawn from the private examples, each in its proper place. And in bringing the work to completion, we will apply that diligence joined with that speed of execution <note>original: "celerità del fare"</note>, so that the weariness of others does not discourage us from finishing it, nor does the desire to finish too quickly make us rush. It is necessary...

<summary>Alberti discusses the importance of mastering various subjects—animals, ships, and humans—to create a rich "historia," citing classical examples of specialized artists. He emphasizes the need for careful preparation through sketches, models, and the use of a grid for accurate composition.</summary>
<keywords>narrative painting, historia, artistic specialization, preparatory sketches, models, perspective grid, diligence, classical painters</keywords>

<page-num>46</page-num>
<header>ON PAINTING</header>

sometimes to set aside the labor of the work and recreate the spirit. Nor should one do what many do, who set themselves to make many works, and begin this one, and leave the one already started imperfect. But those works that you have begun, you ought to finish entirely in every part. Apelles <note>A celebrated painter of ancient Greece, often cited as the pinnacle of artistic skill.</note> replied to one who showed him a painting of his, saying: "I painted it quickly just now": "Even without you saying so, it was clearly visible; indeed, I marvel that you have not painted an infinite number in this manner." I have seen some painters and sculptors, and orators and poets as well—if any are to be found in this our age who can be called orators or poets—having set themselves with most ardent study to make some work, who, once that ardor of the intellect <note>original: "ingegno"</note> has failed, leave the begun and rough work imperfect, and driven by a new desire, set themselves to wanting to do some other newer thing: which men I certainly blame. For all those who desire that their works be pleasing and dear to posterity must first think very well upon said work, and bring it with the greatest diligence to perfection. Since in many things, diligence is no less pleasing than any talent whatsoever. But one must flee that superfluous superstition of those—to call it such—who, while they want their labors to have not even the slightest defect, and seek for them to be even too polished, act in such a way that their works seem consumed by old age before they are finished. The ancient painters used to blame Protogenes <note>A Greek painter and contemporary of Apelles, known for his extreme fastidiousness.</note> because he never knew how to take his hands off a panel: and reasonably so, certainly. For it is necessary to strive to put as much diligence into things as is sufficient, according to the value of the intellect. But to want in every thing more than what you are able, or what is fitting, is a thing of an intellect rather obstinate than diligent. One must therefore put a moderate diligence into things, and ask the opinion of friends; indeed, in putting said labor into effect, it is well to stand and listen, and call almost everyone from time to time to see it: and in this way the work of the painter is bound to be pleasing to the multitude. The judgment and the censure of the multitude will not then be despised, when you are still able to satisfy <note>Likely a variant or typo for 'sodisfare' (satisfy).</note> the diverse opinions. They say that Apelles used to hide himself behind the panel, so that those who looked at it could speak more freely, and he could listen more honestly to the defects of his works that they recounted. I would wish, therefore, that our painters would stay in the open to hear often, and to seek out everyone who might tell them freely what they think of it: since this helps to understand the truth of things, and to acquire much of a certain grace. For there is no one who does not attribute it to an honorable thing to have to say his opinion regarding the labors of others. Beyond this, there is no reason to doubt that the judgment of those who blame, and who are envious, can detract at all from the praises of the painter. Let the painter, therefore, stand to listen to everyone, and first examine the thing with himself, and amend it.

These are the things that it seemed to me I had to say of painting in these my commentaries. And if these things are such that they bring to painters...

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>BOOK THREE.</header>

benefit or utility, I expect as the principal reward for my labors that they might portray me in their histories, so that in this way they may demonstrate to those who come after that they have been mindful and grateful for the benefit, and show that I have been a devoted student of this art. And if I have not satisfied all that they expected of me, at the least let them not blame me for having had the daring to set myself to such an undertaking. For if my talent <note>original: "ingegno" — referring to innate creative power or wit</note> has not been able to bring to completion that which is praiseworthy to attempt, let them remember that in the greatest things, it is customary to attribute praise for having wanted to apply oneself to that which is most difficult. Perhaps others will follow who will supply what I have lacked, and who will be able to be of much more use to painters in this most excellent and worthy art; if they should happen to succeed me, I pray them, as much as I know how and am able, that they take up this labor with a joyful and ready spirit, in which they may both exercise their talents and lead this most noble art to the height of excellence. I, nonetheless, shall have the pleasure of having been the first to have acquired the palm <note>A symbol of victory or triumph</note> in having exerted myself to write upon this most ingenious art. Which truly difficult undertaking, if I have not known how to lead it to that perfection of expectation held by those who read, the fault should be given to nature rather than to me, who seems to have imposed that law upon things, that there is no art which did not take its beginnings from defective things: for it is said that nothing is born perfect. And those who come after me, if any shall come who are of greater study and talent than I, shall perhaps lead this art of painting to the highest perfection.

<meta>This page begins a new section: a dedicatory letter from the translator Cosimo Bartoli to the sculptor Bartolomeo Ammannati, introducing Leon Battista Alberti's treatise on sculpture (De Statua).</meta>

# COSIMO BARTOLI
## TO THE VIRTUOUS BARTOLOMEO AMMANNATI
-> most excellent architect and sculptor. <-

I know well, my most virtuous Master Bartolomeo, that for you—who are today most excellent and highly practiced in both architecture and sculpture—there is no need for the instructions that the most judicious LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI gave regarding the statue in his own time. However, I have judged that it should not displease you that such instructions be addressed to you, as to an excellent judge of the fine genius of the said LEON BATTISTA. He, in those times when there was little or no knowledge of sculpture—since almost all the good arts and disciplines in Italy had been annihilated, or rather completely extinguished, by the floods of the Barbarians <note>A common Renaissance trope referring to the "Dark Ages" following the fall of Rome</note>—strove with his most refined judgment to open a simple and secure path for inexperienced youths who delighted in this most noble art, and to awaken them to work well within it using firm and stable rules. 

This was perhaps a good reason why, in the course of time, such progress was made in the said art as we see today. For in this century of ours, we need not envy the most beautiful statues of the most praised ancient Roman sculptors, as was demonstrated first by our Donato <note>Donatello (c. 1386–1466)</note>, and not many years ago by the forever divine Michelangelo Buonarroti, and after him Baccio Bandinelli, Benvenuto Cellini, and lastly yourself. 

In such a manner, besides the many other statues by all of you that are to be found, one sees—not without great wonder from men—upon the piazza of the royal palace of Their Highnesses <note>The Piazza della Signoria in Florence, outside the Palazzo Vecchio</note>: the most beautiful *Judith*, the most marvelous colossus of *David*, the most robust and fierce *Hercules*, the most masterly *Perseus* with its most praised ornaments, and your *Neptune*, the greatest of all the others, carved along with the other three statues with such masterly art out of one single piece of marble. These works do not only cause those who look at them attentively to marvel, but to remain almost stunned, considering the genius, the art, the industry, the diligence, the love, and the never-sufficiently-praised mastery of all of you. 

Be content, then, that these instructions—whatever they may be, yet surely useful to inexperienced youth—should emerge from the shadows under your name and come into the light; and remember to love your friends as you usually do, among whom I do not consider myself the least. Stay healthy.

<summary>Cosimo Bartoli dedicates his translation of Alberti's work on sculpture to Bartolomeo Ammannati, praising the revival of the arts in Florence and listing the masterpieces standing in the Piazza della Signoria.</summary>
<keywords>Cosimo Bartoli, Bartolomeo Ammannati, Leon Battista Alberti, Sculpture, Florence, Michelangelo, Donatello, Benvenuto Cellini, Baccio Bandinelli, Piazza della Signoria, Neptune Fountain</keywords>

### LEON

# BATTISTA ALBERTI

## ON THE STATUE.

**I** THINK that the arts of those who set themselves to wanting to express and portray with their works the effigies and likenesses of bodies created by nature, had their origin in this: that they perhaps occasionally perceived in tree trunks, or in the earth, or in many other such bodies, certain outlines which—by changing something within them—they could make similar to the faces created by nature. They began, therefore, to consider with the mind and to examine with every diligence, attempting and striving to see what they could either add or take away, or what was required, to ensure that nothing seemed to be missing to make that effigy appear almost true and proper, and to finish it perfectly. Therefore, as the object itself guided them, correcting in such appearances now the lines and now the surfaces, and cleaning and polishing them, they achieved their desire, and truly not without their own delight. Nor is it a wonder that in doing these such things, the studies of men grew from one day to the next, until—without seeing any longer in the raw materials those initial aids of partial likenesses—they could express in them any effigy whatsoever, though some in one way and others in another; for they did not all learn to do this by one and the same path or rule. For some began to give perfection to their initiated works both by adding and by taking away, as do those who, working in wax, stucco, or clay, are called by our people "masters of stucco" <note>Alberti is referring to modelers or plasticators who work with soft materials</note>. Some others began to do this only by taking away—as if by removing that which is superfluous in the said material, they carve and make appear in the marble a form or figure of a man which was previously hidden and "in potential" <note>A reference to the Aristotelian concept that the form exists within the stone before the sculptor reveals it</note> there: these we call sculptors. Brothers to these are perhaps those who go about carving into seals the outlines of faces that were hidden therein. The third species is that of

<vocab>Leon Battista Alberti, On the Statue, sculpture, masters of stucco, sculptors, marble, wax, earth/clay</vocab>

<summary>Alberti theorizes that sculpture originated from humans noticing natural shapes in wood or stone that resembled faces and then refining them. He distinguishes between three types of artists: those who add and subtract material (modelers), those who only subtract (sculptors), and those who engrave seals.</summary>
<keywords>origins of art, mimesis, sculpture, modeling, Aristotelian potentiality, marble carving, Leon Battista Alberti</keywords>

<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>OF THE</header>

those who create certain works only by adding to them, such as the **goldsmiths** <note>original: "argentieri" — specifically silversmiths or those working in precious metals</note>, who, by beating the silver with hammers and extending or widening it to that size of form they desire, always add something to it until they have made the image they want. There will perhaps be some who think that in the number of these one should also include the **painters**, as they too make use of adding colors in their works. But if you ask them, they will answer you that they do not strive so much to imitate those lights of the bodies they see with the eye by means of adding or taking away something from their works, as they do by another artifice of their own, proper and peculiar to them. But we shall treat the painter another time. Truly, those whom I have recounted, although by different paths, nevertheless all follow this goal: to make all their works, which they have set themselves to do, appear as much as possible to those who look at them as very natural and similar to the true bodies made by nature. In doing which, certainly, if they go searching for and taking up that straight and known reason and rule which we shall describe, they will err—truly, I say, they will err much less—and their works will turn out better in every respect. What do you think? If the **carpenters** <note>original: "legnaiuoli"</note> did not have the **square**, the **plumb line**, the **chalk line**, the **level** <note>original: "archipenzolo" — an A-frame leveling tool used before the spirit level</note>, and the **compasses** <note>original: "seste"</note> to make the circle—by which instruments they can arrange angles, level, straighten, and finish their works—do you believe they would have finally succeeded in being able to make them most conveniently and without errors? And that the **statuary** <note>sculptor</note> could make so many excellent and marvelous works by chance, rather than by means of a firm rule and certain guide, drawn and taken from reason? I am resolved to this: that from any art or discipline whatsoever, certain principles, perfections, and rules are drawn from nature; which if we, applying care and diligence, wish to examine and use, everything we set ourselves to do will undoubtedly be done very well. For just as we had from nature herself that from a stump, or a piece of earth, or other matter (as has been said), we recognized—by means of certain outlines found in those materials—that we could make some things similar to her own; so also the same nature has shown us certain aids and certain means by which we can work what we wish with a certain path and sure rule. When we pay attention to these and wish to make use of them, we can very easily and with the greatest convenience arrive at the supreme degree of this art. Now, we must declare what those aids are that are given by nature to the statuaries. Since the statuaries follow after the imitation of likenesses, or rather similarities, one must begin from similarity itself. I could here discourse upon the reason for similarities—that is, why that which we see happen through nature occurs: that she is accustomed in every sort of animal to perpetually observe that each one, namely in its own kind, is in any thing whatsoever very similar to the other. And on the other hand, there is not found (as they say) anyone among the whole number of men who has a voice totally similar to the voice of another, or a nose to a nose, or other parts or similar things. Add to this that the faces of those...

<summary>Alberti argues that all artistic imitation—whether by adding, subtracting, or both—requires firm rules and geometric tools to achieve perfection. He introduces the concept of "similarity," noting that while nature follows general patterns, every individual remains unique.</summary>

whom we have seen as infants, and whom we have then known as
little boys, and later seen as youths, and now see already as old men, we no longer re-
cognize them, so much and such a variety of lines having changed in their faces from day to day
by means of age; from which we can conclude that in
those forms of bodies some things are found which, with the space and mo-
ment of time, go on varying: and that in said forms there is found
also a certain something natural and proper, which continually main-
tains itself stable and firm, so as to preserve the likeness of its kind.
We, therefore, setting aside other things, shall treat very briefly
of those which will be to our purpose, to declare what we have
begun to treat.

The method, and the reason, or rule of capturing likenesses among
sculptors <note>original: "statuarij"</note> is done, if I understand it well, by means of two resolutions; one
of which is, that that likeness, or image, which we shall finally
have made of the animal—as, for example, that of a
man—it should be as similar as possible to said man, nor does it matter to us
that it represents the face of Socrates more than that of Plato, or of an-
other man known to us: since it will seem to us to have done enough if
we have achieved that such a work resembles a man, even
if not known by us. The other resolution is that of those who wish
to represent not so much the likeness of a man in general,
as that of a particular one, as one might say that of Caesar,
or of Cato, standing in this manner, with this clothing, sitting
in the tribunal, or haranguing the people: these people striving to imi-
tate and to express all that habit or attitude of that body, or
the likeness of some other personage known to them. To these two
resolutions or deliberations, to treat the matter as briefly as pos-
sible, correspond two things, namely **measurement** <note>original: "misura"</note>, and the **placing of limits** <note>original: "por de’ termini" — referring to defining the boundaries or coordinates of a form in space</note>. Of
these things, then, we have to treat, what they are, and how they can
serve us to bring the work to perfection: if first, however, I say what
utility is drawn from them: for they truly have a certain mar-
velous and almost incredible power. Because he who shall be instructed in these
things will be able to mark and observe and note with certain very firm
signs the lineaments, the sites, and the positions of the parts of any
body, so that I do not say the day after tomorrow, but a thousand years from now, provided that body
is found in that place, he will be able to establish and place it precisely, and ex-
actly at his will in that same position and site in which it was found
the first time. In such a manner that there will be no part, even the smallest, of said
body that is not replaced and relocated to its original site and point in the
air in which it was originally found. As if by chance,
with your finger extended, you wished to point out the star of Mercury, or the
new Moon as it was rising, at what point in the air would be found there
the angle of your knee, or finger, or elbow, or any other such thing.
You will certainly be able to do it with these our aids or means in a manner that
no error will follow, however small; and you will be certain that you will not have

<summary>Alberti distinguishes between representing a general human likeness and a specific individual portrait. He introduces the two fundamental principles of his method: measurement (dimensio) and the positioning of parts (finitio), claiming they allow for the exact reproduction of a pose across time.</summary>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>OF THE</header>

there is no doubt that the matter stands in that way. Besides this, if by chance it should happen that I had covered with wax, or with earth placed upon it, a statue by Phidias <note>A famous Greek sculptor of the 5th century BCE, known for the Statue of Zeus at Olympia.</note>, until that work had become a thick column, you would be able—with these aids and with these rules—to affirm this for certain: to know where, by boring into it with a drill, you would find in this place the pupil of the eye, and touch it without doing it any harm; and where in that other place would be the navel <note>original: "bellico"</note>; and where in another would finally be the big toe, and all other things similar to these. From this, it will occur to you that you will have gained a most certain knowledge of all the angles and all the lines, how far they are from one another, and where they meet together. You will be able, in every way, by drawing from life or from a model, not only to portray or paint, but also to put into writing the drawing of the lines, the circumferences of the circles, and the positions of the parts. In such a manner, you will not doubt that by means of these your tools and favors, another could be made most similar to that one, or a smaller one, or finally one of such great size—even one of a hundred cubits <note>original: "braccia," a traditional Italian unit of measurement roughly equal to an arm's length.</note>—or such that I will dare to say that you will not doubt that with these your aids one could be made as large as Mount Caucasus; provided that for these greatest undertakings you do not lack the means. 

And what you will perhaps marvel at even more, will be that one could make half of this your statue on the island of Paros <note>Famous for its fine white marble.</note>, if it suits you, and the other half you could carve and finish in the mountains of Carrara <note>The primary source of marble for Italian Renaissance sculptors.</note>. In such a way that the joints and the fittings of all the parts, with the whole body and face of the image, will unite and correspond to the living person or to the model according to which it was made. And the rule and the method of doing such a great thing you shall find so easy, and so clear and expeditious, that, as for me, I believe that they will hardly be able to err, except for those who by set purpose or in trial have not wished to obey what has been said. 

I do not say by this that I am teaching you the craft <note>original: "artificio"</note> by which you can totally make all the universal likenesses of bodies, or that by this one learns how to know how to make and to portray whatever diversities or similarities there may be. For I confess that I do not make a profession of teaching you by this path the way you must make the face and the countenance of Hercules while he fights with Antaeus <note>In Greek mythology, Hercules wrestled the giant Antaeus, who gained strength from touching the earth.</note>, so that he represents as much as possible the bravery and the fierceness appropriate to that; or how you must make him with a benign and joyful and laughing aspect when he caresses his Deianira <note>The wife of Hercules.</note>, truly very dissimilar from the other aspect, even if it represents the same face of Hercules. But occurring in all bodies are diverse and various figures and attitudes, by means of the turnings or bendings of the limbs and their positions; because in one way the features <note>original: "lineamenti"</note> and the outlines of one who stands on foot are seen to be finished, in another way those of one who sits, and in another those of one who lies down, and in another those of people who turn or lower themselves toward one side or the other, and similarly also those of other attitudes. Of which things it is our intention to treat: that is, in what way, with what firm, certain, and true rule, said attitudes can be imitated and portrayed. Which rules, as I said,

<summary>Alberti argues that his mathematical system of measurement allows a sculptor to replicate a statue perfectly at any scale or even in separate locations. He distinguishes between the objective measurement of proportions and the artistic skill required to depict emotion and specific mythological scenes.</summary>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>S T A T U E.</header>

are two: namely, <term>measurement</term> <note>original: "misura"</note> and the <term>placing of limits</term> <note>original: "porre de' termini"</note>. We shall treat first, then, of measurement, which certainly is nothing other than a stable, firm, and certain observation and notation, by which one knows and records in numbers and measures the <term>habit</term> <note>The general physical build or posture of a figure.</note>, <term>proportion</term>, and <term>correspondence</term> that all the parts of the body have among themselves, one with another, as much in height as in thickness, and that which they also have with the entire length of said body.

And this observation or knowledge is achieved by means of two things: namely, with a large <term>ruler</term> <note>original: "regolo"</note> and with two movable <term>squares</term> <note>original: "squadre"</note>. With the said ruler we measure and take the lengths of the limbs, and with the squares all the other diameters of the said limbs. Along the length of this ruler, a straight line is drawn as long as the length of the body we wish to measure—that is, from the top of the head to the sole of the foot. Wherefore it must be noted that to measure a man of small stature one must take a smaller ruler, and for a man of great stature one must take a larger, that is, a longer one. But regardless of the length of such a ruler, we shall divide it into six equal parts, and these parts we shall call <term>feet</term>, and from the name of the feet we shall call this ruler the <term>foot-rule</term> <note>original: "modine del piede." Alberti is creating a proportional scale rather than a fixed unit like a modern meter.</note>. We shall then subdivide each of these feet again into ten equal parts, which small parts we shall call <term>inches</term> <note>original: "once." Note that these are decimal inches (1/10th of a foot) rather than the standard 1/12th.</note>.

The entire length of this rule will therefore be sixty of these inches. Again, we shall subdivide each of these inches into another ten equal parts, which smaller parts I call <term>minutes</term>. From these divisions, it will follow that the entire rule will be six feet, and these will be 600 minutes, and each single foot will be 100 minutes. We shall make use of this rule in this manner: if by chance we wish to measure a human body, we shall place this rule near it, and we will observe and note with it each limit of the limbs—that is, how high it is from the sole of the foot upwards, and how far one limb is from another limb. For example: how far it is from the knee to the navel, or to the pit of the throat, or similar points—namely, how many inches and how many minutes. Sculptors and painters should not mock this method, for it is most useful and entirely necessary. Because once the number of inches and minutes of all the limbs is known, we will have the determination of those limbs ready and most expedited, so that no error can be made. Nor should you care to listen to that arrogant man who might say: "This limb is too long," or "This other is too short"; for your rule will be that with which you have determined and given order to the whole, which will tell you the truth more than anything else. And I do not doubt at all that, having well examined these things, you will realize for yourself that this rule will bring you infinite other conveniences; for through it you will come to know the method you can hold to establish and determine your lengths in a smaller statue, and likewise in a larger one. For if you had to make, by chance, a statue of 10 <term>cubits</term> <note>original: "braccia." A traditional Italian unit of length.</note>, you will see to having your ruler or rule be 10 cubits long, and having divided it into six equal parts, which correspond among themselves just as...

<summary>Alberti describes the "modine," a proportional ruler divided into 6 feet, 60 inches, and 600 minutes, used to record the exact dimensions of the human body. He argues that this mathematical system is essential for artists to maintain accuracy and scale their work regardless of the statue's final size.</summary>

<page-num>54</page-num>
<header>O F</header>

the parts of the smaller <term>modine</term> <note>A proportional measuring rod or scale.</note> correspond to one another; and having done the same for the <term>ounces</term> <note>original: "once"; here, a proportional subdivision of the "foot" on the scale.</note> and the <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "minuti"; the smallest proportional subdivision.</note>, you will see that the use, method, and rule of employing it will be the same as that of the other larger scale. This is because half of the numbers of the larger scale have the same proportion to its whole as half of the numbers of the smaller scale have to the whole of the smaller one. And therefore, it will be necessary for you to have constructed your scale in this manner.

Now let us come to treat of the <term>squares</term> <note>L-shaped measuring tools, similar to a carpenter's square.</note>. We make two of them, one of which shall be made in this way: that is, of two <term>rules</term> <note>Straight-edged bars.</note> A, B, and C. We call A-B the "upright rule" and B-C the other rule, which serves as the "base." The size of these rules must be such that each of their bases is at least no less than 15 ounces of its kind. By "of its kind," I mean of that same sort of ounces that you have made on your scale, according to the body you wish to measure; which, as I told you above, will be large on a large scale and small on a small one. These ounces, then—let them be as thick as one likes—marked from the scale with their points and minutes, you shall begin to count on the base from the point of the angle B, going toward C, equal (as was said) to the ounces and minutes of the scale.

This square marked in this way—as, for example, is A-B-C—we place over another similar square, called D-F-G, in such a way that the whole of G-F serves as a straight line and as a base for both. And let us say that I wish to measure the diameter of the thickness of the head H-I-K. Moving them, I will then distance or bring closer to said head the upright rules A-B and D-F of both squares, until they touch the thickness of the head, applying the lines of the bases of said squares interchangeably to a determined and identical straight line. In this way, by means of the points H-I of the contacts made by said squares—or to put it better, the upright rules of the squares—I will see what the diameter of said head shall be.

<page-num>55</page-num>
<header>S T A T U E. 55</header>

And with this same order or rule, I will be able to most exactly take all the thicknesses and widths of whatever member one desires. I could recount many conveniences and many services that can be derived from this <term>modine</term> <note>The proportional measuring rod previously described.</note> and from these squares, if I did not think it more convenient to remain quiet. And especially since such things are of the sort that any mediocre intellect will be able to consider and notice for himself in what way he can measure the diameter of any member; as would be, by way of example, if he wished to know the diameter between one ear and the other—that is, from the right to the left—and in what place it intersects the other diameter that goes from the forehead to the nape, or similar things. Ultimately, this craftsman, if he believes me, will make use of this scale and these squares as most faithful and true guides and counselors, not only when he sets himself to do the work, or while doing it, but he will prepare himself much earlier with the help of these instruments to set himself to the work, so that no part of the statue is found, even the smallest, that he has to make, which he has not considered, examined, and made most familiar to himself.

Take this as an example: who would dare to profess to be a master of shipbuilding if he did not know what the parts of a ship are, and in what way one ship is different from another, and what those parts are that are expected in any sort of vessel? And who among our sculptors—let him be as considered and shrewd as he likes—who, if he were asked, "For what reason have you made this member in this way?" or "What proportion does it have with this or that other member?" or "What is the proportion of these members to the whole habit <note>The general build or constitution of the body.</note> of the body?"—who, I say, would be the one who has been so diligent and accurate that he has considered and noticed everything enough, or as is reasonable, and as is expected of one who wants to know how to perform his art well, of which he makes profession? Arts are undoubtedly learned principally through reason, rule, and the path one has for doing them. Nor will there ever be anyone who performs any art well, whatever it may be, if he has not first learned the parts of that art.

We have treated of measurement, and how one may take it well both with the scale and with the squares; now it remains for us to treat of the **placing of boundaries** <note>original: "porre i termini"; referring to the definition of outlines and the positioning of points in space.</note>. The placing of boundaries is that determination or establishment made by drawing all the lines and unfolding them, of fixing the angles, the hollows, and the reliefs, placing them all with a true and certain rule in their proper places. And a determination made in this way will then be excellent when, from a plumb line of a certain center placed in the middle, all the distances and all the extremities of all the lines are noted and marked, down to the final boundaries of the said body. Between the measurement mentioned above and this placing of boundaries, there is this difference: that measurement follows behind and gives and takes certain more common and universal things, which are more firmly and with more stability implanted by nature in bodies—such as the lengths, thicknesses, and widths of the members; and the placing of boundaries gives us the momentary varieties of the members caused by new attitudes and movements of the parts, and teaches us how to set and place them.

To know, therefore, how to do this thing well, we have need of an instru—

He introduces the distinction between "measurement" (fixed proportions) and the "placing of boundaries" (capturing movement and specific poses).</summary>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>OF THE</header>

instrument, which instrument is made of three parts or members; that is, it is made of a horizon, a <term>linda</term> <note>original: "linda"; a pivoting ruler or radial arm.</note>, and a plumb line. The horizon is a flat surface with a circle drawn upon it, divided into equal parts and marked with their numbers. The <term>linda</term> is a straight ruler, which has one of its ends fixed in the center of the said circle, and the other rotates around at your pleasure, so that it can be moved to any of the divisions made in the circle. The plumb line is a thread, or a straight line, that falls at a right angle from the tip of the <term>linda</term> down to the ground, or onto the floor upon which the statue stands—or rather the figure in which one must determine and place the boundaries of the limbs and the lines already mentioned. And this instrument is made in this way: Take a flat board, well-planed and clean, and draw a circle on it, the diameter of which is three feet; and the circumference of said circle, at its outer edge, is divided into equal parts, similar to those that astrologers <note>In this period, "astrologer" often referred to astronomers or makers of scientific instruments.</note> draw on astrolabes, which parts I call degrees. And each of these degrees I subdivide again into as many other parts as I wish; for example, let each be subdivided into six smaller parts, which I call minutes. And to all the degrees I add their numbers, that is 1, 2, 3, and 4, and the others in order, until I have placed their numbers on all the degrees. This circle, thus made and ordered, is called the **horizon**. And to this circle I fit the movable <term>linda</term>, which is made in this way: I take a thin, straight little ruler, three feet long of its kind, and with one of its ends I fix it with a pin to the center of its horizon or circle, so that it stays firm there, yet in such a way that it can rotate; and the other end will reach outside the circle, so that it can be freely moved and transported all around. On this <term>linda</term> I mark with points those ounces that fit there, similar to those of the <term>modine</term> <note>The proportional scale described in previous chapters.</note> mentioned above; and these ounces I also subdivide again into smaller equal parts, as was done on the <term>modine</term>, and starting from the center I add to the ounces their numbers: 1, 2, 3, and 4. To this <term>linda</term> I attach a small lead weight, and this whole instrument made of the horizon, the <term>linda</term>, and the plumb line, I call the **definer** <note>original: "diffinitore"; Alberti's famous device for measuring 3D forms.</note>; and it is exactly as I have described it.

I use this **definer** in this way: Let us say that the living person, or the model, from which I wish to take the measurements is a statue by Phidias <note>A famous ancient Greek sculptor.</note>, which stands beside a cart and restrains a horse with the left hand. I place the **definer** on top, above the head of the statue, in such a manner that it stays level in every direction from its center, placed on the top of the statue, where I fix it with a pin. And I note and observe the point on the head of said statue where the center of the circle stays fixed, and I mark it, placing a needle or a pin there. Then, from the determined place on the horizon, I establish and set—by turning the instrument—the first degree already marked, so that I know which way it is facing. This is done in this way: I lead this movable ruler, that is the <term>linda</term> to which the thread or plumb line is attached, to where it reaches the first degree of the horizon; and having stopped it there, I turn it with the whole circle of the horizon around until the thread of the plumb line reaches or touches some principal part of this statue, such as...

<meta>Continuing from page 56, where Alberti describes the construction of the "horizon" (a circular scale) and the "linda" (a pivoting arm) used to measure the proportions of a statue.</meta>

<header>S T A T U E.</header>

...would be to say a limb more known than all the others, namely the finger of the right hand: from here I will be able, how and toward where it pleases me, to move this <term>definer</term> <note>original: "diffinitore"; Alberti’s name for his specialized measuring instrument.</note> anew each time, and bring it back—even if it has been moved—exactly as it stood before atop the said statue; that is, so that the pin from the top of the statue’s head, penetrating through the center of the definer, and the plumb line that fell from the first degree of the horizon, returns hanging to touch that same thumb of the right hand. These things being placed and ordered: Let us say that I wish to mark or note the angle of the left elbow, and learn it by heart, and even write it down; I do it in this way: I fix this definer and instrument with its center placed atop the head of the statue, in this stated condition and place, such that the board on which the horizon is drawn remains entirely firm and immobile: and I turn the <term>linda</term> <note>The pivoting radial arm of the instrument.</note> around until the plumb line touches that left elbow of the said statue that we wished to note. From doing this in this manner, three things will occur that will serve our purpose: First, we will note how far the <term>linda</term> on the horizon is from that place where we first moved it, noting which degree of the horizon the said <term>linda</term> strikes, whether the twentieth, or the thirtieth, or any other such number. Secondly, you will note in the inches and minutes marked on the <term>linda</term> how far that elbow is distant from the middle center of the circle. Lastly, for the third, you will note—having placed the <term>modine</term> <note>original: "modine"; a vertical measuring rod or ruler.</note> on the level of the floor of the said statue—how many inches and how many minutes the said elbow is

<summary>Alberti explains how to use the 'diffinitore' to record the precise three-dimensional coordinates of a statue's limbs, using the left elbow as a practical example. By measuring the rotation (horizon), distance from center (linda), and height (modine), a sculptor can map any point in space.</summary>
