--- Page 250 ---
<meta>The previous page (57) described the practical application of the "definer" to measure specific points on a statue, such as an elbow. This page (56) appears to be the formal introduction and technical construction guide for the instrument itself, explaining its three components: the horizon, the linda, and the plumb line.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>O F</header>
<meta>catchword: would do</meta>

ment, which instrument is made of three parts, or members; that is, it is made of a <term>horizon</term> <note>original: "orizonte"; a circular base plate used as a coordinate system.</note>, a line, and a <term>plumb line</term> <note>original: "piombo".</note>. The <term>horizon</term> is a flat plane with a circle drawn upon it, divided into equal parts and marked with their numbers. The <term>linda</term> <note>original: "linda"; a pivoting ruler or radial arm.</note> is a straight ruler, which stays fixed with one of its ends in the center of the said circle, and the other turns around at your pleasure, such that it can be moved to each of the divisions made in the circle. The <term>plumb line</term> is a thread, or a straight line that falls at a right angle from the tip of the <term>linda</term> down to the ground, or onto the pavement upon which the statue or figure rests, in which one must determine and place the limits of the limbs and the lines already mentioned. And this instrument is made in this way: Take a flat board, well-planed and clean, and draw a circle upon it, the diameter of which shall be three feet, and the circumference of said circle, at its edge, shall be divided into equal parts, similar to those that astrologers <note>In this period, "astrologer" often referred to what we now call astronomers.</note> draw on <term>astrolabes</term>, which parts I call <term>degrees</term> <note>original: "gradi".</note>: and each of these <term>degrees</term> I subdivide again into as many other parts as I wish—for example, let each be subdivided into six smaller parts, which I call <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "minuti".</note>: and to all the degrees I add their numbers, that is 1, 2, 3, and 4, and the others in order, until I have placed their numbers for all the degrees. This circle, thus made and ordered, is called the <term>horizon</term>. And to this circle I fit the movable <term>linda</term>, which is made in this way: I take a thin, straight little ruler, three feet long of its kind, and with one of its ends I fix it with a <term>pin</term> <note>original: "perno".</note> to the center of its <term>horizon</term> or circle, in such a way that it stays firm there, yet also so that it can be turned; and the other end will reach outside the circle, so that it can be freely moved and transported all around. On this <term>linda</term> I mark with points those inches that fit there, similar to those of the <term>modine</term> <note>original: "modine"; a standard measuring scale or ruler mentioned previously.</note> spoken of above; and these inches I also subdivide again into smaller but equal parts, as was done with the <term>modine</term>, and starting from the center I add to the inches their numbers, 1, 2, 3, and 4. To this <term>linda</term> I attach a small <term>plumb weight</term> <note>original: "piombinetto".</note>, and this whole instrument made of the <term>horizon</term>, the <term>linda</term>, and the <term>plumb line</term>, I call the <term>definer</term> <note>original: "diffinitore".</note>; and it is exactly as I have described it.

I use this <term>definer</term> in this way: Suppose that the living person, or the model, from which I wish to take the measurements is a statue by <term>Phidias</term> <note>A famous Greek sculptor of the 5th century BCE.</note>, which stands beside a chariot and restrains a horse with the left hand. I place the <term>definer</term> on top, above the head of the statue, in such a manner that it stays level in every direction from its center, placed on the top of the statue, where I fix it with a <term>pin</term>: and I note and observe the point on the head of said statue where the center of the circle stays fixed, and I mark it, putting a needle or a <term>pin</term> there. Then, from the determined place on the <term>horizon</term>, I establish and place, by turning the instrument, the first marked <term>degree</term>, so that I know toward where it is turned. Which is done in this way: I lead this movable ruler, that is the <term>linda</term>, to which the thread or <term>plumb line</term> is attached, to where it reaches the first <term>degree</term> of the <term>horizon</term>, and having fixed it there, I turn it with the whole circle of the <term>horizon</term> around until the thread of the <term>plumb line</term> reaches or touches some principal part of this statue, such as...

<summary>Leon Battista Alberti describes the construction of the "definer" (diffinitore), a precision instrument consisting of a circular scale (horizon), a rotating arm (linda), and a plumb line. He explains how to calibrate the device atop a statue's head to record three-dimensional coordinates of the figure's limbs.</summary>

<header>O F  T H E</header>

reliefs above the said floor. And you shall write these measurements upon your sheet or notebook in this manner, that is: The angle of the left elbow in the **horizon** <note>The circular degree scale of the instrument.</note> comes to 10 degrees and 5 minutes; on the <term>linda</term> <note>original: "linda"; the rotating radial arm or ruler.</note> at 7 degrees and 3 minutes; and from the floor on the <term>modine</term> <note>original: "modine"; here referring to the graduated scale on the plumb line or a measuring rod used to find height.</note> at 40 degrees and 4 minutes. And so, with this same rule, you will be able to note all the other most notable parts of the said statue or model, how and where they are found—as for example the angles of the knees, and of the shoulders, and the other reliefs, or similar things. 

But if you wish to note or observe the concavities or the depths, when they are so hidden or recessed that the <term>plumb line</term> <note>original: "filo del piombo".</note> cannot reach them—as happens in the hollow between the shoulders at the small of the back—you shall note them conveniently in this way: You will add to the **linda** another plumb line, which falls to the said concavity, and let it be as far as you wish from the first line, for it does not matter. Because by means of these two plumb lines, it will happen that by their straightness—as they are hung from a rod on the flat surface above—which cuts or intersects both these lines of the threads and goes penetrating even into the center of the statue, you will be able, I say, to find through their operation how much the second line, or the thread of the second plumb, is closer than the first to the center of the <term>definitor</term> <note>original: "diffinitore"; the name of the entire measuring device.</note>, which is called the center plumb.

If these things are sufficiently understood, you will easily have learned that which we warned you of above: that is, if by chance the said statue had been covered to a certain thickness with wax or clay, you could, I say, by piercing it in a prompt, certain, and most convenient way, go to find immediately any point or limit noted on the statue. For it is manifest that with the turning of this **linda**, a plumb line is made such that it describes a curved line in the manner of the surface of a cylinder, by which cylinder this statue is encompassed and surrounded. If this is so, in the same way that you could, by that same rule, penetrate the air to note and observe point T while your statue was not occupied by any wax or clay—which for the sake of argument let us say was the relief of the chin—you will be able with the same rule to do the same, penetrating the wax or the clay just as when you penetrated the air, accounting that the air has been converted into wax or clay. 

By means of these things that have been recounted, it will happen that one can most conveniently do what was said a little above: that is, to make half of your statue in **Carrara** <note>A city in Italy famous for its white marble.</note> and finish the other half on the island of **Paros** <note>original: "Paro"; a Greek island famous for its fine "Parian" marble.</note>. For if the said statue or model by **Phidias** <note>original: "Fidia"; the most famous sculptor of Classical Greece.</note> is sawn through the middle into two parts, and let this sawing or cut be of a flat surface, there, so to speak, where we gird ourselves <note>i.e., at the waist.</note>. Without doubt, trusting in the help of this our <term>definitor</term> or instrument, and aided by it, I will be able to note as many points as I have proposed to note in the circle of the **definitor** pertaining to the sawn surface. If you grant me that these things can be done, you will undoubtedly be able to note and mark also in the whole model any part that you have taken at your will. For you will draw on the model a small red line which in that place will serve you in stead of the intersection of the horizon, where that...

<summary>Alberti explains how to record three-dimensional coordinates (degrees, radial distance, and height) using the definitor. He argues that this mathematical precision allows a sculptor to replicate a statue perfectly, even if the marble is worked in two different locations or if the model is covered in clay.</summary>

<header>S T A T U E.</header>

section—as if the statue were cut through—and the points noted in this place would give you the opportunity to be able to finish the work. The other things will be done for you as was said. Finally, by means of all those things that have been said up to this point, it is seen clearly enough that one can take the measurements and the determinations from a model or from life most conveniently, to make a work or a piece that is perfect by means of reason and art. I desire that this way of working be familiar to my painters and sculptors, who, if they believe me, will rejoice in it. And so that the matter may be more manifest through examples, and that my labors may be of greater benefit, I have taken this trouble to describe the principal measurements that are in man; and not the particulars only of this or that other man, but as far as has been possible for me, I wish to set down that exact beauty, granted as a gift of nature and bestowed as if with certain determined proportions upon many bodies, and I wish to put it also in writing. In this, I imitate him <note>A reference to the ancient Greek painter Zeuxis.</note> who, having to make the statue of the goddess for the people of Croton, went choosing from various virgins—and the most beautiful of all others—the most excellent, rare, and honored parts of beauty that he saw in those young women, and then put them into his statue. In this same way, I have chosen many bodies held to be most beautiful by those who know most, and from all of them I have taken their measurements and proportions; having then made a comparison of these together, and having set aside the extremes (if there were any that exceeded or were exceeded by the others), I have taken from various bodies and models those <term>mediocrities</term> <note>In the classical sense, "mediocrity" refers to the "golden mean" or the ideal average, rather than something of poor quality.</note> that seemed to me the most praised. Having measured, therefore, the principal and most notable lengths, widths, and thicknesses, I have found them to be as follows: for the lengths of the limbs are these.

Through these things, one will be able to easily consider what the proportions are that all the parts of the limbs have toward one another in relation to the total length of the body, and the proportions and correspondences they have among themselves, and in what way they vary or are different. I judge that this must be known, because such science will be very useful. And many things could be recounted which in a man are constantly changing and varying, whether he is sitting, or bending toward this or that other side. But I leave these things to the diligence and accuracy of the practitioner. It will also be of great help to know the number of the bones, and of the muscles, and the extensions of the nerves <note>In this period, "nerve" often referred to tendons or ligaments as well as actual nerves.</note>. And besides this, it will also be greatly useful to know by what rule we shall separate the circumferences and the divisions of bodies by means of the views of the parts that are not seen; as if by chance someone were to saw straight down through the middle of a cylinder, in such a way that the part presented to the eye were divided and severed from that part which is not seen by our eye, so that from this cylinder two bodies were made, of which the base of one would be in every way similar to the base of the other, and would have the same form, the whole being comprised of the same lines and circles, which are four. Similar to this, then, must be the notation, or observation, or separation of the bodies that have been mentioned; since the drawing of that line by which the figure is bounded, and with which one has to separate that surface which presents itself to the eye from that other which is hidden from the eye, must be done in the aforementioned manner. Which drawing inue-

<meta>This page concludes the treatise "On the Statue" (De Statua), transitioning from the specific measurements of the human body to a theoretical discussion on the difference between the painter's and the sculptor's perspective on depth and shadow.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
<header>ON THE STATUE.</header>

...ted <note>Continuing from the previous page's "recounted".</note> by lines; if one draws upon a wall, in the manner required for a wall, it will represent in that place a figure very similar to a shadow that might be cast upon it by a light that happened to be placed in between, and which illuminated it from that same point in the air where the eye of the viewer was previously located. But this sort of division or separation, and this rule of observing things to be drawn in this manner, belongs more to the **painter** than to the **sculptor**, and I shall treat of them at another time. Beyond this, it belongs to whoever wishes to make a profession of this art to know principally how far each **relief** <note>The protruding parts of a sculpture.</note> or **recession** <note>original: "sfondo"; the hollows or depths.</note> of any member whatsoever is distant from a certain determined position of lines.

# THE THREE BOOKS ON PAINTING
## BY LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI
### TRANSLATED FROM LATIN INTO THE VULGAR TONGUE

<column-break/>

->**PREFACE**<-

I used to wonder, and indeed grieve, that so many excellent and superior arts and sciences, which we see flourished in those highly talented and learned ancients, are now lacking and almost entirely lost. For we see that the works of the musicians, geometricians, rhetoricians, philosophers, and all those other noble minds are today so rare that there is hardly anyone to be found who can even understand them, let alone equal them.

<margin>The decline of the arts since antiquity.</margin>

Therefore, I believed it was as many others said: that Nature, the mistress of all things, had already grown old and weary, and no longer produced those giant and generous spirits which, in her youthful and more glorious days, she brought forth so abundantly. 

<insert>But since I have returned from exile to this, our city of Florence,</insert> I have recognized in many—but especially in you, **Filippo Brunelleschi** <note>The architect of the Florence Cathedral dome.</note>, and in our very dear friend **Donatello** the sculptor, and in others like **Nello**, **Luca della Robbia**, and **Masaccio**—a talent for every praiseworthy thing that is not second to any ancient and famous artist. 

<margin>Praise for the Florentine masters.</margin>

I have therefore realized that the power to achieve fame in any great endeavor lies more in our own industry and diligence than in the favor of Nature or the times. I confess that for the ancients, who had many models to imitate and learn from, it was less difficult to rise to the knowledge of those supreme arts which are so arduous for us today. But for that very reason, our fame ought to be much greater if we, without teachers and without any examples, find arts and sciences never before seen or heard of.

<summary>Alberti begins his famous treatise on painting with a preface addressed to Filippo Brunelleschi, arguing that modern Florentine artists are equal to the ancients despite lacking their historical models. He rejects the idea that Nature has "grown old," instead praising human industry and the genius of his contemporaries.</summary>

<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, Painting, Filippo Brunelleschi, Donatello, Masaccio, Florence, Renaissance, Antiquity vs. Modernity</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous treatise "On the Statue" to Alberti's seminal work "On Painting." The preface establishes the Renaissance theme of surpassing the ancients through modern Florentine genius.</meta>

<page-num>63</page-num>

# THE THREE BOOKS ON PAINTING
## BY LEON BATTISTA ALBERTI
### TRANSLATED FROM LATIN INTO THE VULGAR TONGUE <note>original: "in volgare." Alberti originally wrote this in Latin for scholars and then translated it into Italian (the "vulgar" or common tongue) for practicing artists.</note>

<column-break/>

->**PREFACE**<-

I used to wonder, and indeed grieve, that so many excellent and superior arts and sciences, which we see flourished in those highly talented and learned ancients, are now lacking and almost entirely lost. For we see that the works of the musicians, geometricians, rhetoricians, philosophers, and all those other noble minds are today so rare that there is hardly anyone to be found who can even understand them, let alone equal them.

<margin>The decline of the arts since antiquity.</margin>

Therefore, I believed it was as many others said: that Nature, the mistress of all things, had already grown old and weary, and no longer produced those giant and generous spirits which, in her youthful and more glorious days, she brought forth so abundantly. 

<insert>But since I have returned from exile to this, our city of Florence,</insert> I have recognized in many—but especially in you, **Filippo Brunelleschi** <note>The architect of the Florence Cathedral dome.</note>, and in our very dear friend **Donatello** the sculptor, and in others like **Nello**, **Luca della Robbia**, and **Masaccio**—a talent for every praiseworthy thing that is not second to any ancient and famous artist. 

<margin>Praise for the Florentine masters.</margin>

I have therefore realized that the power to achieve fame in any great endeavor lies more in our own industry and diligence than in the favor of Nature or the times. I confess that for the ancients, who had many models to imitate and learn from, it was less difficult to rise to the knowledge of those supreme arts which are so a... <unclear>arduous</unclear>

<summary>Leon Battista Alberti introduces his treatise on painting by praising contemporary Florentine artists like Brunelleschi and Donatello, arguing that modern talent can equal or surpass the achievements of Antiquity. He rejects the idea that Nature has become too "old" to produce great geniuses.</summary>

<keywords>Leon Battista Alberti, On Painting, Filippo Brunelleschi, Donatello, Luca della Robbia, Masaccio, Florence, Renaissance art theory, Antiquity vs. Modernity</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface to "On Painting," where Alberti transitions from praising his contemporaries to explaining his motivation for writing the first theoretical manual on the art of painting.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>

I confess that for the ancients, who had many models to imitate and learn from, it was perhaps less difficult to rise to the knowledge of those supreme arts which today are so laborious for us. But for that very reason, our fame ought to be much greater if we, without teachers and without any examples to follow, find arts and sciences never before seen or heard of. 

<margin>The difficulty of modern invention.</margin>

Who could be so envious or so unrefined as not to praise the architect **Filippo Brunelleschi**, seeing here such a great structure, rising above the heavens, wide enough to cover all the Tuscan people with its shadow? <note>Referring to the massive dome of the Santa Maria del Fiore cathedral in Florence.</note> This work was achieved without the help of any wooden scaffolding or excessive use of timber—a feat which, if I judge correctly, was as incredible to the ancients as it is perhaps impossible in our own time.

<margin>The Dome of Florence.</margin>

But let us set aside these matters. I have decided to write down these brief books on painting for your sake, **Filippo**, so that you might see my devotion to the arts. I have written them in the common tongue <note>original: "in lingua volgare."</note> so that they might be understood by painters who are not learned in Latin. 

<column-break/>

I ask you to read them with care, and if anything seems to you to need correction, let me know. I am well aware that a painter must be a man of broad learning, but I will be satisfied if he understands at least these basic principles of geometry which I have set forth.

->**BOOK ONE**<-

To write about painting in these brief commentaries, we will first take from mathematicians those things which concern our subject. Having understood these, we will then explain the art of painting from its very first principles of nature, as far as our intellect allows.

<margin>The mathematical foundations of art.</margin>

In all this discussion, I beg you to consider me not as a mathematician but as a painter writing of these things. Mathematicians measure the shapes and forms of things with the mind alone, separated from all matter. Since we wish to make things visible, we will use a more "fat" wisdom <note>original: "pingui minerva." A Latin idiom meaning a practical, common-sense approach rather than abstract theory.</note>.

<margin>Practical vs. theoretical geometry.</margin>

We say, therefore, that the first thing to know is that a point is a sign which cannot be divided into parts. A line is a length produced from one point to another. Lines are of many kinds: some are straight, others are curved. 

<summary>Alberti concludes his preface by praising Brunelleschi's dome and introduces Book One, establishing that his theory of painting is built upon practical geometry.</summary>
<keywords>Filippo Brunelleschi, Florence Cathedral, geometry, point, line, practical wisdom, artistic theory</keywords>

# The Binding

-> [Exterior Cover] <-

<note>The volume is bound in dark brown calfskin, a common and durable material for scholarly and personal libraries in the early modern period.</note>

The cover features decorative **blind-tooling** <term>blind-tooling: a technique where heated brass tools are pressed into dampened leather to create a design without the use of gold leaf or pigment</term>. The design is composed of concentric rectangular frames, a style often referred to as a "panelled" binding.

At each of the four outermost corners of the central frame, there is a stamped **fleur-de-lis** <note>original: "fleur-de-lis", a stylized lily used as a decorative symbol, often associated with French heraldry or general elegance</note>. Diagonal lines connect the corners of the inner and outer frames, creating a sense of depth and geometric precision.

<margin>The leather shows signs of wear consistent with frequent use, particularly along the hinges and corners.</margin>

<column-break/>

The spine of the volume (not pictured) likely contained raised bands, which are the horizontal ridges formed by the cords used to sew the pages together. This style of binding was intended to be both functional and a display of the owner's status.

<summary>This page describes the physical exterior of the book, a leather-bound volume featuring traditional blind-tooled geometric patterns and fleur-de-lis motifs characteristic of 17th or 18th-century craftsmanship.</summary>
<keywords>bookbinding, calfskin, blind-tooling, fleur-de-lis, early modern, decorative arts</keywords>

# The Binding

-> [Exterior Surface] <-

The surface of the volume is characterized by a dense, pebble-grain texture. <note>This "pebbled" effect is typical of certain types of processed leather, such as morocco or specially treated calfskin, designed to resist scuffing and provide a tactile grip.</note>

The material appears dark, almost black in color, though this may be the result of age and the accumulation of oils from handling over several centuries. There are no visible titles or gilt decorations on this specific section of the cover.

<column-break/>

<note>The absence of text on the cover was common for private manuscripts; titles were often written on the spine or on a paper label pasted to the front at a later date.</note>

The uniform texture across the surface suggests a high level of craftsmanship in the preparation of the hide, ensuring that the binding would remain durable throughout the life of the manuscript.
